### [1] [IDSPACE: A Novel Document Generator for Reliable Evaluation of Digital Identity Verification Systems [Extended Technical Report]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03052)
*Lulu Xie,Yancheng Wang,Kanchan Chowdhury,Rolando Garcia,Yingzhen Yang,Jia Zou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: IDSpace is a synthetic identity document generation system that addresses the scarcity of real identity documents for evaluating fraud detection tools. It introduces model-guided Bayesian optimization to tune generation parameters, decouples user metadata from control parameters, and supports scanned/mobile-captured documents. The system improves evaluation consistency by 15-45% and training accuracy by up to 9% while releasing a dataset of 359,240 synthetic documents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As services move online, trust institutions (banks, lenders, governments) need to verify remote user identities. While fraud detection tools exist, evaluating and fine-tuning them is difficult because identity documents are sensitive and scarce. Synthetic data generation offers a solution, evidenced by prior work being downloaded over 11,000 times.

Method: IDSpace extends prior research in three directions: (1) Model-guided Bayesian optimization that tunes generation parameters to maximize visual similarity and prediction consistency with target-domain models using only a few target domain samples; (2) Decoupling user-specified metadata (demographics, fraud patterns, capture device) from automatically tuned control parameters (font styles, noise levels, image quality); (3) Expanding beyond template images to support scanned and mobile-captured documents.

Result: IDSpace improves evaluation consistency by 15-45% over baselines (CycleGAN, diffusion inpainting, non-guided optimization) using only a few real samples, while improving training accuracy by up to 9% and SSIM similarity with target domain by 10%. A new dataset of 359,240 high-quality synthetic documents across ten European ID types was also released.

Conclusion: IDSpace successfully enables effective evaluation and fine-tuning of fraud detection tools using synthetic identity documents, requiring only a few real samples from the target domain. The approach combines model-guided optimization with user-friendly configuration, making it accessible without low-level expertise.

Abstract: As services move online, trust institutions such as banks, lenders, and governments must verify the identity of remote users. Fraud detection tools are widely available, but evaluating and fine-tuning them remains difficult because identity documents are sensitive and therefore scarce. Synthetic data generation offers a path forward, and demand is clear: our prior work in this area has been downloaded over $11{,}000$ times (aggregated from eight parts). We introduce IDSpace, extending this line of research in three directions. First, we propose model-guided Bayesian optimization, which tunes generation parameters to maximize both visual similarity and prediction consistency with target-domain models given only a few samples from a target domain. Second, we decouple user-specified metadata (demographics, fraud patterns, capture device) from automatically tuned control parameters (font styles, noise levels, image quality), allowing users to configure evaluations without low-level expertise. Third, we expand beyond template images to support scanned and mobile-captured documents. Experiments show IDSpace improves evaluation consistency by $15-45\%$ over baselines including CycleGAN, diffusion inpainting, and non-guided optimization, using only a few real samples, while improving training accuracy by up to $9\%$ and SSIM similarity with the target domain by $10\%$. We also released a new dataset consisting of $359{,}240$ high-quality synthetic documents across ten European ID types.

</details>


### [2] [Mudragen: Geometrically Supervised Generation of Interacting Two-Hand Mudras for Preserving Indian Classical Dance Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03415)
*Jagadish Kashinath Kamble,Jayanta Mukhopadhyay,Debaditya Roy,Partha Pratim Das*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MudraGen is a conditional diffusion framework that synthesizes realistic RGB images of Samyukta Hasta Mudras (two-hand gestures from Bharatanatyam) using geometry-aware supervision with three novel objectives: Keypoint Loss, Joint Offset Loss, and Shape Consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Indian classical dance gesture datasets are low-resource, and canonical Sanskrit definitions of mudras lack precise textual descriptions, limiting conventional text-conditioned image generation models. This challenges the preservation and transmission of Bharatanatyam dance traditions.

Method: MudraGen introduces three geometry-aware objectives: (1) Keypoint Loss for 3D joint alignment, (2) Joint Offset Loss for inter-hand spatial coherence, and (3) Shape Consistency as an anatomical regularizer encouraging consistent hand morphology while allowing independent hand poses. These guide a diffusion model toward anatomically plausible and well-coordinated hand configurations.

Result: MudraGen surpasses existing state-of-the-art generative approaches in visual realism, anatomical correctness, and preservation of fine hand-pose structure, enabling faithful reproduction of complex Samyukta Hasta mudras.

Conclusion: The method successfully generates culturally grounded and structurally consistent gestures, demonstrating practical applications in cultural preservation and dance education for Indian classical dance forms.

Abstract: Automatic generation of hand gestures is essential for the transmission of Indian classical dance and critical for its preservation. Indian classical dance gesture datasets are inherently low-resource, and the canonical Sanskrit definitions of many mudras lack precise textual descriptions, limiting the effectiveness of conventional text-conditioned image generation models. We present \textbf{MudraGen}, a conditional diffusion framework that synthesizes realistic RGB images of \textit{Samyukta Hasta Mudras} -- interactive two-hand gestures from Bharatanatyam (an Indian classical dance form). Unlike prior work on simple hand signs or single-hand gestures, MudraGen introduces geometry-aware supervision to capture the precise coordination, anatomical validity, and cultural nuance of interacting hands. We formulate three geometry-aware objectives: Keypoint Loss for 3D joint alignment, Joint Offset Loss for inter-hand spatial coherence, and Shape Consistency, which serves as an anatomical regularizer by encouraging consistent hand morphology while allowing independent hand poses. Together, these objectives guide the diffusion model toward anatomically plausible and well-coordinated hand configurations, enabling the synthesis of photorealistic and pose-accurate gesture images. Experimental results show that MudraGen surpasses existing state-of-the-art generative approaches in visual realism, anatomical correctness, and preservation of fine hand-pose structure, enabling faithful reproduction of complex Samyukta Hasta mudras. Beyond quantitative gains, its ability to generate culturally grounded and structurally consistent gestures highlights practical applications in cultural preservation and dance education.

</details>


### [3] [Position: Unlabeled IS NOT Equal to No Human Supervision in Visual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03077)
*Dong Lao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This position paper argues that "unsupervised learning" in computer vision is a misnomer, as different methods embed various forms of human supervision through data curation and training objectives. The authors urge the community to disclose these implicit priors more explicitly for better comparison and methodological clarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that while unsupervised visual learning has grown rapidly, the term "unsupervised" has become an umbrella label that obscures important differences between methods. They note a decline in papers using "unsupervised" in titles at flagship CV conferences since 2021, suggesting the community recognizes this ambiguity but lacks standardized ways to address it.

Method: This is a position paper that analyzes the conceptual landscape of unsupervised learning in computer vision. The authors examine how different data curation schemes and training objectives embed different human priors, and document the trend of declining "unsupervised" terminology in recent CV publications.

Result: The authors find that various "unsupervised" methods rely on substantially different human priors through data selection and learning objectives, yet these distinctions are hidden under the single "unsupervised" label. This makes it difficult to compare research conducted under different assumptions.

Conclusion: The authors advocate for standardized disclosure practices where authors explicitly describe priors in data selection and learning objectives, and specify which pipeline components depend on which assumptions. This would improve academic communication, enable fairer comparisons, and preserve methodological diversity in the field.

Abstract: This position paper argues that the absence of labels does not imply the absence of human supervision in visual learning, and urges the research community to identify sources of supervision more explicitly. Many recent methods in computer vision build upon representations learned from large-scale unlabeled data, and are therefore grouped under the same umbrella term ``unsupervised.'' However, different data curation schemes and training objectives embed substantially different human priors on which models rely, and we argue that one ``unsupervised'' umbrella term is no longer capturing these distinctions. This ambiguity makes it harder to compare unsupervised learning research conducted under different assumptions, coinciding with a sharp decline in papers titled with ``unsupervised'' in flagship computer vision conferences since 2021, despite continued growth of the field. While we fully embrace pre-training as a strong foundation for modern computer vision, we advocate for a community-level effort toward greater conceptual clarity: authors are encouraged to disclose priors in data selection and learning objectives, and to specify which components of a learning pipeline depend on which assumptions. Standardized disclosure practices can improve academic communication, ensure fairer comparisons, and preserve methodological diversity in unsupervised learning.

</details>


### [4] [Exemplar: Classical Priors Complement Frozen Features for Few-Shot Microscopy Segmentation at Native Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03080)
*Michal Průšek,Adam Novozámský,Filip Šroubek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Exemplar is a few-shot biomedical image segmenter that combines frozen DINOv3 self-supervised features with a fixed bank of classical filter responses in one lightweight head, achieving 0.782 IoU on an 11-dataset panel and outperforming 5 forward-pass methods in 54/55 comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of segmenting new biomedical datasets without extensive annotation by leveraging both classical image processing priors and modern self-supervised foundation models in a complementary fusion approach.

Method: Fuses a frozen DINOv3 backbone with a fixed bank of classical native-resolution filter responses in one lightweight head, fitted from support masks alone. The classical bank includes filter responses from classical image processing techniques, while DINOv3 provides self-supervised deep features.

Result: Fused approach achieves 0.782 on 11-dataset panel (vs 0.693 classical bank alone and 0.672 features alone). Beats 5 forward-pass few-shot methods in 54/55 comparisons (52 significant after Holm correction). From single mask: 0.703 vs 0.682 for from-scratch nnU-Net. nnU-Net overtakes at 8 masks but takes 16-77x longer to fit.

Conclusion: Classical priors and frozen self-supervised features are complementary in few-shot segmentation; their fusion in one head achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse biomedical imaging datasets with minimal training data and computational cost.

Abstract: Segmenting a new biomedical dataset usually means a domain-specific model trained on substantial annotation, or a foundation model steered at inference time. We present Exemplar, a few-shot segmenter that fuses a frozen DINOv3 backbone with a fixed bank of classical native-resolution filter responses in one lightweight head, fitted from the support masks alone. In the few-mask, native-resolution regime, classical priors and frozen self-supervised features are complementary: fused in one head, a single fixed configuration spans eleven biomedical imaging datasets. Under the same head, the classical bank alone reaches 0.693 on the eleven-dataset panel, scored by foreground intersection-over-union or centreline Dice, and the frozen features alone 0.672; the bank leads on seven of the eleven and the features on the rest, and fused they reach 0.782. Against five forward-pass few-shot methods, Exemplar leads in 54 of 55 method-dataset comparisons, 52 of them significant after Holm correction. From a single annotated mask it reaches 0.703 on the same panel, against 0.682 for a from-scratch nnU-Net trained on that same mask. At eight masks nnU-Net overtakes it on the panel mean, chiefly on centreline agreement, but takes 16-77x longer to fit.

</details>


### [5] [PointGT: Simultaneous Geometry and Texture Editing for Point-Based Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03341)
*Yanshu Zhang,George Shramko,Pratul P. Srinivasan,Ke Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PointGT is a point-based 3D representation enabling simultaneous editing of object geometry and appearance, combining point-based representations with learned UV mapping to overcome limitations of volumetric methods like 3D Gaussian Splatting that cannot handle geometry and texture edits together.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing volumetric 3D representations (e.g., 3D Gaussian Splatting) produce high-quality photorealistic results but are difficult to edit. Current texture editing methods for such representations are incompatible with geometry edits and deformations, limiting practical applications.

Method: The method combines two key components: (1) a point-based representation well-suited for geometry deformations, and (2) a learned UV mapping technique that enables high-resolution texture editing. This hybrid approach allows simultaneous manipulation of both geometry and texture.

Result: PointGT enables fine-grained editing of both geometry and texture in point-based neural representations while maintaining high rendering quality.

Conclusion: The proposed point-based approach successfully bridges the gap between high-quality 3D reconstruction and editable representations, enabling simultaneous geometry and texture editing that was not previously possible with volumetric methods.

Abstract: We present PointGT, a point-based 3D representation that enables simultaneous editing of object geometry and appearance. Existing reconstruction and view synthesis techniques produce volumetric 3D representations that are high-quality and photorealistic, but are difficult to edit. In particular, recent efforts to enable texture editing for 3D Gaussian Splatting representations are not compatible with geometry edits and deformations. Our method combines a point-based representation that is well-suited for geometry deformations with a learned UV mapping technique that enables high-resolution texture editing. We show that PointGT enables fine-grained editing of both geometry and texture in point-based neural representations with high rendering quality.

</details>


### [6] [Solving the Needle-in-a-Haystack Problem in Mammography Vision-Language Model with Differentiable Subset Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03085)
*Young Seok Jeon,Beatrice Brown-Mulry,Rohan Satya Isaac,Anjana Dissanayaka,Theo Dapamede,Mohammadreza Chavoshi,Judy Gichoya,Hari Trivedi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TopKSigLIP is a vision-language model for mammography that addresses limitations of standard CLIP by introducing a TopK-Patch module for efficient high-resolution patch sampling and a Sup-sigmoid loss to handle report homogeneity. It outperforms existing VLMs on zero-shot mammography tasks including density assessment, BI-RADS classification, finding subtyping, and cancer prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CLIP-style VLM pretraining for mammography shows limited zero-shot performance in clinically important tasks. This is attributed to two overlooked characteristics: (1) the high-resolution nature of mammography images, and (2) the homogeneity of radiology reports driven by predominance of negative/benign findings.

Method: Two key innovations: (1) TopK-Patch module learns to sample sparse high-resolution patches likely containing lesions, bypassing the resolution-batch size tradeoff; (2) Sup-sigmoid loss replaces contrastive loss by extending SigLIP with soft labels from structured data to address report homogeneity.

Result: TopKSigLIP outperforms existing open-source mammography and general medical VLMs on internal and external benchmarks for density assessment, BI-RADS classification, finding subtyping, and cancer prediction under zero-shot evaluation. It remains competitive under linear probing despite using smaller vision encoder and training batches. The TopK-Patch module also achieves superior lesion localization over post-hoc Grad-CAM.

Conclusion: TopKSigLIP successfully addresses the unique challenges of mammography VLM training through architectural innovation (TopK-Patch) and improved loss function (Sup-sigmoid), achieving state-of-the-art zero-shot performance across multiple clinically relevant tasks while maintaining efficiency with smaller models and batches.

Abstract: There is growing interest in adopting CLIP-style vision--language model (VLM) pretraining for mammography. However, models that directly employ the standard CLIP architecture and training objective exhibit limited zero-shot performance in clinically important tasks such as cancer, finding-type, and BI-RADS predictions. We argue that this underwhelming performance is due to neglecting two characteristics of mammography data: (1) its high-res nature, and (2) homogeneity of radiology reports, largely driven by a predominance of negative/benign findings on examinations. We propose TopKSigLIP, a VLM designed to address these two limitations through a novel architecture and learning objectives. Instead of downscaling high-res mammography images to satisfy GPU memory constraints, TopKSigLIP introduces TopK-Patch module that learns to sample a sparse set of high-res patches likely to contain lesions, sidestepping the resolution--batch size tradeoff of VLM training. The sampled patch locations additionally serve as a built-in localization tool. To address report homogeneity, we replace the contrastive loss, which falsely repels semantically similar pairs, with a Sup-sigmoid loss. Sup-sigmoid loss extends the sigmoid loss from SigLIP with soft labels derived from structured data. TopKSigLIP outperforms existing open-source mammography and general medical VLMs on both internal and external benchmarks on density assessment, BI-RADS classification, finding subtyping, and cancer prediction under zero-shot evaluation. TopKSigLIP remains competitive under linear probing despite using a significantly smaller vision encoder and smaller training batches than baselines. The TopK-Patch module additionally achieves superior lesion localization over post-hoc Grad-CAM. Code and weights are made public:https://github.com/Youngseok0001/TopKSigLIP.

</details>


### [7] [P-CORE: Self-Supervised Surface Consistency for Point-Based Neural Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03349)
*Yanshu Zhang,Shichong Peng,Mehran Aghabozorgi,Alireza Moazeni,Ke Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a self-supervised method to enable point-based neural representations to handle large deformations in 3D scene editing, using consistency between deformed and original point cloud predictions without requiring ground truth deformed geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point-based neural representations are desirable for multi-view reconstruction due to their lack of fixed connectivity, but they struggle with holes and surface discontinuities under large deformations. Existing methods lack the ability to adapt to large deformations without ground truth multi-view images of deformed geometry.

Method: The approach generates random deformations and ensures consistency between the predicted surface before and after deformation. Specifically, the surface prediction from the deformed point cloud should equal the deformation applied to the surface prediction from the original point cloud. This is incorporated into attention-based point representations using a learned interpolation kernel (instead of Gaussian kernels) that can adapt to large deformations without adding/removing points.

Result: The framework significantly enhances robustness to large deformations. Experiments on synthetic benchmarks (Neural Editor, Objaverse) show it outperforms existing point-based methods in zero-shot editing and substantially reduces artifacts. Qualitative results on real-world datasets (DTU, Mip-NeRF 360) demonstrate effectiveness.

Conclusion: The self-supervised consistency approach successfully enables point-based representations to handle large deformations without requiring ground truth training data, outperforming existing methods in both synthetic and real-world scenarios.

Abstract: Advances in neural rendering have enabled high-fidelity multi-view reconstruction of 3D scenes. However, free-form non-rigid shape editing remains a significant challenge. Point-based neural representations are highly desirable for multi-view reconstruction because they lack fixed connectivity, which does not constrain the learned surface topology to that of the initialization. Yet this same property causes point-based representations to struggle with holes and surface discontinuities under large deformations. To address this, we propose a novel self-supervised method to enable point-based representations to adapt to large deformations without requiring ground truth multi-view images of deformed geometry. The key idea is to generate random deformations and to ensure consistency in the predicted surface before and after deformation. In particular, the surface prediction from the deformed point cloud should be the same as the deformation applied to the surface prediction from the original point cloud. We incorporate our approach into attention-based point representations, which differ from splatting-based point representations in their use of a learned interpolation kernel between points as opposed to a Gaussian kernel around each point. This learned interpolation kernel can learn to adapt to large deformations, without requiring addition or removal of points. We show that our framework significantly enhances its robustness to large deformations. Experiments on synthetic geometry editing benchmarks (Neural Editor, Objaverse) demonstrate that our approach outperforms existing point-based methods in zero-shot editing and significantly reduces artifacts. Furthermore, qualitative results on the DTU and Mip-NeRF 360 datasets demonstrate our method's effectiveness on real-world scenes.

</details>


### [8] [WireSeg-32K: A Physics-Grounded Synthetic Dataset for Wire Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03102)
*Zilin Dai,Lehong Wang,Yi Yang,Xiang Fei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WireSeg-32k is a synthetic dataset of 32,000 RGB images with instance masks and depth maps for wire instance segmentation, generated using DeformX—a co-simulation pipeline combining Cosserat-rod dynamics with photorealistic Isaac Sim rendering. When used to fine-tune SAM3 via LoRA, it improves real-world mAP@75 by 10.2% over the baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deformable linear objects (wires/cables) are challenging to segment due to their thin geometry, high deformability, and frequent self-occlusions. Additionally, obtaining large-scale instance-level annotations in real scenes is expensive. Existing resources either focus on constrained cable tracing/semantic segmentation or generate visually plausible but physically unrealistic wire deformations.

Method: The authors developed DeformX, a co-simulation pipeline that couples Cosserat-rod dynamics with photorealistic Isaac Sim rendering to generate physically plausible, contact-consistent wire shapes with CAD-based wire assets in diverse visually grounded scenes. They created WireSeg-32k with 32,000 RGB images, instance masks, depth maps, and a complementary real-world test set with annotations. As a baseline, they performed LoRA fine-tuning of SAM3 on this dataset.

Result: LoRA fine-tuning SAM3 on WireSeg-32k alone improves real-world mAP@75 by 10.2% over the off-the-shelf model, demonstrating that physically grounded synthetic data can transfer effectively to real wire perception tasks.

Conclusion: Physically grounded synthetic data (generated via the DeformX pipeline) can successfully transfer to real-world wire perception, providing a practical solution for training data scarcity in deformable linear object segmentation.

Abstract: Deformable linear objects such as wires and cables are difficult to segment because they are thin, highly deformable, and frequently self-occluded, while large-scale instance-level annotations are expensive to obtain in real scenes. Existing resources either focus on cable tracing or semantic segmentation under constrained settings, or generate visually plausible images without physically grounded wire deformation. We present WireSeg-32k, a synthetic dataset for wire instance segmentation with 32,000 RGB images, instance masks, depth maps, and a complementary real-world test set with annotations. To generate this dataset, we develop DeformX, a co-simulation pipeline that couples Cosserat-rod dynamics with photorealistic Isaac Sim rendering, enabling physically plausible, contact-consistent wire shapes, CAD-based wire assets, and diverse visually grounded scenes. As a simple baseline, LoRA fine-tuning SAM3 on WireSeg-32k alone improves real-world mAP@75 by 10.2% over the off-the-shelf model, showing that physically grounded synthetic data can transfer to real wire perception.

</details>


### [9] [TruncGradGS: Improved 3D Gaussian Splatting via Truncated Gradient Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03534)
*Theo Morales,Nhat-Quynh Le-Pham,Robin Atkins,Binh-Son Hua*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a piecewise truncated gradient formulation to address the gradient vanishing problem in 3D Gaussian Splatting optimization, where distant pixels have diminishing gradient magnitudes that lead to suboptimal reconstruction. The method improves optimization stability and robustness across static and dynamic settings, and the authors also introduce a new dataset for benchmarking dynamic Gaussian Splatting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gradient vanishing phenomenon in 3D Gaussian Splatting causes pixels far from Gaussian primitives to have diminishing gradient magnitudes, which negatively impacts primitive attributes and results in suboptimal scene reconstruction. This limitation hinders robust learning from visual input.

Method: The authors propose a piecewise truncated gradient formulation that modifies how gradients are computed and applied during optimization. This approach improves stability and robustness to initializations, and is shown to be generalizable across both static and dynamic Gaussian Splatting settings.

Result: The method consistently improves 3D Gaussian Splatting with both random and COLMAP initializations. It demonstrates effectiveness in static and dynamic settings on public benchmarks and a newly proposed synthetic 3D dataset for dynamic scene benchmarking.

Conclusion: The proposed piecewise truncated gradient formulation successfully addresses the gradient vanishing problem in 3D Gaussian Splatting, providing improved optimization stability and robustness. The introduced dataset fills a gap in dynamic scene benchmarking for Gaussian Splatting methods.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting has become a de facto scene representation for novel view synthesis, yet robustly learning 3D Gaussian primitives from visual input remains challenging. Standard optimization relies on gradient-based updates, but a common issue is the gradient vanishing phenomenon: a pixel far from a Gaussian primitive often has diminishing gradient magnitudes to influence primitive attributes, resulting in suboptimal scene reconstruction. In this paper, we propose a method to address gradient vanishing with a piecewise truncated gradient formulation that improves the optimization stability and robustness to initializations. We show that our method consistently improves 3D Gaussian Splatting with random and COLMAP initializations while being generalizable across static and dynamic Gaussian Splatting. As a by-product, we also examine the limitations of current benchmarks for dynamic scenes, and introduce a novel dataset for benchmarking dynamic Gaussian Splatting using synthetic 3D scenes. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in both static and dynamic settings for the public benchmarks and our proposed dataset.

</details>


### [10] [SLIDEFORGE: An LLM Agent for Controllable Editing of Slides as Structured Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03109)
*Haozhen Zheng,Fulin Wang,Tianhu Xiong,Yingjie Yu,Shengyi Qian,Hanchao Yu,Alex Schwing,Klara Nahrstedt,Mingyuan Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SLIDEFORGE is an agentic framework for controllable AI-assisted slide editing that builds a Deck State Graph linking visual decomposition, native pptx object structure, and perceptual organization. It supports theme-preserving reconstruction while retaining native editability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents can describe slides but struggle with slide editing because outputs must retain layout, style, component structure, and native editability. Existing agents operate on screenshots or weak document representations, causing problems like fragmenting visual units, rasterizing editable content, or breaking layout.

Method: SLIDEFORGE builds a Deck State Graph—an executable slide state that links visual decomposition, native pptx object structure, and perceptual organization. It recovers human-referable components while retaining fine-grained editable structure, supports theme-preserving reconstruction through slide-native operations, and uses rendered-state verification. A new evaluation paradigm measures component recovery, preservation, restyling consistency, visual quality, and native editability.

Result: Experiments show SLIDEFORGE outperforms direct prompting, screenshot-based agents, and generic code-agent baselines across multiple evaluation dimensions.

Conclusion: SLIDEFORGE provides a viable approach for controllable AI-assisted slide editing that preserves layout, style, and native editability, outperforming existing approaches.

Abstract: Current AI agents compellingly describe slides. However, AI-assisted slide editing requires more than understanding: the output must retain layout, style, component structure, and native editability. Towards, AI-assisted slide editing, existing agents operate on screenshots or weak document representations and often fragment coherent visual units, rasterize editable content, or break layout. In contrast, for controllable slide editing, we introduce an agentic framework, SLIDEFORGE, which builds a Deck State Graph, an executable slide state that links visual decomposition, native pptx object structure, and perceptual organization. By recovering human-referable components while retaining fine-grained editable structure, SLIDEFORGE supports theme-preserving reconstruction through slide-native operations and rendered-state verification. We further introduce an evaluation paradigm for controllable slide transformation that jointly measures component recovery, preservation, restyling consistency, visual quality, and native editability. Experiments show that SLIDEFORGE outperforms direct prompting, screenshot-based agents, and generic code-agent baselines across these dimensions. Code is available at https://github.com/UIUC-MONET/SLIDEFORGE.

</details>


### [11] [Building Pretraining Data for World Models: An Unreal Engine-Based Pipeline for Action-Conditioned Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03557)
*Haoyu Wang,Songchun Zhang,Haoran Li,Haoyang Huang,Zeyue Xue,Nan Duan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A synthetic data production pipeline built on Unreal Engine that generates action-conditioned, multi-view video for training world models. The system uses a two-stage approach: real-time physics recording followed by offline rendering, deployed across a 25-server cluster with RTX 5090 GPUs, producing over 8,700 hours of video from 429 levels and 40 characters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Action-conditioned video models require large-scale visual data paired with temporally aligned control signals. However, obtaining such supervision from real-world video is challenging because the actions causing visual changes are typically unknown. This creates a data gap for training world models.

Method: Two-stage pipeline: Stage I runs real-time physics in Unreal Engine's Play In Editor (PIE) to record per-frame character states, control inputs, and camera states into an intermediate trajectory representation. Stage II replays these trajectories in a separate engine process and renders them offline using Movie Render Queue (MRQ). The system includes distributed production infrastructure with cache-aware task partitioning, node-local slot scheduling, automated scene screening, aesthetic/luminance filtering, partial-output recovery, asynchronous upload, and continuous cluster health monitoring.

Result: The production cluster (25 servers × 8 NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPUs) processed 2,384 asset packs, retaining 429 levels and 40 humanoid characters. Output: 2,691 hours of 1080p video and 6,076 hours of 720p video (total 8,767 hours).

Conclusion: The pipeline successfully addresses the synthetic data generation challenge for world model training. The system architecture and implementation decisions emerged from production failures. Limitations include using perceptual quality proxies for world-model data curation, which may not perfectly capture the data quality needed for model training.

Abstract: Action-conditioned video models require large-scale visual data paired with control signals that are temporally aligned with the resulting scene transitions. Such supervision is difficult to obtain from ordinary real-world video because the actions that caused each visual change are typically unknown. We present a large-scale synthetic data production pipeline built on Unreal Engine for generating action-conditioned, multi-view video. To accommodate the different execution requirements of real-time physics and high-quality offline rendering, the pipeline executes trajectory generation and final rendering in two stages: Stage I runs real physics in PIE and records per-frame character states, control inputs, and camera states into an intermediate trajectory representation; Stage II replays those trajectories in a new engine process and renders them offline with Movie Render Queue (MRQ). Around this core, we develop a distributed production system with cache-aware task partitioning, node-local slot scheduling, automated scene screening, aesthetic and luminance filtering, partial-output recovery, asynchronous upload, and continuous cluster health monitoring. The production cluster contains 25 servers with eight NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPUs per server. From 2,384 asset packs, 429 levels were retained for production together with a pool of 40 humanoid characters. The pipeline has produced 2,691 hours of 1080p video and 6,076 hours of 720p video. We describe the system architecture, the implementation decisions that emerged from production failures, and the limitations of using perceptual quality proxies for world-model data curation. The pipeline described in this report constitutes the Unreal Engine synthetic-data production component used in EchoWM.

</details>


### [12] [Beyond Small Patches: Black-Box Detection and Purification of Diverse Backdoor Triggers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03139)
*Ahmed Abdelnaby,Mohamed Elmahallawy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TRIM is a deployment-oriented black-box defense against backdoor attacks that works at inference time by identifying and purifying malicious trigger regions without requiring model internals, training data, or clean samples. It uses region-based segmentation, adaptive trigger discovery through inpainting/diffusion-based reconstruction, and selective purification, achieving ASR as low as 1.16% while preserving clean accuracy up to 87.87%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backdoor defenses require access to model internals, training data, or clean validation samples, making them difficult to deploy in real-world scenarios where only black-box access to a trained model is available. There is a need for a practical defense that can operate at inference time without any auxiliary data.

Method: TRIM employs three key components: (1) region-based segmentation using deep feature representations, (2) adaptive trigger discovery through inpainting and diffusion-based reconstruction to isolate regions responsible for misclassification without assumptions about trigger type/shape/location, and (3) selective region purification that cleans poisoned regions while retaining benign content. It also caches feature embeddings of previously identified triggers for efficient recognition.

Result: TRIM consistently outperforms existing black-box defenses across diverse datasets and backdoor types (blended, sparse, varying-size, and multiple triggers), reducing attack success rates to as low as 1.16% while preserving clean accuracy of up to 87.87%.

Conclusion: Effective backdoor mitigation is possible at inference time even when the defender has no access to any auxiliary data, demonstrating the feasibility of practical black-box defenses for real-world deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Deep neural networks (DNNs) are increasingly deployed in real-world vision systems, yet their predictions can be covertly manipulated by backdoor attacks, in which malicious triggers cause targeted misclassification while preserving high clean accuracy. Existing defenses often rely on model internals, training data, or clean validation samples, making them difficult to deploy when only black-box access to a trained model is available. We propose TRIM (Trigger Removal by Identifying Manipulated Regions), a deployment-oriented black-box defense that detects and selectively removes backdoor triggers at inference time without requiring model internals, training data, or clean samples. The key insight behind TRIM is to identify image regions that are responsible for anomalous model behavior and purify only those regions while preserving benign content. TRIM innovates via three key components: (i) region-based segmentation with deep feature representations, (ii) adaptive trigger discovery through inpainting and diffusion-based reconstruction to isolate regions responsible for misclassification---without assumptions about trigger type, shape, or location, and (iii) selective region purification that cleans poisoned regions while retaining benign content. To support practical deployment, TRIM further caches feature embeddings of previously identified triggers, enabling efficient recognition and avoiding redundant detection and purification. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets and backdoor types, including blended, sparse, varying-size, and multiple triggers, show that TRIM consistently outperforms existing black-box defenses, reducing attack success rates (ASR) to as low as 1.16% while preserving clean accuracy of up to 87.87%. These results demonstrate that effective backdoor mitigation is possible at inference time even when the defender has no access to any auxiliary data.

</details>


### [13] [VeriPhy: Agentic Physical Reasoning for World Model Evaluation and Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03153)
*Wenzhuo Xu,Yuchen Zhu,Chongjian Ge,Xuan Shen,Jing Shi,Jason Kuen,Yongxin Chen,Molei Tao,Christopher McComb,Noelia Grande Gutiérrez,Jiuxiang Gu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VeriPhy is an auditable physical-verification system for generated video that identifies physical violations in AI-generated videos by using a text-only planner to compile prompts into typed physical obligations, with execution that gates observations through frozen low-level experts and returns provenance-carrying evidence records mapped to three-valued states (supported, contradicted, or unknown).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual fluency in generated video does not imply physical reliability, and existing scalar quality scores cannot indicate which physical obligation a clip violates or when it fails. There is a need for auditable physical verification that can trace verdicts to evidence.

Method: VeriPhy uses a text-only planner that compiles prompts into typed physical obligations and a statically validated execution plan before any frame is observed. During execution, observations gate and scope calls to frozen low-level experts (segmentation, tracking, counting, 11 typed physical measurements, depth, OCR, audio-event detection). Each action returns provenance-carrying evidence records. Typed resolvers map usable records to a three-valued state (supported, contradicted, or unknown) with full provenance.

Result: On a 149-clip core with 304 flaw records, VeriPhy accounts for 228, compared to 164 for a published question-decomposition evaluator and 222 for prompting the backbone monolithically. The key differentiator is that each decision retains its evidence record and provenance, making traces auditable.

Conclusion: VeriPhy provides auditable physical verification with full provenance, enabling each verdict to be traced to its evidence. This makes the system usable as an interface through which critic verdicts can be written back into generation, addressing the gap between visual fluency and physical reliability in AI-generated videos.

Abstract: Visual fluency in generated video does not imply physical reliability, and a scalar quality score alone is incapable of indicating the obligation a clip violates or the moment it fails. We present VeriPhy, an auditable physical-verification system in which a text-only planner compiles the prompt into typed physical obligations and a statically validated execution plan before any frame is observed. During execution, observations gate and scope only declared calls to frozen low-level experts (e.g., segmentation and tracking, counting, eleven typed physical measurements over the resulting tracks, depth, OCR, and audio-event detection). Each action returns a provenance-carrying evidence record whose payload, when usable, is either a typed measurement or an explicitly tagged learned state. Typed resolvers and fixed composition map usable records to a three-valued state (supported, contradicted, or unknown, surfaced as plausible, implausible, or abstain) with full provenance, so that every verdict is traceable to the evidence that produced it. We anchor evaluation in a 1,500-clip corpus of human-annotated flaw records that localize real generation failures in prompt reference, space, and time. On a 149-clip core carrying 304 such records, VeriPhy accounts for 228, against 164 for a published question-decomposition evaluator given the same clips and the same claims. Recall alone does not separate it from prompting the same backbone monolithically, which reaches 222; what separates them is that each decision retains its evidence record and provenance, making the traces auditable one verdict at a time and usable as the interface through which a critic verdict could be written back into generation.

</details>


### [14] [Who Speaks for the Pruned? Visual Token Pruning as Coverage Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03158)
*Qingchan Zhu,Weihang You,Hanqi Jiang,Changdi Yang,Tianming Liu,Geng Yuan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoverPruner is a training-free visual token pruning method for vision-language models that asks which surviving token best represents a removed token, rather than just which tokens to keep. It formulates pruning as Representational Coverage Maximization (RCM) using projector-space coverage and a first-layer attention probe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual token pruning methods only ask "which tokens to keep" (supply-side), leading to redundant high-scoring tokens being retained while discarded tokens lack close representatives. This work introduces a complementary demand-side question: after removal, which surviving token represents the removed one?

Method: CoverPruner formulates pruning as Representational Coverage Maximization (RCM), covering the full projected visual-token set with query-weighted demand. It instantiates RCM using projector-space coverage and a lightweight first-layer attention probe. The method is training-free.

Result: CoverPruner achieves the best average accuracy among all compared methods across multiple VLM architectures and compression rates, with the largest gains appearing under aggressive compression.

Conclusion: By asking the complementary demand-side question about token representation after removal, CoverPruner outperforms existing methods, particularly under aggressive compression settings, demonstrating the value of representational coverage in token pruning.

Abstract: Visual token pruning reduces the inference cost of vision-language models (VLMs), but most methods only ask which tokens to keep. This retained-token view can keep redundant high-scoring tokens while leaving discarded evidence without a close representative. We propose CoverPruner, a training-free pruner that asks the complementary demand-side question: after a token is removed, which surviving original token represents it for the target VLM? CoverPruner formulates pruning as Representational Coverage Maximization (RCM), covering the full projected visual-token set with query-weighted demand. It instantiates RCM with projector-space coverage and a lightweight first-layer attention probe. Across multiple VLM architectures and compression rates, CoverPruner achieves the best average accuracy among all compared methods, with the largest gains usually appearing under aggressive compression.

</details>


### [15] [RoboTok: An Internet-Scale Data Engine for Human Demonstration Retrieval and Dexterous Manipulation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03199)
*Howard Qian,Yiting Chen,Yunfei Xie,Kejia Ren,Podshara Chanrungmaneekul,Gaotian Wang,Bowen Wen,Chen Wei,Kaiyu Hang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RoboTok is an internet-scale data engine that retrieves manipulation-relevant human demonstrations from web videos for training dexterous robot policies by learning a latent motion space from 3D hand trajectories in actor-centered reference frames.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot learning requires broad and diverse demonstrations, but collecting robot data is expensive and cannot cover the long tail of real-world tasks. There is a need to leverage internet-scale video data as a scalable source of supervision.

Method: Learns a latent motion space from 3D hand trajectories expressed in estimated actor-centered reference frames. This representation enables comparison of manipulation behaviors across variations in camera viewpoint, scene appearance, and actor occlusions, while remaining compact for efficient search and indexing over internet-scale video collections.

Result: RoboTok retrieves more relevant manipulation demonstrations compared to existing robot-data retrieval approaches and improves downstream task success on robot policies.

Conclusion: Hand-pose trajectory-aware retrieval makes web video a scalable and continuously growing source of supervision for robot learning, establishing this approach as a way to address the data bottleneck in robot learning.

Abstract: Robot learning increasingly depends on broad and diverse demonstrations, yet collecting robot data remains expensive and poorly suited to covering the long tail of real-world tasks. To address this bottleneck, we introduce RoboTok, an internet-scale data engine that, given a query human manipulation video, retrieves manipulation-relevant human demonstrations from web videos for training dexterous robot policies. Specifically, we learn a latent motion space from 3D hand trajectories expressed in estimated actor-centered reference frames. This representation enables manipulation behaviors to be compared across variations in camera viewpoint, scene appearance, and actor occlusions, while remaining compact enough for efficient search and continual indexing over internet-scale video collections. We evaluate RoboTok against existing robot-data retrieval approaches on retrieval benchmarks and downstream robot policy performance. Our results show that RoboTok retrieves more relevant manipulation demonstrations and improves downstream task success, establishing hand-pose trajectory-aware retrieval as a way to make web video a scalable and continuously growing source of supervision for robot learning.

</details>


### [16] [Learning to Zoom Efficiently with a Contrastive Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03206)
*Falko Helm,Iryna Gurevych*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an intrinsic reward method for teaching multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) to use zoom-in tools without requiring warm-start supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The approach uses an InfoNCE-style reward with curriculum learning of increasingly hard negative tool calls. Experiments on V*, HRBench, and MME-RealWorld show competitive performance while being more efficient. When used as a drop-in replacement for SFT, it outperforms all baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for teaching visual agents to use zoom-in tools require extensive warm-start supervised fine-tuning (SFT), which is inefficient and requires additional labels. The authors argue this SFT phase is unnecessary and propose learning tool use through intrinsic rewards instead.

Method: The authors propose an InfoNCE-style intrinsic reward that uses a curriculum of increasingly hard negative tool calls as a contrastive training signal. They also introduce the Muffin&Chihuahua (M&C) synthetic dataset - a grid of images where each cell shows either a muffin or chihuahua, with region-of-interest labels - to directly measure zoom-in ability.

Result: The approach achieves competitive performance on V*, HRBench, and MME-RealWorld while being more efficient than SFT-based methods. When used as a drop-in replacement for SFT, it outperforms all baselines. Analysis of the M&C dataset revealed that recall is the metric that most strongly correlates zoom-in region selection with final task performance.

Conclusion: Intrinsic rewards with curriculum learning can effectively teach MLLMs to use zoom-in tools without warm-start SFT, offering a more efficient and label-free alternative. The M&C dataset provides a scalable way to evaluate zoom-in capabilities, with recall being the key performance metric.

Abstract: Using a zoom-in tool is an important foundational part of modern visual agents, because it allows to efficiently handle tasks involving high-resolution images. Most previous methods need an extensive warm-start supervised fine-tuning phase for teaching models zoom-in. We show that this is not necessary by proposing a new intrinsic reward for learning tool use in MLLMs without the need for additional labels or warm-start SFT. Our InfoNCE-style reward uses a curriculum of increasingly hard negative tool calls as a contrastive training signal. Empirical experiments on $V^*$, HRBench and MME-RealWorld show that our approach is competitive while being more efficient. When used as a drop-in replacement for SFT, we even outperform all baselines. To directly measure the zoom-in ability of models, we further introduce the scalable synthetic Muffin&Chihuahua (M&C) dataset. Each image consists of a grid with every cell either showing a muffin or chihuahua. Leveraging the M&C dataset's unique region of interest labels, we find that recall is the metric that most strongly correlates the zoom-in region with final task performance. Our model and code for reproduction is publicly available under https://github.com/UKPLab/emnlp2026-zoom-in

</details>


### [17] [ProgResViT: Progressive Resolution and Width for Adaptive Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03216)
*Ali Hojjat,Janek Haberer,Olaf Landsiedel*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ProgResViT is an input-adaptive Vision Transformer that performs inference progressively across multiple rounds, starting with low-resolution/narrow subnetwork and refining predictions when confidence is low by reusing representations for higher-resolution/wider subnetwork processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision Transformers typically process every image using fixed input resolution and model width, even though many images can be classified with substantially less computation, leading to inefficient resource usage.

Method: The method introduces Progress-Conditioned Soft Gating (PSG) that conditions token fusion and layer outputs on the current round, block, and input resolution. All rounds share a single backbone, with inference terminating when prediction is sufficiently confident.

Result: Applied to DeiT, ProgResViT yields better accuracy-compute trade-offs than adaptive-width, adaptive-depth, and dynamic-token baselines. With knowledge distillation, a DeiT-based ProgResViT achieves 84.9% top-1 accuracy, slightly exceeding DeiT-III-S. The design also provides favorable trade-offs for self-supervised DINO representations and downstream semantic segmentation.

Conclusion: ProgResViT successfully enables adaptive computation in ViTs through progressive inference, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy-compute trade-offs while maintaining flexibility across different vision tasks including classification and semantic segmentation.

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) typically process every image using a fixed input resolution and model width, even though many images can be classified with substantially less computation. We introduce ProgResViT, an input-adaptive ViT that performs inference progressively across multiple rounds. The first round processes a low-resolution image with a narrow subnetwork. Inference terminates when the prediction is sufficiently confident; otherwise, the model reuses the representations produced in the current round and proceeds with a higher-resolution input and a wider subnetwork to refine its prediction. As all rounds share a single backbone, we propose Progress-Conditioned Soft Gating (PSG), which conditions token fusion and layer outputs on the current round, block, and input resolution. On image classification, applying ProgResViT to DeiT yields better accuracy-compute trade-offs than adaptive-width, adaptive-depth, and dynamic-token baselines. With knowledge distillation, a DeiT-based ProgResViT achieves 84.9% top-1 accuracy, slightly exceeding the reported DeiT-III-S accuracy under a comparable evaluation setting. We show that the same design also provides favorable accuracy-compute trade-offs for self-supervised DINO representations and downstream semantic segmentation. Code is available at https://github.com/ds-kiel/ProgResViT.

</details>


### [18] [Counting Animals in Camera-Traps Image Sequences without Count Labels: Winning Solution to the iWildCam 2021 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03233)
*Fagner Cunha,Juan G. Colonna,Eulanda M. dos Santos*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Camera traps are essential for wildlife monitoring, but counting unique individuals across image sequences is challenging due to temporal discontinuities and lack of count annotations. This paper presents MaxBoxCount, the winning solution to the iWildCam 2021 Challenge, which combines species classification with a counting heuristic using MegaDetector detections to estimate animal counts without requiring count annotations for training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the problem of counting unique individuals in camera trap image sequences, which is important for ecological applications but difficult due to: (1) large temporal discontinuities (approximately 1 frame per second), (2) lack of count annotations for training, and (3) inadequacy of conventional multi-object tracking methods.

Method: The proposed approach, MaxBoxCount, combines: (1) a strong species classification pipeline, and (2) a simple yet effective counting heuristic based on MegaDetector detections to estimate the number of unique individuals without requiring count annotations.

Result: MaxBoxCount won the iWildCam 2021 Challenge, which introduced a benchmark for counting animals at the sequence level under realistic annotation constraints where count annotations are unavailable for training.

Conclusion: The solution demonstrates that a simple counting heuristic combined with strong species classification can effectively estimate animal counts in camera trap images without requiring count annotations, making it practical for real-world ecological monitoring applications.

Abstract: Camera traps have become an essential tool for wildlife monitoring, motivating the development of computer vision methods for the automated extraction of information from these data. While most prior work has focused on species identification, many ecological applications also require estimating the number of unique individuals appearing across short image sequences. This task is particularly challenging because camera traps typically acquire bursts of images at approximately one frame per second, creating large temporal discontinuities that may make conventional multi-object tracking methods unreliable, and because manually collecting individual count annotations is prohibitively expensive. In this work, we describe the winning solution to the iWildCam 2021 Challenge, which introduced a benchmark for counting animals at the sequence level under realistic annotation constraints where count annotations are unavailable for training. Our approach, MaxBoxCount, combines a strong species classification pipeline with a simple yet effective counting heuristic based on MegaDetector detections to estimate the number of unique individuals without requiring count annotations. Code is available at https://github.com/alcunha/iwildcam2021ufam.

</details>


### [19] [An Ensemble-Based Self-Taught Learning Approach for Parking Space Classification Under Limited Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03258)
*Lucas de Oliveira Cunha,Joelton Deonei Gotz,Paulo Lisboa de Almeida,Andre Gustavo Hochuli*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a self-taught learning framework for parking spot classification that uses unsupervised representation learning with convolutional autoencoders to learn transferable visual representations from unlabeled data, combined with an ensemble of heterogeneous autoencoders to improve robustness and reduce annotation requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most deep learning approaches for parking spot classification rely on large amounts of annotated data and exhibit limited generalization across heterogeneous environments, making them impractical for real-world deployment where labeled data is scarce.

Method: The proposed approach employs: 1) Convolutional autoencoders for unsupervised representation learning from unlabeled data; 2) Learned encoders used as fixed feature extractors for supervised classification with limited annotated samples; 3) Ensemble of heterogeneous autoencoders with independent classifier heads and prediction fusion at inference time to enhance robustness and mitigate architectural bias.

Result: Experiments on PKLot and CNRPark benchmarks under cross-dataset evaluation protocols show that the ensemble-based strategy substantially reduces annotation requirements while improving robustness under significant domain shifts, achieving accuracies between 93% and 96% in data-constrained scenarios.

Conclusion: The self-taught learning framework with ensemble of heterogeneous autoencoders effectively addresses the data annotation bottleneck and domain generalization challenges in parking spot classification, achieving high accuracy (93-96%) with limited annotated data while maintaining robustness across heterogeneous environments.

Abstract: Parking spot classification is a fundamental task in intelligent transportation systems, yet most deep learning approaches rely on large amounts of annotated data and exhibit limited generalization across heterogeneous environments. To address these limitations, we investigate a self-taught learning framework based on unsupervised representation learning with convolutional autoencoders. The proposed approach learns transferable visual representations from unlabeled data and reuses the learned encoders as fixed feature extractors for supervised classification with limited annotated samples in the target domain. To further enhance robustness and mitigate architectural bias, an ensemble of heterogeneous autoencoders is employed, with independent classifier heads and prediction fusion at inference time. Experiments conducted on the PKLot and CNRPark benchmarks under cross-dataset evaluation protocols show that the proposed ensemble-based strategy substantially reduces annotation requirements while improving robustness under significant domain shifts, achieving accuracies between 93\% and 96\% in data-constrained scenarios.

</details>


### [20] [MedQA-MM: Shortcuts Behind Medical Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03261)
*Benlu Wang,Yifan Zhang,Jiaqing Yu,Chin Siang Ong,Juncheng Huang,Zhuohao Li,Zhenyu Zhang,Arman Cohan,Hong Yu,Zonghai Yao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper identifies "reasoning inflation" in medical multimodal multiple-choice questions, where benchmark scores can be achieved through cues other than intended image reasoning (e.g., answer wording, non-visual text, image artifacts). The authors propose "route" as an observable input path for answer selection, and through audits and ablations across 6 datasets and 13-model configurations, demonstrate that models often rely on shortcuts rather than true image reasoning. They create MedQA-MM, a shortcut-mitigated subset, to show text-only and options-only accuracy drops significantly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical multimodal MCQ benchmarks credit final answers without distinguishing how they were obtained. Correct answers can come from intended image findings or from spurious cues in answer wording, clinical text, image artifacts, or context. This leads to "reasoning inflation" - overstating model's image reasoning capabilities based on score alone.

Method: The authors conduct prompt- and image-side audits, modality ablations, and matched repairs across six medical multimodal MCQ datasets using a 13-configuration open-model panel. They identify and remove specific cue types (length-gap, absolute/conspicuous, spatial/prepositional) to measure their impact. They also construct MedQA-MM, a 1,000-item shortcut-mitigated subset.

Result: Full-input accuracy: 62.63%; Text-only: 53.96%; Options-only: 29.71%. Removing length-gap cues reduces accuracy by 6.58pp, absolute/conspicuous cues by 3.50pp, and spatial/prepositional cues by 4.77pp. In MedQA-MM, text-only drops to 5.21% and options-only to 12.33%.

Conclusion: Medical image-reasoning claims require route-level evidence rather than score-level interpretation. Models may achieve correct answers through non-visual cues, not true image understanding. The proposed "route" concept provides a framework for evaluating whether models genuinely reason from images.

Abstract: A benchmark score credits final answers, but not the route by which an item can be answered. In medical multimodal multiple-choice questions (MCQs), this distinction matters because a correct answer can be supported by the intended image finding or by benchmark-preserved cues in the wording of answers, non-visual clinical text, visible image text, artificial annotations, or device/context artifacts. We call the resulting score-level overinterpretation reasoning inflation. Here, a route is an observable input path that can support answer selection, not a claim about the model's hidden cognition. Across six medical multimodal MCQ datasets, we separate candidate cues from behavioral evidence through prompt- and image-side audits, modality ablations, and matched repairs that preserve the medical target and answer key. In a 13-configuration open-model panel, full-input accuracy is 62.63%, while text-only and options-only settings achieve 53.96% and 29.71%, respectively. Removing length-gap, absolute/conspicuous, and spatial/prepositional cues lowers accuracy by 6.58, 3.50, and 4.77 percentage points. We also construct MedQA-MM, a 1,000-item shortcut-mitigated subset, where text-only and options-only accuracy fall to 5.21% and 12.33%. This does not imply that models never use images; it shows that medical image-reasoning claims require route-level evidence.

</details>


### [21] [Tensor-based Brain Surface Modeling and Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03302)
*Moo K. Chung,Keith J. Worsley,Steve Robbins,Alan C. Evans*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified computational framework for tensor-based morphometry (TBM) to detect brain surface shape differences between clinical groups using MRI. The method combines surface modeling, Laplace-Beltrami operator-based diffusion smoothing, and statistical analysis to identify localized cortical regions showing structural differences, with demonstration in pediatric brain development studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cerebral cortex has a complex 2D highly convoluted topology, and between different clinical groups, the local surface area and curvature may differ. These shape differences are likely non-uniform across the cortex. Traditional methods needed improvement in detecting and localizing these regional variations effectively.

Method: The approach combines three components in a unified mathematical framework: (1) Surface modeling to represent cortical geometry, (2) Diffusion smoothing using explicit estimation of Laplace-Beltrami operator to increase signal-to-noise ratio in surface metrics, and (3) Statistical analysis to identify regions of significant structural differences. The method computes local surface area and curvature metrics to localize regions of most rapid structural differences.

Result: The method provides a unified framework for detecting and localizing cortical shape differences between clinical groups. The diffusion smoothing technique using Laplace-Beltrami operator improves signal quality. The approach was demonstrated in longitudinal pediatric brain MRI studies to localize gray matter tissue growth and loss regions.

Conclusion: This unified tensor-based surface morphometry approach provides an effective tool for detecting and localizing brain surface shape differences in clinical neuroimaging studies, with particular utility in developmental studies tracking structural changes over time.

Abstract: We present a unified computational approach to tensor-based morphometry in detecting the brain surface shape differences between two clinical groups based on magnetic resonance images. Our approach is novel in a sense that we combined surface modeling, surface data smoothing and statistical analysis in a coherent unified mathematical framework. The cerebral cortex has the topology of a 2D highly convoluted sheet. Between two different clinical groups, the local surface area and curvature of the cortex may differ. It is highly likely that such surface shape differences are not uniform over the whole cortex. By computing how such surface metrics differ, the regions of the most rapid structural differences can be localized. To increase the signal to noise ratio, diffusion smoothing based on the explicit estimation of Laplace-Beltrami operator has been developed and applied to the surface metrics. As an illustration, we demonstrate how this new tensor-based surface morphometry can be applied in localizing the cortical regions of the gray matter tissue growth and loss in the brain images longitudinally collected in the group of children.

</details>


### [22] [Laplacian Frequency Hierarchies for Efficient 3D Gaussian Splatting Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03334)
*Yixiong Yang,Sisheng Zhang,Qingsong Yan,Shaohuai Shi,Qiang Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method to accelerate 3D Gaussian Splatting training by combining Laplacian image decomposition with frequency-staged training. Lower-frequency Gaussian fields are archived after fitting, allowing subsequent fields to optimize higher-frequency residuals without carrying full primitive burden. Achieves 1.73x and 1.21x speedups at 1K resolution, and 1.74x and 1.33x at 4K resolution when combined with Taming-3DGS and FastGS backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting training suffers from continual growth of Gaussian primitives, which increases optimization cost and slows convergence, especially at high resolutions. This creates a key bottleneck for practical applications.

Method: The proposed Laplacian Frequency Hierarchies scheme combines Laplacian image decomposition with coarse-to-fine, frequency-staged training. After fitting lower-frequency structure, the corresponding Gaussian field is archived so subsequent fields can optimize higher-frequency residuals without carrying the full primitive burden. Rendered components are composed in the image domain via Laplacian-style reconstruction at inference time.

Result: The method achieves average speedups of 1.73x and 1.21x at 1K resolution, and 1.74x and 1.33x at 4K resolution on Taming-3DGS and FastGS backbones, respectively. Larger gains are observed on more challenging scenes, with increasingly pronounced benefits at higher resolutions.

Conclusion: Laplacian Frequency Hierarchies is a simple, efficient, and plug-and-play scheme that reduces the number of active Gaussians during training, lowering optimization overhead and accelerating training. It is orthogonal to prior 3DGS accelerations and can be directly combined with strong backbones like Taming-3DGS and FastGS to improve training speed while maintaining competitive reconstruction quality.

Abstract: A key bottleneck in 3D Gaussian Splatting training is the continual growth of Gaussian primitives, which increases optimization cost and slows convergence, especially at high resolutions. We propose Laplacian Frequency Hierarchies, a simple yet efficient 3DGS scheme that combines Laplacian image decomposition with coarse-to-fine, frequency-staged training. After fitting lower-frequency structure, we archive the corresponding Gaussian field so that subsequent fields can optimize higher-frequency residuals without carrying the full primitive burden, and we compose the rendered components in the image domain via a Laplacian-style reconstruction at inference time. This design reduces the number of active Gaussians during training, thereby lowering optimization overhead and accelerating training. The proposed scheme is plug-and-play and orthogonal to prior 3DGS accelerations: it can be directly combined with strong backbones such as Taming-3DGS and FastGS to improve training speed with competitive reconstruction quality. It achieves average speedups of 1.73x and 1.21x at 1K setting, and 1.74x and 1.33x at 4K setting on Taming-3DGS and FastGS, with larger gains on more challenging scenes and increasingly pronounced benefits at higher resolutions.

</details>


### [23] [When Depth Hurts: Reliability-Aware Geometry Distillation for Depth-Free RGB-D Salient Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03378)
*Xuehao Wang,Jiaxin Hua,Runmei Li,Zhenyu Wu,Chenglizhao Chen,Ke Gu,Aimin Hao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reliability-aware geometry distillation framework for RGB-D salient object detection that uses a frozen Depth Anything V2 model as a training-time teacher to transfer geometry knowledge to an RGB-only network, eliminating the need for dataset-provided depth during inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Depth information in RGB-D SOD is not uniformly reliable - issues include missing regions, blurred boundaries, and structural artifacts that can propagate through multimodal fusion and make RGB-D detectors less accurate than RGB-only counterparts. Existing quality-aware approaches remain dependent on potentially defective depth modality.

Method: The method uses a frozen Depth Anything V2 model as a training-time teacher that transfers dense relative geometry, hierarchical spatial attention, and boundary structure to a compact edge-aware geometry branch. Pooled bidirectional interaction aligns geometry with appearance, and a pixel-wise reliability estimator selectively injects compatible geometry. The teacher is removed after training, leaving an RGB-only inference network. Trained on 2,985 RGB-mask pairs.

Result: Achieves best or tied-best result in 26 of 36 metric-dataset comparisons against ten recent RGB-D SOD methods. Shows 13.4% relative MAE reduction on ReDWeb-S. When retrained on DUTS-TR, improves strongest prior F-measure by 4.2% on PASCAL-S, demonstrating transferability beyond specific sensor or dataset domains.

Conclusion: The proposed geometry distillation framework effectively transfers depth-derived geometry knowledge from a teacher model to an RGB-only student network, achieving superior performance while eliminating dependency on potentially defective depth sensors during inference. The method demonstrates strong cross-domain generalization.

Abstract: Depth can resolve appearance ambiguity in RGB-D salient object detection (SOD), yet sensor depth is not uniformly reliable. Missing regions, blurred boundaries, and structural artifacts can propagate through multimodal fusion and make an RGB-D detector less accurate than its RGB-only counterpart. Existing quality-aware approaches regulate observed depth but remain dependent on the same potentially defective modality. We propose \method, a reliability-aware geometry distillation framework developed for RGB-D SOD benchmarks without using dataset-provided depth during training or inference. A frozen Depth Anything V2 model serves only as a training-time teacher, transferring dense relative geometry, hierarchical spatial attention, and boundary structure to a compact edge-aware geometry branch. Pooled bidirectional interaction aligns geometry with appearance, and a pixel-wise reliability estimator selectively injects geometry that is compatible with the current RGB representation. The teacher is removed after training, leaving an RGB-only inference network. Trained on 2,985 RGB-mask pairs, \method{} achieves the best or tied-best result in 26 of 36 metric-dataset comparisons against ten recent RGB-D SOD methods, including a 13.4\% relative MAE reduction on ReDWeb-S. When retrained on DUTS-TR, it also improves the strongest prior $F$-measure by 4.2\% on PASCAL-S, showing that the distilled geometry transfers beyond a particular sensor or dataset domain. Code will be released upon publication.

</details>


### [24] [FoRIS: Progressive Foreground Refinement for Training-Free In-Context Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03384)
*Ming Hu,Jianfu Yin,Mingyu Dou,Miaomiao Zhang,Yao Wang,Cong Hu,Bingliang Hu,Quan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FoRIS is a training-free in-context segmentation framework that treats ICS as a coarse-to-fine progressive refinement process, consisting of three stages: Foreground Purification, Foreground Localization, and Foreground Consolidation. It achieves SOTA performance with 4.5 and 4.8 mIoU improvements in 1-shot and 5-shot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ICS methods directly predict final masks through reference-query matching, which may not effectively handle ambiguous foreground responses. The authors revisit ICS from a classical segmentation perspective, viewing it as a coarse-to-fine progressive refinement process to progressively refine segmentation from coarse/ambiguous to precise/complete foreground structures.

Method: FoRIS proposes a training-free framework with three progressive stages: (1) Foreground Purification - suppresses background distractions, (2) Foreground Localization - localizes discriminative target regions, (3) Foreground Consolidation - recovers complete foreground structures through semantic aggregation.

Result: FoRIS achieves SOTA performance across semantic and part segmentation tasks, with average improvements of 4.5 mIoU in 1-shot setting and 4.8 mIoU in 5-shot setting over existing approaches.

Conclusion: The coarse-to-fine progressive refinement perspective is effective for in-context segmentation, and FoRIS's three-stage approach successfully suppresses distractions, localizes target regions, and recovers complete structures without requiring training.

Abstract: In-Context Segmentation (ICS) aims to precisely segment arbitrary semantic concepts, such as objects or parts, given one or a few annotated visual exemplars. In this paper, we revisit ICS from a more classical segmentation perspective, viewing it as a coarse-to-fine progressive refinement process. Rather than directly predicting the final mask through reference-query matching, we progressively refine the segmentation from coarse and ambiguous foreground responses to precise and complete foreground structures. Building upon this perspective, we propose a training-free in-context segmentation framework, termed FoRIS. Specifically, FoRIS consists of three key stages: Foreground Purification, Foreground Localization, and Foreground Consolidation, which progressively suppress background distractions, localize discriminative target regions, and recover complete foreground structures through semantic aggregation. Experimental results demonstrate that FoRIS achieves SOTA performance across semantic and part segmentation tasks, with average improvements of 4.5 and 4.8 mIoU points over existing approaches in the 1-shot and 5-shot settings, respectively. Code: https://github.com/Xi-Mu-Yu/FoRIS.

</details>


### [25] [Exploring the Potential of Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training for Multi-Source Remote Sensing Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03391)
