Key techniques include: (1) prefix-compatible tree entropy model using causally available decoded contexts, (2) rate-aware refinement scheduling for spatial blocks under prefix budget, and (3) hierarchical prefix supervision to ensure internal nodes are directly decodable at low rates.

Result: Tree-VQ achieves superior performance-efficiency trade-off, delivering the best perceptual compression results with much fewer parameters and lower latency than competing methods.

Conclusion: Tree-VQ provides a practical progressive compression framework that enables decoded images to be progressively improved as more branch symbols are received, rather than requiring re-encoding for different target rates.

Abstract: Vector-quantization based image compression has achieved strong rate--distortion performance, yet most of them still produce a separate compressed representation for each target bitrate. Such variable-rate behavior allows one model to operate at multiple rates, but it does not necessarily provide a progressive bitstream whose prefixes are themselves decodable and can be refined by appending additional bits. We propose \textbf{Tree-VQ}, a progressive tree-structured vector quantization framework for learned image compression. Tree-VQ organizes discrete codewords as a hierarchical binary tree and represents each latent token by a routed root-to-leaf path. Crucially, every prefix of this path corresponds to a valid quantized representation, so shallow internal nodes serve as coarse reconstruction codes and deeper nodes provide successive refinements. This allows a compressed image to be decoded from an early prefix and progressively improved as more branch symbols are received, rather than being re-encoded for different target rates. To make this structure practical for compression, we introduce a prefix-compatible tree entropy model that codes progressive continuation decisions and routed branch refinements using only causally available decoded contexts. We further use rate-aware refinement scheduling to decide which spatial blocks should receive additional tree bits under a given prefix budget, and hierarchical prefix supervision to ensure that internal nodes are directly decodable at low rates. Experiments show that Tree-VQ achieves a superior performance--efficiency trade-off, delivering the best perceptual compression results with much fewer parameters and lower latency than competing methods.

</details>


### [48] [PL-SCEA: Reconfiguring Pretrained Attention for Few-Shot Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03655)
*Xiaoyu Yang,Qixing Wu,Huixian Zhao,Changlong Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes PL-SCEA (Power-Law Self-Correlation Enhanced Attention) to reconfigure the attention mechanism of frozen Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) for improved few-shot industrial anomaly detection. The method constructs token-adaptive self-correlations over value features while retaining semantic context, then uses positive-correlation filtering and power-law reweighting to emphasize salient relations. A lightweight VAE models the resulting features for reconstruction-based anomaly scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) have attention mechanisms designed for semantic aggregation, which may not adequately expose localized texture and structural deviations needed for anomaly localization. The hypothesis is that attention computation can be reconfigured as a task-relevant component for anomaly detection.

Method: PL-SCEA retains semantic context from pretrained query-key attention while constructing token-adaptive self-correlations over contextualized value features. Positive-correlation filtering and power-law reweighting emphasize relations salient relative to each token's relational background, without additional trainable attention projections. A lightweight variational autoencoder (VAE) provides reconstruction-based anomaly representation.

Result: Across MVTec AD and VisA datasets, the framework achieves competitive image-level detection and consistently strong pixel-level localization across evaluated few-shot settings. Ablations show PL-SCEA improves localization with either VAE or memory bank.

Conclusion: Task-aligned attention reconfiguration can improve the anomaly-localization capability of frozen pretrained representations, supporting the view that adapting attention mechanisms to task-specific objectives enhances performance in industrial anomaly detection.

Abstract: Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) provide transferable patch representations for few-shot industrial anomaly detection, but their attention computation is typically inherited from pretraining objectives centered on semantic aggregation. This creates a potential mismatch: token relations that support semantic recognition may not adequately expose the localized texture and structural deviations required for anomaly localization. We therefore investigate the hypothesis that the attention computation of a frozen VFM can be reconfigured as a task-relevant component of anomaly detection. We instantiate this idea with Power-Law Self-Correlation Enhanced Attention (PL-SCEA), which retains the semantic context of pretrained query-key attention while constructing token-adaptive self-correlations over contextualized value features. Positive-correlation filtering and power-law reweighting then emphasize relations that are salient relative to each token's relational background, without introducing additional trainable attention projections. The resulting features are modeled by a lightweight variational autoencoder that provides a fixed-size reconstruction-based representation of category-specific normality. The two stages serve complementary roles: attention reconfiguration shapes how local relational deviations are represented, while reconstruction-based modeling converts deviations from learned normality into anomaly scores. Across MVTec AD and VisA, the complete framework achieves competitive image-level detection and consistently strong pixel-level localization across the evaluated few-shot settings. Ablations further show that PL-SCEA improves localization with either the VAE or a memory bank under the tested setting. These results support the view that task-aligned attention reconfiguration can improve the anomaly-localization capability of frozen pretrained representations.

</details>


### [49] [Rethinking 3D Noise: Learning 3D-Aware Video Priors via Optimization-Free Morphological Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03657)
*Onat Şahin,Mohammad Altillawi,George Eskandar,Carlos Carbone,Ziyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces "3D Morphological Perturbations" as an optimization-free regularizer for 3D scene representations (particularly 3D Gaussian Splatting) to address artifacts in sparse-view settings. It treats each Gaussian as a fundamental building block and applies perturbations via scale, rotation, and pruning to preserve spatial consistency without requiring per-scene optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D scene representations (NeRF, 3DGS) suffer severe artifacts in sparse-view settings. Existing generative 3D artifact fixers require costly paired corrupted/clean renders with per-scene reconstructions. While 2D image augmentations exist as regularizers, there are no explicit equivalents for 3D representations to preserve spatial consistency across views - an essential property for 3D-aware training.

Method: The proposed 3D Morphological Perturbations method: (1) Leverages explicit 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), treating each Gaussian as a fundamental building block analogous to a 2D pixel; (2) Applies perturbations across its morphological parameter space via scale, rotation, and pruning operations; (3) Eliminates per-scene 3DGS optimization loops from dataset curation; (4) Enables models to learn stronger geometric priors than sparse-view baselines; (5) Scaled to a 14B-parameter video model via ControlNet.

Result: The method: (1) Maintains visual fidelity; (2) Reduces mean depth error by 12.5% over state-of-the-art image-to-image 3D artifact refiners; (3) Boosts downstream robotics policy success rates by up to 8.0% across 3 of 4 manipulation tasks; (4) Enables stronger geometric priors than sparse-view baselines in diagnostic ablations on a lightweight video diffusion sandbox.

Conclusion: 3D Morphological Perturbations provides an effective optimization-free regularizer for 3D representations that preserves spatial consistency across views. It eliminates the need for costly per-scene 3DGS optimization during dataset curation while achieving superior performance in depth estimation and downstream robotics tasks compared to existing state-of-the-art methods.

Abstract: 3D scene representations like NeRF and 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) suffer severe artifacts in sparse-view settings. Recent generative 3D artifact fixers attempt to address this, but rely on paired corrupted and clean renders requiring costly, per-scene reconstructions across varying view configurations. While 2D image augmentations act as instant regularizers, no explicit equivalents exist for 3D representations to preserve spatial consistency across views, an essential property for 3D-aware training. We propose 3D Morphological Perturbations as an optimization-free regularizer that preserves spatial consistency. Leveraging explicit 3DGS, we treat each Gaussian as a fundamental building block - analogous to a 2D pixel - and apply perturbations across its morphological parameter space via scale, rotation, and pruning. Our method eliminates per-scene 3DGS optimization loops from dataset curation while enabling models to learn stronger geometric priors than sparse-view baselines in diagnostic ablations conducted on a lightweight video diffusion sandbox. Scaled to a 14B-parameter video model via ControlNet, our approach maintains visual fidelity while reducing mean depth error by 12.5% over state-of-the-art image-to-image 3D artifact refiners, ultimately boosting downstream robotics policy success rates by up to 8.0% across 3 of 4 manipulation tasks.

</details>


### [50] [Cross-Dataset Transfer and Reliability of Explainable Artificial Intelligence for RhythmFormer Remote Photoplethysmography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03663)
*Louis Chen,Torbjörn E. M. Nordling*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper quantifies explanation methods for remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) models, evaluating whether attention-based explanations can predict model performance across different conditions (illumination, motion). The study finds that skin coverage and faithfulness metrics provide complementary but not predictive information about model accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current rPPG explanations rely on heatmap inspection without quantitative evidence. The researchers aimed to determine if explanation methods transfer between datasets and whether they can predict model performance.

Method: Trained 8 condition-specific RhythmFormer models on NCKU-rPPG under various conditions (3 illumination levels, speaking, rotation, cycling). Compared 4 explanation methods (Raw attention, rollout, attention flow, Beyond Intuition) using skin coverage and SaCo metrics. Evaluated on both NCKU-rPPG and UBFC-rPPG datasets.

Result: Beyond Intuition performed best (coverage 0.789, SaCo 0.837 on Static level 3). Within-participant analysis showed no relationship between explanation metrics and performance measures (186/252 coefficients below |ρ|=0.10). Only Beyond Intuition's coverage correlated with performance (ρ=-0.43 to +0.57). The method failed at 40 lux (coverage 0.180, SaCo -0.178).

Conclusion: Skin coverage and SaCo provide complementary but not predictive information about model performance. Attributing to skin does not guarantee accurate estimates. Attribution reveals where the model looks, not how faithfully the map is ordered.

Abstract: Background. Remote photoplethysmography estimates the cardiovascular pulse from facial video, and its explanations have rested on inspecting heatmaps rather than on quantitative evidence about where a model reads it. We quantified the explanations and asked whether such explanations transfer between datasets and track model performance. Method. We trained eight condition-specific RhythmFormer models on NCKU-rPPG, recorded under three illumination levels, speaking, rotation, and cycling, estimated one heart rate per 5.12-second clip, and set them beside a UBFC-rPPG reproduction. Raw attention, rollout, attention flow, and Beyond Intuition were assessed by skin coverage and the Salience-guided Faithfulness Coefficient (SaCo). Results. Beyond Intuition ranked highest on both datasets, at median coverage 0.789 and SaCo 0.837 on Static level 3 against 0.826 and 0.917 on UBFC-rPPG; lower ranks differed. Within one participant of one condition, neither measure was related to a clip's heart-rate error, waveform correlation, or signal-to-noise ratio on either dataset: 186 of the 252 coefficients fell below $|ρ|=0.10$ and 28 reached $p<0.05$ against the 13 expected by chance. Across the eight scenarios only Beyond Intuition's coverage followed the three performance measures, at $ρ=-0.43$, $+0.57$, and $+0.43$, while the attention-only methods' SaCo ran opposite to each. It failed at 40 lux alone, its median coverage falling to 0.180 and its median SaCo to $-0.178$, whereas motion degraded the estimates far more without such a drop. Conclusions. Skin coverage and SaCo carry information complementary to the performance measures rather than a proxy for them: attributing to the skin does not guarantee an accurate estimate. What an attribution reveals about a condition is where the model looks rather than how faithfully its map is ordered.

</details>


### [51] [ARCOS: Zero-shot Boundary Localization for Corneal Layer Segmentation Across Optical Coherence Tomography Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03668)
*Nuno Vivas Brás,Benjamin Memmi,Maëlle Bouhassane,Cristina Georgeon,Vincent Borderie,Karsten Plamann,Anatole Chessel*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ARCOS is a patch-based zero-shot framework for automatic segmentation of corneal layers in anterior-segment OCT images. It predicts boundary heatmaps from overlapping patches, uses multi-scale feature fusion with self-conditioned refinement, and achieves high boundary localization accuracy (95.1% on matched-device, 84.3% on cross-device) with low thickness estimation errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic corneal layer segmentation in OCT is challenging due to thin interfaces, speckle noise, and device variability. Existing methods struggle with cross-device generalization, necessitating a zero-shot approach that can adapt to different acquisition devices without retraining.

Method: Patch-based zero-shot boundary localization framework using: (1) overlapping native-resolution patches for boundary heatmap prediction, (2) multi-scale feature fusion, (3) self-conditioned refinement module using intermediate boundary information, (4) stitching patch-level predictions across full B-scans to obtain continuous layer segmentations.

Result: On matched-device test set: 95.1% off-by-one boundary localization accuracy, 0.514 pixels mean absolute boundary error. On zero-shot cross-device evaluation: 84.3% average off-by-one accuracy, 0.855 pixels mean absolute boundary error. Low thickness estimation errors across corneal regions.

Conclusion: ARCOS outperforms baseline models in both boundary localization and cross-device generalization, demonstrating robust performance for quantitative corneal OCT analysis across different acquisition devices without requiring device-specific training.

Abstract: Accurate segmentation of corneal layers in optical coherence tomography (OCT) is essential for quantitative assessment of corneal morphology, including layer thickness and structural changes associated with disease or surgery. However, automatic segmentation remains challenging because corneal interfaces are thin, affected by speckle noise, and variable across acquisition devices. In this work, we propose ARCOS, a patch-based zero-shot boundary localization framework for corneal layer segmentation in clinical anterior-segment OCT images. Rather than performing conventional region classification, the method predicts boundary heatmaps for the main corneal interfaces from overlapping native-resolution patches. Patch-level predictions are stitched across the full B-scan and converted into boundary locations to obtain continuous, anatomically ordered layer segmentations. The network combines multi-scale feature fusion with a self-conditioned refinement module that uses intermediate boundary information to improve local heatmap predictions while preserving spatial detail. The method was evaluated on clinical OCT images acquired from multiple devices and compared with representative segmentation baselines using boundary localization and derived thickness metrics. The proposed method achieved an off-by-one boundary localization accuracy of 95.1% and a mean absolute boundary error of 0.514 pixels on the matched-device test set. In zero-shot cross-device evaluation, it maintained an average off-by-one accuracy of 84.3% and a mean absolute boundary error of 0.855 pixels across unseen acquisition devices, outperforming the baseline models. Thickness estimates derived from the predicted boundaries showed low error across corneal regions, supporting the method's use for quantitative corneal OCT analysis.

</details>


### [52] [Do Video Generators Track the World Across Segments? A Benchmark and Method for World-State Reasoning in Video Continuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03673)
*Yingmao Miao,Pengfei Zhang,Chaoran Xu,Meng Yu,Jing Tang,Xiangxiang Chu,Chao Shen,Chenhao Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Statebench is a benchmark for evaluating video continuation models on their ability to track and predict world states across three categories: past-visible states, occluded-process states, and complex-transition states. StateAgent is a proposed method that maintains an explicit entity-state representation to improve controlled video continuation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video generators use historical observations (recent frames, key frames, memory banks) as memories, but they fail to reliably transform this evidence into a coherent world-state interface. The key problem is that past frames may not describe the state needed for the next segment - some states must be inferred from occluded or implicit changes rather than copied directly from observed frames. The paper addresses this gap by asking: given a previous video, its prompt, and a new prompt, can a model generate a continuation that reflects the state determined by both the historical video and the new prompt?

Method: The paper introduces StateAgent, which explicitly maintains an entity-state representation, updates it under the new prompt, grounds the predicted post-action state as a future end frame, and renders the next video. The method is evaluated on Statebench, a benchmark targeting three state categories: (1) past-visible states, (2) occluded-process states, and (3) complex-transition states.

Result: The method improves controlled video continuation by raising the all-case state score (SCS-All) from 45.2 to 69.3, and also benefits story generation at the one-minute scale.

Conclusion: StateAgent demonstrates that explicitly maintaining and updating entity-state representations significantly improves video continuation quality. The approach successfully handles state inference from occluded or implicit changes rather than relying solely on directly observed frames. The benchmark and code are publicly available.

Abstract: Video generators build long videos by composing shorter parts, either by generating segments one after another or by autoregressively extending chunks. Each new part usually depends on memories of historical observations, such as recent frames, selected key frames, memory banks, or cached features. These memories preserve visible evidence from the past, but current generators do not reliably turn such evidence into a world-state interface: what holds in the video world after previous actions and how it should change under the next prompt. A past frame remains valid history, but it may not describe the state needed by the next segment; some states must instead be inferred from occluded or implicit changes rather than copied from a directly observed frame. This creates a simple but overlooked question for video continuation: given a previous video, its prompt, and a new prompt, can a model generate a continuation that reflects the state determined by both the historical video and the new prompt? To answer this question, we introduce Statebench, a benchmark that targets this gap by testing continuations over three state categories: past-visible states, occluded-process states, and complex-transition states. We further propose Stateagent, which explicitly maintains an entity-state representation, updates it under the new prompt, grounds the predicted post-action state as a future end frame, and renders the next video. Experiments show that our method improves controlled video continuation by raising the all-case state score (SCS-All) from 45.2 to 69.3, and also benefits story generation at the one-minute scale. Code is avaliable at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/StateAgent.

</details>


### [53] [CoFiE: Coarse-to-Fine Evidence Selection for Efficient Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03675)
*Jing Jiang,Yiran Ling,Ruonan Li,Dimitrios Stamoulis,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoFiE proposes a coarse-to-fine evidence selection framework for efficient streaming video understanding in Vision Language Models. It performs query-agnostic filtering before vision encoding to reduce redundant frame encoding, followed by query-specific refinement during LLM prefill, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-off with up to 2.54x latency improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing token pruning and memory-bank methods reduce visual tokens only after expensive frame encoding, leaving the encoding cost unaddressed. This limits end-to-end latency reduction. The key insight is that substantial redundancy exists before encoding, and query-specific refinement can occur once semantic information becomes available.

Method: CoFiE introduces two novel mechanisms: (1) Novelty-Guided Frame Filtering - a coarse, query-agnostic stage that filters frames before the vision encoder by retaining visually distinctive candidate frames; (2) Query-Specific Evidence Refinement - a fine, query-specific stage during LLM prefill that selects the most relevant frames to the user query. This decoupling removes redundancy before encoding while preserving query-specific selection capabilities.

Result: CoFiE achieves 78.86% accuracy on StreamingBench and 68.72% on OvO-Bench, improving up to 3.15% over prior methods. Even with up to 80% evidence-frame filtering, it outperforms strong open-source multimodal models.

Conclusion: The coarse-to-fine evidence selection paradigm effectively addresses the efficiency bottleneck in streaming video understanding. By filtering redundant frames before encoding and refining evidence based on query semantics during LLM prefill, CoFiE establishes a new state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-off, demonstrating that early filtering combined with late refinement is a promising direction for real-time video understanding systems.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding requires Vision Language Models (VLLMs) to process growing video streams and answer user questions under tight latency constraints. Existing methods improve efficiency through token pruning and memory-bank schemes, but mainly reduce visual tokens after visual encoding. Consequently, downstream token pruning alone cannot substantially reduce end-to-end latency because the expensive frame encoding cost has already been incurred. We propose CoFiE, a Coarse-to-Fine Evidence Selection framework that decouples evidence selection into a coarse, query-agnostic filtering stage before the vision encoder and a fine, query-specific refinement stage during LLM prefill. CoFiE introduces Novelty-Guided Frame Filtering to retain visually distinctive candidate frames and Query-Specific Evidence Refinement to select the frames most relevant to the user query. This design removes substantial redundancy before frame encoding while preserving query-specific refinement once semantic information becomes available. Experiments show that CoFiE establishes a new state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-off across multiple video understanding benchmarks, reaching 78.86% accuracy on StreamingBench and 68.72% on OvO-Bench, with improvements of up to 3.15% over prior methods. Even with up to 80% evidence-frame filtering, CoFiE outperforms strong open-source multimodal models while improving end-to-end inference latency by up to 2.54 times.

</details>


### [54] [Understanding Autonomous Driving Datasets by Describing Differences between Image Subsets in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03677)
*Julian Truetsch,Felix Hauser,Christoph Stiller,Frank Bieder*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces "set difference captioning" for autonomous driving datasets - a method to automatically generate natural-language descriptions of differences between two subsets of images (e.g., different geographic domains). It proposes an object-centric approach using detection-derived patches and introduces a new benchmark (AD-Diff Bench) for evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shift between locations in autonomous driving can cause dangerous performance degradation when the operating environment differs from training data. Existing data analysis methods rely on limited metadata, predefined labels, or manual inspection, which lack semantic depth and don't scale. There is a need for practical, human-interpretable dataset introspection tools.

Method: The approach adapts a two-stage set difference captioning formulation to autonomous driving by focusing on object-centric patches derived from object detection. This simplifies aggregation and enables attribution of differences to specific object instances or categories. Experiments use open-weight models for reproducibility. The method is evaluated on the newly introduced AD-Diff Bench benchmark with low-concentration experiments assessing sparse, real-world differences.

Result: The paper presents a new benchmark (AD-Diff Bench) for evaluating set-difference-captioning in autonomous driving contexts. Low-concentration experiments demonstrate the suitability of the approach for detecting sparse, real-world differences between datasets. The object-centric patch-based method enables attribution of differences to specific object instances or categories.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark and analysis represent a step toward practical, human-interpretable dataset introspection for autonomous driving datasets. The approach enables scalable semantic analysis of dataset composition differences, potentially helping identify domain shift issues before deployment. Implementation and benchmark data are made publicly available.

Abstract: Understanding the composition of large-scale autonomous driving datasets is essential for safety, robustness, and reliable operation across domains. For example, domain shift between locations could lead to the operating environment being misaligned with the training data, resulting in potentially dangerous performance degradation. Yet, existing data analysis pipelines largely rely on metadata, predefined labels, or manual inspection, which provide limited semantic insight or do not scale. This paper studies set difference captioning: given two subsets of images, the goal is to produce a natural-language hypothesis describing differences between the target and reference set. Building on a two-stage formulation, we adapt the method to autonomous driving by focusing on object-centric patches derived from object detection, which simplifies aggregation and enables attribution of differences to specific object instances or categories. To evaluate this setting in-domain, we introduce a new benchmark, AD-Diff Bench. Low-concentration experiments assess the suitability of set-difference-captioning approaches to sparse, real-world differences. We restrict our experiments to open-weight models to support reproducibility and ease of deployment. The proposed benchmark and analysis provide a step towards practical, human-interpretable dataset introspection for autonomous driving datasets. Our implementation and benchmark dataset are available at https://github.com/KIT-MRT/AD-Diff

</details>


### [55] [DropClick: Semi-Automated One-Click Segmentation for Agricultural Robotic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03680)
*Patrick Zimmer,Michael Halstead,Chris McCool*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DropClick is a click-guided semi-automated segmentation tool for agricultural robotics that reduces annotation effort by generating pseudo-labels from single-click inputs, achieving mIoU of 70.0/72.6 on SB20/BUP20 datasets while maintaining performance even with 50% missing clicks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Labelling vision datasets for segmentation tasks is laborious and costly, particularly in agricultural robotics where specialized datasets are needed for plant and fruit segmentation.

Method: DropClick uses single-click inputs on objects to generate pseudo-labels. It is a semi-automated approach that does not require a click for every object in the scene. The model is first trained on a small subset of just 5 images, then deployed as a one-click segmentation system. For validation, DropClick outputs were used to train a Mask2Former model in a semi-supervised manner.

Result: DropClick achieves mIoU of 70.0 and 72.6 on SB20 and BUP20 respectively as a one-click system. With 50% clicks missing, it maintains mIoU of 68.9 and 71.3. As a pseudo-labeling approach with Mask2Former, it achieves AP50 of 70.1 vs 70.7 (with all clicks) for SB20 and 77.0 for both for BUP20, while saving 46.3% and 31.9% of input effort respectively.

Conclusion: DropClick effectively reduces annotation effort in agricultural robotics datasets. It maintains high performance even with significant reduction in user clicks (50%), and can be used as a pseudo-labeling tool to train other segmentation models like Mask2Former with minimal performance loss but substantial input savings.

Abstract: Labelling vision datasets, especially for segmentation tasks, is a laborious and costly process that stymies novel developments in agricultural robotics. In this paper, we present DropClick, a click-guided segmentation tool that simplifies the annotation process. Our system utilises single-click inputs on objects to generate pseudo-labels, which can replace manual annotations. DropClick stands out as it is a semi-automated approach and does not require a click for every object in the scene. It can therefore further reduce the required amount of user input drastically. We evaluate our method on two challenging agricultural robotic datasets, SB20 and BUP20 for plant and fruit segmentation, respectively. DropClick is first trained on a small subset of just 5 images from the original training data. This DropClick model can then be deployed as a one-click segmentation system and achieves comparable or higher performance than other one-click methods achieving an mIoU of 70.0 and 72.6 points, for SB20 and BUP20 respectively. DropClick then excels at maintaining high performance when clicks are not given (e.g. dropped); when 50% of the clicks are missing it still maintains an mIoU of 68.9 and 71.3 points, for SB20 and BUP20 respectively. We validate DropClick as a pseudo-labelling approach by taking its outputs to train a Mask2Former instance-based segmentation model in a semi-supervised manner. In this process, partially removing user input from DropClick yields similar high performance when compared to providing all clicks, at 70.1 vs 70.7 points AP50 for SB20 and no difference for BUP20 at 77.0 for both models; at the same time saving 46.3% of total input for SB20 and 31.9% for BUP20.

</details>


### [56] [ToPO: Token-Conditioned Preference Routing for Attention-Based Latent Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03688)
*Juntao Xu,Shihong Li,Hoi Fan Au,Ning Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ToPO (Token-Oriented Preference Optimization) is a new method for optimizing attention-based, noise-prediction latent diffusion models that outperforms Diffusion-DPO by using a per-minibatch spatial-temporal route with branchwise squared-residual contrast and preferred-branch cross-attention, achieving higher endpoint estimates on SD-1.5, SDXL metrics and in blind A/B studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pairwise preference labels rank complete images, yet Diffusion-DPO applies their effect over many spatial and denoising-time coordinates, which may be inefficient. ToPO aims to address this by constructing a more targeted, token-oriented approach for preference optimization in latent diffusion models.

Method: ToPO constructs a per-minibatch, detached, separable spatial-temporal route from branchwise squared-residual contrast in a frozen reference denoiser. It uses preferred-branch cross-attention with content tokens to modulate the spatial factor, and adds an auxiliary pixel-midpoint ordering term without local labels or a learned reward model.

Result: ToPO has higher endpoint estimates than Diffusion-DPO on all five reported SD-1.5 metrics and on HPSv2, ImageReward, and CLIP for SDXL. It also receives larger raw win shares in an aggregate blind SDXL A/B study.

Conclusion: ToPO outperforms Diffusion-DPO in preference optimization for latent diffusion models, though these findings are scoped to the reported equal-update U-Net protocols rather than an equal-compute comparison.

Abstract: Pairwise preference labels rank complete images, yet Diffusion-DPO applies their effect over many spatial and denoising-time coordinates. For attention-based, noise-prediction latent diffusion, ToPO (Token-Oriented Preference Optimization) constructs a per-minibatch, detached, separable spatial-temporal route from branchwise squared-residual contrast in a frozen reference denoiser. Preferred-branch cross-attention uses content tokens to modulate the spatial factor, and an auxiliary pixel-midpoint ordering term is added without local labels or a learned reward model. In matched three-seed retrainings with a shared update schedule, ToPO has higher endpoint estimates than Diffusion-DPO on all five reported SD-1.5 metrics and on HPSv2, ImageReward, and CLIP for SDXL. It also receives larger raw win shares in an aggregate blind SDXL A/B study. These findings are scoped to the reported equal-update U-Net protocols rather than an equal-compute comparison.

</details>


### [57] [Semantic-Aware Subgraph State Space Model for WSI Classification in Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03689)
*Feixing Chen,Hao Lu,Lin Luo,Yan Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A semantic-aware subgraph state space model (SASG-SSM) for whole slide image (WSI) classification that preserves spatial organization of tissue structures by adaptively grouping patches into semantic-aware subgraphs and combining graph neural networks with Mamba-based state space encoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Histopathological subtyping relies on recognizing histological patterns that span irregularly shaped tissue regions (semantic units). Conventional patch-based methods fragment these units and fail to preserve internal spatial organization, while efficiently modeling relationships among spatially separated units remains challenging.

Method: The framework uses Semantic-Aware Subgraphs (SASGs) to approximate irregular semantic units by adaptively grouping spatially connected patches guided by class-agnostic visual-semantic priors. Patches are represented as graph nodes with adjacency edges to preserve spatial organization. A Subgraph State Space Module (SG-SSM) combines a graph neural network encoder for intra-subgraph topology encoding with a Mamba-based state space encoder for efficient contextualization across subgraphs.

Result: Extensive experiments across four WSI subtyping datasets demonstrate consistent advantages over representative state-of-the-art methods. Further evaluations under small-cohort and few-shot settings demonstrate robustness and data efficiency under limited training data.

Conclusion: SASG-SSM provides a flexible and efficient framework for WSI classification that addresses limitations of conventional patch-based methods by preserving spatial organization and efficiently modeling relationships among spatially distributed regions, showing particular promise in data-limited scenarios.

Abstract: Histopathological subtyping relies on the recognition of characteristic histological patterns. These patterns may be expressed by individual tissue structures or by the spatial distribution and co-occurrence of multiple structures, and they often span irregularly shaped tissue regions, termed semantic units in this work. However, conventional patch-based representations may fragment such units and fail to explicitly preserve their internal spatial organization, while efficiently modeling relationships among numerous spatially separated units remains challenging. To address these limitations, we propose the Semantic-Aware Subgraph State Space Model (SASG-SSM), a flexible and efficient framework for whole slide image (WSI) classification. Semantic-Aware Subgraphs (SASGs) first approximate irregularly shaped semantic units by adaptively grouping spatially connected patches guided by class-agnostic visual-semantic priors. By representing patches as graph nodes with adjacency edges, SASGs preserve their internal spatial organization rather than treating them as an unordered set. A Subgraph State Space Module (SG-SSM) subsequently combines a graph neural network encoder for intra-subgraph topology encoding with a Mamba-based state space encoder for efficient contextualization across large numbers of subgraphs. This module integrates local structural information within semantic units with global contextual information arising from their distribution and co-occurrence across the WSI, while efficiently modeling a large number of spatially distributed regions. Extensive experiments across four WSI subtyping datasets demonstrate consistent advantages over representative state-of-the-art methods. Further evaluations under small-cohort and few-shot settings demonstrate robustness and data efficiency under limited training data. Code will be released at https://github.com/HLSvois/SASG-SSM.

</details>


### [58] [MetaStructAtlas: A Grounded 3D Vision-Language Dataset and Benchmark for Functional and Structural Reasoning in Whole-Body PET/CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03690)
*Chenguang Zheng,Le Xue,Yichi Zhang,Wenbo Zhang,Zehui Ling,Gang Feng,Xin Gao,Yuan Qi,Yuan Cheng,Zixin Hu,Mei Tian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MetaStructAtlas is a large-scale dataset for grounded whole-body PET/CT interpretation that combines multimodal imaging (490 PET/CT volumes) with anatomical, metabolic, and semantic annotations (50,470 organ segmentation masks and grounded radiology reports), accompanied by MetaStructVQA benchmark with 100,565 QA pairs for 3D visual question answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3D medical vision-language models are limited to regional CT imaging, creating a critical gap in comprehensive whole-body PET/CT analysis, which is essential for clinical diagnosis combining metabolic function and anatomical structure.

Method: Developed MetaStructAtlas with 490 co-registered 3D PET/CT volumes containing 50,470 organ-level segmentation masks and grounded radiology reports. Created MetaStructVQA benchmark with 100,565 QA pairs that explicitly link diagnostic queries to visual evidence across modalities (anatomical, morphological, and metabolic characteristics).

Result: Established a robust foundation for multimodal representation learning and integrated whole-body reasoning in nuclear medicine by evaluating state-of-the-art 3D medical VLMs on the MetaStructVQA benchmark.

Conclusion: MetaStructAtlas addresses the void in whole-body PET/CT analysis by providing comprehensive multimodal annotations and a standardized 3D grounded VQA benchmark, enabling interactive reasoning and supporting future research in nuclear medicine imaging.

Abstract: The joint interpretation of metabolic function and anatomical structure is essential for clinical diagnosis in whole-body PET/CT. Although recent advances in 3D medical vision-language models have demonstrated remarkable progress, current efforts are limited to regional CT imaging, leaving a critical void in comprehensive whole-body PET/CT analysis. In this work, we introduce MetaStructAtlas, a large-scale dataset for grounded whole-body PET/CT interpretation that synthesizes multimodal imaging with integrated anatomical, metabolic, and semantic annotations. MetaStructAtlas provides 490 co-registered 3D PET and CT volumes with 50,470 organ-level segmentation masks and grounded radiology reports. To facilitate interactive reasoning, we further developed MetaStructVQA, a standardized 3D grounded visual question-answering benchmark containing 100,565 QA pairs. This framework explicitly links diagnostic queries to visual evidence across modalities, encompassing anatomical, morphological, and metabolic characteristics. Finally, we evaluate state-of-the-art 3D medical VLMs on MetaStructVQA, establishing a robust foundation for multimodal representation learning and integrated whole-body reasoning in nuclear medicine.

</details>


### [59] [Observation-Conditioned Latent Energy Priors for Sparse Implicit Neural Shape Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03694)
*Paul Büschl,Ezequiel de la Rosa,Julia Wolleb,Julian McGinnis,César Nombela-Arrieta,Bjoern Menze*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A post-hoc observation-conditioned latent energy prior for frozen INR decoders that scores standardized latents conditioned on permutation-invariant encoding of sparse observations, combined with L2 latent prior to prevent latent drift toward implausible geometries when sparse SDF samples underconstrain inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When optimizing latent codes from sparse off-grid SDF samples in autodecoder-style INR models, the latent can drift toward regions that fit observations but decode implausible unobserved geometry.

Method: Post-hoc observation-conditioned latent energy prior: energy scores standardized latents conditioned on permutation-invariant encoding of sparse observation set, used as residual expert alongside validation-selected L2 latent prior.

Result: Improves consistently over L2 baseline in sparsest cell-nucleus regimes; outperforms both L2 and six-component GMM latent-density prior on MedShapeNet across all readouts. Shuffled-context ablation is weaker than matched context, supporting observation-specific contribution.

Conclusion: Lightweight conditional energies can make pretrained INR decoders more observation-aware without retraining.

Abstract: Implicit neural representations (INRs) can model continuous 3D shapes with a shared coordinate decoder and per-instance latent codes. At test time, autodecoder-style models commonly freeze the decoder and optimize a new latent code from sparse off-grid SDF samples. When these samples underconstrain inference, the latent can drift toward regions that fit the observations but decode implausible unobserved geometry. We propose a post-hoc observation-conditioned latent energy prior for frozen INR decoders. The energy scores standardized latents conditioned on a permutation-invariant encoding of the sparse observation set and is used as a residual expert alongside an L2 latent prior selected on validation data. We evaluate on a controlled cell-nucleus SDF dataset and a public MedShapeNet-derived SDF completion dataset. The proposed L2 objective augmented with conditional energy improves consistently over a validation-selected L2 baseline in the sparsest cell-nucleus regimes and, on MedShapeNet, outperforms both L2 and a six-component GMM latent-density prior across all reported readouts. A shuffled-context ablation is consistently weaker than matched context, supporting an observation-specific contribution. These results suggest that lightweight conditional energies can make pretrained INR decoders more observation-aware without retraining.

</details>


### [60] [SignSeek: Learning Transferable Representations for Sign Dictionary Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03695)
*Sobhan Asasi,Ozge Mercanoglu Sincan,Richard Bowden*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SignSeek is a sign language retrieval system that uses contrastive learning with saliency-guided articulator masking to learn signer-independent sign representations. It achieves state-of-the-art cross-corpus retrieval performance across multiple sign languages (ASL, CSL) and demonstrates zero-shot generalization to unseen BSL, outperforming methods explicitly trained on it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sign language dictionaries require automatic retrieval from query videos, but existing sign representation learning methods are designed for closed-set recognition and fail to generalize to open-set, signer-independent retrieval settings due to natural variability between signers.

Method: SignSeek employs contrastive learning with Articulator Saliency-Guided Masking (ASGM) to identify the most critical articulator per sign. This drives two complementary objectives: (1) Masked Contrastive Alignment (MAC) loss that views signs through a single articulator, and (2) Masked Prediction (MAP) loss that reconstructs signs in latent space from surrounding spatio-temporal context. Pretrained on 266K samples (~5,700 glosses) across multiple sign languages.

Result: SignSeek achieves new state-of-the-art performance in cross-corpus retrieval on ASL-Citizen, WLASL, and NMFs-CSL without downstream fine-tuning. It demonstrates zero-shot generalization to unseen British Sign Language (BSL), surpassing methods explicitly trained on BSL. It also transfers to isolated sign recognition and subtitle alignment, outperforming prior skeleton-based methods.

Conclusion: Contrastive learning with saliency-guided articulator masking effectively bridges the gap between closed-set recognition and open-set retrieval for sign language. The approach enables strong cross-corpus generalization and zero-shot transfer to unseen languages, demonstrating the importance of focusing on critical articulators for robust sign representation learning.

Abstract: Sign language dictionaries are essential resources for sign language learners, yet automatically retrieving a sign from a dictionary, given only a query video, remains a challenging problem due to the natural variability between signers. Existing sign representation learning methods are built for closed-set recognition, producing embeddings that do not generalise to the open-set, signer-independent setting that retrieval demands. \textbf{SignSeek} closes this gap by contrastively learning sign representations with saliency-guided articulator masking. A contrastive objective aligns same-gloss signs across signers, while our Articulator Saliency-Guided Masking (ASGM) pinpoints the single most critical articulator per sign. This drives two complementary objectives, a masked contrastive alignment (MAC) loss that sees the sign through a single articulator and a masked prediction (MAP) loss that reconstructs it in latent space from the surrounding spatio-temporal context. Pretrained on 266K samples ($\sim$5,700 glosses) across multiple sign languages, \textbf{SignSeek} sets a new state-of-the-art performance in cross-corpus retrieval on ASL-Citizen, WLASL, and NMFs-CSL without any downstream fine-tuning. Strikingly, it achieves zero-shot generalisation to an entirely unseen British Sign Language (BSL), surpassing methods explicitly trained on BSL, and transfers seamlessly to isolated sign recognition and subtitle alignment, outperforming prior skeleton-based methods.

</details>


### [61] [Unfold The World: Factorize 4D Properties in Reinforcing Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03729)
*Yijun Yang,Shenghe Zheng,Wenbo Li,Jianhui Liu,Haoze Sun,Yanbing Zhang,Jiaxiu Jiang,Lin Song,Haoyang Huang,Nan Duan,Lei Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FactoSR is a factorized reinforcement learning framework that addresses the spatial reasoning limitations of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) by decomposing the problem of world-consistent reasoning into three orthogonal geometric sub-objectives: planar correspondence (XY), depth consistency (Z), and temporal reversibility (T). This approach transforms 2D projection recovery into tangible reasoning steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs remain fundamentally "flat" when reasoning about the physical world due to a dimensional mismatch - they are trained to interpret 2D projections but true spatial reasoning requires recovery of latent 3D geometry and temporal continuity.

Method: FactoSR uses a "divide and conquer" paradigm that decomposes the monolithic problem into three verifiable constraints: planar correspondence (XY), depth consistency (Z), and temporal reversibility (T). These are optimized within a unified policy learning mechanism to transform ill-posed projection recovery into concrete reasoning steps.

Result: Achieved a 5.9% boost on VSI-Bench and 4.5% on All-Angles-Bench in 3D and 4D reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Reinforcing explicit, factorized 4D consistency is a critical step toward evolving VLMs into robust, world-aware reasoners. The factorized approach effectively addresses the spatial bottleneck caused by dimensional mismatch in visual projection.

Abstract: Despite the remarkable prowess of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) in general multimodal tasks, they remain fundamentally ``flat'' when reasoning about the physical world. We argue that this spatial bottleneck stems from a profound dimensional mismatch: while VLMs are trained to interpret 2D projections, true spatial reasoning demands the recovery of latent 3D geometry and temporal continuity. To conquer this high-dimensional complexity, we advocate a shift from monolithic learning to a ``divide and conquer'' paradigm. We present FactoSR, a factorized reinforcement learning framework that explicitly interpret the dimensions collapsed by visual projection. At its core, FactoSR decomposes the monolithic problem of world-consistent reasoning into three orthogonal, geometric sub-objectives: planar correspondence ($XY$), depth consistency ($Z$), and temporal reversibility ($T$). By optimizing these verifiable constraints within a unified policy learning mechanism, we effectively transform an ill-posed projection recovery problem into a series of tangible reasoning steps. Extensive evaluations on multi-view and video benchmarks demonstrate that this elegant decomposition yields substantial gains in 3D and 4D reasoning, achieving a 5.9% boost on VSI-Bench and 4.5% on All-Angles-Bench. Our findings suggest that reinforcing explicit, factorized 4D consistency is a critical step toward evolving VLMs into robust, world-aware reasoners.

</details>


### [62] [Fill My Mirror: Geometry-Constrained Mirror Inpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03740)
*Ofek Basson,Shimon Vainer,Yacov Hel-Or,Ohad Fried*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses mirror inpainting in real-world images using a training-free approach that combines scene geometry estimation with diffusion models. The key insight is that mirror reflections are geometrically constrained by the visible scene, so instead of hallucinating mirror content, the method projects visible content into the mirror and uses a two-mask diffusion strategy to complete the region while balancing geometric constraints with learned priors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mirrors are common in real-world images but producing geometrically consistent reflections with generative models is challenging because mirror appearance depends on scene geometry and viewpoint, making it difficult to synthesize using learned appearance priors alone.

Method: The method involves: 1) Estimating scene geometry, 2) Projecting visible content into the mirror to recover reflection regions determined by geometry, 3) Using a generative model with a two-mask diffusion strategy that balances geometric constraints with the model's learned priors to reduce projection artifacts and improve reflection consistency. The approach is training-free and applicable to complex real-world scenes.

Result: The method shows that explicitly using scene geometry improves consistency on both synthetic (MirrorBench-V2) and real images, using standard and geometry-aware metrics.

Conclusion: By explicitly incorporating scene geometry rather than relying solely on learned priors, the method achieves better geometric consistency in mirror reflections. The training-free approach makes it practical for complex real-world scenes.

Abstract: Mirrors are common in real-world images, yet producing geometrically consistent reflections with generative models remains challenging. Unlike most objects, mirror appearance depends on scene geometry and viewpoint, making it hard to synthesize using learned appearance priors alone. We address this in the mirror inpainting setting, where the scene is fixed and only the mirror region is generated. Our key insight is that much mirror content is geometrically constrained by the visible scene and need not be hallucinated. We estimate scene geometry and project visible content into the mirror to recover reflection regions determined by geometry. A generative model then completes the mirror region via a two-mask diffusion strategy balancing geometric constraints with the model's learned priors, reducing projection artifacts and improving reflection consistency. The method is training-free and applicable to complex real-world scenes. We evaluate on MirrorBench-V2 (synthetic) and real images. Using standard and geometry-aware metrics, we show that explicitly using scene geometry improves consistency.

</details>


### [63] [KnowVis: Knowledge-Centric Visual Summarization for Video Lectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03742)
*Yi Xu,Yifan Hou,Xiaoyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: KnowVis is a novel framework that transforms linear video lectures into pedagogically grounded visual narratives (concept maps) to reduce cognitive overload for novice learners. It extracts concept maps from multimodal video content, identifies threshold concepts, constructs knowledge units, and synthesizes visual summaries. The approach includes a dataset of 125 educational videos across 10 disciplines with 1,079 visual summaries, and demonstrates improved learning outcomes in both automated and human evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video lectures deliver information linearly and transiently, but human learning requires constructing interconnected cognitive networks. This creates a fundamental pedagogical mismatch that overwhelms novice learners lacking prior domain knowledge. Existing video summarization methods only produce text-heavy, linear condensations that still demand high cognitive effort, failing to address this core problem.

Method: KnowVis operates in three stages: (1) Extract detailed concept maps from multimodal video content (audio, visual, text) to identify important and challenging threshold concepts; (2) Construct structured knowledge units from these concepts; (3) Synthesize engaging visual summaries. The system was evaluated using 125 educational videos across 10 academic disciplines with 1,079 generated visual summaries, through both automated metrics and human studies.

Result: KnowVis generates more accurate and clear visuals compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The visual summaries successfully reduce cognitive load and significantly improve student learning effectiveness and knowledge retention.

Conclusion: KnowVis effectively addresses the pedagogical mismatch in video-based learning by transforming linear video lectures into visual narratives. The framework represents a significant advancement in educational video summarization, with demonstrated benefits for novice learners across multiple disciplines. The introduced dataset provides a valuable resource for future research in this direction.

Abstract: Video lectures are valuable educational resources, but their dense and lengthy formats often overwhelm novice learners. This difficulty stems from a fundamental pedagogical mismatch: while videos deliver transient information linearly, human learning requires constructing interconnected cognitive networks, a task that induces severe cognitive overload for novice learners lacking prior domain knowledge. Existing video summarization methods fail to resolve this mismatch, as they primarily produce text-heavy, linear condensations that still demand high cognitive effort. To bridge this gap, we propose KnowVis, a framework that transforms linear video lectures into pedagogically grounded visual narratives. KnowVis first extracts a detailed concept map from multimodal video content to identify important and challenging threshold concepts, then constructs structured knowledge units, and finally synthesizes engaging visual summaries. Alongside the framework, we introduce a curated dataset of 125 educational videos across 10 academic disciplines, paired with 1,079 generated visual summaries. Extensive automated evaluations and a human study demonstrate that, compared to state-of-the-art baselines, KnowVis generates more accurate and clear visuals that successfully reduce cognitive load and significantly improve student learning effectiveness and knowledge retention.

</details>


### [64] [ENEAS: Embedding-guided Neural Ensemble for Adaptive Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03756)
*Javier del Pino,Salvador Rodríguez,Alejandro Garabito,Javier Álvarez,Chema Garabito*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ENEAS is a unified method for instance tracking and semantic discovery in videos that addresses limitations of text-promptable segmentation models (SAM 3), including temporal hallucinations, spatial fragmentation, and semantic misclassification. It combines precise tracking via an extended SeC architecture with text-prompting, and open-concept discovery through a semantic verification layer using visual embedding matching and VLM refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-promptable segmentation models like SAM 3 suffer from temporal hallucinations, spatial fragmentation, and semantic misclassification—they fail to report target absence, segment local textures instead of complete objects, and cannot distinguish visual artifacts (statues, paintings, reflections) from real objects. This is particularly problematic for 3D reconstruction where misclassified distractors corrupt assets.

Method: ENEAS extends the geometrically robust SeC architecture with a text-prompting adapter and leverages its temporal memory for tracking. For discovery, it uses a verification layer combining high-speed visual embedding matching with conditional VLM refinement, invoking semantic reasoning only for ambiguous candidates.

Result: ENEAS enables precise tracking and high-quality segmentation of unique instances, and open-concept discovery of every instance a text query names. It filters out ontological errors (distinguishing true instances from doppelgangers) while maintaining low latency.

Conclusion: ENEAS unlocks high-quality semantic tracking and segmentation for video, broad libraries, and temporally/spatially unordered data collections, with the discrimination capability to tell true instances from visual look-alikes. Code and models are available.

Abstract: We present ENEAS, a unified, text-promptable method for instance tracking and semantic discovery. Text-promptable segmentation models, including the latest foundation models such as SAM 3, still suffer from temporal hallucinations, spatial fragmentation, and semantic misclassification: they fail to report target absence when an object leaves the field of view, segment local textures instead of the complete object during extreme close-ups, and prioritize visual features over ontological reality, so that visually similar artifacts such as statues, paintings, or reflections are segmented as target entities.
  ENEAS works two ways from a single method: precise tracking and high-quality segmentation of a unique instance, and open-concept discovery of every instance a text query names, resolved by a semantic verification layer. For tracking, we extend the geometrically robust SeC architecture, previously limited to point interactions, with a text-prompting adapter and leverage its temporal memory, so that the target is held through disappearance without drifting to distractors and kept whole even when it fills the entire view. For discovery, the verification layer combines high-speed visual embedding matching with conditional VLM refinement, invoking semantic reasoning only for ambiguous candidates, which filters out the ontological errors that visual-only models cannot distinguish while keeping latency low. Designed with 3D reconstruction in mind, where a single misclassified distractor corrupts the asset, ENEAS unlocks high-quality semantic tracking and segmentation of video, of broad libraries, and of collections of temporally or spatially unordered data, together with the discrimination to tell true instances from their doppelgangers: things that look alike but are not the same. The code and models are available at https://github.com/speridlabs/eneas

</details>


### [65] [RealCADBench: Benchmarking Parametric CAD Modeling from Industrial Design Intents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03773)
*JoyIndustrial VisCAD Team,Linxin Cai,Qiuhe Hong,Zhichao Huang,Guanlin Li,Zongzhen Li,Hongsen Liu,Yichen Long,Wei Wang,Yuchen Wang,Dongyue Yang,Huimu Yu,Xianwen Zhong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RealCADBench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating intent-to-program CAD modeling from real industrial design intents, containing 12,632 tasks across 19 categories with multiple input modalities. It evaluates generated FreeCAD Python code using executability, Solid IoU, Surface IoU, and a visual-semantic identity Judge. Testing nine frontier models reveals no single model dominates all metrics, with significant performance gaps across executability (0.565-0.812), Solid IoU (0.2841-0.5379), and Surface IoU (0.112-0.217).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CAD benchmarks have limitations: they focus on synthetic or CAD-native settings, use limited input modalities, or evaluate only executability and IoU metrics. There is a need for a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates CAD modeling from real industrial design intents across multiple input types and uses multiple evaluation criteria including visual-semantic identity.

Method: RealCADBench contains 12,632 tasks from 19 factory-automation categories spanning text descriptions, 2D engineering drawings, real product pictures, and rendered images for Part and Assembly modeling. Methods generate FreeCAD API Python code, which is executed to export 3D models. Evaluation uses executability, Solid IoU, Surface IoU, and a rubric-based visual-semantic identity Judge. The evaluation slice includes 1,745 Part tasks across four input regimes and RCB-Assm25 (25-task assembly study).

Result: Among nine standalone frontier models, no model leads all four metrics. Across six frontier models, executability ranges from 0.565 to 0.812, Solid IoU from 0.2841 to 0.5379, and Surface IoU from 0.112 to 0.217. The highest regime-balanced composite comes from a different model than leaders on individual metrics. On assembly tasks, Codex with GPT-5.5 improves executability and IoU metrics over standalone GPT-5.5 but lowers Judge score by 6.98 percentage points. Key failure modes include missing fine structures, loss of part identity, and incorrect assembly placement.

Conclusion: Execution alone is insufficient to characterize realistic CAD modeling. Frontier models and agents differ substantially across executability, IoUs, and visual-semantic identity metrics. The benchmark reveals that current AI models still struggle with fine-grained structural details, part identification, and assembly placement in CAD modeling tasks.

Abstract: Parametric computer-aided design (CAD) modeling is difficult to evaluate with a single metric. Existing CAD benchmarks often emphasize synthetic or CAD-native settings, limited input modalities, or executability and IoUs alone. We introduce RealCADBench, a benchmark for intent-to-program CAD modeling from real industrial design intents. It contains 12,632 tasks from 19 factory-automation categories and spans text descriptions, 2D engineering drawings, real product pictures, and rendered images for both Part and Assembly modeling. We report results on a 1,770-task evaluation slice: 1,745 Part tasks across four input regimes and RCB-Assm25, a 25-task assembly study used in every reported assembly comparison. Each method generates FreeCAD API Python, which a shared runtime executes to export the 3D model. We evaluate the exported model using executability, Solid IoU, Surface IoU, and a rubric-based visual-semantic identity Judge. Among the nine standalone frontier large models evaluated, no model leads all four metrics. Across six frontier-scale large models, executability ranges from 0.565 to 0.812, Solid IoU from 0.2841 to 0.5379, and Surface IoU from 0.112 to 0.217 across the four Part regimes. The highest regime-balanced composite comes from a different model than the leaders on the four component metrics. On RCB-Assm25, Codex with GPT-5.5 improves executability and both IoU metrics over standalone GPT-5.5, but lowers the Judge score by 6.98 percentage points, leaving GPT-5.5 as the Judge leader. We also observe recurring failure modes, most notably missing fine structures, loss of part identity, and incorrect assembly placement. These results show that execution alone is insufficient to characterize realistic CAD modeling and that frontier models and agents differ substantially across executability, IoUs, and visual-semantic identity.

</details>


### [66] [A Reverse Sign Language Dictionary: Open-Vocabulary Sign Recognition from Continuous Signing via Video Captioning and Description Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03788)
*Santiago Poveda-Gutiérrez,Hideki Nakayama,Mayumi Bono*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel open-vocabulary approach for Isolated Sign Language Recognition (ISLR) that uses a vision-language model to caption sign-level clips into procedural descriptions, then retrieves matching descriptions from a vocabulary using a multilingual sentence encoder. This "reverse sign language dictionary" approach requires no gloss supervision and can generalize to unseen signs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional ISLR uses closed-set classification over gloss labels, which cannot generalize to signs unseen in training and requires deployment to be tied to a gloss-annotated lexicon. The authors aim to create an open-vocabulary system that can recognize signs without gloss supervision.

Method: The approach has two components: (1) Caption sign-level clips into free-form procedural descriptions using an open-weight vision-language model; (2) Retrieve the closest entry from a target description vocabulary using a multilingual sentence encoder. The captioner was fine-tuned on 1,300 sign-level segments from a Japanese Sign Language (JSL) dialogue corpus annotated with procedural descriptions.

Result: Against a 2% chance floor over 503-entry vocabulary, fine-tuning raised seen-class top-10 retrieval from 4.5% to 49%, becoming statistically indistinguishable from a supervised I3D closed-set classifier on 2 of 3 test sets. Unseen-class retrieval improved significantly from 11.5% to 21.0% top-10 (p=0.0094). The sentence encoder already recovers close to 100% of paraphrased gold descriptions, indicating the main gap is in captioning quality.

Conclusion: This is the first description-based, open-vocabulary sign lookup from continuous signing without gloss supervision, and the first for Japanese Sign Language. Future work will focus on improving captioning quality to close the gap with the matcher-side upper bound.

Abstract: Isolated Sign Language Recognition (ISLR) is conventionally cast as closed-set classification over gloss labels, which cannot generalize to signs unseen in training and ties every deployment to a gloss-annotated lexicon. We instead recognize signs extracted from continuous signing by (1) captioning a sign-level clip into a free-form procedural description of the articulation with an open-weight vision-language model, and (2) retrieving the closest entry from a vocabulary of target descriptions with a multilingual sentence encoder: a reverse sign language dictionary that needs no gloss supervision and admits an open vocabulary. On 1,300 sign-level segments from a Japanese Sign Language (JSL) dialogue corpus annotated with procedural descriptions (against a 2% top-10 chance floor over the 503-entry target vocabulary), fine-tuning the captioner substantially improves seen-class retrieval: language and vision tower fine-tuning raises top-10 retrieval on seen classes from 4.5% (untrained) to 49%, becoming statistically indistinguishable from a standard supervised closed-set classifier (I3D) on two of the three test sets where a closed-set classifier can be evaluated at all. More importantly, unseen-class retrieval also improves significantly over the untrained pipeline (11.5% -> 21.0% top-10, p=0.0094), a regime in which the closed-set classifier cannot participate. A matcher-side empirical upper-bound analysis shows the sentence encoder already recovers close to 100% of paraphrased gold descriptions, locating a gap in captioning quality that we aim to address in future work. To our knowledge this is the first description-based, open-vocabulary sign lookup from continuous signing without gloss supervision, and the first for JSL.

</details>


### [67] [LLaDA-Image: Building Strong Image Generators with Fully Open Training Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03796)
*Chuyan Chen,Haoxing Chen,Kun Chen,Zhenglin Cheng,Long Cui,Ruishan Fang,Zhangxuan Gu,Zhicheng Huang,Zhenzhong Lan,Yuanting Lei,Haoquan Li,Jianguo Li,Rongchuan Li,Sidu Li,Tao Lin,Deyuan Liu,Jiacheng Liu,Lin Liu,Yuxuan Lou,Zhisheng Lu,Yuxin Ma,Shuheng Shen,Peng Sun,Chaoyang Wang,Hongjun Wang,Xiaomei Wang,Yongxin Wang,Chengzhang Wu,Hongru Wu,Jun Xie*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LLaDA-Image is a unified framework combining a 6B Diffusion Transformer (DiT) with a frozen vision-language understanding module (LLaDA2.0-Mini) for photorealistic image generation and fine-grained editing. The model uses image-only pre-training and mid-training to build a strong visual prior before incorporating text instructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to build a strong visual generative model without heavily relying on paired image-text data from the beginning. The approach focuses on developing a robust visual generative prior through image-only training before integrating language understanding capabilities.

Method: The framework uses a 6B Diffusion Transformer trained from scratch, paired with a frozen vision-language understanding module based on LLaDA2.0-Mini. Training uses 220M samples (98% real images), parameter-free RMSNorm throughout the DiT, and the Muon optimizer for efficient optimization. The model is then distilled into LLaDA-Image-Turbo for fast inference in 2-4 sampling steps.

Result: LLaDA-Image achieves state-of-the-art results on Qwen-Image-Bench with scores of 53.53 (English) and 53.38 (Chinese), setting new SOTA among open-source models on both tracks. The model produces highly photorealistic images while accurately following fine-grained editing instructions.

Conclusion: The unified framework successfully combines visual generation with language understanding, achieving SOTA performance on benchmarks. The authors release model weights, training code, and detailed recipes to support further research on capable and efficient generative models.

Abstract: We introduce LLaDA-Image, a unified framework that pairs a 6B Diffusion Transformer (DiT) trained from scratch with a frozen vision-language understanding module built on the LLaDA2.0-Mini diffusion language model backbone. Instead of relying heavily on paired image-text data from the beginning, we first build a strong visual generative prior through image-only pre-training and mid-training. The generation pipeline comprises 220M samples, 98 of which are real images. For efficient and scalable optimization, we use parameter-free RMSNorm throughout the DiT together with the Muon optimizer. The resulting unified model produces highly photorealistic images while accurately following fine-grained editing instructions. We further distill LLaDA-Image into LLaDA-Image-Turbo, enabling fast inference in 2-4 sampling steps. On Qwen-Image-Bench, LLaDA-Image achieves overall scores of 53.53 and 53.38 on the English and Chinese tracks, respectively, setting a new state-of-the-art among open-source models on both tracks. To support further research on capable and efficient generative models, we release our model weights, training code, and detailed recipes.

</details>


### [68] [Urban Boundaries, Social Barriers: A Benchmark and Vision-Centric Framework for Mapping Gated Communities and Equity Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03804)
*Minwei Zhao,Weiming Zhang,Jiawang Du,Qiming Liu,Weiming Zhuang,Pei Nie,Cai Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces GBA-GCs, a large-scale multimodal benchmark for recognizing gated and open residential communities in China's Greater Bay Area, comprising 37,444 compounds with satellite imagery and metadata. The authors propose MCGC, a vision-centric multimodal framework using DINOv3-SAT with cross-attention and adaptive gating, which outperforms baselines. The model is applied to reveal equity issues including spatial clustering, privatized green space, and reduced pedestrian connectivity in gated communities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous studies on Chinese fengbi xiaoqu (gated communities) are largely qualitative or small-scale, limiting reproducible city-scale analysis. There is a need for a comprehensive benchmark to enable systematic, large-scale study of residential community types.

Method: The authors created GBA-GCs, a multimodal benchmark with 37,444 residential compounds including boundary polygons, high-resolution satellite imagery, Chinese metadata, and structured attributes with expert-verified labels. They developed MCGC (Multimodal Classifier for Gated Community) using DINOv3-SAT with modality-aware cross-attention and adaptive gating to fuse imagery, text, and structured cues while mitigating modality imbalance.

Result: MCGC consistently outperforms strong unimodal and multimodal baselines. The validated model applied to metropolitan-scale mapping reveals spatial clustering of gated communities, privatized green space, and reduced pedestrian connectivity.

Conclusion: The proposed GBA-GCs benchmark and MCGC framework enable large-scale reproducible analysis of gated communities in urban China, providing valuable insights for urban planning and equity considerations.

Abstract: Communities are fundamental spatial units that shape urban form and social life. Whether a residential compound is spatially open or enclosed affects mobility, access to public services, and equity, yet studies of Chinese fengbi xiaoqu remain largely qualitative or small-scale, limiting reproducible city-scale analysis. We address this gap by introducing GBA-GCs, a metropolitan-scale multimodal benchmark for locally grounded gated/open community recognition in China's Greater Bay Area, covering 37,444 residential compounds with aligned boundary polygons, high-resolution satellite imagery, Chinese metadata, and structured attributes, together with expert-verified labels, inter-annotator reliability, and official evaluation splits. Built on this benchmark, we present Multimodal Classifier for Gated Community (MCGC), a vision-centric multimodal framework based on DINOv3-SAT that fuses imagery, text, and structured cues via modality-aware cross-attention and adaptive gating to mitigate modality imbalance. MCGC consistently outperforms strong unimodal and multimodal baselines. Finally, we apply the validated model to metropolitan-scale mapping and report equity-oriented findings including spatial clustering of GCs, privatized green space, and reduced pedestrian connectivity. The benchmark, code, and release documentation are available at https://github.com/MinweiZhao/GBA-GCs.

</details>


### [69] [VisCAD: A Foundation Model Suite with Multimodal Industrial CAD Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03811)
*JoyIndustrial VisCAD Team,Linxin Cai,Qiuhe Hong,Zhichao Huang,Guanlin Li,Hongsen Liu,Ziqi Liu,Yichen Long,Luya Wang,Yuchen Wang,Wenxiang Wu,Huimu Yu,Ning Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VisCAD is a foundation model suite for CAD design that combines broad generalization with strong CAD capability. VisCAD-M1 (27B) achieves state-of-the-art performance on part-level generation benchmarks (0.5540), and when used as a test-time verifier, reaches 0.5797 score (5% relative improvement over previous SOTA). A domain-specific harness also demonstrates advantages for assembly-level generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing specialized CAD models are trained on narrow input domains (renders, texts) and generalize poorly, while general-purpose frontier models perform inconsistently across CAD domains. There's a need for a model that provides both broad generalization and strong CAD capability for industrial products.

Method: 1) VisCAD-M1, a 27B model trained through mid-training and post-training for part-level design generation; 2) Reusing VisCAD-M1 as a test-time verifier for enhanced accuracy; 3) Domain-specific harness leveraging frontier models for complex assembly generation

Result: On PubCADBench and RealCADBench: VisCAD-M1 achieves highest average part-level score (0.5540) vs strongest frontier model (0.5496). Test-time verifier raises score to 0.5797 (~5% relative improvement over previous SOTA). Domain-specific harness shows advantages over general-purpose harnesses in both quantitative and qualitative evaluations.

Conclusion: VisCAD successfully provides both broad generalization and strong CAD capability for realistic industrial products, with VisCAD-M1 achieving state-of-the-art performance on part-level generation and the domain-specific harness demonstrating advantages for assembly-level generation.
