Abstract: AI-assisted computer-aided design (CAD) for industrial products involves two challenging phases. Part-level generation maps diverse forms of user intent, including renders, text descriptions, 2D drawings, and real photographs, to executable programs in a CAD domain-specific language. Assembly-level generation must additionally handle interacting parts, plan mating relations, estimate poses, and place all parts correctly. Existing specialized CAD models are commonly trained on narrow input domains, such as renders or texts, and often generalize poorly, while general-purpose frontier models cover broader inputs but perform inconsistently across CAD domains. We present VisCAD, a foundation model suite designed to provide both broad generalization and strong CAD capability for realistic industrial products. At its core is VisCAD-M1, a 27B model trained through mid-training and post-training for part-level design generation. On PubCADBench and RealCADBench, VisCAD-M1 achieves the highest average part-level score among the evaluated models, reaching 0.5540 compared with 0.5496 for the strongest frontier model. Reusing VisCAD-M1 as a test-time verifier can further raise the score to 0.5797, an approximately 5 percent relative improvement over the previous state of the art. VisCAD also includes a domain-specific harness that leverages frontier models for complex assembly generation and demonstrates advantages over general-purpose harnesses in both quantitative and qualitative evaluations.

</details>


### [70] [SPARK: Input-Conditioned Sparse Activation Modulation for Frozen DiT-based Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03813)
*Federico Putamorsi,Leonardo Zini,Marcella Cornia,Lorenzo Baraldi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces SPARK, a lightweight method to adapt frozen Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based super-resolution models by modulating only dominant channels (8 per stream/block) through predicted affine transformations, without fine-tuning the backbone or VAE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DiT-based SR models have internal activations dominated by a small number of massive channels, but this activation space remains unexplored for adaptation. Existing methods for improving perceptual quality require fine-tuning or additional adapters, which is computationally expensive.

Method: The authors first characterize dominant channel behavior in pretrained SR backbones and demonstrate through controlled interventions that these channels strongly affect reconstruction quality. They then propose SPARK, which uses an online activation-ranking procedure to identify dominant channels and trains a small predictor to generate bounded per-channel affine transformations conditioned on low-resolution VAE latents, while keeping the SR backbone and VAE frozen.

Result: Experiments on three DiT-based SR backbones across DIV2K, RealSR, and DRealSR datasets show consistent gains in both fidelity and perceptual quality while modulating only eight channels per stream and block. Controlled comparisons demonstrate these gains cannot be explained by parameter budget or access to selected channels alone.

Conclusion: Dominant channels can serve as a compact and effective adaptation interface for frozen DiT-based SR models, achieving improved perceptual quality without requiring fine-tuning or additional adapters.

Abstract: Real-world image super-resolution (SR) increasingly relies on Diffusion Transformer (DiT) backbones, whose internal activations can be dominated by a small number of massive channels. Yet improving perceptual quality in these models still typically requires fine-tuning the network or attaching additional adapters, leaving this structured activation space largely unexplored for adaptation. We investigate whether dominant channels can instead serve as a compact adaptation interface for frozen DiT-based SR models. We first characterize their behavior in pretrained SR backbones and show through controlled interventions that they strongly affect reconstruction quality. Building on this observation, we introduce SPARK, a lightweight input-conditioned controller that predicts bounded per-channel affine transformations for only the selected channels, while keeping the SR backbone and VAE frozen. Dominant channels are identified through an online activation-ranking procedure, and only a small predictor conditioned on the low-resolution VAE latent is optimized. Experiments on three DiT-based SR backbones across DIV2K, RealSR, and DRealSR show consistent gains in both fidelity and perceptual quality while modulating only eight channels per stream and block. Controlled comparisons further show that these gains cannot be explained by parameter budget or access to the selected channels alone.

</details>


### [71] [Select, Compress, Reinvest: A Controlled Study of Visual-Token Allocation in Long-Video MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03820)
*Prakhar Khatri*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates frame selection strategies for long-video language models, which can only process a small fixed number of frames from hours of video. The study systematically tests selection methods, spatial compression, and reinvestment of saved tokens, finding that selection is the most impactful factor, and that a decades-old algorithm (Orthogonal Matching Pursuit) matches purpose-built selectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frame selection in long-video language models is typically treated as a preprocessing detail rather than a formal research question. The authors aim to determine whether frame selection matters and which approach is most effective, since existing comparisons confound multiple variables (frame scorer, prompt boundary, resolution policy, answering model).

Method: The authors conducted controlled experiments holding all variables fixed except one, varying: (1) selection rules (6 training-free methods), (2) spatial compression, and (3) reinvestment of savings. Experiments were run across 3 long-video benchmarks and 2 answering models. The study tested Uniform sampling, query-selected frames, and Orthogonal Matching Pursuit (OMP) among other selectors.

Result: Key findings: (1) Selection is the largest single lever—8 query-selected frames beat 16 uniformly spaced ones by 6.9 points on LongVideoBench's hour-long bin; (2) OMP matches or comes within 1 point of every purpose-built selector across all benchmarks; (3) Compression is nearly free—halving spatial budget costs at most 0.44 points; (4) Reinvestment yields 2-3 additional points; (5) An AKS baseline implementation bug was found; (6) A 0.07 to 3.74 point gap exists between two harnesses running the same rules.

Conclusion: Frame selection is a critical and understudied factor in long-video language models. Simple algorithms like OMP perform as well as purpose-built selectors. Compression is cost-effective only when savings are reinvested into more frames. The authors advocate for comparisons within a controlled harness rather than across papers, and open-source their code to enable reproducible research.

Abstract: Long-video language models cannot look at every frame: an hour sampled once per second is 3,600 images, and a system keeps only a small fixed slice of that pool. Which frames survive that slice is usually treated as a preprocessing detail; we test whether it should be. Published selectors make the comparison hard because they change the frame scorer, the prompt boundary, the resolution policy, and the answering model all at once. We hold each fixed and vary one decision at a time: selection, spatial compression, and reinvestment of the savings, across six training-free selection rules, three long-video benchmarks, and two answering models. Selection is the largest single lever: on LongVideoBench's hour-long bin, eight query-selected frames beat sixteen uniformly spaced ones by 6.9 points, and Orthogonal Matching Pursuit, an unmodified decades-old sparse-approximation algorithm, matches or comes within a point of every purpose-built selector we compare it against, across all three benchmarks. Compression is close to free: halving each frame's spatial budget at fixed timestamps costs at most 0.44 points. Reinvestment is where that budget turns back into accuracy: spending the freed tokens on twice as many compressed frames, at a measured cost no higher than the original eight, returns a further two to three points; compression only pays off once its savings are spent this way. Along the way, an implementation bug in our own AKS baseline and a 0.07 to 3.74 point gap between two harnesses running the same published rules at the same budget show why these comparisons need to happen inside one controlled harness rather than across papers.

</details>


### [72] [VI3: Grounding Pretrained 3D Foundation Models with Inertial Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03824)
*Ernesto Lozano,Alberto Jaenal,Javier Civera*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VI3 is a model-agnostic framework that uses IMU readings to recover metric scale predictions from pretrained 3D foundation models (3DFMs), which typically lack absolute scale information from monocular images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D foundation models excel at zero-shot generalization for camera pose and dense depth prediction from multiple views, but cannot observe metric scale from monocular images. IMUs, present in most devices, naturally complement cameras by observing scaled motion.

Method: VI3 initializes and preintegrates IMU readings to obtain a metric motion reference, then uses this to recover the scale of 3DFM outputs. The method includes adaptable anchoring strategies tailored to diverse 3DFM architectures.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real aerial datasets demonstrate that VI3 recovers metric scale without ground-truth supervision while preserving geometric consistency. It acts as a fine refinement under well-conditioned motion and as a strong prior when motion is less informative.

Conclusion: VI3 provides a practical solution for metric scale recovery in 3DFMs using only IMU data, offering a plug-and-play approach that works across different 3DFM architectures without requiring additional supervision.

Abstract: 3D foundation models (3DFMs) excel at predicting camera poses and dense depth from multiple views of a scene, showcasing strong zero-shot generalization. However, as metric scale is not observable from monocular images, their absolute scale predictions are typically inaccurate. Inertial measurement units (IMUs), present in most devices, naturally complement monocular cameras by observing scaled motion. We introduce VI3, a model-agnostic framework that metrically anchors a pretrained 3DFM using only IMU readings. VI3 initializes and preintegrates the IMU to obtain a metric motion reference, which is then used to recover the scale of the 3DFM outputs. Our method includes adaptable anchoring strategies tailored to diverse 3DFM architectures. Experiments on synthetic and real aerial datasets demonstrate that VI3 recovers metric scale without ground-truth supervision while preserving geometric consistency, acting as a fine refinement under well-conditioned motion and as a strong prior when motion is less informative.

</details>


### [73] [The impact of phase information for few-shot fine-grained image classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03829)
*Ruiling Liu,Linyue Zhang,Wenyi Zeng,Jiamiao Lu,Weichuang Zhang,Changming Sun,Zejun Zhang,Xiao Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses few-shot fine-grained image classification (FSFGIC) by introducing phase information as a critical yet underutilized feature. The authors propose a plug-and-play amplitude-phase integration (API) module and a dedicated PSF-Net that adaptively fuses phase-based spatial and frequency information. The method can be integrated into standard episodic training architectures and outperforms state-of-the-art methods on five public datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-shot fine-grained image classification requires distinguishing between highly similar images with limited labeled examples. While amplitude information has been extensively studied in frequency domain analysis, the critical role of phase information in capturing structural relationships within images remains underutilized. This work aims to address this gap by leveraging phase information for more comprehensive feature representation.

Method: The authors propose two key contributions: (1) A plug-and-play amplitude-phase integration (API) module that effectively combines local and global frequency amplitude and phase information for obtaining more comprehensive feature descriptors. (2) A dedicated network named PSF-Net that adaptively fuses phase-based spatial and frequency information for FSFGIC. The designed PSF-Net can be easily integrated into standard episodic training architectures for end-to-end training from scratch.

Result: Extensive experiments on five public datasets demonstrate that the method outperforms existing state-of-the-art benchmarks.

Conclusion: The integration of phase information, combined with amplitude information through the proposed API module and PSF-Net, provides a more comprehensive feature representation for few-shot fine-grained image classification. This approach successfully captures structural relationships that amplitude-only methods miss, leading to improved classification performance.

Abstract: Few-shot fine-grained image classification (FSFGIC) aims to classify similar images with limited labeled examples. This work highlights the critical yet underutilized role of phase information in capturing structural relationships within an image. This study introduces a novel plug-and-play amplitude-phase integration (API) module that effectively combines local and global frequency amplitude and phase information for obtaining more comprehensive feature descriptors. Additionally, a dedicated network, named PSF-Net, is proposed that adaptively fuses phase-based spatial and frequency information for FSFGIS. The designed PSF-Net can be easily integrated into standard episodic training architectures for end-to-end training from scratch. Extensive experiments on five public datasets demonstrate that the method outperforms existing state-of-the-art benchmarks.

</details>


### [74] [GraFT: A Training-Free Framework for Spatial Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models via 3D Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03892)
*Junqing Du,Fernando Ropero,Erkin Turkoz,Yanfeng Zhang,Lu Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GraFT is a training-free framework that enhances 3D spatial reasoning in multimodal large language models by integrating a compact 3D scene graph (3DSG), providing deterministic geometry, allocentric layout rendering, and visual-attribute grounding without expensive fine-tuning or specialized encoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs struggle with 3D spatial reasoning tasks, including precise geometric measurement, viewpoint transformations between egocentric and allocentric perspectives, and fine-grained visual attribute grounding. Existing solutions require costly supervised datasets or backbone-specific 3D encoders, creating dependency and maintenance challenges.

Method: GraFT introduces three spatial reasoning capabilities derived from a compact 3D scene graph: (1) deterministic geometry via symbolic tools, (2) allocentric layout through bird's-eye-view (BEV) rendering, and (3) visual-attribute grounding via task-relevant egocentric frames. The framework is training-free and easily maintainable.

Result: On ScanQA, GraFT improves every metric over the same-backbone baseline, increasing CIDEr by 27%. On VSI-Bench, GraFT enhances frozen MLLMs by up to 65%, outperforming all proprietary and general-purpose open-source baselines, as well as several prominent fine-tuned spatial models.

Conclusion: GraFT demonstrates that a training-free approach using 3D scene graphs can effectively bridge the 3D spatial reasoning gap in MLLMs, achieving state-of-the-art results without costly fine-tuning or specialized encoders, while remaining backbone-agnostic and easily maintainable.

Abstract: 3D spatial reasoning underpins understanding and acting in the physical world, yet it remains unreliable in current multimodal large language models (MLLMs). These models falter at precise geometric measurement, at transforming between egocentric and allocentric viewpoints, and at grounding fine-grained appearance. The most common remedies fine-tune the model on large-scale curated spatial-reasoning datasets or attach dedicated encoders for 3D geometry, which typically couples the solution to costly supervision and a specific backbone. We instead introduce GraFT, a training-free framework that supplies the missing 3D structure through a compact, easily maintained 3D scene graph (3DSG). From this 3DSG, GraFT provides three spatial reasoning capabilities: (1) deterministic geometry through symbolic tools, (2) allocentric layout through a bird's-eye-view (BEV) rendering, and (3) visual-attribute grounding through task-relevant egocentric frames. On ScanQA, GraFT improves every metric over the same-backbone baseline, raising CIDEr by 27%. On VSI-Bench, GraFT improves frozen MLLMs by up to 65%, surpassing every proprietary and general-purpose open-source baseline, and several prominent fine-tuned spatial models.

</details>


### [75] [Concept of a Sensor Test Environment for Dusty Agricultural Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03895)
*Peter Buckel,Johannes Hermann,Jonas Wollmann,Thomas Dietmueller,Timo Oksanen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a proving ground concept for testing autonomous agricultural machinery under dusty conditions. The facility includes an indoor area with a laboratory test bench (closed dust circulation system) and a test hall for life-size objects, plus an outdoor area with dedicated test setups for reproducible real-world agriculture data recording with and without dust. The entire proving ground is visualized in 3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dust in agriculture poses a significant challenge for autonomous agricultural machinery as it can impair the performance of sensors and algorithms, potentially compromising operation safety and efficiency.

Method: The authors developed a proving ground concept consisting of two main components: (1) An indoor area with a laboratory test bench featuring a closed dust circulation system and a test hall for placing life-size objects; (2) An outdoor area with dedicated test setups that enable reproducible data recording during real-world agriculture work, both with and without dust conditions. The proving ground and test setups are visualized in 3D.

Result: A conceptual design for an indoor-outdoor proving ground facility that can simulate and test dusty conditions for autonomous agricultural machinery in a reproducible manner.

Conclusion: The proposed proving ground concept provides a systematic approach for evaluating and improving the dust resistance of sensors and algorithms used in autonomous agricultural machinery, enabling reproducible testing under controlled conditions.

Abstract: Dust in agriculture presents a significant challenge for autonomous agricultural machinery. Dust can impair the performance of sensors and algorithms. This work, therefore, presents a concept for a proving ground consisting of an indoor and outdoor area. The indoor area comprises a laboratory test bench where dust circulates in a closed system and a test hall where life-size objects can be placed. The outdoor area features dedicated test setups that enable reproducible data to be recorded with and without dust during real-world agriculture work. The proving ground and the setups are visualized in 3D.

</details>


### [76] [OctWorld: Long-Range World-Consistent Video Generation with Octree-Based 3D Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03919)
*Zelong Lv,Sicheng Xu,Jianfeng Xiang,Ruicheng Wang,Yue Dong,Yu Deng,Guangzhong Sun,Jiaolong Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OctWorld is a video diffusion framework with persistent 3D memory that generates explorable, world-consistent, high-fidelity visual scenes from a single image. It uses OctMap, a dynamic sparse octree-based 3D memory with TSDF fusion, to maintain spatial consistency during long-range camera trajectory generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key challenge in long-range world generation (extended camera paths with wide viewpoint coverage) is preserving spatial consistency when previously generated regions are revisited. Existing methods struggle with this issue.

Method: OctWorld introduces OctMap, an extensible and spatially adaptive 3D memory that progressively fuses generated visual observations and depth maps into a global representation. It employs TSDF fusion within a dynamic sparse octree whose spatial resolution adapts to image evidence, preserving geometric and appearance details across diverse scene scales while maintaining low memory overhead.

Result: OctWorld generates long-range, spatially consistent videos and outperforms prior methods on existing benchmarks and challenging long-range generation settings. OctMap provides advantages over point-based caches and fixed-resolution TSDF volumes.

Conclusion: The combination of video diffusion with persistent 3D memory (OctMap) successfully enables stable autoregressive world generation along user-specified camera trajectories while maintaining spatial consistency across extended camera paths.

Abstract: We present OctWorld, a video diffusion framework with persistent 3D memory for generating explorable, world-consistent, and high-fidelity visual scenes. Given a single image, OctWorld performs stable autoregressive world generation along user-specified camera trajectories. We focus on long-range generation, characterized by extended camera paths and wide viewpoint coverage, where preserving spatial consistency is particularly challenging when previously generated regions are revisited. To address this problem, we introduce OctMap, an extensible and spatially adaptive 3D memory that progressively fuses generated visual observations and their corresponding depth maps into a global representation. OctMap employs TSDF fusion within a dynamic sparse octree whose spatial resolution adapts to image evidence. This design preserves geometric and appearance details across diverse scene scales while maintaining low memory overhead. Experiments demonstrate that OctWorld generates long-range, spatially consistent videos and outperforms prior methods on both existing benchmarks and challenging long-range generation settings. OctMap also provides clear advantages over point-based caches and fixed-resolution TSDF volumes. Project page: https://maxtirerror.github.io/octworldpage/

</details>


### [77] [Sparse auto-regressive modeling for scene generation from multi-view images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03931)
*Thomas Lucas,Maxime Pietrantoni,Philippe Weinzaepfel,Wonjune Cho,Bardienus Pieter Duisterhof,Vincent Leroy,Jerome Revaud*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPAR3S introduces a sparse voxel-aligned 3D latent generative model for conditional scene completion from sparse views without requiring ground-truth 3D supervision. It uses a compact voxel-aligned 3D latent space representing only occupied voxels, learned via differentiable 3D Gaussian Splatting, and employs a masked autoregressive transformer to predict missing latent tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenge is that existing feed-forward reconstruction methods are limited to content visible in input images, while 3D generative modeling suffers from high computational cost of dense volumetric representations and scarcity of large-scale 3D supervision. The goal is to generate complete 3D scenes from sparse, unconstrained views in a computationally tractable manner.

Method: The method formulates 3D scene generation in a structured, compact, voxel-aligned 3D latent space where only occupied voxels are represented. This sparse latent space is learned directly from multi-view images using photometric supervision via differentiable 3D Gaussian Splatting. Given partial observed voxels from sparse input views, scene completion is achieved by training a masked autoregressive transformer that jointly models voxel occupancy and latent token values to predict missing tokens and their spatial support within the voxel grid.

Result: The method demonstrates effectiveness on synthetic indoor scenes, achieving higher novel-view quality than prior work. It also generalizes well to real-world data, as validated on the RealEstate10k dataset.

Conclusion: SPAR3S successfully enables efficient and spatially consistent generation of unseen regions in 3D scenes from sparse views without requiring ground-truth 3D supervision, balancing computational tractability with the ability to reason beyond observed content.

Abstract: Generating complete 3D scenes from sparse, unconstrained views is a fundamental challenge in 3D vision which requires reasoning beyond observed content while remaining computationally tractable. Existing feed-forward reconstruction methods are inherently limited to content visible in the input images, while 3D generative modeling is hindered by the high computational cost of dense volumetric representations and the scarcity of large-scale 3D supervision. We introduce SPAR3S, a sparse voxel-aligned 3D latent generative model for conditional scene completion without requiring ground-truth 3D data for supervision. Our key insight is to formulate 3D scene generation in a structured, compact, voxel-aligned 3D latent space where only occupied voxels are represented. We learn this sparse latent space directly from multi-view images using photometric supervision via differentiable 3D Gaussian Splatting. Given a partial set of observed voxels encoded from sparse input views, scene completion reduces to predicting the missing latent tokens and their spatial support within the voxel grid. To this end, we train a masked autoregressive transformer that jointly models voxel occupancy and latent token values, enabling efficient and spatially consistent generation of unseen regions. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on synthetic indoor scenes, achieving higher novel-view quality than prior work. We further validate its generalization on RealEstate10k, highlighting its applicability to real-world data.

</details>


### [78] [WorldReward: Reward Modeling for Camera-Conditioned World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03952)
*Yibin Wang,Zehan Wang,Junshu Tang,Zhimin Li,Yujie Zhou,Jiazi Bu,Pengyang Ling,Feng Han,Zhixiong Zhang,Long Xing,Shengyuan Ding,Ziang Li,Cheng Jin,Yuhang Zang,Jiaqi Wang,Tianyu Pang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WorldReward is a VLM-based reward model that evaluates both action consistency and visual quality in camera-conditioned world model videos by decomposing videos into action-aligned chunks, aggregating decisions through voting, and achieving superior human agreement compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing rewards for camera-conditioned world models are limited: geometry-based rewards cannot judge visual quality, while image-based rewards cannot capture action execution or temporal dynamics. A unified approach is needed that can evaluate both action-consistency and visual-quality in long videos.

Method: WorldReward decomposes paired videos into action-aligned chunks, organizes each chunk into structured visual evidence, and aggregates chunk-level decisions by voting into separate video-level action and visual-quality preferences. Training uses a large-scale reasoning-augmented preference dataset generated by a frontier VLM, refined through tool-based agent auditing and targeted human review.

Result: WorldReward achieves the highest agreement on all three dimensions (action consistency, appearance quality, motion quality), exceeding GPT-5.5 by 3.42, 1.45, and 3.56 percentage points respectively. When used for RL post-training of HY-WorldPlay 1.5, it consistently improves both action execution and visual quality across short- to long-term horizons.

Conclusion: The proposed WorldReward approach successfully unifies action-consistency and visual-quality evaluation using VLM-based pairwise preferences, demonstrating superior human agreement and effective RL post-training performance for world models.

Abstract: Camera-conditioned world models generate interactive videos in which commanded actions should induce the expected scene changes while appearance, geometry, and temporal dynamics remain coherent. Existing rewards assess these requirements separately: geometry-based rewards estimate trajectory execution but cannot judge the visual quality of the executed motion, whereas image-based rewards measure frame quality without capturing action execution or temporal dynamics. We posit that a vision-language model (VLM) offers a shared reasoning space for relating actions to their visual outcomes. However, judging a complete long video against its full action sequence creates a lengthy, noisy context in which short-lived local action evidence can be missed or diluted. We present WorldReward, a VLM-based pairwise preference reward model that unifies action-consistency and visual-quality evaluation for camera-conditioned world models. WorldReward decomposes paired videos into action-aligned chunks, organizes each chunk into structured visual evidence, and aggregates chunk-level decisions by voting into separate video-level action and visual-quality preferences. To train it, we construct a large-scale reasoning-augmented preference dataset using structured judgments generated by a frontier VLM and refined through tool-based agent auditing and targeted human review. We further introduce WorldReward-Bench, a human-annotated benchmark measuring reward-model agreement with human preferences across action consistency, appearance quality, and motion quality. WorldReward achieves the highest agreement on all three dimensions, exceeding GPT-5.5 by 3.42, 1.45, and 3.56 percentage points, respectively. When used for RL post-training of HY-WorldPlay 1.5, it consistently improves both action execution and visual quality across short- to long-term horizons.

</details>


### [79] [RARF: Region-Aware Rectified Flows for 3D Brain MRI Inpainting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03956)
*Tomas Guija-Valiente,Blanca Rodriguez-Gonzalez,Norberto Malpica,Angel Torrado-Carvajal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RARF is a task-agnostic region-aware rectified flow framework for 3D brain MRI inpainting, designed to reconstruct healthy tissue within pathological regions for improved automated brain MRI analysis. The framework is submitted to the BraTS Inpainting Challenge 2026.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical image inpainting has the potential to improve automated brain MRI analysis by reconstructing healthy tissue within pathological regions. Current methods may not adequately preserve patient-specific anatomical context while filling missing regions.

Method: RARF restricts the stochastic interpolation process to the inpainting region while keeping observed voxels fixed. A 3D neural network receives the partially voided image with Gaussian noise in the missing region, along with the inpainting mask and timestep. Training uses masked flow-matching and reconstruction-consistency objectives, combined with mask-aware preprocessing and data augmentation. During inference, the learned velocity field transports initial noise toward plausible reconstruction.

Result: Experiments under the BraTS evaluation protocol show that the proposed approach produces competitive reconstructions while maintaining anatomical consistency.

Conclusion: The RARF framework demonstrates effectiveness for 3D brain MRI inpainting, achieving competitive performance while preserving anatomical consistency. Source code is publicly available.

Abstract: Medical image inpainting has the potential to improve automated brain MRI analysis by reconstructing healthy tissue within pathological regions. We introduce RARF, a task-agnostic region-aware rectified flow framework for masked data generation. We instantiate the framework for 3D brain MRI inpainting as our submission to the BraTS Inpainting Challenge 2026. RARF restricts the stochastic interpolation process to the inpainting region, while the observed voxels remain fixed and provide patient-specific anatomical context. A three-dimensional neural network receives the partially voided image, with Gaussian noise filling the missing region, together with the inpainting mask and the corresponding timestep. The model is trained using masked flow-matching and reconstruction-consistency objectives, combined with mask-aware preprocessing and data augmentation. During inference, the learned velocity field transports the initial noise toward a plausible reconstruction of the missing tissue, which is then combined with the unchanged observed anatomy. Experiments under the BraTS evaluation protocol show that the proposed approach produces competitive reconstructions while maintaining anatomical consistency. Source code is available at: https://github.com/TomasGuija/rarf.

</details>


### [80] [Sharpening the Ensemble: An SSIM-Aligned Residual Refiner for Brain-MRI Inpainting Post-Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03981)
*Kubilay Kağan Kömürcü,İlkay Öksüz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper proposes a post-processing method to address blur artifacts in Brain-MRI inpainting by combining a deep ensemble of two top 2025 BraTS models with a lightweight residual refiner trained under L1 loss augmented with a structural-similarity term. This approach achieves modest but statistically significant SSIM improvements (0.8767→0.8780 on held-out, 0.8555→0.8572 on validation) with 62.6% of cases improved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current state-of-the-art Brain-MRI inpainting models produce blurry synthesized regions due to the mean-seeking behavior of L1 and MSE training losses, limiting the applicability of downstream analysis tools built for healthy brains.

Method: The authors form a deep ensemble of two co-first-place 2025 BraTS models and train a lightweight residual refiner on the ensemble's outputs using L1 loss augmented with a structural-similarity term (weight λ). They also conduct two ablation studies: (1) adding a third model to the ensemble, and (2) applying classical unsharp masking.

Result: At moderate λ, the refiner improves SSIM from 0.8767 to 0.8780 on held-out data and from 0.8555 to 0.8572 on the official validation leaderboard, with minimal MSE change. The improvement is consistent (62.6% of cases, p=2.2×10^{-7}), but over-weighting the structural term reverses the gain. Ablations show that adding any third model degrades performance, and classical unsharp masking fails to improve SSIM (best 0.8765 vs 0.8767).

Conclusion: The proposed post-processing stage is a cheap, reproducible method that improves an already strong ensemble without large-scale retraining. The gain reflects learned sharpening rather than indiscriminate sharpening, as demonstrated by failed ablations with additional models and classical unsharp masking.

Abstract: Brain-MRI inpainting replaces a masked region of a scan with synthesized, anatomically plausible healthy tissue, so that analysis tools built for healthy brains can be applied to images they would otherwise reject. On the BraTS local-synthesis benchmark, which ranks submissions on the structural similarity index (SSIM), the peak signal-to-noise ratio, and the mean squared error (MSE) jointly, the strongest recent models are accurate, but several report blurry synthesized regions and attribute this to the mean-seeking behavior of the $\ell_1$ and MSE terms in their training losses. We address this in post-processing, forming a deep ensemble of the two co-first-place 2025 models and training a lightweight residual refiner on the ensemble's own outputs under an $\ell_1$ loss augmented with a structural-similarity term whose weight $λ$ we vary. At a moderate $λ$ the refiner improves SSIM over the ensemble, from $0.8767$ to $0.8780$ on a held-out reproduction of the official scorer and from $0.8555$ to $0.8572$ on the official validation leaderboard, with essentially no change in MSE. The gain is small but consistent, improving $62.6\%$ of the held-out cases with a signed-rank $p=2.2\times10^{-7}$, whereas over-weighting the structural term reverses it. Two ablations bound the effect. Adding any third model to the two-model ensemble degrades it, and classical unsharp masking fails to improve SSIM at any strength (best $0.8765$ against $0.8767$), so the gain reflects learned rather than indiscriminate sharpening. The result is a cheap, reproducible post-processing stage that improves an already strong ensemble without any large-scale retraining.

</details>


### [81] [IchthyoNoma: Nomenclature and Context Sensitivity of Zero-Shot Biological Vision--Language Models for Bangladeshi Freshwater Fish Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03985)
*Nazim-E-Alam,Tarek Rahman,Md Kishor Morol*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study audits zero-shot vision-language models (CLIP, BioCLIP, BioCLIP2, and multilingual Jina CLIP v2) for freshwater fish species recognition in Bangladesh, revealing that performance reflects multiple factors beyond visual species knowledge, including multilingual alignment, nomenclature, and prompt formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot VLMs are increasingly used as training-free species recognizers, but reported accuracy may reflect factors beyond actual visual species knowledge. The researchers aimed to audit whether these models truly recognize species visually or rely on other cues like linguistic alignment or naming conventions.

Method: The researchers tested four VLM variants (CLIP, BioCLIP, BioCLIP2, and multilingual Jina CLIP v2) on two Bangladeshi freshwater fish datasets (BFF-15 with 10,321 images and SylFishBD) using various naming conventions (English common names, scientific names, Bengali names) and intervention tests (blur, gray masking, white masking).

Result: BioCLIP2 achieved 72.36% on BFF-15 with English common names and 68.91% on SylFishBD with scientific names, compared to generic CLIP's 25.15% and 14.40%. Bengali prompts showed near-chance performance (14.22-14.29%). Jina partially recovered Bengali discrimination (21.89% and 16.36%), but bare Bengali names returned to chance. Interventions showed species-dependent effects: no significant weak-blur effect, modest losses from stronger blur/gray masking, and a strong white-mask artifact.

Conclusion: Zero-shot biological VLM scores jointly reflect biological specialization, multilingual alignment, nomenclature, prompt formulation, and context. Performance cannot be attributed solely to visual species knowledge, as multiple factors contribute to recognition accuracy.

Abstract: Zero-shot vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as training-free species recognizers, but reported accuracy can reflect more than visual species knowledge. We audit CLIP, BioCLIP, BioCLIP2, and a multilingual Jina CLIP v2 control on seven freshwater-fish categories from two Bangladeshi sources (10,321 images). BioCLIP2 reaches 72.36% on BFF-15 with English common names and 68.91% on SylFishBD with scientific names, versus 25.15% and 14.40% for generic CLIP. BioCLIP2 Bengali prompts are near chance in balanced accuracy (14.22-14.29%); Jina partially recovers Bengali discrimination to 21.89% and 16.36%, but bare Bengali names return to 14.29% on both sources. Paired SylFishBD interventions show no significant weak-blur effect, modest losses from stronger blur/gray masking, a larger white-mask artifact, and strong species dependence. Zero-shot biological VLM scores therefore jointly reflect biological specialization, multilingual alignment, nomenclature, prompt formulation, and context.

</details>


### [82] [Catalogue Photography as a Cold Start: Toward Deployable Carbide Burr Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03995)
*Abilash Philip Madavath,Chandra Yuvesh Aubeeluck,Augustin Raju,Nicolas Pyschny,Felix Hackelöer,Florian Zwanzig*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses automated quality assurance for milling tools and carbide rotary burrs using computer vision under cold-start conditions (no labeled field data). The researchers investigate how well catalogue photography can support an industrial recognition pipeline when transferred to field photographs, measuring the domain shift gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quality assurance task of verifying manufactured batches of milling tools against production order sheets remains manual and error-prone. Automating this with computer vision faces a critical cold-start constraint since no labeled field imagery exists, leaving manufacturer catalogue photography as the only supervision source. The motivation is to understand if catalogue supervision can effectively transfer to real-world deployment.

Method: The researchers used metric learning approaches with off-the-shelf frozen feature extractors and targeted representation learning. They evaluated transfer performance from catalogue images to held-out field photographs, measuring clustering quality using adjusted Rand index. They tested simple domain adaptation techniques including grayscale conversion and Hungarian assignment for constrained retrieval.

Result: Three key insights emerged: (1) Off-the-shelf frozen feature extractors fail to reliably separate head shape and tooth profile attributes, requiring targeted representation learning. (2) Metric learning achieves near-perfect unsupervised cluster discovery on catalogue images (adjusted Rand index 0.94-0.97), but less than 50% of this performance transfers to field photographs. (3) The largest transfer gains come from simple domain-reducing changes: grayscale conversion (+0.22) and Hungarian assignment using known order sheet constraints (+0.11).

Conclusion: Catalogue photography serves as a useful cold-start capability but is not deployment-ready as a training domain. The study provides empirical baselines and an evaluation protocol for catalogue-to-field transfer in precision tool manufacturing. The key takeaway is that simple domain-sensitivity reductions outperform model scale or representation complexity for cross-domain transfer in this industrial setting.

Abstract: Verifying that manufactured batches of milling tools or carbide rotary burrs conform to production order sheets remains a largely manual and error-prone quality assurance task. Automating this process with computer vision faces a critical cold-start constraint since no labelled imagery is available, leaving manufacturer catalogue photography as the sole source of supervision. We investigate how far catalogue supervision can support an industrial recognition pipeline under domain shift, explicitly measuring the gap between catalogue separability and performance on held-out field photographs. Our findings reveal three key insights. First, off-the-shelf frozen feature extractors do not reliably separate the two task attributes, head shape and tooth profile, motivating targeted representation learning. Second, metric learning produces near-perfect unsupervised cluster discovery on catalogue images (adjusted Rand index 0.94--0.97), but less than half of this gain transfers to field photographs. Third, the largest transfer gains do not come from model scale or representation complexity, but from simple changes that reduce domain sensitivity: converting images to grayscale (+0.22) and constraining retrieval using the known order sheet via Hungarian assignment (+0.11). We therefore treat catalogue photography as a useful cold start rather than a deployment-ready training domain, and provide empirical baselines and an evaluation protocol for catalogue-to-field transfer in precision tool manufacturing.

</details>


### [83] [The Blind Spot in 2D Infants' Pose Estimation:Robust Learning from Noisy Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04009)
*Emanuele Cardinale,Marco Proietti,Alessandro Cacciatore,Maria Francesca Spadea,Lucia Migliorelli,Sara Moccia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses noisy annotations in pose estimation for preterm infants, introducing REMIND - a clustering-based method that uses training dynamics to identify noisy keypoint labels without prior knowledge of noise distribution. Evaluated on a proprietary dataset of 46 preterm infant videos, it achieves up to 93% AUC across three PE architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Noisy annotations severely impair deep learning model performance. While label noise robustness is well-studied in classification, it remains underexplored in pose estimation. This is critical in clinical settings (neonatology) where preterm infant pose estimation supports neurodevelopmental assessment, but annotations are prone to errors due to keypoint self-occlusions and caregiver interference.

Method: REMIND - a clustering-based keypoint-selection strategy that exploits keypoint-wise training dynamics to identify noisy labels. It requires no prior knowledge of the noise distribution, enabling noise-free model training. Evaluated with three different PE architectures on the NeoPose dataset (46 videos of 46 preterm infants in real clinical settings).

Result: REMIND achieves up to 93% Area Under the Curve (AUC) in identifying noisy annotations across multiple corruption scenarios, using three different PE architectures.

Conclusion: This is the first study to explicitly address label noise in preterm infants' pose estimation, paving the way for trustworthy learning-based algorithms for infant monitoring when data quality cannot be guaranteed.

Abstract: Noisy annotations pose a significant challenge for supervised deep learning, as neural networks rely on large-scale, high-quality labeled data whose corruption can severely impair model performance. Although robustness to label noise has been extensively studied for classification tasks, it remains relatively underexplored in Pose Estimation (PE). This limitation becomes critical in clinical contexts, including neonatology, where PE of preterm infants is used to support the assessment of spontaneous motility, a key indicator of neurodevelopmental trajectories. In such settings, infants' images labeling is further hindered by visual challenges (e.g., keypoint self-occlusions, caregiver interference), making the annotation process inherently susceptible to errors. To tackle noisy annotations in PE, we introduce REliable keypoint selection via Memory of traINing Dynamics (REMIND), a clustering-based keypoint-selection strategy that exploits keypoint-wise training dynamics to identify noisy labels without assuming any prior knowledge of the noise distribution, thus enabling noise-free model training. When evaluated on the proprietary NeoPose dataset, comprising 46 videos of 46 preterm infants recorded in real clinical settings, REMIND correctly identifies noisy annotations across multiple corruption scenarios, achieving up to 93\% Area Under the Curve (AUC) with three different PE architectures used in the relevant literature. To our knowledge, this is the first study to explicitly address label noise in preterm infants' PE, paving the way for the design of trustworthy learning-based algorithms for infants'monitoring support when data quality cannot be guaranteed.

</details>


### [84] [Stable and Scalable Bundle Adjustment of Holistic 3D Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04026)
*Shaohui Liu,Rémi Pautrat,Daniel Barath,Richard Hartley,Viktor Larsson,Marc Pollefeys*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified framework that extends traditional Bundle Adjustment (BA) to jointly optimize geometric features (points, lines) and higher-order relations (coplanarity, parallelism) by modeling groups as camera-like entities and expressing constraints through 2D reprojection measurements, preserving sparsity while improving geometric accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Bundle Adjustment was developed for optimizing camera intrinsics, poses and sparse 3D points. While extensions to incorporate lines and other primitives exist, integrating richer geometric structures like parallelism, coplanarity, or wireframes often introduces significantly increased computational cost and reduced numerical stability. The authors seek to address this limitation.

Method: The authors propose: (1) A taxonomy distinguishing scalable geometric features with direct 2D measurements (points, lines) from groups encoding higher-order relations (coplanarity, parallelism); (2) Modeling groups as camera-like entities within the BA framework; (3) Expressing group constraints and cross-feature relations through 2D reprojection measurements; (4) Formulating group-induced and cross-feature reprojection errors to preserve sparsity structure of classical point-based BA under Schur elimination.

Result: Experiments on real-world and synthetic datasets demonstrate runtime performance comparable to classical point-only bundle adjustment, while producing significantly richer 3D structures and improved geometric accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed unified framework successfully extends bundle adjustment to jointly optimize geometric features and higher-order relations, achieving computational efficiency comparable to classical point-only BA while delivering richer 3D structures and improved geometric accuracy.

Abstract: Bundle Adjustment (BA) is a cornerstone of 3D computer vision and has benefited from decades of advances in sparse optimization and numerical methods. It was originally developed for jointly optimizing camera intrinsics, poses and sparse 3D points. While extensions incorporate lines and other primitives, integrating richer geometric structures such as parallelism, coplanarity, or wireframes often introduces significantly increased computational cost and reduced numerical stability. In this paper, we propose a unified framework that extends bundle adjustment to jointly optimize geometric features and higher-order relations. We first introduce a taxonomy that distinguishes scalable geometric features with direct 2D measurements (e.g., points and lines), from groups encoding higher-order relations (e.g., coplanarity, parallelism, etc.), where we show that groups can be modeled as camera-like entities within the bundle adjustment framework. Building on this formulation, we propose that both group constraints and cross-feature relations (i.e., point-line associations) can be expressed through 2D reprojection measurements. By formulating group-induced and cross-feature reprojection errors, we preserve the sparsity structure of classical point-based BA under Schur elimination, while avoiding direct 3D regularization that degrades the conditioning and stability. Experiments on both real-world and synthetic datasets demonstrate runtime performance comparable to classical point-only bundle adjustment, while producing significantly richer 3D structures and improved geometric accuracy.

</details>


### [85] [DSAQuant: Denoising-Stage-Aligned Quantization-Aware Training for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04031)
*Shuaiting Li,Zelin Gao,Haibin Shen,Yujun Shen,Haotong Qin,Yinghao Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DSAQuant addresses the challenge where quantization-aware training for video diffusion models (VDMs) causes severe degradation in visual details, texture fidelity, and sharpness. The paper proposes aligning quantization training and inference with the denoising stages - early steps focus on global structure/motion while later steps refine details. The method achieves up to 6.60 improvement in VBench average score under W3A3 quantization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video diffusion models achieve impressive text-to-video generation but have high memory/computational costs. Quantization-aware training (QAT) compresses these models but causes severe degradation in visual details, texture fidelity, and sharpness, while preserving prompt semantics, global layout, and coarse motion.

Method: DSAQuant proposes two key techniques: 1) Denoising-Stage Oriented Supervision - uses teacher distillation in early denoising steps for stable structure planning, shifts later steps toward target-driven optimization for detail reconstruction; 2) Denoising-Stage Gated Guidance - disables CFG (Classifier-Free Guidance) in final denoising steps to prevent amplifying quantization errors into high-frequency artifacts.

Result: Extensive experiments on Wan and CogVideoX families under W4A4 and W3A3 settings show DSAQuant consistently outperforms SOTA QAT baseline, improving VBench average score by up to 6.60 under aggressive W3A3 quantization while preserving strong text-video alignment.

Conclusion: Effective VDM quantization requires not only reducing quantization error, but also aligning quantization training and inference with the stage-wise nature of video diffusion. The paper demonstrates that understanding the denoising process is crucial for successful quantization.

Abstract: Video diffusion models (VDMs) have achieved impressive progress in text-to-video generation, but their high memory and computational costs hinder practical deployment. Quantization-aware training (QAT) is an effective solution for compressing and accelerating advanced generative models without runtime overhead at inference. However, existing QAT methods suffer from a distinctive challenge in VDMs: while they often preserve prompt semantics, global layout, and coarse motion, the quantized model severely degrades visual details, texture fidelity, and sharpness. In this paper, we trace this degradation to the timestep-agnostic design of conventional quantization pipelines, which overlooks the stage-wise functionality of video denoising. In VDMs, early denoising steps mainly establish global structure and motion, whereas middle and late steps refine local appearance and high-frequency details. Based on this insight, we propose DSAQuant, a Denoising-Stage-Aligned Quantization-aware training framework for VDMs. During training, Denoising-Stage Oriented Supervision preserves teacher distillation in early steps for stable structure planning, while shifting later steps toward target-driven optimization to enhance detail reconstruction. During inference, Denoising-Stage Gated Guidance disables CFG in the final denoising steps to prevent it from amplifying quantization-induced errors into high-frequency artifacts. Extensive experiments on the Wan and CogVideoX families under W4A4 and W3A3 settings show that DSAQuant consistently outperforms the SOTA QAT baseline, improving the VBench average score by up to 6.60 under aggressive W3A3 quantization while preserving strong text-video alignment. These results demonstrate that effective VDM quantization requires not only reducing quantization error, but also aligning quantization training and inference with the stage-wise nature of video diffusion.

</details>


### [86] [Editable Visual Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04034)
*Junyan Ye,Wei Liu,Dongzhi Jiang,Zichen Wen,HaoDong Li,Zhutao Lv,Jiaxin Lin,Jinhua Yu,Jun He,Zilong Huang,Rui Chen,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes "Editable Visual Design," a new paradigm that combines the strengths of diffusion-based image generation (visual expressiveness) and code-based generation (precise layout control). The system uses a VLM as a "creative brain" and an image generation model as a "visual world simulator," operating under an "imagine first, then act" closed-loop workflow to produce editable artifacts with decoupled layers and real text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses a fundamental limitation in current visual generation approaches: diffusion models produce flattened bitmaps with error-prone text that cannot be post-edited layer-by-layer, while code-based generation lacks global aesthetic intuition and struggles with complex visual assets. The authors seek to bridge these two approaches to achieve both refined aesthetics and production-grade editability.

Method: The proposed method uses a Coding Agent-driven paradigm with a VLM as the "creative brain" for requirement comprehension, task planning, and aesthetic judgment. An image generation model serves as an "on-demand visual world simulator" to synthesize standalone visual assets. The workflow follows an "imagine first, then act" closed-loop process: the agent generates isolated assets, writes native HTML/CSS, and iteratively refines the design against visual rendering feedback. The system also includes "Agent Design Replay" to faithfully reproduce the creative and reasoning trajectory.

Result: The system achieves both refined aesthetics and production-grade editability. It delivers editable artifacts with decoupled layers and real text, enabling users to perform intuitive mouse dragging and layout adjustments on a graphical user interface. Validations on posters, infographics, and other scenarios demonstrate the paradigm's effectiveness.

Conclusion: The "Editable Visual Design" paradigm successfully addresses the limitations of both diffusion-based and code-based visual generation by combining them through a Coding Agent approach. This hybrid method achieves the visual quality of diffusion models while maintaining the editability and precision of code-based generation, representing a promising new direction in automated visual design.

Abstract: While diffusion base models such as GPT-Image-2 and Nano-Banana exhibit remarkable visual expressiveness, their end-to-end generation inherently yields flattened bitmaps with error-prone text, precluding layer-wise post-editing. Conversely, code-based visual generation via Coding Agents provides precise layout control and decoupled layers, yet remains constrained by a lack of global aesthetic intuition and the difficulty of coding complex visual assets.
  To address this, we propose Editable Visual Design, a new paradigm driven by a Coding Agent. We designate the VLM as the ``creative brain'' for requirement comprehension, task planning, and aesthetic judgment, while utilizing the image generation model as an on-demand ``visual world simulator'' to synthesize standalone visual assets. Operating under an ``imagine first, then act'' closed-loop workflow, the agent generates isolated assets, writes native HTML/CSS, and iteratively refines the design against visual rendering feedback.
  Furthermore, Agent Design Replay faithfully reproduces the creative and reasoning trajectory akin to that of professional human designers. Ultimately, the system delivers editable artifacts with decoupled layers and real text, enabling users to perform intuitive mouse dragging and layout adjustments on a graphical user interface. Validations on posters, infographics, and other scenarios show that this paradigm successfully achieves both refined aesthetics and production-grade editability.

</details>


### [87] [Continuous Actions from Discrete Minds: Latent-Aligned Planning for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04070)
*Ruoyu Yao,Yusen Xie,Qingzhao Liu,Pei Liu,Zewei Yang,Yipeng Zhu,Xiaolong Wang,Jun Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LaPla is a unified Vision-Language-Action (VLA) framework that bridges discrete reasoning of VLMs with continuous physics-constrained autonomous driving through latent-aligned planning. It uses a residual VQ-VAE action tokenizer to encode vehicle kinematics into a latent space, then projects multimodal inputs (images, actions, text) directly into this pretrained latent space, eliminating quantization errors and enabling single forward pass action generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is bridging the gap between the discrete reasoning nature of Vision-Language Models and the continuous, physics-constrained requirements of autonomous driving. Existing VLA methods suffer from quantization errors due to discrete codebook lookups and time-consuming autoregressive generation.

Method: LaPla introduces: (1) An action tokenizer based on residual VQ-VAE capturing vehicle kinematics and trajectory features; (2) A physical prior approach repurposing the VQ-VAE latent space to bridge semantic and action modalities; (3) Concurrent action queries for causal attention to multimodal context (multi-view images, historical actions, textual instructions) in a single forward pass; (4) A frozen decoder that translates continuous latents into physically plausible actions without quantization errors.

Result: On nuScenes benchmark: 15.52% reduction in long-horizon L2 error compared to state-of-the-art VLA methods. On NVIDIA AlpaSim simulator: 33.34 percentage points improvement in success rate with significantly reduced inference latency.

Conclusion: LaPla successfully unifies semantic understanding with precise motion execution through latent-aligned planning, achieving superior performance in both open-loop (lower L2 error) and closed-loop (higher success rate, lower latency) autonomous driving scenarios.

Abstract: Bridging the gap between the discrete reasoning of Vision-Language Models and the continuous, physics-constrained nature of autonomous driving remains a significant challenge. In this work, we introduce LaPla, a unified Vision-Language-Action (VLA) framework featuring latent-aligned planning to seamlessly ground semantic understanding in precise motion execution. We first design an action tokenizer based on a residual vector-quantized variational autoencoder (VQ-VAE), capturing vehicle kinematics and encoding trajectory features into a structured latent space. Rather than discrete codebook lookups that inevitably introduce quantization errors, LaPla repurposes this representation as a physical prior to bridge the modality gap between high-dimensional semantics and the raw action space. Specifically, given multimodal inputs integrating multi-view images, historical actions, and textual instructions, LaPla incorporates concurrent action queries to causally attend to the multimodal context in a single forward pass, projecting hidden states directly into the pretrained VQ-VAE latent space. The frozen decoder then translates these continuous latents into actions, effectively eliminating quantization errors and ensuring physically plausible trajectories while bypassing time-consuming autoregressive generation. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes benchmark demonstrate that LaPla achieves competitive open-loop performance, reducing long-horizon L2 error by 15.52% compared to state-of-the-art VLA methods. Closed-loop evaluations on the NVIDIA AlpaSim simulator further confirm its superior capability in ensuring smooth driving progress, improving the success rate by 33.34 percentage points with significantly reduced inference latency.

</details>


### [88] [TAP-Path: Task-Adaptive Structural and Token Pruning for Efficient and Trustworthy Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04071)
*Mehedi Hasan,Ashfak Yeafi,Md Khairul Islam*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TAP-Path is a task-adaptive compression framework for histopathology foundation models that directly restructures a pretrained Virchow2 encoder through transformer-block selection/pruning, input-adaptive patch-token pruning, multi-depth feature recovery, and a lightweight task head. The method reduces encoder parameters by 24.96% and compute by 35.20% while achieving competitive or improved accuracy on a 32-class histopathology benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pathology foundation models improve transferable representation learning for histopathology, but recent gains rely on encoders with hundreds of millions of parameters and high inference cost, making them computationally expensive for practical deployment.

Method: TAP-Path combines five key techniques: (1) validation-driven transformer-block selection, (2) physical removal of redundant blocks, (3) input-adaptive patch-token pruning, (4) multi-depth feature recovery, and (5) a lightweight gated task head. The final model retains 24 of 32 transformer blocks and 70% of patch tokens.

Result: TAP-Path achieved 87.98±0.067% test accuracy, 81.26±0.49% balanced accuracy, and 82.38±0.48% macro-F1 on a 32-class histopathology benchmark, compared with 86.89% for full Virchow2 and 87.67% for UNI2-h. It reduced encoder parameters by 24.96% (631.24M to 473.70M) and analytical encoder compute by 35.20% (340.13G to 220.40G FLOPs). Brier score was 0.1800±0.0005 and failure-detection AUROC was 0.9047±0.0060. On external evaluation (433 CPTAC samples), it achieved 91.22±0.83% accuracy and 91.10±0.81% balanced accuracy.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive structural and token sparsification can improve the accuracy-efficiency trade-off of large pathology foundation models while preserving reliability under internal and external evaluation. The approach successfully compresses a large foundation model without separate distillation, maintaining or slightly improving accuracy while significantly reducing computational cost.

Abstract: Pathology foundation models improve transferable representation learning for histopathology, but recent gains often rely on encoders with hundreds of millions of parameters and high inference cost. We propose TAP-Path, a task-adaptive compression framework that directly restructures a pretrained Virchow2 encoder rather than distilling it into a separate student. TAP-Path combines validation-driven transformer-block selection, physical removal of redundant blocks, input-adaptive patch-token pruning, multi-depth feature recovery, and a lightweight gated task head. The final model retains 24 of 32 transformer blocks and 70% of patch tokens after pruning, reducing encoder parameters by 24.96% (631.24M to 473.70M) and analytical encoder compute by 35.20% (340.13G to 220.40G FLOPs). Across three task-head optimization seeds, TAP-Path achieved $87.98 \pm 0.067%$ test accuracy, $81.26 \pm 0.49%$ balanced accuracy, and $82.38 \pm 0.48%$ macro-F1 on a 32-class histopathology benchmark, compared with 86.89% for full Virchow2 and 87.67% for UNI2-h. TAP-Path achieved a Brier score of $0.1800 \pm 0.0005$ and failure-detection AUROC of $0.9047 \pm 0.0060$. A validation-only rare-aware objective improved rare-class balanced accuracy in a secondary operating analysis. Frozen external evaluation on 433 CPTAC samples yielded $91.22 \pm 0.83%$ accuracy and $91.10 \pm 0.81%$ balanced accuracy. These results show that task-adaptive structural and token sparsification can improve the accuracy-efficiency trade-off of large pathology foundation models while preserving reliability under internal and external evaluation.

</details>


### [89] [CORE: Improving Compositional Reasoning in MLLM Embedding via Reranker Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04083)
*Tingyu Song,Mingxin Li,Yanzhao Zhang,Dingkun Long,Chu Liu,Pengjun Xie,Yilun Zhao,Shu Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CORE is a method that distills compositional judgments from a cross-attentive reranker into an embedding model by using a Rank-KL objective and five-level compositional matching candidates. It achieves state-of-the-art results on compositional reasoning benchmarks while maintaining standard retrieval performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLM-based embedding models struggle with compositional retrieval - failing to distinguish scenes with same concepts but different attribute-object bindings. However, the same backbone can make these distinctions when used as a cross-attentive reranker, suggesting the potential to transfer this capability to the embedding model.

Method: CORE synthesizes candidate lists spanning five compositional matching levels and introduces a Rank-KL objective that trains the embedding model to reproduce the reranker's fine-grained ranking. The paper compares three training approaches: contrastive learning, pairwise CoSENT, and listwise Rank-KL under identical data and tuning budgets.

Result: Rank-KL achieves strongest overall performance. CORE-RERANKER-8B achieves 82.7% total average on COLA, SUGARCREPE++, and NEGBENCH benchmarks, outperforming Jina-Reranker by 10.7 points. CORE-EMBED-8B achieves the best total average (0.666) among all evaluated embedding models. Improvements transfer to MCMR benchmark without sacrificing retrieval performance on COCO and Flickr30K.

Conclusion: The Rank-KL objective effectively distills compositional reasoning from the reranker to the embedding model. Both CoSENT and Rank-KL outperform contrastive learning when using multi-level supervision, with Rank-KL being the most effective. The method achieves new state-of-the-art on compositional reasoning benchmarks while preserving standard retrieval capability.

Abstract: MLLM-based embedding models remain limited in compositional retrieval, often failing to distinguish scenes containing the same concepts but different attribute-object bindings. Yet the same backbone can resolve such distinctions when used as a cross-attentive reranker, motivating us to distill its compositional judgments into the embedding model. We propose CORE, which synthesizes candidate lists spanning five compositional matching levels and introduces a Rank-KL objective that trains the embedding model to reproduce the reranker's fine-grained ranking. We further introduce a graded evaluation protocol and compare contrastive learning, pairwise CoSENT, and listwise Rank-KL under the same data and tuning budget. Our comparison shows that both CoSENT and Rank-KL use the multi-level supervision more effectively than contrastive learning, with Rank-KL achieving the strongest overall performance. Across three compositional reasoning benchmarks (COLA, SUGARCREPE++, NEGBENCH), CORE-RERANKER-8B achieves an 82.7% total average, outperforming Jina-Reranker by 10.7 points, while CORE-EMBED-8B achieves the best total average (0.666) among all evaluated embedding models. The improvements transfer to the MCMR benchmark without sacrificing retrieval performance on COCO and Flickr30K.

</details>


### [90] [Efficient Semantic Understanding from Digital Foveation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04088)
*Caterina Caccavella,Vittorio Fra,Andreas Ziegler,Giulia D'Angelo,Yulia Sandamirskaya*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a lightweight active-vision pipeline for efficient semantic segmentation using digital foveated perception, where computational resources are allocated selectively based on saliency rather than uniform dense processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current semantic segmentation methods allocate computational resources uniformly across the entire image, regardless of scene complexity. This is inefficient. The authors are inspired by biological vision systems (foveal vision) and aim to achieve semantic understanding more efficiently through selective, adaptive observation.

Method: The proposed pipeline combines five key components: (1) Saliency-driven fixation selection to determine where to look, (2) High-resolution foveal observations for detailed analysis of focused regions, (3) Low-resolution contextual information for scene understanding, (4) Semantic accumulation to build understanding over multiple fixations, and (5) Adaptive computation to allocate resources dynamically. Evaluation uses both dense prediction metrics and object-level metrics to measure semantic understanding under sparse observations.

Result: On ADE20K-Object dataset: A single foveated observation achieves 95.9% of baseline Top-1 accuracy and 96.9% of baseline Top-3 accuracy while using only 4.7% of computational cost. At scene level, semantic accumulation recovers 90.6% of baseline object recall while using 58.6% of computation.

Conclusion: Substantial semantic understanding can emerge from sparse observations when computation is allocated selectively. Active vision represents an efficient alternative to uniform dense processing. The authors argue that evaluation protocols beyond conventional pixel-wise segmentation metrics are needed to properly assess such approaches.

Abstract: Dense semantic segmentation allocates computational resources uniformly across the entire image, regardless of scene complexity or task relevance. Inspired by biological vision, we investigate whether semantic understanding can be achieved more efficiently through digital foveated perception. We introduce a lightweight active-vision pipeline that combines saliency-driven fixation selection, high-resolution foveal observations, low-resolution contextual information, semantic accumulation, and adaptive computation. Beyond conventional dense prediction metrics, we use object-level evaluation to measure semantic understanding under sparse observations. On ADE20K-Object, a single foveated observation achieves 95.9% of the baseline Top-1 accuracy and 96.9% of the baseline Top-3 accuracy while requiring only 4.7% of the computational cost. At the scene level, semantic accumulation recovers 90.6% of the baseline object recall while using 58.6% of the computation. These results suggest that substantial semantic understanding can emerge from sparse observations when computation is allocated selectively, highlighting active vision as an efficient alternative to uniform dense processing and motivating evaluation protocols beyond conventional pixel-wise segmentation metrics.

</details>


### [91] [The Shape of Time: Video-Token Contrast for Temporal Understanding in VideoLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04110)
*Yumeng Shi,Quanyu Long,Yin Wu,Wenya Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VT-Contrast is a representation-level temporal counterfactual objective for Video Language Models (VideoLMs) that addresses the mismatch between ordered video input and text-based supervision. It supervises late-layer last-frame video tokens and contrasts order-preserving views with reordered counterfactuals graded by Kendall tau distance, without requiring architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern VideoLMs receive ordered video streams but are supervised on generated text rather than video-token representations where event dynamics should emerge. This allows models to learn temporal answers from shortcuts (objects, scenes, language priors) without requiring internal video representations to capture event progression.

Method: VT-Contrast supervises selected late-layer last-frame video tokens where temporal information is expected to be integrated before language generation, and contrasts order-preserving views with same-video reordered counterfactuals graded by Kendall tau distance. It requires no architectural changes and is compatible with diverse VideoLM training tasks.

Result: VT-Contrast improves overall performance across temporal understanding benchmarks.

Conclusion: VT-Contrast provides an effective representation-level temporal supervision approach that addresses the temporal supervision gap in VideoLMs, achieving improved temporal understanding without architectural modifications.

Abstract: Seeing frames in order does not mean representing time. Modern VideoLMs receive ordered video streams, yet their main supervision acts on generated text rather than video-token representations where event dynamics should first emerge. This mismatch allows models to learn temporal answers from shortcuts such as objects, scenes, and language priors, without requiring internal video representations to capture event progression. To address this, we propose VT-Contrast, a representation-level temporal counterfactual objective for VideoLMs. Its design asks where temporal supervision should act and what temporal differences it should expose. VT-Contrast supervises selected late-layer last-frame video tokens, where temporal information is expected to be integrated before language generation, and contrasts order-preserving views with same-video reordered counterfactuals graded by Kendall tau distance. It requires no architectural changes, is compatible with diverse VideoLM training tasks, and improves overall performance across temporal understanding benchmarks. Our code is available at https://github.com/ANDgate99/VT-Contrast.

</details>


### [92] [BooM-VVT: Boosting Mask-Free Video Virtual Try-On with Image-Level Pseudo Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04120)
*Wei Zhang,Xin Li,Peishu Shi,Jialin Gao,Xuekang Peng,Zhichao Lian,Yeying Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BooM-VVT is a mask-free video virtual try-on (VVT) framework that generates realistic videos of persons wearing target garments without requiring masks for localization. It addresses key limitations of existing keyframe-driven methods including mask dependency, coarse keyframe sampling, and scarcity of multi-view data through multi-stage training with pseudo data, garment-sensitive keyframe sampling, and Frame-Shared 3D-RoPE. The method also introduces OmniView, a large-scale multi-view try-on dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VVT methods rely on masks to localize try-on regions, making them vulnerable to large motions and severe occlusions. While mask-free image-based try-on methods exist, extending them to videos is prohibitively expensive due to the need for costly video-level pseudo data. Additionally, coarse keyframe sampling and lack of multi-view try-on data limit garment consistency and handling of diverse try-on tasks.

Method: The paper proposes three key technical contributions: (1) A multi-stage training strategy leveraging image-level pseudo data for mask-free localization learning, reducing the need for expensive video-level pseudo data; (2) Garment-Sensitive Keyframe Sampling that selects keyframes based on garment-relevant body regions; (3) Frame-Shared 3D-RoPE to establish spatiotemporal correspondences between keyframes and target video frames for accurate garment-detail transfer. A new dataset OmniView is also constructed.

Result: BooM-VVT achieves superior temporal consistency and garment fidelity over existing methods in extensive experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed BooM-VVT successfully addresses the mask dependency issue in VVT through innovative multi-stage training, improves keyframe selection for better garment capture, and introduces Frame-Shared 3D-RoPE for accurate spatiotemporal transfer. The OmniView dataset enables reliable try-on video generation under complex camera viewpoints.

Abstract: Video virtual try-on (VVT) aims to generate realistic videos of a person wearing a target garment. Recent methods leverage a keyframe-driven video generation paradigm to improve in-the-wild performance, yet they still rely on masks to localize try-on regions, making them vulnerable to large motions and severe occlusions. Although mask-free image-based try-on methods have shown promising results by leveraging large-scale pseudo data, extending this paradigm to videos remains difficult, as constructing video-level pseudo data is prohibitively expensive.
