Furthermore, coarse keyframe sampling and the scarcity of multi-view try-on data limit existing keyframe-driven methods in maintaining garment consistency and handling diverse try-on tasks.
  To address these challenges, we propose BooM-VVT, a mask-free VVT framework built upon the keyframe-driven paradigm. To achieve mask-free VVT, we introduce a multi-stage training strategy that leverages image-level pseudo data for mask-free localization learning, substantially reducing the need for costly video-level pseudo data. To improve garment consistency, we propose Garment-Sensitive Keyframe Sampling, which selects keyframes based on garment-relevant body regions to better capture garment appearance. We further introduce Frame-Shared 3D-RoPE to establish spatiotemporal correspondences between keyframes and target video frames for accurate garment-detail transfer. Finally, we construct OmniView, a large-scale multi-view try-on dataset to support reliable try-on video generation under complex camera viewpoints and diverse try-on tasks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BooM-VVT achieves superior temporal consistency and garment fidelity over existing methods. Project page: https://boomvvt.github.io/boomvvt.

</details>


### [93] [Beyond Retrieval: Progressive Latent Memory Evolution for Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04131)
*Hongyu Qu,Guangming Yao,Ling Xing,Xiaobin Hu,Rongxing Ding,Guibin Zhang,Fan Zhang,Yi Yuan,Xiangbo Shu,Shuicheng Yan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LatentStream introduces a progressive latent working memory framework for streaming video understanding that shifts from store-and-retrieve to retrieve-and-internalize paradigm. It organizes visual history into hierarchical memory levels and enables latent memory tokens to iteratively retrieve and internalize historical evidence for compact, confident streaming reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming video understanding approaches use store-and-retrieve paradigms that keep historical evidence as external visual context, preventing internalization into a compact evolving latent memory. This limits the ability to continuously guide streaming reasoning under strict causality and bounded memory constraints.

Method: Three key components: (1) Query-agnostic Hierarchical Streaming Memory organizes visual history into short-, mid-, and long-term levels using Jenks-guided adaptive consolidation under fixed memory budget. (2) Hierarchical Latent Memory Evolution equips latent memory tokens with progressively expanding receptive fields to iteratively retrieve and internalize historical evidence. (3) Progressive Confidence-guided Latent Memory Optimization uses hierarchical progression reward from group-wise predictive entropy to jointly refine latent memory tokens and retrieved evidence.

Result: LatentStream achieves new state-of-the-art results on existing online and offline video benchmarks.

Conclusion: The retrieve-and-internalize paradigm with progressive latent working memory successfully enables more compact and confident streaming reasoning compared to traditional store-and-retrieve approaches.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to process continuous visual inputs and respond to user queries under strict causality and bounded memory. Existing approaches typically compress historical observations into an external memory bank and retrieve query-relevant evidence as additional visual context. Though effective, this store-and-retrieve paradigm keeps historical evidence as external visual context, preventing it from being internalized into a compact, evolving latent memory that can continuously guide streaming reasoning. To bridge this gap, we introduce LatentStream, a progressive latent working memory framework that shifts streaming memory from store-and-retrieve to retrieve-and-internalize. Specifically, LatentStream comprises three coordinated components. First, Query-agnostic Hierarchical Streaming Memory organizes visual history into short-, mid-, and long-term levels under a fixed memory budget through Jenks-guided adaptive consolidation. Once a query arrives, Hierarchical Latent Memory Evolution equips groups of latent memory tokens with progressively expanding memory receptive fields, enabling them to iteratively retrieve historical evidence from their corresponding scopes and internalize it into a compact, fixed-length latent memory. Finally, Progressive Confidence-guided Latent Memory Optimization constructs a hierarchical progression reward from group-wise predictive entropy and jointly refines the latent memory tokens and retrieved evidence, encouraging increasingly confident streaming reasoning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LatentStream achieves new state-of-the-art results on existing online and offline video benchmarks.

</details>


### [94] [Persistent Identity Preservation in Generative Image Models: A Benchmark and Evaluation System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04151)
*Mengwei Ren,Xuaner Zhang,Zhihao Xia*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks three paradigms for identity preservation in generative image models (input context, LoRA, and persistent identity layer) across generation, editing, restoration, and multi-subject tasks. The study finds that identity preservation remains a fundamental limitation, with persistent identity layers significantly reducing degradation while maintaining image quality and instruction adherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative image models struggle to preserve subject identity when parameters like pose, expression, appearance, or viewpoint change. Existing methods use fundamentally different approaches to represent identity, but there lacks a systematic comparison of these paradigms. The authors aim to benchmark these approaches to understand their strengths and limitations in identity preservation.

Method: The authors systematically benchmark three identity representation paradigms: (1) input context-based (GPT-Image-2, NB2), (2) trainable subject-specific parameters (LoRA), and (3) persistent identity layers (PHOTA IDENTITY). They evaluate across four task categories: subject-driven generation, editing, restoration, and multi-subject composition, with tasks designed to increasingly stress identity preservation.

Result: Identity preservation remains a distinct limitation of current generative models. Strong image quality and instruction following do not guarantee identity fidelity. Identity degradation worsens under iterative edits, small subject scales, severe image degradation, and multi-subject composition. Persistent identity layers substantially reduce degradation across all tasks while maintaining comparable instruction adherence and perceptual quality.

Conclusion: Identity preservation does not automatically emerge from increasingly capable generative models. Instead, identity should be represented as persistent subject knowledge that can be composed independently with the underlying generative model. This approach offers a promising direction for improving identity fidelity in subject-driven image generation.

Abstract: Generative image models can now produce high-quality images, follow complex instructions, and support precise edits, but they still struggle to preserve who or what is being depicted. When generating or editing images of a specific subject, identity may drift as the pose, expression, appearance, viewpoint, or surrounding scene changes. Existing subject-driven methods make fundamentally different choices about where identity is represented: through the input context (GPT-Image-2, NB2), as trainable subject-specific model parameters (LoRA), or as a persistent identity layer (PHOTA IDENTITY) reusable across generations and edits. We systematically benchmark these paradigms across subject-driven generation, editing, restoration, and multi-subject settings, with tasks designed to increasingly stress identity preservation. Our results show that identity preservation remains a distinct limitation of current generative foundation models: strong image quality and instruction following do not necessarily imply strong identity fidelity, and identity degradation becomes more pronounced under iterative edits, small subject scales, severe image degradation, and multi-subject composition. Persistent identity substantially reduces this degradation across generation, editing, and restoration, consistently improving identity preservation when applied to different foundation models while maintaining comparable instruction adherence and perceptual image quality. These results suggest that identity does not simply emerge from increasingly capable generative models, but can instead be represented as persistent subject knowledge that is composed independently with the underlying generative model.

</details>


### [95] [Zero-Shot Novel Depth Synthesis Using 3D Foundation Models Scene Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04174)
*Denis M. Akola,David F. Fouhey*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Z3D is a novel method that leverages internal representations of 3D Foundation Models (3DFMs) like VGGT to perform novel view synthesis by doing latent diffusion on 3DFM representations, enabling prediction of realistic depth maps for unseen views across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The hypothesis is that 3DFMs learn rich internal representations containing general knowledge about 3D scenes to solve reconstruction tasks. This work investigates whether these internal representations can be decoded to infer 3D structure from new viewpoints, specifically by extracting hidden surface information.

Method: 1) Demonstrate that hidden surfaces can be decoded from internal 3DFM representations. 2) Propose Z3D method that estimates pointmaps in unseen views by performing latent diffusion on 3DFM representations.

Result: Z3D can predict realistic depth maps for new views across multiple datasets, showing strong performance on novel view synthesis task.

Conclusion: Internal representations of 3D Foundation Models contain sufficient 3D scene knowledge to enable novel view synthesis through latent diffusion, validating the hypothesis that these models learn general 3D scene knowledge for reconstruction tasks.

Abstract: 3D Foundation Models (3DFMs) such as VGGT have recently pushed the boundaries of 3D vision by predicting rich unified representations with feed-foward transformers. The scene representations learned by these models enable strong performance on multiple 3D vision tasks. In this paper, we investigate using their internal representations to infer 3D in the scene from new views. Our hypothesis is that in order to solve the task of 3D reconstruction, these models need to learn a representation that includes a large amount of general knowledge about 3D scenes. After showing that it is possible to decode hidden surfaces from internal 3DFM representations, we propose a method, Z3D, that estimates pointmaps in unseen views by doing latent diffusion on 3DFM representation. We show that Z3D can predict realistic depth maps for new views across multiple datasets.

</details>


### [96] [Seeing Before Synthesizing: VLM-Guided Transition Event Discovery for Weakly-Supervised Dense Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04183)
*Ye-Chan Kim,Seunghee Choi,SeungJu Cha,Si-Woo Kim,Hwiseon Kim,Hyungee Kim,Dong-Jin Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SBS is a weakly-supervised dense video captioning framework that uses a VLM to generate frame-level narratives for inter-event gaps and adaptively refines temporal masks by detecting semantic transitions and maximizing vision-language alignment, achieving state-of-the-art results on ActivityNet Captions and YouCook2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous methods synthesize transition captions via LLM which lack visual grounding and are rigidly assigned to every inter-event gap at fixed locations/durations, limiting effectiveness.

Method: 1) Use VLM to generate frame-level narratives for inter-event gaps; 2) Detect transitions from semantic variation across gaps; 3) Refine temporal masks by blending temporal midpoint with semantic change point; 4) Select width that maximizes vision-language alignment.

Result: State-of-the-art performance in both captioning and localization on ActivityNet Captions and YouCook2 datasets.

Conclusion: The adaptive, visually grounded approach with semantic change detection significantly improves weakly-supervised dense video captioning by providing linguistic guidance only where warranted.

Abstract: Weakly-Supervised Dense Video Captioning aims to localize and describe multiple events in untrimmed videos given only an ordered set of event-level captions per video. Recent work synthesizes auxiliary transition captions via LLM to provide additional vision-language alignment, but these captions lack visual grounding and are rigidly assigned to every inter-event gap at a fixed location and duration. To address these, we propose Seeing Before Synthesizing (SBS), a framework that adaptively provides visually grounded linguistic guidance only where warranted. Leveraging a VLM, we generate frame-level narratives for the inter-event gaps and detect transitions from the semantic variation across them. For identified transitions, we then refine inter-event temporal masks by blending the temporal midpoint with the semantic change point and selecting the width that maximizes vision-language alignment. Experiments on ActivityNet Captions and YouCook2 demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in both captioning and localization.

</details>


### [97] [One Editor, Many Edits: A Unified Training-Free Framework for Diverse Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04190)
*Adheesh Sunil Juvekar,Onkar Kishor Susladkar,Kiet A. Nguyen,Muntasir Wahed,Nabeel Bashir,Xiaona Zhou,Tianjiao Yu,Vedant Shah,Ismini Lourentzou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EditVid is a training-free video editing framework that unifies instruction-guided and reference-guided editing paradigms, using sparse causal memory, correspondence-based token injection, and soft latent blending to achieve high-quality video edits while preserving subject identity and local coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video editing spans diverse paradigms, but existing methods struggle to achieve high-quality instruction-guided and subject-guided editing within a single unified framework. The authors address this limitation by proposing a training-free approach that can handle multiple editing tasks without model retraining.

Method: The framework combines three key techniques: (1) Sparse causal memory for maintaining local coherence across frames, (2) Correspondence-based post-attention token injection for long-range identity preservation, and (3) Soft latent blending for edit locality control. This training-free design allows the same framework to support both instruction-guided and reference-guided edits.

Result: EditVid achieves 78.16 FiVE-Acc on the FiVE benchmark, significantly outperforming the strongest training-free baseline (58.95). It also obtains competitive results on IVEBench. In user studies, 51.8% of participants preferred EditVid over 7 competing methods.

Conclusion: EditVid demonstrates that a training-free framework can effectively unify multiple video editing paradigms, achieving state-of-the-art performance on FiVE and strong preference ratings in user studies, while supporting diverse tasks including style transfer, attribute modification, object insertion, part-level editing, and subject replacement.

Abstract: Video editing spans diverse editing paradigms, yet achieving high-quality instruction-guided and subject-guided editing within a single unified framework remains challenging. We introduce EditVid, a training-free framework combining sparse causal memory for local coherence, correspondence-based post-attention token injection for long-range identity preservation, and soft latent blending for edit locality. The same framework supports instruction-guided and reference-guided edits, including style transfer, attribute modification, object insertion, part-level editing, and subject replacement. On FiVE, EditVid achieves 78.16 FiVE-Acc, compared with 58.95 for the strongest evaluated training-free baseline, while obtaining competitive results on IVEBench. A user study further shows a 51.8\% overall preference for EditVid over 7 competing methods.

</details>


### [98] [Puffin-World: Scaling a Unified Multimodal Model with Native 3D World States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04196)
*Kang Liao,Yihang Luo,Xiao-Ming Wu,Linyi Jin,Size Wu,Chunyu Lin,Yao Zhao,Fei Wang,Wei Li,Chen Change Loy*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Puffin-World is a unified multimodal architecture that integrates physical understanding, spatial simulation, and 3D world generation/reconstruction without external offline modules. It jointly models physics, geometry, and appearance states with an Omni-Camera representation, enabling tasks like mimic and self-calibrated world exploration. Trained on Puffin-16M dataset (15M vision-language-camera triplets, 1M trajectories).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reliably construct and interact with 3D worlds, enabling physically consistent and visually stable world generation through unified modeling of physical dynamics, geometry, and appearance within a single generative process.

Method: Jointly models three native world states (physics: gravity field and latitude; geometry: depth; appearance: image) with a unified Omni-Camera representation. Introduces physical dynamics propagation across future frames and couples appearance and geometry within a single generative process to jointly synthesize future views and reconstruct underlying geometry.

Result: Enables interleaved closed-loop applications requiring synergy across multiple tasks, including mimic and self-calibrated world exploration. Achieves physically consistent and visually stable world generation by grounding absolute camera properties in the real world.

Conclusion: Puffin-World presents a unified paradigm for integrated physical understanding, spatial simulation, and 3D world generation. The released code, models, and dataset (Puffin-16M) aim to foster further research in this area.

Abstract: We propose Puffin-World, a unified multimodal architecture that integrates physical understanding, spatial simulation, and 3D world generation and reconstruction without relying on external offline modules. To reliably construct and interact with 3D worlds, our framework jointly models three native world states: physics (gravity field and latitude), geometry (depth), and appearance (image), together with a unified Omni-Camera representation that supports diverse tasks and flexible motions. Beyond modeling these states, we introduce a strategy for propagating physical dynamics across future frames. By grounding absolute camera properties in the real world, Puffin-World enables physically consistent and visually stable world generation. We further couple appearance and geometry within a single generative process, jointly synthesizing each future view and reconstructing its underlying geometry. This unified paradigm enables interleaved closed-loop applications requiring synergy across multiple tasks, including mimic and self-calibrated world exploration. To scale Puffin-World to complex scenarios, we construct Puffin-16M, comprising 15 million vision-language-camera triplets and 1 million trajectories featuring various and challenging motions. To foster further research in this area, we released the code, models, and datasets.

</details>


### [99] [Principia: Relational Physics Tests for Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04200)
*Varun Varma Thozhiyoor,Shivam Tripathi,Venkatesh Babu Radhakrishnan,Anand Bhattad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Principia, a benchmark for evaluating physical reasoning in video models through relational consistency between paired objects, independent of camera calibration. The benchmark covers 8 physical phenomena across different dynamics types. Results show that state-of-the-art video generators fail at physical consistency (max 0.42 score) despite performing well on VBench (~0.8), and vision-language models struggle to detect physics violations (best 67% accuracy).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that evaluating physical reasoning in video models is difficult because absolute motion measurements depend on frame rate, object scale, and camera calibration, which are often ambiguous or unavailable in generated video. Existing benchmarks don't address this calibration dependency issue.

Method: The authors propose a calibration-independent approach: when two objects in the same scene obey the same physical law, their motions must satisfy predictable relationships that hold independent of calibration. They introduce Principia benchmark spanning 8 phenomena (gravity, restitution, friction, rotational inertia, projectile motion, momentum, pendulum, mass-spring oscillation) across translational, rotational, collisional, and oscillatory dynamics. They also propose a calibration-independent consistency score that quantifies physical violation directly in image space.

Result: Across thousands of generations from six state-of-the-art video generators, no model exceeds 0.42 on Principia despite all scoring around 0.8 on VBench. Vision-language models were evaluated on detecting relational physics violations, with the best model achieving only 67% accuracy and most performing near chance level.

Conclusion: Current video generation models and vision-language models fail at physical reasoning tasks. The gap between VBench scores (~0.8) and Principia scores (max 0.42) reveals that VBench does not capture physical validity. The benchmark successfully provides a calibration-independent way to evaluate physical reasoning in video models.

Abstract: Evaluating physical reasoning in video models is difficult because absolute motion measurements depend on frame rate, object scale, and camera calibration, all of which are often ambiguous or unavailable in generated video. We propose a different approach. When two objects in the same scene obey the same physical law, their motions must satisfy predictable relationships, and these relationships hold independent of calibration. We introduce Principia, a benchmark that evaluates Newtonian physics through relational consistency between paired objects. Principia spans eight phenomena - gravity, restitution, friction, rotational inertia, projectile motion, momentum, pendulum, and mass-spring oscillation - across translational, rotational, collisional, and oscillatory dynamics, using real-world scenes recorded under controlled protocols. We also introduce a calibration-independent consistency score that quantifies physical violation directly in image space. Across thousands of generations from six state-of-the-art video generators, no model exceeds 0.42 on Principia despite all scoring around 0.8 on VBench. Vision-language models are evaluated on their ability to detect relational physics violations, with the best model achieving only 67% accuracy and most performing near chance level.

</details>


### [100] [Scal3R: Learning Efficient Multi-Relative Pose Query for Scalable Online 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04201)
*Chin-Yang Lin,Yang-Che Sun,Cheng Sun,Fu-En Yang,Min-Hung Chen,Yen-Yu Lin,Wei-Chen Chiu,Yu-Lun Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Scal3R addresses the problem of online 3D reconstruction failing on long videos by reformulating it as multi-reference relative pose querying instead of regressing poses relative to a fixed first-frame anchor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online 3D reconstruction models perform poorly on long videos because regressing poses relative to a fixed first-frame anchor forces extrapolation beyond training distribution, causing small drifts to accumulate and amplify into geometric collapse. However, per-frame depth remains stable, indicating the backbone's local geometry is intact while only the global pose head breaks down.

Method: Scal3R uses lightweight learnable tokens (~1% of parameters) injected into a completely frozen backbone via asymmetric attention to query poses relative to multiple past keyframes. An online pose-graph optimization system with loop closure suppresses long-range drift.

Result: Scal3R converges in 8 hours on a single GPU, reduces average ATE by over 60% on KITTI compared to the online baseline, and achieves state-of-the-art performance across Virtual KITTI, Sintel, TUM-Dynamic, ScanNet, and 7-Scenes.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates that decoupling local geometry from global pose estimation and using multi-reference relative pose querying can effectively address long-range drift in online 3D reconstruction, achieving significant improvements in accuracy while remaining computationally efficient.

Abstract: Online 3D reconstruction models perform poorly on long videos. This happens because regressing poses relative to a fixed first-frame anchor forces extrapolation far beyond the training distribution. Small drifts accumulate and amplify into significant geometric collapse. However, we observe that per-frame depth remains stable throughout this failure. The backbone's local geometry remains intact; only the global pose head breaks down. Motivated by this decoupling, we introduce Scal3R. This approach reformulates online reconstruction as multi-reference relative pose querying. We use lightweight learnable tokens, which make up about ~1% of the parameters, and inject them into a completely frozen backbone via asymmetric attention. This setup queries poses relative to multiple past keyframes. An online pose-graph optimization system with loop closure suppresses long-range drift. Scal3R reaches convergence in 8 hours on a single GPU. It reduces the average ATE by over 60% on KITTI compared to the online baseline. It also achieves state-of-the-art performance across Virtual KITTI, Sintel, TUM-Dynamic, ScanNet, and 7-Scenes. Project page: https://linjohnss.github.io/scal3r/

</details>


### [101] [TokenMatch: 3D Mesh Correspondence Transformer with Curvature-Guided Tokenisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04202)
*Adeela Islam,Zorah Lähner,Vittorio Murino,Vladislav Golyanik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TokenMatch is a transformer-based model for 3D shape correspondence estimation that handles partial observations and non-isometric deformations. It uses curvature-guided tokenization and self-/cross-attention to learn patch-level and point-level relations, achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks with sub-second inference speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D shape correspondence methods struggle with partial observations and strong non-isometric deformations. Learning-based approaches rely on hand-crafted descriptors or template-based representations, while generative models over functional maps suffer from high inference cost, limited interpretability, and poor generalization to partial shapes.

Method: TokenMatch uses a transformer architecture with self- and cross-attention mechanisms. It adaptively tokenizes meshes into patches using shape curvature guidance, enabling learning of shape-specific geometric descriptors. The model is trained exclusively on BeCoS (a challenging non-isometric partial-to-partial shape-matching dataset) and can generalize to full shapes without retraining or fine-tuning.

Result: TokenMatch achieves consistently high performance on standard benchmarks (CP2P, PSMAL, BeCoS, FAUST, SCAPE, SHREC'19) for both partial and full shape matching, outperforming existing methods in mean geodesic error and intersection-over-union metrics while running at sub-second inference speeds.

Conclusion: TokenMatch successfully addresses the challenges of partial shape matching and non-isometric deformations through a novel transformer-based approach with curvature-guided tokenization, demonstrating strong generalization capabilities and superior performance-speed tradeoffs compared to existing methods.

Abstract: While data-driven 3D shape correspondence estimation has recently seen substantial progress, robust matching under partial observations and strong non-isometric deformations remains challenging. Existing learning-based approaches often rely on hand-crafted descriptors or template-based representations, whereas recent generative models over functional maps suffer from high inference cost, limited interpretability, and poor generalisation to partial shapes. In response to these limitations, this paper introduces TokenMatch, a new transformer-based unified model for estimating 3D shape correspondences. Our feed-forward approach trained exclusively on BeCoS, a challenging non-isometric partial-to-partial shape-matching dataset, can generalise to matching full shapes without retraining or fine-tuning. TokenMatch uses self- and cross-attention mechanisms to efficiently learn patch-level and point-level relations as well as dense correspondences between shape pairs. Our core insight is that meshes can be adaptively tokenised into patches using shape curvature guidance, enabling effective learning of shape-specific geometric descriptors for correspondence estimation. We evaluate TokenMatch on standard benchmarks for partial and full shape matching, including CP2P, PSMAL, BeCoS, FAUST, SCAPE, and SHREC'19. Our method achieves consistently high performance, in most cases outperforming existing methods for partial and full shape matching in the mean geodesic error and intersection-over-union metrics, while also running faster at sub-second inference speeds.

</details>


### [102] [Temporal Self-Distillation: Learning Visual State Tracking in Videos Without Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04203)
*Shravan Venkatraman,Wenshuai Zhao,Mohammad Hassan Vali,Arno Solin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: S³T is the first fully self-supervised framework for continuous video state tracking, using temporal sampling density as privileged information for self-distillation. It achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple benchmarks without labels or additional inference cost, with improvements up to +2.70 on VSTAT and successful transfer from synthetic to real videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core motivation is to develop a fully self-contained framework for continuous video state tracking that requires no external supervision. The hypothesis is that denser temporal sampling provides more accurate running state information, which can be leveraged as privileged information to train a sparse-view student without manual annotations.

Method: S³T employs a self-distillation approach where temporal sampling density serves as privileged information. A denser view of a video clip acts as the teacher, while a sparse-view student with identical weights learns to match the teacher's next-token distribution. The model generates its own training targets, eliminating the need for labels, separate teacher networks, or reward signals.

Result: S³T achieves substantial improvements on LLaVA-OneVision-2-8B: +1.74 VSTAT accuracy as a single model, +2.38 with souping, and +2.70 with additional vision-encoder adaptation. The method shows strong transfer capabilities, improving performance by +7.95 on VSTAT-YouTube state-tracking questions and +4.50 on MVBench Action Count. Notably, prior self-evolving methods showed minimal impact on state tracking.

Conclusion: S³T represents a breakthrough in self-supervised video state tracking, achieving significant improvements (+1.74 to +2.70 on VSTAT) without requiring labels or additional inference cost. The method successfully transfers learned capabilities from synthetic to real videos, demonstrating practical value for real-world applications.

Abstract: We introduce S$^3$T (Self-Supervised Self-Distillation over Time), which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first fully self-contained framework for continuous video state tracking. Our method treats temporal sampling density as privileged information, based on the hypothesis that a denser view of the same clip recovers the running state more accurately. This view serves as the teacher, while a sparse-view student with the same weights learns to match its next-token distribution. The model generates its own target, so training requires no labels, separate teacher, or reward signal, and adds no inference cost. On LLaVA-OneVision-2-8B, S$^3$T improves VSTAT accuracy by $+1.74$ as a single model, $+2.38$ with souping, and $+2.70$ with additional vision-encoder adaptation, while prior self-evolving methods leave state tracking largely unchanged. The capability learned from unlabeled synthetic clips transfers to real videos, improving performance by $+7.95$ on VSTAT-YouTube state-tracking questions and $+4.50$ on MVBench Action Count.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [103] [SISER: Speaker-Invariant Speech Emotion Recognition with Entropy-Based Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02941)
*Eunseo Choi,Hyunku Kang,Chanwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SISER proposes a speaker-invariant speech emotion recognition method combining wav2vec 2.0 and ECAPA-TDNN with adversarial training, achieving 60.63% UA on IEMOCAP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech emotion recognition suffers from two fundamental challenges: (1) scarcity of labeled data, and (2) inter-speaker variability, both limiting generalization ability. While prior adversarial methods address speaker variability, they fail to leverage powerful pre-trained representations.

Method: SISER integrates wav2vec 2.0 as a feature encoder (providing rich self-supervised representations to address data scarcity) and ECAPA-TDNN as a speaker discriminator within an entropy-based adversarial training scheme. ECAPA-TDNN enables stronger adversarial signal for suppressing speaker identity compared to shallow classifiers.

Result: On IEMOCAP, SISER achieves 60.63% UA (unweighted accuracy), outperforming the baseline (51.15%) and wav2vec 2.0 without speaker suppression (56.46%). Ablation studies confirm that the choice of speaker classifier architecture is a key performance factor.

Conclusion: The combination of self-supervised pre-trained representations (wav2vec 2.0) with a stronger speaker discriminator (ECAPA-TDNN) in an adversarial training framework effectively addresses both data scarcity and inter-speaker variability, significantly improving emotion recognition performance.

Abstract: Speech emotion recognition (SER) faces two fundamental challenges: scarcity of labeled data and inter-speaker variability, both of which hinder generalization of emotion recognition systems. While prior adversarial approaches address speaker variability, they fall short in leveraging powerful pre-trained representations. We propose SISER (Speaker-Invariant Speech Emotion Recognition), integrating wav2vec 2.0 as a feature encoder and ECAPA-TDNN as a speaker discriminator within an entropy-based adversarial training scheme. wav2vec 2.0 provides rich self-supervised representations that alleviate dependency on large labeled datasets, while ECAPA-TDNN enables suppression of speaker identity via a stronger adversarial signal than shallow classifiers. Evaluated on IEMOCAP, SISER achieves a UA of 60.63%, outperforming the baseline (51.15%) and wav2vec 2.0 without speaker suppression (56.46%), with ablation emphasizing that the choice of speaker classifier architecture is a key factor.

</details>


### [104] [CRAW: Codec Robust Audio Watermarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03107)
*David Chernin,Ethan Fetaya*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: CRAW is a codec-robust audio watermarking framework that addresses the vulnerability of existing post-hoc watermarking methods to neural codecs and denoisers. It combines distortion-aware training, attention-based pooling, inference-time perceptual masking, and error-correcting codes to achieve robustness while maintaining perceptual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent advances in generative speech models have made it easy to create synthetic audio, enabling fraud and misinformation. While audio watermarking offers a defense by embedding imperceptible signals to verify provenance, existing post-hoc watermarking methods fail under neural codecs and denoisers—transformations commonly used in real-world storage, transmission, and processing—severely limiting practical utility.

Method: CRAW combines four key techniques: (1) distortion-aware training to improve robustness against neural re-synthesis, (2) an attention-based pooling mechanism for better feature aggregation, (3) inference-time perceptual masking to preserve quality, and (4) an error-correcting code to recover fidelity lost during robust training.

Result: CRAW achieves state-of-the-art robustness against neural codecs, denoisers, and vocoders while maintaining perceptual quality comparable to existing post-hoc watermarking methods.

Conclusion: The proposed CRAW framework successfully bridges the gap between robustness and perceptual quality in audio watermarking, making it practical for real-world deployment to combat audio deepfakes and misinformation.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative speech models have made it increasingly difficult to distinguish authentic from synthetic audio, enabling new forms of fraud and misinformation. Audio watermarking offers a promising defense by embedding an imperceptible signal into generated speech that can later be detected to verify its provenance. However, recent studies have shown that existing post-hoc watermarking methods fail under neural codecs and denoisers, transformations routinely applied during real-world storage, transmission, and processing, severely limiting their practical utility. Here we introduce CRAW, a codec-robust audio watermarking framework that jointly improves robustness against neural re-synthesis while maintaining high perceptual quality. CRAW combines distortion-aware training with an attention-based pooling mechanism, inference-time perceptual mask- ing, and an error-correcting code to recover the fidelity lost during robust training. Experiments demonstrate that CRAW achieves state-of-the-art robustness against neural codecs, denoisers, and vocoders while maintaining perceptual quality comparable to existing post-hoc watermarking methods. The code is available at https://github.com/DavidC1212/craw.

</details>


### [105] [VoxReason: Listener-Free Evaluation of Source-Grounded Speech Planning Before Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03203)
*Mengzhe Geng*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: VoxReason introduces a listener-free evaluation task for measuring whether speech synthesis planning decisions (affect, pitch, energy, rate, pause, emphasis, stance) are grounded in cited source records before waveform rendering. The system outputs source-cited speaking-plans with evidence citations, verified for legality, slot agreement, schema validity, and counterfactual locality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expressive speech systems make delivery decisions before any waveform is rendered, yet downstream evaluation cannot determine whether these choices were licensed by the source record. This source-use failure occurs pre-synthesis and remains unmeasured. The authors seek to make this pre-synthesis decision measurable through source-grounded evaluation.

Method: VoxReason uses a deterministic verifier to check citation legality, slot agreement, unsupported state, schema validity, and one-cue counterfactual locality. Experiments include: (1) 1,440 checked source-label cases with key-lookup oracle and emotion prior controls; (2) 100-case learned source-key-disjoint comparison using 7B locality SFT+CF repair. Evaluations measure plan-slot accuracy and locality metrics.

Result: Key-lookup oracle achieves 1.000 plan-slot accuracy on seen keys but fails on source-key-disjoint cases. Emotion prior reaches 0.958 slot accuracy without citing intensity or identity. The 7B SFT+CF repair improves plan-slot accuracy/locality from 0.684/0.141 to 0.919/1.000. Removing source records reduces citation-required grounded score by 0.488. Waveform quality is not evaluated.

Conclusion: Slot accuracy alone is insufficient for evaluating source-grounded speech planning—systems can achieve high accuracy without proper source grounding. The 7B locality repair demonstrates that counterfactual-based training can improve both accuracy and locality. Rendered waveform quality remains outside the scope of current evaluation.

Abstract: Expressive speech systems make a decision before any waveform is rendered: how an utterance is delivered. In dialogue agents, narration, and role-conditioned TTS, that hidden planning step sets affect, pitch, energy, rate, pause, emphasis, and stance, yet downstream audio scores rarely reveal whether those choices were licensed by the source record, a source-use failure that occurs before any waveform exists. VoxReason makes that pre-synthesis decision measurable as a listener-free task for source-grounded speech planning. Before synthesis, VoxReason measures whether delivery choices are grounded in cited source records. Systems output a source-cited speaking-plan with evidence citations, and a deterministic verifier checks citation legality, slot agreement, unsupported state, schema validity, and one-cue counterfactual locality. On 1,440 checked source-label cases, shortcut controls show why slot accuracy alone is unsafe: a key-lookup oracle reaches 1.000 plan-slot accuracy on seen keys, while an emotion prior still reaches 0.958 slot accuracy on source-key-disjoint cases without citing intensity or identity. In a separate 100-case learned source-key-disjoint comparison, a 7B locality SFT+CF repair improves plan-slot accuracy/locality from 0.684/0.141 to 0.919/1.000, and removing source records lowers citation-required grounded score by 0.488. Rendered waveform quality remains outside the present evaluation.

</details>


### [106] [Is Semantics Enough for Speech Mean Opinion Score Prediction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03283)
*Tianyu Lan,Yufei Shi,Yang Ai,Honghao Sun,Huipeng Du,Zhenhua Ling*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates MOS (Mean Opinion Score) prediction for synthesized speech by comparing three representation paradigms: self-supervised learning (SSL) models, acoustic-only neural audio codecs (NACs), and unified NACs that integrate semantics. The key finding is that features combining semantic understanding with fine-grained acoustic modeling outperform SSL-only approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automatic MOS predictors rely heavily on SSL models that prioritize high-level semantics but potentially compromise on capturing critical acoustic details essential for speech quality assessment.

Method: Systematic benchmarking of three representation paradigms on the standard BVCC dataset and multiple out-of-domain (OOD) datasets, comparing SSL features, acoustic-only NAC features, and unified NAC features that integrate semantic understanding with acoustic reconstruction.

Result: Features that synergize semantic understanding with fine-grained acoustic modeling achieve a higher performance upper bound in speech quality assessment compared to semantics-only approaches.

Conclusion: Semantics alone are insufficient for robust MOS prediction; a dual focus on both semantic content and acoustic fidelity is essential for building effective speech quality assessment models.

Abstract: Mean Opinion Score (MOS) is the gold standard for evaluating synthesized speech naturalness. However, current automatic MOS predictors are dominated by self-supervised learning (SSL) models that prioritize high-level semantics, potentially compromising their ability to capture critical acoustic details. In this paper, we systematically investigate representations from three paradigms: SSLs, acoustic-only neural audio codecs (NACs), and unified NACs that integrate semantics into reconstruction-based architectures. Extensive benchmarking on the standard BVCC and multiple out-of-domain (OOD) datasets demonstrates that features synergizing semantic understanding with fine-grained acoustic modeling achieve a higher performance upper bound in speech quality assessment. Ultimately, our findings highlight that semantics alone are not enough; a dual focus on semantic content and acoustic fidelity is essential for robust MOS prediction.

</details>


### [107] [Beyond .WAV: Design and Software Verification of VocalCap, a Traceable Browser-Based Audio Capture System for Vocal Biomarker Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03320)
*Augusto Camargo*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: VocalCap is a browser-based system for self-guided voice capture in remote studies, providing comprehensive provenance evidence for each recording through multiple artifact retention and verification steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote voice studies often produce final audio files with limited evidence about how they were captured, transferred, processed, and accepted, compromising data integrity and reproducibility.

Method: VocalCap uses a versioned protocol workflow with browser-native objects, client-lossless Float32 WAV, and server-canonical mono PCM16 WAV. IndexedDB preserves artifacts until server confirmation. Topology-aware active-channel selection handles channel topology issues. System was tested with malformed objects, interruptions, and channel variants.

Result: Post hoc audit of 39 pilot recordings: 25 sample-identical stereo files and 14 with left-channel-only signal. Topology-aware selection limited RMS level difference to < 0.001 dB (vs. 6.02 dB attenuation from stereo averaging). End-to-end verification yielded 10 accepted recordings and 30 artifacts passing integrity checks in Chromium and WebKit.

Conclusion: VocalCap successfully provides comprehensive provenance evidence for voice recordings in browser-based remote studies. Device-level acoustic agreement, target-population usability, clinical validity, and biomarker performance require separate studies.

Abstract: Remote voice studies often retain a final audio file with limited evidence about how it was captured, transferred, processed, and accepted. This paper presents VocalCap, an institution-controlled, browser-based system for self-guided capture of voice and related acoustic signals by participants without technical training. A versioned protocol drives the workflow. Each accepted recording retains a browser-native object, a client-lossless Float32 WAV derived from the same MediaStream, and a server-canonical mono PCM16 WAV, linked to evidence of capture execution, technical quality, byte-level integrity, recovery, and transformation provenance. IndexedDB preserves accepted browser artifacts until server confirmation, while session completion requires successful verification of every task and artifact. Software tests challenged the acquisition contracts with malformed or altered objects, exact-zero interruptions, channel-topology variants, and interrupted or repeated operations. A post hoc technical audit of 39 consented pilot recordings found 25 sample-identical stereo files and 14 files with signal confined to the left channel. Topology-aware active-channel selection limited the canonical root-mean-square level difference to less than 0.001 dB in all 14 affected files; equal-weight stereo averaging would have introduced approximately 6.02 dB of attenuation. Production end-to-end verification completed two five-task profiles in Chromium and WebKit, yielding 10 accepted recordings and 30 retained artifacts that passed server-side integrity and format checks. The results verify VocalCap's software behavior under the tested browser-engine conditions. Device-level acoustic agreement, target-population usability, clinical validity, and biomarker performance remain subjects for separate studies.

</details>


### [108] [Neural Music Enhancement with Dual Time-Frequency Spectral Representations for Prediction and Discrimination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03357)
*Fei Liu,Yang Ai,Zhen-Hua Ling*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: DSME is a music enhancement model using dual time-frequency spectral representations (STFT and CQT) within a generative adversarial framework. It addresses background noise and reverberation in non-professional music recordings by leveraging STFT for waveform reconstruction and CQT's log-frequency structure for discriminator design, along with a chroma-spectrum loss for pitch consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-professional music recordings shared online often suffer from background noise and reverberation, which degrades perceived quality and limits reuse. Existing enhancement methods may not adequately address both issues while preserving musical characteristics like pitch and harmonics.

Method: DSME uses a generative adversarial framework with dual spectral representations: (1) STFT spectra for the generator - leveraging its fixed window, invertibility, and predictability to estimate clean amplitude-phase spectra from degraded inputs and reconstruct waveforms via inverse STFT; (2) CQT spectra for the discriminator - exploiting its log-frequency, variable-window structure aligned with musical octaves to design an octave-segmented CQT discriminator. Additionally, a chroma-spectrum loss is introduced to emphasize pitch and harmonic consistency.

Result: Experiments show DSME outperforms baselines in both objective and subjective tests, validating the effectiveness of the dual-spectrum approach.

Conclusion: The dual-spectrum approach combining STFT and CQT within a GAN framework is effective for music enhancement, preserving musical characteristics while removing noise and reverberation.

Abstract: Non-professional music recordings shared online often suffer from background noise and reverberation, degrading perceived quality and limiting reuse. This paper proposes DSME, a music enhancement model based on dual time-frequency spectral representations. Within a generative adversarial framework, DSME uses short-time Fourier transform (STFT) spectra for generation and constant-Q transform (CQT) spectra for discrimination. Leveraging STFT's fixed window, invertibility, and predictability, the generator estimates clean amplitude-phase spectra from degraded inputs and reconstructs waveforms via inverse STFT. Exploiting CQT's log-frequency, variable-window structure aligned with musical octaves, we design an octave-segmented CQT discriminator. We also introduce a chroma-spectrum loss to emphasize pitch and harmonic consistency. Experiments show DSME outperforms baselines in objective and subjective tests, validating the effectiveness of the dual-spectrum approach.

</details>


### [109] [Local Chord Corruption Is Not Recognizer Replay: Chord-Condition Propagation in MIDI-SAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03584)
*Weiwen Huang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper compares two evaluation approaches for singing accompaniment generation (SAG) systems: synthetic chord corruption (controlled stress testing) and complete automatic chord recognition (ACR) replay (measuring deployed system conditions). Using CNN-CRF and DeepChroma+CRF predictors through a fixed MIDI-SAG generator across 30 tracks, the study finds that synthetic corruption produces larger output changes than ACR replay, with tritone substitutions showing 2.88x larger effects than quality flips. Joint matching reduces replay distance, and the matched surrogate approach provides closer approximation to replay.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the difference between synthetic chord corruption (a controlled stress test) and complete ACR replay (the real-world condition delivered to deployed SAG systems). This addresses how we should evaluate chord-conditioned music generation systems - whether controlled laboratory tests or realistic deployment conditions are more appropriate.

Method: The researchers replayed CNN-CRF and DeepChroma+CRF predictions through one fixed MIDI-SAG generator, holding track, seed, context, and scoring window constant. They tested across 30 paired tracks with three seeds each, using STFT, CQT, and CENS representations. They analyzed tritone substitutions, relative-root substitutions vs. same-root quality flips, and used matching replay support and relation composition to reduce mismatch.

Result: Key findings: (1) Central four-second tritone produced larger changed-target and inside-output effects than CNN-CRF replay across STFT, CQT, and CENS; (2) CENS target gap was positive on 29/30 tracks (mean 0.462); (3) Matching replay support and relation composition reduced mismatch; (4) Joint matching gave lowest replay distance in full-30 analysis; (5) Relative-root substitutions produced 2.88-fold larger CENS full-window output change than same-root quality flips at near-equal dose; (6) Matched surrogate was closer to replay for both recognizer paths.

Conclusion: Localized corruption tests mechanisms (how the system works under controlled conditions), whereas complete replay evaluates deployed chord-condition propagation (real-world performance). The two approaches serve different evaluation purposes and provide complementary insights into SAG system robustness and deployment readiness.

Abstract: Synthetic chord corruption provides a controlled stress test for singing accompaniment generation (SAG), whereas complete automatic chord recognition (ACR) replay measures the condition delivered to a deployed system. We compare them by replaying CNN-CRF and DeepChroma+CRF predictions through one fixed MIDI-SAG generator, holding track, seed, context, and scoring window constant. Across 30 paired tracks and three seeds, a central four-second tritone produced larger changed-target and inside-output effects than CNN-CRF replay in STFT, CQT, and CENS; the CENS target gap was positive on 29/30 tracks (mean 0.462). Matching replay support and relation composition reduced this mismatch, with joint matching giving the lowest replay distance in the full-30 analysis. Relative-root substitutions produced 2.88-fold larger CENS full-window output change than same-root quality flips at near-equal dose. The matched surrogate was closer to replay for both recognizer paths. We conclude that localized corruption tests mechanisms, whereas complete replay evaluates deployed chord-condition propagation.

</details>


### [110] [PACodec: A Low-bitrate Neural Speech Codec with Parallel Additive Vector Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03363)
*Fei Liu,Yang Ai,Xiao-Hang Jiang,Zhen-Hua Ling*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: PACodec is a novel low-bitrate neural speech codec using parallel additive vector quantization (PAVQ) with a "global-local-global" (GLG) design, achieving 30% bitrate reduction compared to baselines while maintaining similar quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mainstream neural speech codecs use residual vector quantization (RVQ) with sequentially dependent VQs, which may not be optimal for bitrate efficiency. The paper aims to address this limitation by introducing parallel quantization.

Method: PAVQ uses parallel independent VQs that each focus on local components of global encoded features. The outputs are aggregated through addition to produce the final quantization result. This GLG framework allows each VQ to capture different aspects of speech (content, timbre, acoustic details).

Result: PACodec achieves 30% bitrate reduction compared to baseline codecs at the same decoding quality, with only minor model complexity increase. The method demonstrates disentanglement properties, with different VQs capturing different speech attributes.

Conclusion: PAVQ is an effective alternative to RVQ for neural speech coding, offering both bitrate efficiency and built-in disentanglement that shows promise for downstream tasks like voice conversion.

Abstract: This paper proposes PACodec, a novel low-bitrate neural speech codec based on parallel additive vector quantization (PAVQ). Unlike the mainstream residual vector quantization (RVQ) used in most neural speech codecs, where vector quantizers (VQs) are sequentially dependent, the PAVQ strategy adopted in PACodec aggregates parallel quantization results to optimize bitrate usage. Specifically, the PAVQ adopts a "global-local-global" (GLG) design: the global encoded features are quantized in parallel by multiple independent VQs, each attending to a local component of the representation, and their outputs are aggregated through addition to yield the final global quantization result for decoding. Experimental results show that PACodec, as each VQ focuses only on local information, supports smaller codebooks and reduces bitrate by 30% compared with baselines at the same decoding quality, with only minor model complexity. Further analysis shows that, owing to the GLG framework of PAVQ, the proposed PACodec is disentanglement-friendly, and each independent VQ captures different aspects of speech, e.g., content, timbre, and acoustic details, suggesting potential for application to downstream tasks such as voice conversion.

</details>


### [111] [StrixAE: An Intelligent Agent for Audio Enhancement under Complex Distortion Coupling in Real-World Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03414)
*Chenglin Wu,Junjie Wu,Jinhang Chen,Mingyang Chen,Zixu Lin,Jiabian Chen,Xinghao Ding,Xiaotong Tu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: StrixAE is an MLLM-based agent that coordinates multiple audio enhancement and personalization models for real-world audio restoration. It uses a two-stage training process: CoT supervised fine-tuning on AcoustBench followed by Audio Perception Reinforcement Learning (APRL) that optimizes format validity, structural coherence, and perceptual quality. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on real-world test datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world audio enhancement involves complex distortion couplings and requires personalized enhancement. Existing solutions fail to address both challenges simultaneously, and current methods lack robustness and autonomous operation capabilities in diverse real-world scenarios.

Method: The proposed StrixAE uses an MLLM as a controller to coordinate multiple audio enhancement and personalization models. Training involves two stages: (1) Chain-of-Thought supervised fine-tuning on AcoustBench for basic reasoning and tool invocation; (2) Audio Perception Reinforcement Learning (APRL) with structured rewards that enforce executable pipelines, logical section ordering, and jointly optimize format validity, structural coherence, and perceptual quality.

Result: StrixAE outperforms most existing open-source and proprietary solutions, achieving state-of-the-art performance across multiple perceptual metrics and demonstrating strong generalization robustness on real-world test datasets.

Conclusion: The two-stage training approach with APRL enables the agent to produce reliable, interpretable enhancement plans without hallucinated tools, successfully addressing both complex distortion coupling and personalized enhancement needs in real-world audio scenarios.

Abstract: Audio enhancement in real-world scenarios involves complex distortion couplings and requires personalized enhancement. Existing solutions struggle to address both simultaneously. To improve robustness and enable autonomous operation in such scenarios, we propose StrixAE, an agent based on a multimodal large language model (MLLM). StrixAE leverages the MLLM as a controller to coordinate multiple audio enhancement and personalization models. To further enhance system robustness, reduce artifacts, and improve generalization across diverse real-world scenarios, StrixAE is trained through a two-stage process: first, CoT supervised fine-tuning on AcoustBench to ground basic reasoning and tool invocation; second, Audio Perception Reinforcement Learning (APRL), a reward design specifically tailored for audio restoration pipelines that jointly optimizes format validity, structural coherence, and perceptual quality. Unlike generic RL fine-tuning, APRL introduces structured rewards that enforce executable pipelines and logical section ordering, enabling the agent to produce reliable, interpretable enhancement plans without hallucinated tools. Based on real-world test datasets, our proposed method outperforms most existing open-source and proprietary solutions, achieving state-of-the-art performance across multiple perceptual metrics and demonstrating strong generalization robustness.

</details>


### [112] [Test-time adaptation for speech enhancement with an autoregressive speech prior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03622)
*Sofiene Kammoun,Simon Leglaive,Xavier Alameda-Pineda,Timo Gerkmann*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A test-time adaptation (TTA) method for speech enhancement that uses an autoregressive prior trained on clean speech latent representations from a neural audio codec, adapting models at test time by minimizing KL divergence to improve performance under mismatched acoustic conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech enhancement models often suffer performance degradation when deployed in acoustic conditions that differ from training data. The motivation is to improve model robustness without requiring labeled target data, addressing the challenge of training-testing noise mismatch.

Method: The proposed method uses a single-utterance TTA approach that: 1) Extracts clean speech latent representations using a neural audio codec; 2) Trains an autoregressive prior on these clean speech latents; 3) Adapts a pretrained speech enhancement model at test time by minimizing Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between the enhanced speech distribution and the clean speech prior.

Result: Experiments across multiple noisy speech datasets show consistent improvements in speech quality, particularly under training-testing noise mismatch conditions.

Conclusion: Test-time adaptation with an autoregressive prior offers an effective solution for improving speech enhancement robustness without requiring additional labeled data, demonstrating particular value in mismatched acoustic scenarios.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) offers a promising direction for improving speech enhancement models under mismatched acoustic conditions, without requiring access to labeled target data. In this work, we propose a single-utterance TTA method that regularizes a pretrained speech enhancement model using an autoregressive prior trained on clean speech latent representations extracted from a neural audio codec. Adaptation is performed by minimizing the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the enhanced speech distribution and the clean speech prior. Experiments across multiple noisy speech datasets show consistent improvements in speech quality, particularly under training-testing noise mismatch conditions. Code and audio examples are available online.

</details>


### [113] [Masked Autoregressive Speech Enhancement with Continuous Neural Audio Codec Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03940)
*Yoto Fujita,Simon Leglaive,Laurent Girin*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes MARSE (masked autoregressive speech enhancement), a method that uses continuous latent representations from neural audio codecs (NAC) for speech enhancement through iterative decoding of masked clean speech frames. The study investigates different decoding policies while controlling for the same DNN (Conformer), NAC (DAC codec), and training setup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on speech enhancement using masked generative modeling relied on discrete token representations from neural audio codecs. This study addresses the question of whether continuous latent representations from NACs can provide advantages for SE in terms of speech quality and intelligibility.

Method: The proposed MARSE method uses iterative decoding of masked clean speech frames with continuous NAC representations. The study systematically investigates different decoding policies while keeping all other variables constant: the same DNN (Conformer model), the same NAC (DAC codec), and the same training setup.

Result: MARSE enables a flexible trade-off between speech enhancement performance and computational cost. The results demonstrate that continuous latent representations from NACs can be advantageous for SE compared to discrete token representations.

Conclusion: The proposed MARSE approach successfully leverages continuous NAC representations for speech enhancement, offering a configurable balance between quality and computational efficiency. Audio examples and code are made available online.

Abstract: Most previous work on speech enhancement (SE) based on masked generative modeling relied on discrete token representations of audio signals, obtained using neural audio codecs (NACs). However, a recent study has shown that continuous latent representations of NACs can be advantageous for SE in terms of speech quality and intelligibility. In this work, we propose masked autoregressive SE (MARSE), a method for SE based on iterative decoding of masked clean speech frames using continuous NAC representations of speech. In particular, we investigate a set of different decoding policies, ceteris paribus, that is, using the same DNN (a Conformer model), the same NAC (the DAC codec) and the same training setup. The results show that MARSE enables a flexible trade-off between SE performance and computational cost. Audio examples and code are available online.

</details>


### [114] [Compressing Streaming Neural Audio Encoders via Latent-Space Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04102)
*Prasanth Yadla,Mohammad Samragh,Dongseong Hwang,Mingbin Xu,Yuanyuan Zhang,Chung-Cheng Chiu,Yongqiang Wang,Yuan Liu,Zhen Huang,Xiaodan Zhuang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper presents a method to compress tokenizers for on-device speech recognition on Apple devices using knowledge distillation. Rather than distilling to discrete tokens or output distributions, the method trains a student encoder to regress the teacher's pre-quantizer latent representations - the actual representations consumed by the language model. This approach works for both token interfaces and applies to tokenizers pretrained alone or jointly trained with a language model. The method achieves 2.8x compression while staying within 1.9% relative WER degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On Apple devices, System-wide Dictation runs entirely on-device with a sparsely activated language model. The always-on tokenizer competes for the same DRAM memory as the language model experts. Since the tokenizer's parameter count directly impacts power and latency, there is a need to compress the tokenizer while maintaining transcription accuracy.

Method: The method uses knowledge distillation where the student encoder is trained to regress the teacher's per-frame latent representations (the pre-quantizer latent that the model actually consumes) under a squared-error objective. A single affine layer absorbs the teacher-student width mismatch. Since the target precedes both the quantizer and the language-model bridge, one recipe covers both token interfaces.

Result: At 2.8x compression, the distilled student stays within 1.9% relative WER of its teacher on five of six teacher-student pairs without any fine-tuning. It improves on an independently trained tokenizer of identical capacity by 3.9% relative.

Conclusion: Distillation to pre-quantizer latents is an effective approach for compressing on-device tokenizers, achieving significant compression while maintaining low WER degradation. The method is general enough to apply to both token interfaces and both pretrained and jointly trained tokenizers.

Abstract: System-wide Dictation on Apple devices runs entirely on-device, and the speech it transcribes reaches the foundation model through a tokenizer: an encoder that maps short windows of waveform onto the representation the language model reads. Because that model is sparsely activated under Instruction-Following Pruning, only a small subset of its experts occupies DRAM at any time, so the always-on tokenizer competes for the same memory, and its parameter count bears directly on power and latency. In this work we study how to compress such a tokenizer by distillation, taking as the supervision target neither the discrete token nor the output distribution but the pre-quantizer latent the model actually consumes - the last representation the two token interfaces share. We train only the student encoder to regress the teacher's per-frame latent under a squared-error objective, with a single affine layer absorbing the teacher-student width mismatch. Because the target precedes both the quantizer and the language-model bridge, one recipe covers both token interfaces we support, and applies both to a tokenizer pretrained alone and to one jointly trained with a language model. At 2.8x compression the distilled student stays within 1.9% relative WER of its teacher on five of six teacher-student pairs without any fine-tuning, and improves on an independently trained tokenizer of identical capacity by 3.9% relative.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [115] [CAPQ-FAST: Content-Adaptive Perceived Quality Assessment for Faster Audiovisual Playback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03498)
*Jiarun Song,Yuxin Song,Fuzheng Yang,Weisi Lin*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper proposes CAPQ-FAST, a unified content-adaptive perceived quality assessment model for faster audiovisual playback. The study conducts subjective experiments to analyze how playback speed affects perceived quality across video, audio (speech and music), and audiovisual content, using content-specific features (TI, WPM, BPM) to develop predictive models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Faster playback is common in online audiovisual services, but different content modalities (video, audio, audiovisual) have varying requirements for understandability under faster playback. Users' perceived quality at different playback speeds remains insufficiently investigated, creating a gap in understanding user experience across content types.

Method: The study conducts series of subjective experiments to analyze the relationship between playback speed and perceived quality for video, audio (speech and music), and audiovisual content. Content-specific intrinsic features are extracted: Temporal Information (TI) for video, Words Per Minute (WPM) for speech, and Beats Per Minute (BPM) for music. Predictive models are developed separately for each content type, then integrated into a unified model (CAPQ-FAST).

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed CAPQ-FAST model effectively predicts perceived quality under different playback speeds. The model can help service providers understand users' viewing intentions and perceptual experiences under faster playback.

Conclusion: The CAPQ-FAST model enables more adaptive personalized recommendations and playback control to enhance user experience adaptability. Service providers can use this to better understand user preferences and optimize playback experiences across different content types.

Abstract: Faster playback has become a common feature in modern online audiovisual services, allowing users to consume content in less time while still maintaining a coherent viewing experience. However, different modalities of media content, such as video, audio (including speech and music), and audiovisual, exhibit varying requirements for understandability and information integrity under faster playback. These differences lead to noticeable variations in perceived quality depending on the content type. Nevertheless, users' perceived quality at different playback speeds remains insufficiently investigated. To address this gap, this paper conducts a series of subjective experiments to analyze the relationship between playback speed and perceived quality for video, audio, and audiovisual content. Content-specific intrinsic features are extracted to capture temporal dynamics, including temporal information (TI) for video, words per minute (WPM) for speech, and beats per minute (BPM) for music. Predictive models of perceived quality under faster playback are then developed separately for video, speech, and music. By integrating these models, a unified content-adaptive perceived quality assessment model (CAPQ-FAST) is proposed for faster audiovisual playback. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model effectively predicts perceived quality under different playback speeds. This model can help service providers better understand users' viewing intentions and perceptual experiences under faster playback, thereby enabling more adaptive personalized recommendations and playback control to enhance user experience adaptability.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [116] [Structure and Implementation of New Practical English Textbooks Driven by Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02981)
*Ya Wang,Lei Zhang,Xueguang Yang,Bo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an AI-driven adaptive English textbook system with a five-layer architecture and evaluates its effectiveness through an 8-week experiment with 186 undergraduates, showing significant improvements in learning outcomes and teacher efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Artificial intelligence is transforming applied English materials from static content to adaptive learning systems. The motivation is to address the limitations of traditional fixed paper sequences by creating a system that can diagnose learners, recommend personalized tasks, and provide formative feedback.

Method: The study proposes a five-layer architecture: (1) knowledge mapping, (2) learner profiling, (3) task generation, (4) feedback orchestration, and (5) teacher-side governance. A prototype was tested on 186 non-English-major undergraduates for eight weeks and compared with a static digital textbook.

Result: The AI-driven system increased unit completion accuracy from 72.4% to 84.9%, raised average speaking task scores by 10.8 points, and reduced teacher's correction time by 31.6%.

Conclusion: An AI-driven textbook can maintain curriculum stability while providing personalized learning paths, rich practice materials, and traceable classroom data. The system successfully balances standardization with personalization in English language learning.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is changing the form of applied English materials from fixed paper sequences to adaptive learning systems that can diagnose learners, recommend tasks, and provide formative feedback. This paper studies the structure and application of a new practical English textbook driven by artificial intelligence. A five-layer architecture is proposed: knowledge mapping, learner profiling, task generation, feedback orchestration, and teacher-side governance. A prototype was tested on 186 non-English-major undergraduates for eight weeks of teaching. Compared with a static digital textbook, the proposed system increased the unit completion accuracy from 72.4% to 84.9%, raised the average score for speaking tasks by 10.8 points, and reduced the teacher's correction time by 31.6%. Therefore, an AI-driven textbook can maintain the stability of the curriculum while providing personalised learning paths, rich practice materials and traceable classroom data.

</details>


### [117] [MasterControl Seventeen Every Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03209)
*MasterControl AI Lab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a governed enterprise analytics approach where LLMs interpret user questions but deterministic policies execute pre-approved analytical programs. The study shows this architecture achieves 100% accuracy (110/110) on answer-and-evidence contracts compared to 0% for runtime-planning agents, while maintaining expressiveness through relational operations, aggregation, comparison, windows, ranking, and similarity functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise analytics requires reliable, auditable, and replayable results. The authors investigate whether restricting LLM flexibility through deterministic policy execution can maintain expressiveness while ensuring governance and reproducibility - critical requirements for enterprise applications.

Method: The study compared three 8B parameter models (generating SQL and selecting tools at runtime) against a Qwen3-8B model that only interpreted intent, with policy executing approved programs. Experiments covered 440 runs across multiple test datasets, evaluating the "full answer-and-evidence contract" - meaning both correct results and proper evidence were required.

Result: Runtime-planning agents (330 episodes) failed to match the full answer-and-evidence contract across all test datasets. In contrast, the policy-executed analyzer achieved 110/110 matches (100% success rate). The approach remains expressive within a defined analytical class using relational operations, aggregation, comparison, windows, ranking, and similarity.

Conclusion: The policy-executed approach achieved 100% match rate (110/110) compared to 0% success for runtime-planning agents across 330 episodes. This demonstrates that governed architectures can ensure reliability and replayability in enterprise analytics, though the authors emphasize this is a configuration-specific result and not a general conclusion about runtime agents.

Abstract: We study a governed approach to enterprise analytics: a language model interprets the question, while deterministic policy selects and runs a pre-approved analytical program that returns both results and evidence. We show that this restriction can remain expressive within a defined analytical class, using relational operations plus aggregation, comparison, windows, ranking, and similarity. Fixed meaning, policy, data, and execution rules also make results replayable. Across 440 runs, three 8B models generated SQL and selected tools at runtime, while Qwen3-8B interpreted intent only and policy executed the approved program. None of 330 runtime-planning episodes matched the full answer-and-evidence contract across all test datasets; the policy-executed analyzer matched 110 of 110. This is a configuration-specific result, not evidence that runtime agents cannot succeed under other designs.

</details>


### [118] [Speculative Macro Commit for Faster Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03236)
*Zeyu Liu,Souvik Kundu,Peter A. Beerel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Speculative Macro Commit (SMC) is a runtime mechanism for LLM agents that uses a two-tier system: a large authoritative actor model and a faster speculative drafter model. The drafter predicts and pre-executes future action chains on an isolated environment snapshot.
