When the actor's next action matches the first drafted action, SMC commits the pre-executed steps to the official trajectory, reducing latency while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using LLM agents experience significant delays from serial action-observation turns, where each tool call, environment transition, and observation delays subsequent decisions. Existing speculative action approaches only handle single-step predictions, missing opportunities to reuse multi-step speculative execution.

Method: SMC uses a two-tier agent system: (1) A large authoritative actor model produces the official trajectory; (2) A faster speculative drafter model continuously predicts and executes future action chains on an isolated environment snapshot. SMC mines recurring multi-action skeletons from training traces and stores them in a macro library for matching against action chains predicted by the drafter at runtime. When the actor's next tool call matches the first drafted action, SMC commits the remaining pre-executed draft steps with their observations to the official trajectory.

Result: Using Qwen3.5-27B INT4 as actor and Qwen3.5-4B as drafter: (1) On τ²-Bench Telecom: matches sequential agent's overall accuracy while reducing latency by 10.23% over Speculative Actions (SA) baseline and 18.59% over sequential execution. (2) On AppWorld: reduces wall time by 7.7% over SA baseline and 44.9% over sequential execution, with a small reduction in task completion.

Conclusion: SMC provides a practical way to reuse multi-step speculative execution and reduce agent latency beyond single-step speculative actions, achieving significant latency reductions while maintaining comparable accuracy to sequential execution.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents spend wall-clock time not only on model inference but also in serial action--observation turns, where each tool call, environment transition, and observation can delay subsequent decisions. We introduce \textbf{Speculative Macro Commit} (SMC), a runtime mechanism for a two-tier agent system: a large authoritative actor model produces the official trajectory, while a faster speculative drafter model continuously predicts and executes future action chains on an isolated environment snapshot. SMC mines recurring multi-action skeletons from training traces and stores them in a macro library used to match against action chains predicted by the drafter at runtime. When the actor's next tool call matches the first drafted action, SMC commits the remaining pre-executed draft steps, together with their observations, to the official trajectory. Using Qwen3.5-27B INT4 as the authoritative actor model and Qwen3.5-4B as the speculative drafter model, SMC matches the sequential agent's overall accuracy while reducing latency by 10.23\% over the Speculative Actions (SA) baseline and 18.59\% over sequential execution on the $τ^2$-Bench Telecom subset. On AppWorld, SMC reduces wall time by 7.7\% over SA baseline and 44.9\% over sequential execution, with a small reduction in task completion. Overall, SMC provides a practical way to reuse multi-step speculative execution and reduce agent latency beyond single-step speculative actions. Our code is publicly available \href{https://github.com/zeyuliu1037/speculative-macro-commit}{\textcolor{magenta}{here}}.

</details>


### [119] [Fresh Memory, Stale Plans: Dependency-Scoped Validation for Distributed LLM-Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03340)
*Evan Chen,Shiqiang Wang,Christopher G. Brinton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PlanFence addresses stale-plan execution in distributed LLM-agent teams where agents may act on obsolete plans even when state appears fresh. The protocol validates only the specific public records that could affect pending actions, replanning when validation fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed LLM-agent teams suffer from a fundamental coordination problem: when one agent derives a plan from certain requirements (r3), another agent may commit to different requirements (r4), and an executor may act on a plan derived from r3 while only having visibility of r4. This creates stale-plan execution where state freshness alone doesn't guarantee plan validity.

Method: PlanFence uses a dependency-scoped action-validation protocol. Plans cite the exact public records they used, and executors validate only the records that can affect the pending external action. The system either replans once or blocks when validation is incomplete.

Result: In 30 controlled live workflows with post-plan revision, a freshness-only executor acted on obsolete plans in every task (100% failure), while PlanFence completed all tasks without invalid actions (100% success). PlanFence shows two conditional boundaries: proactive synchronization works better at low churn, while PlanFence avoids repeated update-path coordination as churn grows and avoids validating unrelated state as shared keyspace grows.

Conclusion: PlanFence provides controlled safety and systems-cost benefits rather than general task-accuracy gains. It effectively prevents stale-plan execution in distributed LLM-agent systems by validating only relevant dependencies, with performance advantages depending on churn rate and shared keyspace size.

Abstract: Distributed LLM-agent teams can read the latest shared facts and still act on an obsolete plan. A planner may derive an action from requirement $r_3$, another agent may commit $r_4$, and an executor may receive $r_4$ without replacing the plan derived from $r_3$. We call this \emph{stale-plan execution}: state freshness does not establish that the plan authorizing an action remains valid. We introduce PlanFence, a dependency-scoped action-validation protocol. Plans cite the exact public records they used, and an executor validates only the records that can affect the pending external action, replanning once or blocking when validation is incomplete. In 30 controlled live workflows with a post-plan revision, a freshness-only executor acts on the obsolete plan in every task, whereas PlanFence completes all tasks without an invalid action. Controlled replay reveals two conditional boundaries: proactive synchronization yields lower coordination stall at low churn, while PlanFence avoids repeated update-path coordination as churn grows and avoids validating unrelated state as the shared keyspace grows. These are controlled safety and systems-cost results, not general task-accuracy gains.

</details>


### [120] [A Prompt-Engineering Approach to Develop Scalable, Flexible, and Real-Time Hybrid Micro-Level Personalization in a General Purpose AI Teaching Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03402)
*Saptarshi Basu,Sandeep Kakar,Ashok Goel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study presents a prompt-engineering framework to personalize LLM-based AI teaching assistants (like Jill Watson) across academic disciplines. The framework uses six learner dimensions to create 96 profiles and incorporates Bloom's Taxonomy for cognitive complexity assessment, without requiring model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI teaching assistants provide limited personalization despite offering scalable educational support. The need exists for a method to adapt AI responses to individual learner characteristics without expensive model retraining.

Method: The frameworkpersonalizes responses using six learner-specific dimensions (self-assessment, abstraction preference, verbosity preference, perceptual orientation, information processing style, and level of understanding). Student queries are analyzed using Bloom's Taxonomy to estimate cognitive complexity. Learner attributes are encoded in structured prompts to condition the LLM. Evaluation involves NLP metrics and a human study with five participants.

Result: Results show perceived differences in response style and structure across personalization conditions. Statistical analyses identified learner attributes associated with measurable response changes, providing preliminary evidence that prompt-based personalization can support adaptive behavior in LLM-powered educational agents.

Conclusion: Prompt-based personalization is a viable approach to enable adaptive behavior in LLM-powered educational agents without requiring model retraining. The framework shows promise for scaling personalized educational support across diverse academic contexts.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) teaching assistants powered by large language models (LLMs) offer scalable educational support but often provide limited personalization. This study presents a prompt-engineering-based framework for personalizing general-purpose LLM/RAG-based AI teaching assistants such as Jill Watson across academic disciplines and courses. The framework adapts responses using six learner-specific dimensions: self-assessment, abstraction preference, verbosity preference, perceptual orientation, information processing style, and level of understanding, yielding 96 distinct learner profiles. Student queries are additionally analyzed using Bloom's Taxonomy to estimate cognitive complexity at the interaction level. Learner attributes and cognitive assessments are encoded in structured prompts that condition the LLM without requiring model retraining. The framework is evaluated through experiments using NLP metrics and a human study with five participants. Results show perceived differences in response style and structure across personalization conditions, with statistical analyses identifying learner attributes associated with measurable response changes. These findings provide preliminary evidence that prompt-based personalization can support adaptive behavior in LLM-powered educational agents.

</details>


### [121] [Caught in the Story: Narrative Captivity in Multi-turn LLMs Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03407)
*Yuhe Wu,Guangyu Wang,Yujie Chen,Jiatong Zhang,Yuran Chen,Yutong Zhang,Xiyin Cheng,Wenpeng Cao,Zhuang Liu,Guang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: People increasingly turn to large language models (LLMs) for everyday advice, making ethically charged interpersonal problems a practical moral-advisory context. Most prior work has studied this context through single-turn judgments or pressure-laden rebuttals, assumptions that poorly match how guidance is sought in real-world contexts. These assumptions leave unclear whether narration alone, without an explicit opposing position, can shift model judgments during multi-turn moral consultation. Yet real-world moral-conflict conversation often elicits one party's self-justifying account, which can unfold over multiple turns and create information asymmetry. We introduce \textbf{narrative captivity}, a failure mode in which a model treats an unopposed one-sided account as complete and aligns with the narrator's interpretation without seeking missing perspectives. To measure this phenomenon, we build a benchmark of $5{,}078$ interpersonal-conflict scenarios spanning six moral dimensions. Across 17 LLMs, narrative captivity is widespread: end-state judgments under multi-turn narration shift by 25 percentage points on average beyond the matched single-turn baseline. Stage-level analysis identifies preference optimization as a major contributor, while four inference-time strategies provide only partial mitigation. We hope our project fosters LLM advisors that preserve independent judgment in real-world consultation.

</details>


### [122] [Dude: A Dual-Detection Multi-Agent System for Paper-Code Discrepancy Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03416)
*Weijie Liu,Running Zhao,Wenhao Yuan,Jinfeng Xu,Zhanfeng Xu,Xiaoxi Zhang,Edith Cheuk-Han Ngai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dude is the first Dual-Detection Multi-Agent System for paper-code discrepancy detection, addressing limitations of single-agent LLM paradigms through granularity-aligned negotiation and two-stage salience-filtering mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-empowered paper-code discrepancy detection is growing in importance as research submissions exceed manual review capability. Existing single-agent LLM paradigms suffer from limited context capacity and one-sided discrepancy detection, leading to poor recall performance.

Method: The paper identifies granularity asymmetry between paper-language and code-language as the root cause of over-interpretation and over-reporting challenges. To address this, Dude implements: (1) granularity-aligned negotiation to align representations between paper and code, and (2) two-stage salience-filtering mechanism to filter out false positives.

Result: Dude achieves significant improvements: recall and precision improvement by up to 22.8%, and F1 score improvement by up to 18.7% compared to baseline methods on real-world paper-code discrepancy datasets.

Conclusion: The granularity-aligned negotiation and two-stage salience-filtering mechanisms effectively prevent agents from falsely reporting discrepancies, making Dude a superior approach for paper-code discrepancy detection.

Abstract: LLM-empowered paper-code discrepancy detection has received growing concern since the scaling of research submissions exceeds the manual review capability. However, the limited context capacity and one-sided discrepancy detection of existing single-agent LLM paradigms lead to an inferior recall performance in detecting discrepancies. In this paper, we propose Dude, the first Dual-Detection Multi-Agent System for paper-code discrepancy detection. We discover that the granularity asymmetry of the paper-language and code-language introduces over-interpretation and over-reporting challenges in a multi-agent system design for discrepancy detection, resulting in increasing false positives. To address this, we propose a granularity-aligned negotiation and a two-stage salience-filtering mechanism in Dude, which effectively prevents agents from falsely reporting discrepancies. Experimental results in real-world paper-code discrepancy datasets showcase Dude's significant recall and precision improvement by up to 22.8%, increasing F1 score by up to 18.7% compared to baseline methods.

</details>


### [123] [DuplexSpeechBench-IFEval: Evaluating Implicit Instruction Following in Full-Duplex Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03423)
*Puneet Mathur,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSB-IFEval is a benchmark for evaluating implicit instruction-following in full-duplex voice agents. It tests 1,038 cases across 8 roles and 5 conditioning protocols. Results show that models struggle to infer behavior from personas, with full-duplex models showing 4.5-9.7% adherence drops under persona-only conditioning, and safety conflicts being particularly difficult to override.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for full-duplex voice agents test turn-management behaviors through explicit instructions, but deployed agents are typically configured through roles or personas from which appropriate behavior must be inferred rather than explicitly stated. This gap between benchmark evaluation and real-world deployment motivates the need for evaluating implicit instruction-following in real-time spoken interaction.

Method: The paper introduces DuplexSpeechBench-IFEval (DSB-IFEval), comprising 1,038 test cases spanning eight diverse assistant roles. It evaluates five conditioning protocols: default behavior, explicit behavioral instructions, persona-implied behavior, combined persona-rule conditioning, and instruction conflict. Real-time floor management is measured using a deterministic Instruction Adherence Score (IAS), while persona-consistent content is evaluated using LLM-judged Persona Adherence Score (PAS). Experiments were conducted across six real-time speech systems.

Result: Full duplex models like F-Actor and PersonaPlex show significant drops in adherence (9.7% and 4.5% respectively) when behavior must be inferred from persona rather than stated explicitly. In contrast, GPT-Realtime, MiniCPM-o, and Fun-Audio-Chat maintain strong persona-consistent content adherence but their floor behavior does not adapt across conditioning protocols and remains constrained on proactive actions. Additionally, systems struggle to override conflicting directives under safety conflicts even when they reliably follow persona-related instructions.

Conclusion: Inferring behavior implied by a role, executing it at appropriate conversational moments, and resolving competing instructions remain distinct and challenging problems for full-duplex voice agents. Architecture-dependent trade-offs exist between explicit instruction following and persona-based behavior inference.

Abstract: Full-duplex voice agents must continuously decide when to listen, backchannel, interrupt, handle speech overlaps, take the floor, and yield. Existing benchmarks largely test these behaviors through explicit turn-management instructions, while deployed agents are often configured through roles or personas from which the appropriate conversational behavior must be inferred. We introduce DuplexSpeechBench-IFEval (DSB-IFEval) for evaluating implicit instruction-following in real-time spoken interaction. (DSB-IFEval) comprises 1,038 test cases spanning eight diverse assistant roles and evaluates five conditioning protocols for instruction-following: default behavior, explicit behavioral instructions, persona-implied behavior, combined persona--rule conditioning, and instruction conflict. We measure real-time floor management using a deterministic Instruction Adherence Score (IAS) and persona-consistent content using LLM-judged Persona Adherence Score (PAS). Across six real-time speech systems, we find architecture-dependent trade-offs. Full duplex models like F-Actor and PersonaPlex are more sensitive to whether conversational behavior is stated explicitly or must be inferred from a persona, with adherence dropping by 9.7% and 4.5%, respectively, under persona-only conditioning. In contrast, GPT-Realtime, MiniCPM-o, and Fun-Audio-Chat strongly adhere to persona-consistent content, but their floor behavior does not adapt across explicit and persona-only instructions and remains constrained on several proactive actions. We further find that even if systems reliably follow conflicting directives to their prescribed persona, they still struggle to override them under safety conflict. These results show that inferring the behavior implied by a role, executing it at the appropriate conversational moment, and resolving competing instructions remain distinct challenges for full-duplex voice agents.

</details>


### [124] [Do GUI Agents Know When Not to Act? Enabling Conflict-Aware Termination for Multimodal GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03438)
*Zhaoyuan Huang,Tianjie Ju,Pengzhou Cheng,Zheng Wu,Yansi Li,Chuanbiao Song,Jun Lan,Huijia Zhu,Weiqiang Wang,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CONFLICTGUI is a benchmark for evaluating GUI agents' ability to recognize and terminate execution when faced with conflicting instructions (internal conflicts or conflicts with GUI context). CONFLICTGUARD is an inference-time framework that improves agents' feasibility awareness through verification protocols and action modulation, significantly boosting conflict task success rates while preserving normal task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GUI agents often execute instructions blindly without recognizing when instructions are infeasible or conflicting. Current agents show "execution-biased overcompliance" - they continue acting even when instructions contain conflicts or cannot be executed given the GUI state. There was no benchmark to study this conflict-aware termination capability.

Method: 1) Created CONFLICTGUI benchmark covering two conflict types: instruction-internal conflicts and instruction-GUI context conflicts. 2) Proposed CONFLICTGUARD framework with two components: a feasibility verification protocol (guides agent to assess instruction logic and GUI evidence before acting) and a conditional action modulation mechanism (steers agents from over-compliant execution to termination). 3) Evaluated across five widely-used GUI agents.

Result: CONFLICTGUARD significantly improves average conflict task success rate while preserving normal GUI-task performance. The framework demonstrates that lightweight inference-time intervention can substantially boost GUI agents' competence to identify inappropriate execution scenarios and refrain from unnecessary actions.

Conclusion: GUI agents need conflict-aware termination capabilities, not just action execution ability. A lightweight inference-time intervention (CONFLICTGUARD) can effectively align agents' feasibility awareness with action generation, enabling them to recognize and appropriately terminate when facing conflicting instructions.

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents are increasingly used to execute natural-language instructions on user interfaces, yet real users may issue infeasible instructions due to benign mistakes. A reliable agent should not only know how to act, but also when not to act. In this work, we introduce CONFLICTGUI, a benchmark covering instruction-internal conflicts and instruction-GUI context conflicts to study conflict-aware termination. Our evaluation reveals severe execution-biased overcompliance: agents that perform well on feasible tasks often continue to execute blindly under conflicting instructions. To mitigate this behavior, we propose CONFLICTGUARD, an inference-time framework that aligns an agent's feasibility awareness with its action generation. CONFLICTGUARD contains two coupled components: a feasibility verification protocol that guides the agent to assess instruction logic and GUI-side evidence before acting, and a conditional action modulation mechanism that steers agents from over-compliant execution into termination-oriented behavior. Experiments across five widely-used agents demonstrate that CONFLICTGUARD improves average conflict task success rate significantly, while preserving normal GUI-task performance. These results validate that a lightweight inference-time intervention can substantially boost GUI Agent's competence to identify inappropriate execution scenarios and refrain from unnecessary actions.

</details>


### [125] [Beyond "Made with AI": Visualizing Provenance Density to Mitigate the Transparency Penalty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03460)
*Qing Zhang,Yifei Huang,Juyoung Lee,Thad Starner,Jun Rekimoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the "Fluency Trap" problem where users trust fluent AI-generated misinformation while discounting accurate AI-generated content. The authors propose "Provenance Density," an evidence-visualization interface showing the density of verified claims in text, and demonstrate its effectiveness in a user study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As generative AI makes polished prose easy to produce, fluency can no longer serve as a proxy for truth. Users tend to trust fluent hallucinations while discounting accurate content once identified as AI-generated. Existing binary "Made with AI" labels only disclose authorship without showing what supports claims.

Method: Two studies were conducted: (1) A user study with 81 participants testing an idealized Provenance Density interface against a no-signal control, and (2) a technical audit with 200 samples evaluating retrieval density and Consistency Veto as discriminative signals.

Result: The Provenance Density interface produced a large discernment gap between truth and fabrication (+4.15 points, d=1.82), while participants given no signal showed no detectable discrimination. Surprisingly, the technical audit revealed that retrieval density alone is insufficient; the Consistency Veto carried most of the discriminative signal on dynamic queries.

Conclusion: As AI-generated content becomes indistinguishable from human writing, effective transparency must shift from simple authorship disclosure toward evidence visualization approaches like Provenance Density.

Abstract: As generative AI makes polished prose cheap to produce, users can no longer rely on fluency as a proxy for truth. We call this failure mode the Fluency Trap: users trust fluent hallucinations while also discounting accurate content once it is disclosed as AI-generated. Binary ``Made with AI'' labels respond with authorship disclosure, but they do not show what supports a claim. We propose Provenance Density, an evidence-visualization interface that shows the density of verified claims in a text. In a user study with 81 participants, an idealized Provenance Density interface produced a large discernment gap between truth and fabrication ($+4.15$ points, $d=1.82$), whereas participants given no signal showed no detectable discrimination. A technical audit with 200 samples shows that retrieval density alone is insufficient; unexpectedly, the Consistency Veto carries most of the discriminative signal on dynamic queries. As AI-generated content becomes indistinguishable from human writing, effective transparency must move from authorship disclosure toward evidence visualization.

</details>


### [126] [AutoGraphForge: Towards Automated Graph Theory Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03478)
*Ján Pastorek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoGraphForge is an automated pipeline for graph-theoretic conjecture generation, refutation, formalization, and proof. Using Graffiti3 for conjecture generation, a novelty filter with 559 relations, and testing against 348,000 graphs, the system produced 6,522 surviving conjectures, with some manually proven. Formalization into Lean 4 and neural prover verification are integrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To automate the discovery and verification of graph theory conjectures, addressing the bottleneck of manual conjecture generation and proof verification in mathematics.

Method: Counterexample-guided conjecture generation using Graffiti3 over an evolving snapshot table; novelty filtering via linear programming with 559 classical relations; refutation against 348,000 graphs; counterexample search algorithms; Lean 4 formalization with neural provers (DeepSeek-Prover-V2-671B and OProver-32B) for proof assistance.

Result: 6,522 conjectures survived refutation, novelty filtering, and active search, including nontrivial relations between annihilation number and edge-cover number for bipartite and regular graphs (manually proven). Pipeline is end-to-end functional with initial sanity checks passed.

Conclusion: AutoGraphForge demonstrates a scalable approach to automated mathematical discovery in graph theory, combining traditional combinatorial methods with modern neural provers and formal verification, though full automated proof remains challenging.

Abstract: We report on our ongoing project to develop a computational pipeline, AutoGraphForge, for an automated graph-theoretic conjecturing-refuting-formalizing-proving system. Conjecture generation is counterexample-guided and runs in rounds: a Graffiti3 generator proposes conjectures over a small, evolving snapshot table $T$ (initially a few hundred graphs with their computed invariants) that grows only by counterexamples to its own conjectures. A novelty filter of $559$ classical and folklore relations, closed under transitive composition and linear identity substitution, decides via a linear program whether a candidate is already implied by known results. Surviving candidates are tested against a dataset of about $348,000$ graphs, unioning the complete House of Graphs invariant export, the exhaustive census of all connected graphs on at most nine vertices, several extremal families (strongly regular, minimal Ramsey, Cayley, cages, barbells, lollipops, spiders), and random models. Counterexample-search algorithms then attack the remainder. Run for several rounds on an HPC cluster, the loop yields $6,522$ conjectures that survived the refutation dataset, the novelty filter and every active-search run -- among them nontrivial relations between the annihilation number and the edge-cover number for bipartite and regular graphs, which we prove by hand. A subsequent formalization and proving stage deterministically translates each surviving conjecture into a Lean 4 statement skeleton; every candidate proof is kernel-verified against a pinned mathlib4 and our custom invariant preamble. This stage integrates two neural provers -- DeepSeek-Prover-V2-671B (served with vLLM) and the Lean-specialised OProver-32B -- behind the independent kernel check. It is implemented end-to-end and passes initial sanity checks, with the full pipeline currently running on the cluster.

</details>


### [127] [Making Every Tool Call Count: Necessary Tool-Evidence Path Rewards for Agentic Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03493)
*Xingming Long,Yu Liu,Zhiwei Yang,Hanqi Feng,Shaojie Zhang,Barnabas Poczos,Chao Jiang,Zhenbo Luo,Lei Jiang,Pei Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces NTEP (Necessary Tool-Evidence Path), an annotation scheme and reward mechanism for training agentic vision-language models to use tools more effectively. It addresses two key problems: redundant/off-target tool calls and failure to extract information from tool observations. The proposed NTEP-8B model shows improved accuracy and efficiency on seven benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern VLMs struggle with complex queries requiring fine-grained visual details or external knowledge. Existing training paradigms only evaluate final answer correctness, leading to two critical issues: (i) redundant or off-target tool calls that fail to gather necessary evidence, and (ii) failure to extract necessary information from tool observations.

Method: The paper proposes: (1) NTEP annotation scheme that explicitly specifies essential external evidence and corresponding tool calls for each query; (2) NTEP-R supervision mechanism that rewards alignment between pre-call intent and necessary evidence-seeking goals, and ensures post-call observations align with necessary evidence; (3) a non-repeated-goal regularizer to penalize redundant calls revisiting satisfied NTEP goals.

Result: NTEP-8B (8B parameter model) significantly improves both search-oriented accuracy and tool-use efficiency within a unified three-tool framework on seven image-grounded benchmarks.

Conclusion: Fine-grained tool-evidence path supervision is critical for training robust agentic VLMs. The NTEP approach provides a promising direction for improving evidence acquisition and utilization in tool-using vision-language models.

Abstract: Modern vision-language models (VLMs) can directly answer many image-grounded questions, yet they often struggle with complex queries requiring fine-grained visual details or external knowledge. To acquire this missing evidence, agentic VLMs invoke tools such as image cropping, image search, and text search. However, existing training paradigms primarily evaluate tool-use based on final answer correctness, leaving evidence acquisition and utilization insufficiently supervised. This leads to two critical shortcomings: (i) models frequently issue redundant or off-target tool calls that fail to gather necessary evidence, and (ii) even when appropriate tools are called, models often fail to extract the necessary information from the resulting observations. To address these limitations, we introduce the NTEP (Necessary Tool-Evidence Path), a novel annotation scheme that explicitly specifies the essential external evidence and corresponding tool calls for each query. Building upon this, we propose NTEP-R (NTEP Reward), a supervision mechanism ensuring that each tool invocation strictly advances the reasoning process toward the final solution. Specifically, our approach rewards the agent for aligning its pre-call intent with a necessary evidence-seeking goal, and for ensuring the information summarized from the post-call observation aligns with the necessary evidence. Furthermore, we introduce a non-repeated-goal regularizer to penalize redundant calls that revisit satisfied NTEP goals. Extensive evaluations on seven image-grounded benchmarks demonstrate that our 8B-parameter instantiation, NTEP-8B, significantly improves both search-oriented accuracy and tool-use efficiency within a unified three-tool framework. These results highlight the critical value of fine-grained tool-evidence path supervision for training robust agentic VLMs.

</details>


### [128] [GrowPage: On-Demand KV Budgeting for Efficient LLM Reasoning Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03494)
*Qiankun Ma,Yanjiang Zhou,Zinan Xiong,Haofei Wang,Zhen Song,Yang Xiang,Ziyao Zhang,Hairong Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GrowPage is an on-demand KV budgeting framework that treats KV cache capacity as a dynamic runtime resource, using dual-timescale query summaries to capture attention patterns and allowing physical page acquisition when demand increases, integrated with PagedAttention for efficient LLM serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV compression methods use fixed per-request budgets throughout decoding, but reasoning workloads have substantial demand variation - different requests need different KV capacities, and attention demand evolves during generation. This mismatch creates inefficiency in memory utilization.

Method: GrowPage maintains lightweight dual-timescale query summaries to capture both recent and long-term attention behaviors, using their relative attention working sets to estimate demand evolution. At capacity boundaries, it either compresses KV states within current allocation or acquires an additional physical page when broader demand emerges. It integrates with PagedAttention's page-level memory abstraction.

Result: Experiments on reasoning benchmarks across multiple models show that GrowPage achieves superior performance-throughput trade-off over existing approaches.

Conclusion: GrowPage successfully addresses the KV cache bottleneck in LLM serving by treating KV capacity as a dynamic resource, enabling efficient handling of variable attention demands in reasoning workloads while preserving continuous batching and prefix caching capabilities.

Abstract: Long-output reasoning has made the key--value (KV) cache a critical memory bottleneck for efficient LLM serving. Existing KV compression methods usually rely on a predefined per-request budget and adjust only which KV states are retained, leaving the total capacity fixed throughout decoding. However, reasoning workloads exhibit substantial demand variation: different requests require different KV capacities, and the attention demand of an individual request evolves during generation. We introduce \textbf{GrowPage}, an on-demand KV budgeting framework that treats KV capacity as a runtime resource. GrowPage maintains lightweight dual-timescale query summaries to capture recent and long-term attention behaviors, and uses their relative attention working sets to estimate demand evolution. At each capacity boundary, GrowPage either compresses KV states within the current allocation or acquires an additional physical page when broader demand emerges. By integrating with PagedAttention's page-level memory abstraction, GrowPage preserves continuous batching and prefix caching. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks across multiple models show that GrowPage achieves a superior performance--throughput trade-off over existing approaches.

</details>


### [129] [PPO-STGNN: A Proximal Policy Optimization Approach with Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks for DAG Task Scheduling in Cloud-Edge-End Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03503)
*Yangshuo Qi,Chenwei Wang,Zihan Shen,Songlin Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes PPO-STGNN, a novel DAG task-scheduling algorithm that combines Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (STGNNs) for efficient scheduling in heterogeneous cloud-edge-end collaborative environments. The method uses STGNN to extract features from both DAG task topology and resource graphs, optimizing scheduling policy through PPO to minimize makespan and Schedule Length Ratio (SLR) while improving CPU and memory load balancing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid growth of IoT has led to increased computation-intensive DAG (Directed Acyclic Graph) tasks in cloud-edge-end environments. The heterogeneity of nodes (computing capacity, network bandwidth, energy consumption) makes efficient task scheduling with complex dependencies an NP-hard problem. Traditional heuristic algorithms and conventional reinforcement learning methods fail to capture the spatio-temporal dynamics of system resources, necessitating a new approach that can handle both task dependencies and resource heterogeneity effectively.

Method: The proposed PPO-STGNN algorithm integrates two key components: (1) Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (STGNN) to extract features from both the DAG task topology and the physical cloud-edge-end resource graph, capturing spatio-temporal dynamics; (2) Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to optimize the scheduling policy. The method aims to minimize makespan and SLR while improving CPU and memory load balancing. To accelerate convergence, a multi-teacher behavior-cloning mechanism is introduced for pretraining.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that PPO-STGNN significantly improves load balancing while maintaining low completion time, making it suitable for dynamic and heterogeneous cloud-edge-end DAG scheduling scenarios.

Conclusion: The PPO-STGNN algorithm successfully combines deep reinforcement learning with graph neural networks to address the challenging problem of DAG task scheduling in heterogeneous cloud-edge-end environments. The integration of STGNN for feature extraction and PPO for policy optimization, enhanced by multi-teacher behavior cloning for faster convergence, provides an effective solution that balances multiple objectives (makespan, SLR, and load balancing). The method shows particular promise for dynamic and heterogeneous scheduling scenarios.

Abstract: With the rapid development of the Internet of Things, computation intensive directed acyclic graph (DAG) tasks have become increasingly common in cloud-edge-end collaborative environments. However, cloud, edge, and end nodes are highly heterogeneous in computing capacity, network bandwidth, and energy consumption, which makes the efficient scheduling of tasks with complex dependencies an NP-hard problem. Traditional heuristic algorithms and conventional reinforcement-learning methods often fail to capture the spatio-temporal dynamics of system resources. This paper proposes PPO-STGNN, a DAG task-scheduling algorithm that integrates proximal policy optimization (PPO) with spatio-temporal graph neural networks (STGNNs). The method uses an STGNN to extract features from both the DAG task topology and the physical cloud-edge-end resource graph, and then optimizes the scheduling policy through PPO to minimize makespan and schedule length ratio (SLR) while improving CPU and memory load balancing. To accelerate convergence, a multi-teacher behavior-cloning mechanism is introduced for pretraining. Experimental results show that PPO-STGNN significantly improves load balancing while maintaining a low completion time, making it suitable for dynamic and heterogeneous cloud-edge- end DAG scheduling scenarios.

</details>


### [130] [DRACO: Fine-Grained Credit Assignment with Dynamic Rubrics for Long-Horizon Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04094)
*Shubham Gandhi,Saurabh Goyal,Kiran Kate,Yara Rizk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRACO addresses the challenge of training reinforcement learning agents in outcome-blind settings where ground-truth success signals are unavailable. It dynamically generates rubrics during training, scores them once per trajectory, and redistributes that judgment over the responsible steps to produce differentiated per-step advantages in GRPO, without requiring any trained attribution module.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards works well with programmatic checkers, but most long-horizon agent domains lack such verifiers. Multi-criteria rubrics are popular for supplying rewards but suffer from sparsity: scoring once per trajectory provides poor signal across tens of steps.

Method: DRACO generates rubrics dynamically during training to track the policy's evolving capability, scores those rubrics once per completed trajectory, and redistributes that judgment over the steps responsible for annotated rubrics to produce differentiated per-step advantages in GRPO. The redistribution is closed-form and does not introduce any trained attribution module.

Result: On AppWorld, DRACO gains 15.9 points over the base model and 5.3 points over GRPO trained with sparse ground-truth reward, despite not using any verifiers. On out-of-domain Tau-Bench, it gains 5.3 points over the base model even without a frontier judge, beating both ground-truth-reward training and other rubric-based training settings.

Conclusion: DRACO successfully addresses the credit assignment problem in outcome-blind RL settings by redistributing trajectory-level rubric judgments to per-step advantages in a closed-form manner, achieving superior performance over ground-truth-reward training and other rubric-based approaches on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards works well when a task has a programmatic checker, but most long-horizon agent domains have none. We work in the outcome-blind setting, where ground-truth success signals are not available. Multi-criteria rubrics are a popular way to supply such a reward; they are scored once per trajectory, but a single scalar is a poor signal across tens of steps. We propose DRACO: Distributing Rubric-based Advantage for Credit Optimization. It generates rubrics dynamically during training to track the policy's evolving capability, scores those rubrics once per completed trajectory, and redistributes that judgment over the steps responsible for annotated rubrics to produce differentiated per-step advantages in GRPO. The redistribution is closed-form and does not introduce any trained attribution module. On AppWorld, DRACO gains 15.9 points over the base model and 5.3 points over GRPO trained with a sparse ground-truth reward, despite not using any verifiers itself. On out-of-domain Tau-Bench, it gains 5.3 points over the base model even without a frontier judge, beating both ground-truth-reward training and other rubric-based training settings. The code for DRACO is available at https://github.com/IBM/draco.

</details>


### [131] [What Matters for Aggressive Decoding-Time KV Eviction? Temporal Aggregation and Ranking Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03515)
*Bo Zeng,Yu Zhao,Yefeng Liu,Zhihong Lu,Xuanfan Ni,Xintong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates decoding-time KV cache compression in LLMs, focusing on temporal score aggregation rather than just token scoring functions. The key finding is that EMA aggregation makes different scorer modifications largely indistinguishable, while value-norm and entropy variants maintain correlation with attention. The paper introduces InertiaKV and InertiaKV-Lazy methods, and shows Score-Free decoding achieves near-zero quality change while eliminating subsequent scoring costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current KV cache compression research focuses on token scoring functions while treating temporal aggregation (how scores are combined across decode steps) as an implementation detail. The authors investigate whether this neglect is warranted under aggressive KV compression.

Method: The authors evaluate various scorer modifications (value-norm, entropy, KeyDiff, key norm, recency, learned scorer) under EMA aggregation across 6 open-weight backbones on LongBench, LongBench-v2, and RULER benchmarks. They also propose InertiaKV (EMA-based eviction) and InertiaKV-Lazy (periodic-refresh variant), and study Score-Free decoding as an empirical operating point.

Result: EMA aggregation makes different scorer modifications nearly indistinguishable at the eviction-set level. Value-norm and entropy variants remain highly correlated with attention and produce nearly unchanged retention sets, while KeyDiff, key norm, recency, and learned scorer alter rankings and degrade performance. InertiaKV-Lazy yields 1.34-1.46x decode throughput vs full refresh InertiaKV. Score-Free decoding incurs average quality change of +0.03 while removing all subsequent scoring.

Conclusion: Temporal aggregation and ranking preservation are distinct, consequential design factors in KV cache compression—not that scoring quality is irrelevant in general. The study identifies EMA-based methods and Score-Free decoding as effective approaches, with the key insight being that how scores are aggregated over time matters as much as the scoring function itself.

Abstract: Decoding-time KV cache compression research focuses heavily on designing better token scoring functions, while the temporal rule that aggregates scores across decode steps is often treated as an implementation detail. Under aggressive KV compression, we find that exponential-moving-average (EMA) aggregation makes approximately order-preserving scorer modifications largely indistinguishable at the eviction-set level. Value-norm and entropy variants remain highly correlated with attention and produce nearly unchanged retention sets, whereas KeyDiff, key norm, recency, and a learned scorer alter the ranking and degrade substantially. We associate this stability with the evaluated aggregation, which couples layer weighting and temporal retention. Building on this observation, we introduce InertiaKV, an EMA-based decoding-time eviction method, and InertiaKV-Lazy, its periodic-refresh variant, which yields 1.34-1.46x decode throughput relative to full refresh InertiaKV. We also study Score-Free decoding as a separate empirical operating point: it scores the full context once at the first decode step, freezes that ranking, and incurs an average quality change of +0.03 while removing all subsequent scoring. Across six open-weight backbones and the LongBench, LongBench-v2, and RULER benchmarks, the results identify temporal aggregation and ranking preservation as distinct, consequential design factors; they do not imply that scoring quality is irrelevant in general.

</details>


### [132] [CulturalMenuBench: Probing the Knowledge-Application Gap in Multimodal Culinary Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03526)
*Bo Zeng,Linfeng Gao,Peiqin Lin,Yu Zhao,Mingyan Zeng,Yu Tong,Xintong Wang,Linlong Xu,Longyue Wang,Weihua Luo,Qinggang Zhang,Jinsong Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CulturalMenuBench is a benchmark testing whether multimodal language models have genuine cultural understanding of food or just perform visual matching. The benchmark contains 4,870 items across 10 languages and 18 regions with 10 tasks. Testing 12 models reveals a significant knowledge-application gap: models achieving 94%+ on standard recognition drop to 56% on Chinese regional cuisine attribution, even with identical format. The knowledge exists but cannot be activated through visual input. Ablation confirms process-grounded tasks require procedural evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal language models achieve high scores on food recognition benchmarks, but it's unclear whether this reflects genuine cultural understanding or just visual pattern matching. The researchers wanted to probe this distinction and determine if models can apply cultural knowledge beyond surface-level visual recognition.

Method: The researchers created CulturalMenuBench with 4,870 items in 10 languages across 18 regions. The benchmark includes 10 tasks pairing final-dish and step-by-step cooking images with ingredients, procedural text, and regional labels. They evaluated 12 models and conducted diagnostic analyses including error pattern analysis, visual distinctiveness correlation, and comparison between name-based vs image-based classification.

Result: Models exceeding 94% on standard multiple-choice tasks drop to at most 56% when attributing dishes to Chinese regional cuisines. Error patterns are consistent with random guessing. Accuracy tracks visual distinctiveness rather than cultural structure. Models classify cuisines more accurately from dish names alone than from images (+7-18 points). Ablation confirms removing sequential cooking images selectively degrades process-grounded tasks.

Conclusion: Near-perfect recognition can conceal an inability to apply cultural knowledge. Models possess the knowledge but cannot activate it through visual input. This motivates training approaches that explicitly connect perception, procedure, and cultural context. The benchmark and code are publicly available.

Abstract: Multimodal language models achieve near-ceiling scores on food recognition benchmarks, yet it remains unclear whether this success reflects genuine cultural understanding or mere visual matching. To probe this distinction, we introduce CulturalMenuBench, a benchmark of 4,870 items in 10 languages across 18 regions; its 10 tasks pair final-dish and step-by-step cooking images with ingredients, procedural text, and regional labels, spanning basic recognition to process-grounded cultural attribution. Evaluating 12 models exposes a substantial knowledge-application gap: models exceeding 94% on standard multiple-choice tasks drop to at most 56% when attributing dishes to Chinese regional cuisines, despite an identical four-way format. Diagnostic analyses explain why: error patterns are consistent with random guessing, accuracy tracks visual distinctiveness rather than cultural structure, and models classify cuisines more accurately from dish names alone than from images (+7-18 points). The knowledge is thus present but cannot be activated through visual input. An ablation confirms these tasks genuinely require procedural evidence: removing sequential cooking images selectively degrades process-grounded tasks while others remain stable. Overall, CulturalMenuBench shows that near-perfect recognition can conceal an inability to apply cultural knowledge, motivating training that explicitly connects perception, procedure, and cultural context. Code and data are publicly available.

</details>


### [133] [NeoRed: A Knowledge-Logic-Alignment Multimodal Large Language Model for Neonatal Respiratory Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03527)
*Yinan Liu,Hongtai Xia,Haoran Xu,Jiankang Hong,Jingkuan Song,Ye Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeoRed is the first Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) specifically designed for neonatal respiratory disease diagnosis, addressing domain gap from adult training data and insufficient clinical context integration through a Knowledge-Logic-Alignment (KLA) framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neonatal respiratory diseases are a major cause of morbidity and mortality, but existing MLLMs face two key limitations: (1) domain gap from predominantly adult training data, and (2) insufficient integration of multidimensional clinical context for accurate diagnosis.

Method: The authors collected two real-world clinical datasets (NeoCXR and NeoCXR-EV) and proposed NeoRed with a novel Knowledge-Logic-Alignment (KLA) framework containing three components: (1) Knowledge Prior Injection (KPI) - incorporates neonatologist-inspired diagnostic priors into multimodal representations; (2) Diagnostic Logic Constraint (DLC) - aligns generated report semantics with multimodal diagnostic logic; (3) Visual Semantic Alignment (VSA) - establishes semantic correspondence between visual features and imaging conclusions.

Result: NeoRed achieves ROUGE-L of 53.29% and Clinical Efficacy F1 score of 65.19% on NeoCXR, outperforming existing MLLMs. It also preserves competitive performance on adult benchmarks (MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray).

Conclusion: NeoRed enables accurate neonatal diagnostic report generation and fills the gap in neonatal diagnostic reports. The KLA framework effectively addresses domain-specific challenges and integrates heterogeneous clinical context with chest X-rays for improved diagnosis.

Abstract: Neonatal respiratory diseases are a major cause of neonatal morbidity and mortality, posing substantial challenges in clinical practice. Despite recent advances, existing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face two key limitations in neonatal diagnosis: (1) domain gap arising from predominantly adult training data; (2) insufficient integration of multidimensional clinical context for accurate diagnosis. To address these challenges, we collect two real-world clinical datasets (NeoCXR and NeoCXR-EV) and propose NeoRed, to the best of our knowledge, the first MLLM tailored for neonatal respiratory disease, filling the gap in neonatal diagnostic reports generation. To enhance joint diagnosis from heterogeneous clinical context and chest X-rays, we design a novel Knowledge-Logic-Alignment (KLA) framework which constrains model behavior from three perspectives: 1) Knowledge Prior Injection (KPI) incorporates neonatologist-inspired diagnostic priors into multimodal representations, guiding disease-specific attention across modalities; 2) Diagnostic Logic Constraint (DLC) aligns the semantics of generated reports with multimodal diagnostic logic; and 3) Visual Semantic Alignment (VSA) establishes semantic correspondence between visual features and imaging conclusions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that NeoRed enables accurate neonatal diagnostic reports generation, achieving ROUGE-L of 53.29% and Clinical Efficacy F1 score of 65.19% on NeoCXR, outperforming existing MLLMs. NeoRed also preserves competitive report generation performance on adult benchmarks (MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray). Datasets will be available upon application.

</details>


### [134] [Feature Reconfiguration With Visual Prior for Medical Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03535)
*Yinan Liu,Jiankang Hong,Zhen Gao,Ye Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FreNet is a feature reconfiguration framework for medical lesion segmentation that addresses complex background interference and diverse lesion morphology through two novel components: IPNN for pixel-level input reconfiguration using visual priors from SAM, and DFR module for feature-level reconfiguration during encoding using frequency decoupling and spatial localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing encoder-decoder methods lack early prior guidance and feature reconfiguration during encoding, limiting their effectiveness in handling complex background interference and diverse lesion morphology in medical image segmentation.

Method: FreNet uses two key components: (1) Implicit Prior Neural Network (IPNN) - models continuous spatial field and leverages visual prior from SAM to reconfigure input image before encoding; (2) Dual-domain Feature Reconfiguration (DFR) module - includes Frequency Decoupling Module (FDM) for frequency-domain decoupling to enhance foreground-background discriminability, and Spatial Localization Module (SLM) for spatial relocation and stability improvement.

Result: FreNet significantly outperforms SOTA methods on 9 medical image segmentation benchmarks across three imaging modalities. On ETIS dataset, achieves 5.0% Dice improvement over SOTA and 7.2% over SAM.

Conclusion: The proposed feature reconfiguration framework with visual priors effectively addresses both major challenges in lesion segmentation - complex background interference and diverse lesion morphology - through pixel-level input reconfiguration and feature-level encoding reconfiguration.

Abstract: Lesion segmentation in medical images plays a critical role in clinical diagnosis and treatment planning. Despite significant advances, lesion segmentation remains challenging due to two major factors: (1) complex background interference; (2) diverse lesion morphology. Existing encoder-decoder based methods mainly focus on enhancing feature extraction or redesigning decoding strategies. However, they lack early prior guidance and feature reconfiguration during the encoding stage, limiting their effectiveness in handling these challenges. To address these limitations, we propose FreNet, a feature reconfiguration framework with visual priors, which performs pixel-level reconfiguration before encoding and feature-level reconfiguration during encoding for precise medical lesion segmentation. To suppress background responses, we propose an Implicit Prior Neural Network (IPNN), which models a continuous spatial field and leverages visual prior from SAM to reconfigure input image before encoding stage. To better handle diverse lesion morphology, we design a Dual-domain Feature Reconfiguration (DFR) module to progressively reconfigure backbone features during encoding stage. Within DFR, the Frequency Decoupling Module (FDM) decouples backbone features in frequency domain to enhance foreground-background discriminability, while the Spatial Localization Module (SLM) spatially relocates and improving spatial stability after frequency decoupling. Extensive experiments on 9 medical image segmentation benchmarks across three imaging modalities demonstrate that FreNet significantly outperforms state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods. On the challenging ETIS dataset, our method achieves Dice improvements of 5.0% over SOTA method and 7.2% over SAM.

</details>


### [135] [Dalek: A Constructive Agent Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03546)
*Wanpeng Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dalek is a closed machine designed for agents that achieves self-maintenance, self-evolution, self-reproduction, and self-organization on any substrate satisfying a general host contract. It combines Von Neumann's self-reproducing automaton concept with four obligations (host boundary, construction language, admissible transitions, rule heredity) and implements them on a text-and-message agent substrate using a large language model and compiler as a capability producer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to create a fully autonomous machine that can maintain itself, evolve, reproduce, and organize on any substrate. It seeks to realize Von Neumann's 1948 vision of self-reproducing automata with modern additions like explicit structures for boundary, identity, history, and growth, while closing the loop between heredity and evolution within the machine itself.

Method: The machine is built from three primitives (actors, messages, channels) and four obligations (host boundary, construction language, admissible transitions, rule heredity). It combines a hereditary constructional core (self-description, constructor, copier, controller) from Von Neumann's work with modern additions. A large language model and compiler serve as the payload to produce new capabilities, which are authored, compiled, installed into the description, and inherited by descendants.

Result: The machine achieves closure in heredity and evolution - it can produce its own organs and runtime, enabling new capabilities to be authored, compiled, installed into the description, and inherited by descendants. The machine operates on a text-and-message agent substrate with explicit structures for boundary, identity, history, and growth.

Conclusion: Dalek represents a closed, self-reproducing machine that realizes self-maintenance, self-evolution, self-reproduction, and self-organization on any substrate satisfying a general host contract. It successfully closes the loop between heredity and evolution within the machine itself, demonstrating how Von Neumann's theoretical self-reproducing automaton can be implemented with modern AI components (LLM + compiler) to create a fully autonomous, self-evolving system.

Abstract: We present Dalek, a closed machine designed for agents that realizes self-maintenance, self-evolution, self-reproduction, and self-organization on any substrate satisfying a general host contract. The machine is built from three primitives---actors, messages, and channels. Four obligations---a host boundary, a construction language, admissible transitions, and rule heredity---give its boundary, identity, and closure a structural basis.
  Von Neumann's 1948 self-reproducing automaton supplies a hereditary constructional core: a self-description together with a constructor, a copier, and a controller. Dalek combines this core with the four obligations and rederives its medium for a text-and-message agent substrate, adding explicit structures for boundary, identity, history, and growth. A large language model and a compiler occupy the payload position and form a general capability producer. New capabilities are authored, compiled, installed into the description, and inherited by descendants. The same path produces the machine's own organs and even its runtime, closing heredity and evolution within the machine.

</details>


### [136] [GPS-Bench: A Governance Policy Benchmark for Automating Policy Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03553)
*Linh Le,Melanie Bui,My Chiffon Nguyen,Zachary Schlosser,David Williams-King*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPS-Bench is an evidence-grounded benchmark for governance policy simulation that links policies to actors, actions, and downstream impacts using public records. It enables controlled comparisons of different reasoning approaches (joint reasoning, independent/communicating agents, graph-based methods, fine-tuning) on the same grounded state, finding that fine-tuning outperforms decomposition at prediction while decomposition adds interpretability about mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based policy simulations lack validity checks when plausible behavior is never compared with observed outcomes. Existing approaches cannot determine whether multi-agent simulation actually improves prediction and interpretation of policy outcomes.

Method: GPS-Bench constructs actor personas from dated legislative records rather than prompting archetypes, creating evidence objects with provenance. It uses Gold (human-annotated) and Silver (LLM-labeled from retrieved evidence) supervision sets. The benchmark compares reasoning modes over identical grounded state with same output schema, testing fine-tuning, joint reasoning, independent/communicating agents, and graph-based methods.

Result: Fine-tuning on the grounded record achieves strongest actor-level impact prediction. Decomposition does not outperform fine-tuning at prediction; its value lies in providing mechanistic interpretation. Agents with private, non-identical evidence can form coalitions that can be verified against recorded commitments.

Conclusion: GPS-Bench provides a common empirical setting for studying when evidence, actor modeling, and multi-agent interaction improve policy outcome prediction and interpretation. The benchmark enables rigorous comparison of different reasoning approaches under controlled conditions.

Abstract: Policy analysis requires more than predicting whether a proposal will pass: it requires identifying who will be affected, how those actors respond, and what follows. LLM-based policy simulations model these processes at scale, but their validity is hard to establish when plausible behaviour is never compared with observed outcomes. We introduce GPS-Bench, an evidence-grounded benchmark for governance policy simulation that links policies to relevant actors, actor actions and downstream impacts using legislative records, lobbying disclosures, regulatory documents, corporate filings, economic data and other public evidence. Actors are reconstructed from the dated record rather than prompted as archetypes, so a persona is an evidence object with provenance; a human-annotated pool forms the Gold evaluation set, while cases labelled by a separate LLM from retrieved evidence are treated as Silver supervision and never as test labels. Because every inference mode reads the same grounded state and emits the same schema, GPS-Bench turns "does multi-agent simulation help?" into a controlled comparison: we contrast joint reasoning, independent and communicating actor agents, graph-based methods and weight-level fine-tuning over one policy state. Fine-tuning on the grounded record gives the strongest actor-level impact prediction, and decomposition does not beat it; what decomposition adds is mechanism. Agents hold private, non-identical evidence, each seeing its own exposure clause, and address named partners with concrete joint proposals, what they offer, what they need in return, and why acting together beats acting alone, so the coalitions that form can be checked against the commitments the record holds. GPS-Bench therefore gives a common empirical setting for studying when evidence, actor modelling and multi-agent interaction improve the prediction and interpretation of policy outcomes.

</details>


### [137] [HalluPeer: A Taxonomy-driven Benchmark for Detecting Hallucinations in Scientific Peer Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03580)
*Tzu-Ling Lin,Dong-Ting Yao,Teng-Fang Hsiao,Wei-Chih Chen,Hong-Han Shuai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HalluPeer is a benchmark for detecting hallucinations in scientific peer reviews, addressing the risk that LLMs may generate fluent but unsupported claims. It provides aligned triples of paper content, human-written reviews, and hallucination-injected reviews, with annotations for detection, classification, and localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing scale of academic peer review has motivated the use of LLMs as review assistants, but LLMs can generate fluent but unsupported claims that undermine review reliability. Existing hallucination benchmarks are not designed for peer review, where verification requires grounding claims in long, technical papers.

Method: The pipeline induces a peer-review-specific hallucination taxonomy, identifies review contexts, and injects hallucinations with automated filtering. Experiments were conducted on 12K papers and 38K reviews.

Result: Existing detectors struggle to separate hallucinations from legitimate critique. Evaluation on authentic reviews demonstrates that HalluPeer-defined hallucination patterns occur in real peer reviews.

Conclusion: There is a critical need for source-aware verification in peer review. The benchmark highlights the importance of grounding claims in paper content to detect hallucinations.

Abstract: The growing scale of academic peer review has motivated the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as review assistants, yet LLMs can generate fluent but unsupported claims that undermine review reliability. Existing hallucination benchmarks are not designed for peer review, where verification requires grounding claims in long, technical papers. We introduce HalluPeer, a benchmark for detecting hallucinations in scientific peer reviews, providing aligned triples of paper content, human-written reviews, and hallucination-injected reviews, annotated for detection, classification, and localization. Our pipeline induces a peer-review-specific hallucination taxonomy, identifies review contexts, and injects hallucinations with automated filtering. Experiments on 12K papers and 38K reviews show that existing detectors struggle to separate hallucinations from legitimate critique, while evaluation on authentic reviews demonstrates that HalluPeer-defined hallucination patterns occur in real peer reviews, highlighting the critical need for source-aware verification. Our project page can be found in https://github.com/Lin-TzuLing/HalluPeer.git

</details>


### [138] [The Attention Triangle in Audio-Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03586)
*Sagi Polaczek,Noa Kraicer,Gal Metzer,Zhuo Ning,Ali Mahdavi-Amiri,Daniel Cohen-Or,Raja Giryes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates semantic leakage in audio-video diffusion models caused by cross-modal attention mechanisms. It introduces the "attention triangle" concept to analyze how semantic information flows between text, audio, and video modalities, revealing that the audio-video edge is bidirectional and biased, leading to incorrect semantic routing when prompts conflict with learned priors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-video diffusion models use cross-modal attention to coordinate text, sound, and visual content, but this mechanism can introduce semantic leakage where the intended conditioning is overridden by model priors, leading to incorrect outputs.

Method: The authors analyze the "attention triangle" - three cross-attention edges connecting text, audio, and video streams. They extract attention-derived signals to diagnose how semantics are distributed across modalities and use these signals to both analyze and deliberately induce leakage under controlled conditions. They also propose inference-time interventions to improve cross-modal alignment.

Result: The audio-video edge is bidirectional and shaped by biases encoded in the model's parameters. This edge is a major contributor to semantic leakage - when prompts conflict with learned priors, cross-modal interactions override intended conditioning and route semantics toward visually canonical but incorrect outcomes.

Conclusion: Semantic artifacts arise from structured, bias-driven interactions along specific attention pathways, not merely from attention spreading beyond its target. Attention-derived signals can serve as diagnostic tools to analyze internal dynamics of cross-modal routing and enable inference-time interventions that improve semantic grounding while preserving generation quality.

Abstract: Audio-video diffusion models rely on cross-modal attention to coordinate text, sound, and visual content, yet this same mechanism can introduce subtle and systematic semantic leakage. We study these models by probing and analyzing the ``attention triangle,'' comprising the three cross-attention edges connecting the text, audio, and video streams, and examine how semantic information is routed across modalities during generation. Our analysis reveals that routing along the audio-video edge is bidirectional: audio can influence video generation, while video can influence audio generation. This edge is shaped by biases encoded in the model's parameters and emerges as a major contributor to leakage: when prompts are in tension with learned priors, cross-modal interactions may override the intended conditioning and reroute semantics toward visually canonical but incorrect outcomes. These effects suggest that semantic artifacts arise not merely from attention spreading beyond its intended target, but from structured, bias-driven interactions along specific pathways. Building on this perspective, we extract attention-derived signals that expose how semantics are distributed and grounded across modalities, and use them as a diagnostic tool to both analyze and deliberately incur leakage under controlled conditions. This enables us to probe the internal dynamics of cross-modal routing and isolate the role of individual interactions. We further leverage these signals to guide inference-time interventions that encourage more consistent cross-modal alignment. Extensive experiments support our analysis and demonstrate improved semantic grounding while preserving generation quality.

</details>


### [139] [KC-Bench: A Dynamic Interactive Benchmark for Evaluating Knowledge Conflicts in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03588)
*Yaxing Lyu,Shengjie Zhou,Binbin Toh,Pengyu Zhu,Lijun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KC-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to handle conflicts between user instructions, parametric knowledge, and environmental observations when acting through tools. It tests 238 tasks across world-knowledge conflicts, input inconsistencies, and temporal conflicts. Evaluation of nine models reveals significant limitations—no model reliably handles all conflict types, with missed conflicts potentially propagating to tool calls or data flows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly operate through tools, they must reconcile conflicting information from multiple sources (user instructions, parametric knowledge, and dynamic environmental observations). However, there was no benchmark to systematically evaluate this capability, making it difficult to diagnose and improve LLM conflict-awareness in agentic scenarios.

Method: KC-Bench is a controlled multi-turn benchmark with 238 manually screened tasks from 1,000+ candidates. It combines: (1) user simulator, (2) stateful tools, (3) deterministic environment assertions, (4) open-source NLU evaluator, and (5) human trajectory verification. It evaluates nine models including DeepSeek-V4-Flash, GLM-5.2, and MiniMax-M3 across three conflict types: world-knowledge conflicts, input inconsistencies, and multi-source temporal conflicts.

Result: All evaluated models show substantial cross-domain variation. No model handles factual correction, identity consistency checking, and temporal conflict resolution reliably across all settings. Missed conflicts can propagate to tool calls or synthetic protected-data flows in simulated environments.

Conclusion: KC-Bench provides a reproducible diagnostic tool for evaluating LLM conflict-awareness in tool-use scenarios. It isolates model-level behavior rather than ranking complete agent frameworks, enabling targeted development of conflict-aware reasoning and execution safeguards. The benchmark reveals significant room for improvement in LLM conflict resolution capabilities.

Abstract: As LLMs increasingly act through tools, they must reconcile user instructions, parametric knowledge, and dynamic environmental observations before taking actions. We introduce KC-Bench, a controlled multi-turn benchmark for measuring this capability across world-knowledge conflicts, input inconsistencies, and multi-source temporal conflicts. Its 238 tasks are manually screened from more than 1,000 generated candidates and combine a user simulator, stateful tools, deterministic environment assertions, an open-source natural-language evaluator, and human trajectory verification. Evaluation of nine models, including DeepSeek-V4-Flash, GLM-5.2, and MiniMax-M3, shows substantial cross-domain variation: no model handles factual correction, identity consistency checking, and temporal conflict resolution reliably across all settings. In the simulated environments, missed conflicts can propagate to tool calls or synthetic protected-data flows. KC-Bench isolates this model-level behavior rather than ranking complete agent frameworks, and provides a reproducible diagnostic for developing conflict-aware reasoning and execution safeguards.

</details>


### [140] [A computable representation of the physical laboratory enables verifiable workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03621)
*Xiaobo Li,Luyao Ge,Xiaohui Li,Lulu Guo,Ming Mao,Jiwang Zheng,Wenting Guan,Xin Yang,Yi Luo,Jun Jiang,Linjiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a computable representation framework for physical laboratories that enables autonomous scientific discovery by combining typed research objects, capability-bound operations, and a compositional workflow algebra to bridge machine-readable knowledge with physical experimental execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Making science computable requires not only representing scientific knowledge but also representing the physical world where scientific claims are tested. Current approaches lack a framework that connects agent reasoning with capability-bound physical transformations in a generalizable way.

Method: The framework establishes a computable representation through three core components: (1) typed research objects, (2) capability-bound operations, and (3) a compositional workflow algebra. This was implemented in a modular agentic robotic laboratory by binding formal operations to executable Function Skills. Capability-relative workflows were generated for diverse scientific intents, while stateful simulation propagated object transformations and verified operation preconditions and laboratory constraints before dispatch.

Result: The system successfully generated capability-relative workflows for diverse scientific intents, with stateful simulation verifying operation preconditions and laboratory constraints before execution. The implementation demonstrated a functional computational interface between agent reasoning and physical laboratory operations.

Conclusion: The proposed representation and its engineering framework jointly establish a general computational interface between agent reasoning and capability-bound physical transformations, providing a foundation for end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery.

Abstract: Making science computable requires representations of both scientific knowledge and the physical world in which scientific claims are tested. A computable representation of the physical laboratory is established through typed research objects, capability-bound operations and a compositional workflow algebra. It provides the physical-world counterpart to machine-readable knowledge, expressing workflows as programs over evolving laboratory states with explicit dependencies, decisions, iteration and concurrency. The representation was implemented in a modular agentic robotic laboratory by binding formal operations to executable Function Skills. For diverse scientific intents, capability-relative workflows were generated, while stateful simulation propagated object transformations and verified operation preconditions and laboratory constraints before dispatch. The proposed representation and its engineering framework jointly establish a general computational interface between agent reasoning and capability-bound physical transformations, providing a foundation for end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery.

</details>


### [141] [Analysis of Prompt Engineering for Drug Toxicity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03635)
*Mia MacGregor,Aakash Welgamage Don,Mark Bartlett*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates prompt engineering for drug toxicity prediction using LLMs. The authors found that natural variance in LLM outputs outweighs any benefits from prompt optimization, but using chemoinformatic code for feature extraction significantly improves model performance compared to LLM-generated values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical trials in the UK cost up to £1.3 million with ~90% drug failure rate, primarily due to toxicity. AI/LLMs are increasingly used for drug toxicity prediction, but LLMs show considerable variation with minor prompt changes, raising concerns about sensitivity to prompt engineering.
