The motivation is to investigate the importance of prompt phrasing for drug toxicity prediction and develop an analysis methodology for prompt engineering in bioinformatics.

Method: The authors constructed prompts to investigate job role, prompt structuring, and rule interpretation. LLMs were prompted to identify chemical properties significant for predicting drug toxicity. Generated datasets using identified features were then passed to machine learning algorithms for toxicity prediction.

Result: The experiments revealed that natural variance occurring in LLMs outweighs any fine-tuning of prompts. However, substantial improvements in model performance were observed when using chemoinformatic code to extract features instead of using LLM-generated values.

Conclusion: The natural variance in LLM outputs is too large to benefit from prompt optimization for drug toxicity prediction. The proposed analysis methodology is applicable to a wide range of prompt types across different areas of bioinformatics. Using domain-specific chemoinformatic tools rather than LLM-generated features yields significantly better results.

Abstract: Clinical trials in the UK can cost up to £1.3 million, with approximately 90% drug failure rate. Toxicity is a major contributing factor in drug failure. Testing is time and cost intensive. In recent years, the use of artificial intelligence has been increasingly explored to aid in the prediction of drug toxicity, with extensive use of large language models (LLMs). However, LLMs can show considerable variation when minor changes are made to prompts, which raises concerns about their sensitivity to prompt engineering. Prompt engineering is used to optimise a prompt given to an LLM to generate the desired output. This paper proposes a method to analyse prompt engineering for drug toxicity prediction. The aim of the paper is to investigate the importance of prompt phrasing for drug toxicity prediction. LLMs were prompted to identify chemical properties of significance when predicting drug toxicity. Prompts were constructed to investigate; job role, prompt structuring, and rule interpretation. LLMs were then used to generate datasets, using the identified features from initial prompting, which were then passed to machine learning algorithms. The experiments show that the natural variance which occurs in LLMs outweighs any fine-tuning of prompts. There were, however, substantial improvements in model performance when using chemoinformatic code to extract features instead of using LLM-generated values. The proposed analysis methodology is applicable to a wide range of prompt types across different areas of bioinformatics.

</details>


### [142] [Synthetic Semantic Supervision for Contrastive Code Representation Learning in Small Transformers: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03702)
*Kenneth Paulsen,Florian Tambon,Mike Papadakis,Shin Yoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes using synthetic natural-language descriptions generated by AI to train compact transformer encoders for code representation, using contrastive learning in a dual-encoder framework. The approach achieves competitive results against much larger models while being more scalable than human annotations or execution traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code embedding methods have limitations: human-written docstrings are labor-intensive and inconsistent, while execution traces are setting-specific and costly to collect. The authors seek a more scalable and effective alternative for training compact code encoders.

Method: The authors use contrastive pretraining with synthetically generated natural-language descriptions that emphasize code functionality and intent. These descriptions are paired with code in a dual-encoder framework during training but discarded at inference time. The approach is benchmarked against pretraining baselines, generalist LLMs, and embedding-specific models on eight tasks across C, C++, and Java.

Result: Synthetic semantic supervision yields statistically significant gains over pretraining baselines of the same inference-time size on five of eight tasks, with parity on two more. Once fine-tuned, it matches or exceeds zero-shot models two orders of magnitude larger on classification tasks, and stays on par with execution-aware supervision at matched pretraining data.

Conclusion: Synthetic semantic supervision via contrastive pretraining offers a scalable, effective alternative to existing code-representation paradigms, achieving comparable performance to execution-aware methods while being more practical to implement.

Abstract: General-purpose code embeddings power tools for code search, classification, and retrieval. Compact transformer encoders for code typically rely on either human-written docstrings (labor-intensive and inconsistent) or mined structural signals such as execution traces (setting-specific and costly to collect). We empirically study an alternative: contrastive pretraining of small encoders with synthetically generated natural-language descriptions emphasizing code functionality and intent, paired with code in a dual-encoder framework at training and discarded at inference. We benchmark this approach against pretraining-based baselines, generalist LLMs, and embedding-specific models on eight retrieval, classification, and generation tasks across C, C++, and Java. Synthetic semantic supervision yields statistically significant gains over pretraining baselines of the same inference-time size on five of eight tasks, with parity on two more; once fine-tuned, it matches or exceeds zero-shot models two orders of magnitude larger on classification, and it stays on par with execution-aware supervision at matched pretraining data, suggesting a scalable, effective alternative to existing code-representation paradigms.

</details>


### [143] [Counterfactual Routing Using Integer Programming with Constraint Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03707)
*Daniël Vos,Sterre Lutz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a solution to the IJCAI 2025 Counterfactual Routing Competition, which aims to find minimal changes to a road network that would make a user's chosen route the optimal route. The method uses integer programming with iterative constraint incorporation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The competition addresses the need for explainable routing recommendations. Counterfactual explanations help users understand why their preferred route was or was not optimal, enabling explanations like "Your suggested route would have been optimal if road X were not a bicycle path." This is important for transparency and user trust in navigation systems.

Method: The solution models the counterfactual routing problem as an integer program, iteratively incorporating constraints until an exact solution is found. This approach balances solution quality with computational efficiency.

Result: The method ranked fourth in solution quality on held-out test instances. However, it achieved the fastest solution time on every instance, with an average runtime of 9.0 seconds compared to 118.8 seconds for the next-fastest submission.

Conclusion: The integer programming approach with iterative constraint incorporation provides an efficient exact solution method for counterfactual routing explanations, achieving the best runtime performance while maintaining competitive solution quality.

Abstract: We present our submission to the IJCAI 2025 'Counterfactual Routing Competition' (CRC 25). The goal of the competition is to find counterfactual explanations for the shortest path problem. This requires deciding what the minimal changes to a road network would make a route chosen by the user the optimal route. This enables explanations such as "Your suggested route would indeed have been optimal, if road X were not a bicycle path." Our solution models the problem as an integer program, iteratively incorporating constraints until an exact solution is found. In the final evaluation on held-out test instances, our method ranked fourth in solution quality and obtained its solution fastest on every instance, with an average runtime of 9.0 seconds compared to 118.8 seconds for the next-fastest submission.

</details>


### [144] [Artificial Intelligence for Energy Optimization in Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03716)
*Mohammed Basharath Ullah,Summaiya Unnisa Begum,Mohammed Nadeem Ullah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic literature review of ~194 papers (63 coded) on AI-driven energy optimization in data centers. The authors identify a critical gap: control studies and sustainability studies treat workload and infrastructure as separate problems. They find that no primary studies account for water withdrawal or embodied carbon, and reported savings across techniques overlap significantly, preventing ranking. The paper proposes CLEAR-DC, a framework coupling control-policy and workload-demand branches with explicit elasticity terms, and introduces a standardized reporting schema.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature treats AI optimization of data centers and AI's increasing load as two unrelated problems. Control studies model workload as exogenous arrival processes, while sustainability studies model infrastructure as fixed multipliers. This disconnect creates a critical gap in understanding the net impact of AI optimization approaches.

Method: Systematic literature review with documented protocol: screened ~194 papers, coded 63 papers, analyzed 28 primary control-oriented studies. Assessed validation methods (simulation vs. physical hardware), environmental metrics (energy, carbon, water, embodied carbon), and reported savings intervals across four technique families.

Result: Key findings: (1) 18/28 control studies validated in simulation alone, only 5 reached physical hardware/production; (2) Zero studies account for water withdrawal or embodied carbon; (3) Reported savings intervals across four technique families overlap almost completely, preventing ranking of methods; (4) Ten recurring gaps identified and scored for consequence and tractability.

Conclusion: The field cannot presently rank its own methods due to overlapping savings intervals. The authors propose CLEAR-DC framework as an architectural and methodological proposal, coupling control-policy and workload-demand branches through explicit elasticity terms, reading out net rather than direct benefit, and emitting schema-conformant records covering energy, carbon, water, embodied share, and validation venue. The empirical contribution is the corpus analysis and derived reporting schema.

Abstract: Data centers are increasingly optimized by artificial intelligence and, at the same time, increasingly loaded by it. The literature treats these as two unrelated problems: control studies model workload as an exogenous arrival process, while sustainability studies model infrastructure as a fixed multiplier. We screen roughly 194 papers retrieved through a documented protocol, code 63 of them, and report what the coding shows. Of 28 primary control-oriented studies, 18 are validated in simulation alone and 5 reach physical hardware or a production facility; none account for water withdrawal, and none account for embodied carbon. Reported savings intervals across four technique families overlap almost completely, which means the field cannot presently rank its own methods. Ten recurring gaps are scored for consequence and tractability, and we set out CLEAR-DC, a framework coupling a control-policy branch to a workload-demand branch through an explicit elasticity term, reads out net rather than direct benefit, and emits a schema-conformant record covering energy, carbon, water, embodied share and validation venue. The framework is an architectural and methodological proposal, not a trained system; the contribution we defend empirically is the corpus analysis and the reporting schema derived from it. Coding sheet, derived statistics and all result artifacts: https://github.com/Kimalice/AI-for-Energy-Optimization-in-Data-Centers-Closing-the-Optimizer-Load-Loop

</details>


### [145] [Proactive Service Agents: A Unified Decision Framework, Methods, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03727)
*Yan Tang,Tingyu Cao,Yuanbo Tang,Huaze Tang,Keer Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey defines proactive service in LLM agents as a sequential decision process where agents must infer opportunities from incomplete signals, choose between silent/asking/assisting/acting, and account for various costs. The authors formulate this as a partially observable decision process and organize methods along a decision pipeline (state estimation, intervention gating, action construction, feedback adaptation).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent systems rely on explicit user instructions as starting points. Proactive service moves decision-making upstream—agents must infer service opportunities from incomplete environmental and user signals, requiring them to decide when and how to intervene without explicit prompts.

Method: The paper provides an operational definition centered on initiative and formulates the problem as a partially observable sequential decision process (POMDP) constrained by authorization and risk. Methods are organized along a decision pipeline: state/need estimation, intervention gating, action construction, and feedback adaptation. Mechanisms include prescribed, predictive, model-based, and return-optimizing approaches.

Result: The synthesis reveals that offline classification performance alone does not predict deployment benefit, and long-term memory is not a defining condition of proactivity. Reliable proactive service requires calibrated incremental intervention value, verifiable authorization, recoverable execution, and counterfactual evidence.

Conclusion: Proactive service in LLM agents requires a shift from explicit instruction-following to inference-based intervention. Key requirements include: (1) calibrated incremental intervention value, (2) verifiable authorization, (3) recoverable execution, and (4) counterfactual evidence. The paper normalizes decision units and metrics across dialogue, screen, video, software engineering, and human-agent collaboration domains.

Abstract: Large language model agents can plan, invoke tools, and modify external states, yet most systems still take an explicit user instruction as a fixed starting point. Proactive service moves the decision upstream: an agent must infer service opportunities from incomplete environmental and user signals, choose among remaining silent, asking, assisting, and acting, and account for interruption, misunderstanding, overreach, and privacy costs. This survey gives an operational definition centered on initiative and formulates the problem as a partially observable sequential decision process constrained by authorization and risk. The formulation represents timing, content, and delivery within one structured action, while making explicit the option value of waiting, the decision value of questions, and feedback-induced state changes. On this basis, we organize existing methods along one decision pipeline (state and need estimation, intervention gating, action construction, and feedback adaptation) and describe prescribed, predictive, model based, and return optimizing mechanisms as nonexclusive policy-construction components. We further normalize decision units and three-axis evidence descriptors across streaming dialogue, screen, video, software-engineering, and human-agent collaboration resources, and formalize metrics for triggering, timing, calibration, user burden, safety, and policy value. The synthesis shows why offline classification performance alone does not predict deployment benefit and why long-term memory is not a defining condition of proactivity. Reliable proactive service instead requires calibrated incremental intervention value, verifiable authorization, recoverable execution, and counterfactual evidence.

</details>


### [146] [SimSkill: A Lifelong Learning AI Agent for Autonomous Mastery of Traffic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03753)
*Qi Liu,Qinzheng Wang,Yiming Bie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimSkill is a self-evolving AI agent that uses the SUMO traffic simulator to autonomously build reusable capabilities by identifying gaps, generating tasks, verifying solutions through action-critic loops, and consolidating experience into episodic, procedural, and semantic memory without updating the backbone model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become more capable, their long-term value depends on transforming experience into durable, reusable competence beyond solving individual requests. Current approaches lack mechanisms for autonomous capability building and memory consolidation in grounded environments.

Method: SimSkill identifies capability gaps, generates and solves environment-grounded tasks using SUMO, verifies solutions through an action-critic loop, and consolidates experience into three memory types (episodic, procedural, semantic) without updating the backbone model. Evaluated on two held-out benchmarks with three backbone LLMs and independent artifact-based verification.

Result: SimSkill improves verified completion by up to 25 percentage points. Ablations show complementary contributions from procedural and semantic memory. However, benefits are backbone- and budget-dependent: memory does not improve every model or uniformly reduce inference cost.

Conclusion: SimSkill demonstrates a design paradigm where natural language preserves computational capabilities while executable tools provide precise execution. This enables self-evolving agents that can build reusable libraries spanning complex workflows without model updates.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly capable, the long-term value of AI systems depends not only on solving individual requests, but also on transforming experience and accumulated knowledge into durable, reusable competence. We introduce SimSkill, a self-evolving agent built around the Simulation of Urban MObility (SUMO) traffic simulator. SimSkill identifies capability gaps, generates and solves environment-grounded tasks, verifies solutions through an action--critic loop, and consolidates experience into episodic, procedural, and semantic memory without updating the backbone model. Through autonomous exploration, it builds a reusable library spanning the traffic-simulation workflow. We evaluate SimSkill on two held-out benchmarks with three backbone LLMs and independent artifact-based verification. SimSkill improves verified completion by up to 25 percentage points, while ablations show complementary contributions from procedural and semantic memory. Its benefits remain backbone- and budget-dependent: memory does not improve every model or uniformly reduce inference cost. More broadly, SimSkill illustrates a design paradigm in which natural language preserves and composes computational capabilities, while executable tools and code provide precise and reproducible execution. All code and experimental data are publicly available at https://github.com/qiliuchn/SimSkill-V1.

</details>


### [147] [Rethinking World Models for Safety-Critical Embodied Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03774)
*Kailang Ma,Heye Huang,Inhi Kim,Kitae Jang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This perspective paper argues that current world models for embodied AI prioritize predictive accuracy over safety-relevant decision-making. The authors propose a Risk-Informed World Model (RIWM) framework organized around consequences, intervention, epistemic uncertainty, and recoverability, with four key capabilities: decision-relevant representation, counterfactual reasoning, safety-critical episodic memory, and runtime safety assurance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world models focus on predictive likelihood and visual fidelity, but these metrics do not ensure preservation of evidence needed for safe decision-making in safety-critical embodied systems. This creates a fundamental mismatch between what world models optimize for and what safety-critical applications require.

Method: The paper presents a conceptual framework (RIWM) that reorganizes world modeling around four interdependent capabilities: (1) decision-relevant representation, (2) counterfactual reasoning, (3) safety-critical episodic memory, and (4) runtime safety assurance. It distinguishes physical, social, and operational consequences while using epistemic uncertainty to qualify evidence supporting actions.

Result: The paper identifies three structural mismatches in current world modeling: likelihood versus risk, prediction versus intervention, and finite-horizon prediction versus accumulated consequences. It proposes five open challenges: identifying consequential futures, validating counterfactual reasoning, maintaining revisable safety memories, translating learned consequences into executable constraints, and determining when evidence is sufficient to act.

Conclusion: Future world models should move beyond predicting likely futures toward identifying which futures matter, revising judgments through experience, and recognizing when to act, revise, sense, defer, or abstain. The RIWM framework provides a decision-centric research direction for safety-critical embodied systems.

Abstract: World models have progressed from compact latent dynamics to generative, controllable, and interactive simulators of embodied environments. However, high predictive likelihood and visual fidelity do not necessarily ensure that a model preserves the evidence required for safe decision-making. This perspective identifies three structural mismatches in current world modeling: likelihood versus risk, prediction versus intervention, and finite-horizon prediction versus accumulated consequences. We propose the Risk-Informed World Model (RIWM) as a decision-centric research direction for safety-critical embodied systems. RIWM organizes world modeling around consequences, intervention, epistemic uncertainty, and recoverability, and integrates four interdependent capabilities: decision-relevant representation, counterfactual reasoning, safety-critical episodic memory, and runtime safety assurance. It distinguishes physical, social, and operational consequences while using epistemic uncertainty to qualify the evidence supporting action. We further discuss open challenges in identifying consequential futures, validating counterfactual reasoning, maintaining revisable safety memories, translating learned consequences into executable constraints, and determining when evidence is sufficient to act. This perspective argues that future world models should move beyond predicting likely futures toward identifying which futures matter, revising judgments through experience, and recognizing when to act, revise, sense, defer, or abstain.

</details>


### [148] [DNative-Twin: Decision Graphs and Digital Twins for Reconstructable Agentic Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03787)
*Junjie Pang,Zhenzhen Xie,Haoke Han,Ying He,Jing Wang,Gang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DNative-Twin is a graph-native digital twin framework that records AI agent decisions as typed trajectories and re-executes them under declared conditions to explain how decisions were made. It links observed state, action path, and authority, then replays and compares under controlled changes. Experiments on enterprise process logs show it can detect decision failures with high recall (up to 1.0) when combining graph structure, replay context, and verification evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents increasingly make decisions that people or software act on, but final outputs alone cannot show which evidence, tool state, rule, authorization, or action path produced them. This lack of traceability creates accountability and explainability issues in enterprise decision processes.

Method: The paper presents DNative-Twin, a graph-native digital twin that: (1) records committed agentic decisions as typed trajectories in a graph structure linking observed state, decision path, and authority; (2) synchronizes this information and replays the decision mechanism in isolation; (3) compares outcomes under controlled changes. Experiments used three public process logs and controlled replay suites, including a three-condition controlled experiment with 300 injected instances and 500-5,000 BPI 2020 cases.

Result: The key finding identifies a specific failure: graph structure can localize represented changes but cannot determine consequences of unobserved tool state. In the three-condition controlled experiment: (1) baseline (graph structure only): unresolved-divergence recall = 0; (2) with replay-contract state added: recall = 0.667; (3) with verification results also available: recall = 1.0. Across 500-5,000 BPI 2020 cases, median end-to-end time increased from 0.794 to 8.889 seconds.

Conclusion: The results successfully separate the roles of graph structure, replay context, and verification evidence in reviewing decision mechanisms. Graph structure alone is insufficient; combining all three components achieves perfect recall (1.0) in detecting decision failures. The approach adds computational overhead but enables comprehensive decision auditability in enterprise contexts.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly gather evidence, invoke tools, apply constraints, and produce decisions that people or software may commit to action. A final output alone cannot show which evidence, tool state, rule, authorization, or action path produced it. We present DNative-Twin, a graph-native digital twin that records a committed agentic decision as a typed trajectory and re-executes its decision mechanism under declared conditions. The graph links the state observed by the agent, the path it followed, and the authority behind the resulting action. The twin synchronizes this information, replays the mechanism in isolation, and compares it under controlled changes. We instantiate the framework in enterprise decision processes using three public process logs and controlled replay suites. The experiments identify a specific failure: graph structure localizes represented changes but cannot determine the consequence of an unobserved tool state. In a three-condition controlled experiment with 300 injected instances, unresolved-divergence recall increased from 0 to 0.667 when replay-contract state was added and to 1.0 when verification results were also available; the held-out set contained no critical-class instance. Across 500--5,000 BPI 2020 cases, median end-to-end time increased from 0.794 to 8.889 seconds on the reported platform. These results separate the roles of graph structure, replay context, and verification evidence in reviewing a decision mechanism.

</details>


### [149] [Transfiver: Human-AI Co-Inference through a Shared Editable State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03797)
*Minji Park,Seunghyun Yoon,Hyuk Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transfiver is a framework for human-AI co-inference that uses a shared editable persistent state (S_t) which both the AI model and human users can update. It distinguishes between implicit stream updates (where the model decides whether to revise or create new state items) and explicit directed edits (where humans inspect and modify state items).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term human-AI interaction is difficult because the information guiding inference is updated implicitly by the model and is not directly inspectable or controllable by users. This lack of transparency and controllability creates a gap between model internal state and user understanding.

Method: The architecture separates pre-trained shared parameters (θ) from a persistent state (S_t) that evolves during deployment. Two update modes are implemented: (1) implicit stream updates where the model interprets interactions and decides whether to revise existing state or create new entries, and (2) explicit directed edits where humans inspect and modify addressed state items directly.

Result: The framework enables human corrections to modify the state that subsequent computation reads, rather than just adding separate instructions. This creates a shared editable state where both model and human updates act on the same underlying data structure.

Conclusion: Transfiver provides a foundation for verifiable and editable human-AI interaction through shared persistent states. However, extending the framework to rich natural-language, relational, and large-scale shared states remains an open challenge for future research.

Abstract: Long-term human-AI interaction is difficult because the information that guides inference is updated implicitly by the model and is not directly inspectable or controllable by the user. We introduce the TRANSparent Framework for Interactive, Verifiable, Editable Representation (Transfiver), an architecture for human-AI co-inference through a shared editable state. Its central idea is that interaction-specific information is maintained in a single persistent state $(S_t)$ that both the model and the human update.
  Transfiver distinguishes two modes of state evolution. In an implicit stream update, the model interprets ongoing interaction and decides whether new information revises an existing state item or creates a new one. In an explicit directed edit, a human inspects and modifies an addressed item. Both act on the same underlying state, so a human correction changes the state that subsequent computation reads, rather than adding another instruction or separate record.
  The architecture separates shared parameters $(θ)$, learned before ordinary use, from the persistent state $(S_t)$, which evolves during deployment without parameter retraining. Extending Transfiver to rich natural-language, relational, and large-scale shared states remains open.

</details>


### [150] [Govern the Model, Not Only the Data: Storage, Circulation, and Learning in Creative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03800)
*Phoenix Perry,George Simms,Elizabeth Wilson,Yasmine Boudiaf,Nick Bryan-Kinns,Tega Brain,R. Luke DuBois,Alix Rule,Rachel Meade Smith,Kelani Nichole,Atharva Pravin Pawar,Rebecca Fiebrink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that federated learning is not inherently privacy-preserving or non-extractive—the outcomes depend on who governs the data and models. The authors propose a creative data commons framework with four design principles to enable creator governance over models and their federation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning is presented as a privacy-preserving advance where personal data remains on devices, but it inverts the federated social web logic by distributing computation while keeping the resulting model with whoever convened the training. The authors argue federation alone doesn't remedy extractive AI because outcomes depend on governance structures.

Method: The authors examine three layers (storage, circulation, learning) through case studies of artist-governed trusts, cooperatives, and consent infrastructures to understand where creator governance can be established.

Result: Creator governance is established at storage and circulation layers but stops at learning: contributors can consent to training but have little say over the resulting model or its federation. The paper maps technical open problems paired with human questions they unfold from.

Conclusion: Four design principles for a creative data commons that governs models and their federation: (1) govern the model, not only the corpus; (2) make the terms legible at the moment of contribution; (3) design for refusal as a first-class state; (4) decide stewardship in the open and account for it.

Abstract: Federated learning is increasingly presented as a privacy-preserving advance: personal data remain on the device, and only model updates are shared. It borrows the vocabulary of the federated social web, yet inverts its logic, distributing computation while the resulting model stays with whoever convened the training. We argue that federation is not in itself a remedy for extractive AI, because outcomes depend on who governs the data and the model and who has agency over the practices that shape them. We describe three layers at which a creative community can hold its work: storage, circulation, and learning. Examining artist-governed trusts, cooperatives, and consent infrastructures, we show that creator governance is established at storage and circulation but stops at learning: contributors can consent to training, yet have little say over the resulting model or its federation. We map the research space this opens, pairing technical open problems with the human questions from which they unfold. We propose four design principles for a creative data commons that governs models and their federation, not only datasets: govern the model, not only the corpus; make the terms legible at the moment of contribution; design for refusal as a first-class state; and decide stewardship in the open and account for it.

</details>


### [151] [SVG-Score: Human-Aligned Evaluation of Text-to-SVG Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03806)
*Marco Cipriano,Leonardo Zini,Alexandra Schild,Valentin Teutschbein,Afsana Mimi,Marcella Cornia,Lorenzo Baraldi,Gerard de Melo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a human-aligned evaluation framework for text-to-SVG generation, addressing the limitation that existing metrics like CLIPScore were designed for natural images and poorly evaluate vector graphics. The authors created a human-annotated dataset for Semantic Alignment and developed two complementary evaluators: aligned CLIP scorers and a supervised fine-tuned VLM judge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation protocols for text-to-SVG generation rely on metrics designed for natural images (e.g., CLIPScore), which were never trained on vector graphics and align only partially with human judgment. This lack of domain-specific evaluation protocols hinders progress in SVG generation research.

Method: The authors conducted controlled caption and image perturbations to analyze how existing metrics (CLIP-based scores and VLM judges) respond to SVG-specific errors (wrong colors, counts, spatial relations). They then built a human-annotated dataset for Semantic Alignment and developed two evaluators: (1) CLIP scorers adapted to vector graphics and aligned to human preferences, and (2) a VLM judge trained with supervised fine-tuning and reward-shaped reinforcement learning.

Result: CLIP-based scores barely react to actual SVG generator errors (wrong colors, counts, spatial relations), while off-the-shelf VLM judges are more sensitive but respond unevenly across error types and SVG styles. The benchmark evaluation of major open-source, commercial, and optimization-based SVG generators revealed these differences.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides both fast large-scale evaluation (via adapted CLIP scorers) and expressive, interpretable assessment (via the trained VLM judge), offering a comprehensive solution for evaluating text-to-SVG generation that better aligns with human judgment.

Abstract: Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) generation is attracting increasing attention as generative models improve in expressiveness and controllability. Progress, however, is held back by the lack of domain-specific evaluation protocols: current practice relies on metrics designed for natural images, most notably CLIPScore, which was never trained on vector graphics and aligns only partially with human judgment. We introduce \textbf{\ours}, a human-aligned evaluation framework for text-to-SVG generation. Through controlled caption and image perturbations, we first show that CLIP-based scores barely react to the errors SVG generators actually make, such as wrong colors, counts, and spatial relations, and that off-the-shelf Vision-Language Model (VLM) judges, while more sensitive, respond unevenly across error types and SVG styles. We then introduce a human-annotated dataset for \textit{Semantic Alignment}, measuring how faithfully a generated SVG reflects its caption. Building on it, we develop two complementary evaluators: CLIP scorers adapted to vector graphics and then aligned to human preferences, for fast large-scale evaluation, and a VLM judge trained with supervised fine-tuning and reward-shaped reinforcement learning, for more expressive and interpretable assessment. Using both, we benchmark major open-source, commercial, and optimization-based SVG generators on an independent caption set.

</details>


### [152] [CauseCollab: Causal Unified and Modality-Agnostic Network for Heterogeneous Collaborative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03818)
*Weize Li,Yang Li,Quan Yuan,Xiaoyuan Fu,Guiyang Luo,Jinglin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CauseCollab is a causal unified network for collaborative perception that addresses semantic inconsistency and error accumulation in heterogeneous multi-agent systems by disentangling semantic factors from modality-specific confounders through causal metric learning, while using a context-guided Unified Converter to ensure cross-modal semantic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative perception enhances environment understanding through multi-agent information sharing, but real-world performance is constrained by heterogeneous sensor modalities and model architectures. Existing two-stage methods map features to a shared protocol space but produce modality-specific pseudo-protocol distributions, causing semantic inconsistency and error accumulation, especially in scenarios with large modality discrepancies.

Method: CauseCollab formulates representation learning in the protocol space from a causal perspective, explicitly disentangling semantic factors from modality-specific statistical confounders via causal metric learning. It uses a context-guided Unified Converter for heterogeneous modalities to ensure cross-modal semantic consistency. New modalities can be integrated by training lightweight adapters with minimal parameters.

Result: CauseCollab achieves state-of-the-art performance on OPV2V and DAIR-V2X datasets, with more significant gains observed in scenarios involving large modality gaps.

Conclusion: The causal approach effectively addresses semantic inconsistency in heterogeneous collaborative perception. The modular adapter design enables scalable integration of new modalities without retraining the entire system.

Abstract: Collaborative perception enhances environment understanding through multi-agent information sharing, but its performance in real-world scenarios is constrained by heterogeneous sensor modalities and model architectures. Recent protocol-based two-stage methods alleviate this problem by mapping heterogeneous features into a shared protocol space; however, independently trained modality-specific converters often generate modality-specific pseudo-protocol distributions, leading to semantic inconsistency and error accumulation, which is particularly pronounced in scenarios with large modality discrepancies. To address this issue, we propose CauseCollab, a causal unified and modality-agnostic network. CauseCollab formulates representation learning in the protocol space from a causal perspective, explicitly disentangling semantic factors from modality-specific statistical confounders via causal metric learning. Meanwhile, CauseCollab adopts context-guided Unified Converter for heterogeneous modalities to ensure cross-modal semantic consistency. In addition, integrating new modalities only requires training adapters with minimal parameters. Extensive experiments on the OPV2V and DAIR-V2X datasets demonstrate that CauseCollab achieves state-of-the-art performance, with more significant gains in scenarios involving large modality gaps.

</details>


### [153] [Semantic Bayesian World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03834)
*Tommaso Soru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This vision paper proposes Semantic Bayesian World Models (SBWMs) to bridge the gap between crisp knowledge graphs and probabilistic reasoning in modern AI systems. The authors envision transforming knowledge graphs from databases of facts into evolving belief fabrics, where ontological axioms constrain priors and Bayesian conditioning updates beliefs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is the mismatch between knowledge graphs (crisp assertions) and foundation models/agents (probabilistic reasoning). Current integration remains a "data-feeding pipeline" rather than a unified reasoning architecture.

Method: The paper proposes: (1) Belief representation over RDF graphs rather than facts, (2) Ontological axioms as priors, (3) Bayesian conditioning for belief updates from observations, (4) Causal interventions via actions, (5) Probabilistic entailment regimes, (6) Semantic calibration layers, and (7) Protocols for exchanging calibrated beliefs among agents.

Result: Four use cases demonstrate gains: (1) Home-security agent distinguishing courier vs. burglar, (2) Actuarial estimation via entailment rather than string frequency, (3) Reliable planning tasks, (4) Estimating quantities not stated in any document.

Conclusion: The community must build: belief annotation over RDF 1.2, probabilistic entailment regimes, semantic calibration layers, and protocols for calibrated belief exchange among unfamiliar agents.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs describe reality in crisp assertions, while the systems now consuming them, foundation models and autonomous agents, reason natively in probabilities. We argue that this mismatch is why the integration of language models and knowledge graphs remains a data-feeding pipeline rather than a unified reasoning architecture. We envision Semantic Bayesian World Models (SBWMs): a Web that describes the world not as a database of facts but as a shared, evolving fabric of beliefs over knowledge graphs, where ontological axioms constrain priors, observations update beliefs by Bayesian conditioning, and actions intervene upon the world. We work through what an agent gains from such a model: a home-security agent deciding whether the figure at the gate is a courier or a burglar, an actuarial estimate aggregated by entailment rather than by string frequency, a planning task that language models reliably fail, and the estimation of quantities that no document has ever stated. We then set out what the community must build to make them possible: belief annotation over RDF~1.2, probabilistic entailment regimes, semantic calibration layers, and protocols by which agents that have never met can exchange, and disagree over, calibrated beliefs.

</details>


### [154] [Adapting to Evolving Requirements: Agentic AI for Retail Supply Chain Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03860)
*Lei Zheng,Liping Yang,Zihao Li,Guodong Lyu,Chaik Ming Koh,Chung-Piaw Teo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A graph-constrained agentic framework that enables LLMs to adapt retail supply chain operations by jointly selecting intervention routes and module-level changes, validated through 100 warehouse requirements across GPT, Qwen, and DeepSeek, achieving 79-83% end-to-end success (up from 72-76%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM methods for supply chain optimization focus on individual models but struggle with heterogeneous decision pipelines where requirements have multiple intervention paths with different downstream effects. Extending these methods to complex, coupled decision modules remains challenging.

Method: Formulated requirement-driven adaptation as joint selection of intervention routes and admissible module-level changes. Proposed a graph-constrained agentic framework with: (1) domain agents exposing admissible reformulation interfaces, (2) a central processor searching over bounded intervention paths, (3) downstream KPI validation and comparison of candidates.

Result: Evaluated on 100 warehouse requirements from practitioner interviews using GPT, Qwen, and DeepSeek as base LLMs. The framework improved correctness and end-to-end success across all three models, raising end-to-end success from 72-76% to 79-83%.

Conclusion: The graph-constrained agentic framework successfully addresses the challenge of adapting coupled decision modules in retail supply chain operations, providing a scalable approach for requirement-driven adaptation with significant performance improvements over direct LLM reformulation.

Abstract: Retail supply chain operations rely on coupled decision modules that must adapt as requirements evolve. LLMs offer a natural-language interface for this task, but existing methods primarily focus on individual optimization models. Extending them to heterogeneous decision pipelines is challenging because a requirement may admit multiple intervention paths with different downstream effects. We formulate requirement-driven adaptation as the joint selection of an intervention route and an admissible module-level change, and propose a graph-constrained agentic framework in which domain agents expose admissible reformulation interfaces and a central processor searches over bounded intervention paths. Candidates are validated and compared using downstream KPIs. In collaboration with a large retail partner, we evaluate 100 warehouse requirements elicited from practitioner interviews, with GPT, Qwen, and DeepSeek as base LLMs. Relative to direct LLM reformulation, our framework improves correctness and end-to-end success across all three models, raising end-to-end success from 72--76% to 79--83%.

</details>


### [155] [Bioinfoysis Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03871)
*Qingyang Shao,Xin Zhang,Zhouyang Yuan,Xianying Chen,Yujia Xiang,Zihao Yang,Tong Ye,Yangqi Zhang,Jiakang Xu,Xiaoqing Yan,Xuan Luo,Keyi Li,Enci Fan,Kai Kang,Zhuohan Liu,Xingyu Jin,Chunran Teng,Tao Li,Xinyu Lv,Minghui Wang,Wenfeng Li,Yidan Gao,Siyu Liu,Mingrui Luo,Zhu Liang,Guanren Qiao,Zhiping Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bioinfoysis is a multi-agent harness for bioinformatics that uses persistent, artifact-grounded analysis runs with global planning and evidence-driven replanning. It achieves 82.4% accuracy on BixBench and significantly improves performance on SeqQA2 (64.13%) and DbQA2 (31.25%) across four language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents focus on producing final answers, treating planning, tool use, and code execution as transient interactions. This design is unsuitable for long-horizon bioinformatics tasks where conclusions must remain connected to data, computations, and intermediate evidence that support them.

Method: Bioinfoysis combines global planning with step-wise evidence-driven replanning. The planner maintains an executable checklist and revises pending steps using structured handoffs returned after each worker execution. These handoffs bind intermediate results to their responsible agent, checklist step, and plan generation. A controlled runtime validates generated scripts, tables, and figures before downstream use, with role-specific context, persistent memory, and governed bioinformatics skills supporting reliable execution.

Result: On BixBench, Bioinfoysis achieves state-of-the-art accuracy of 82.4%. Across four underlying language models, Bioinfoysis increases average accuracy from 27.81% to 64.13% on SeqQA2 and from 3.13% to 31.25% on DbQA2.

Conclusion: Reliable bioinformatics automation depends not only on model capability, but also on the harness that governs planning, execution, memory, and evidence flow. The system demonstrates that persistent artifact-grounded analysis with evidence-driven replanning significantly improves performance on long-horizon bioinformatics tasks.

Abstract: Large language model agents have shown promise in bioinformatics, but most existing systems focus primarily on producing final answers, treating planning, tool use, and code execution as transient interactions. This design is poorly suited to long-horizon bioinformatics tasks, where conclusions must remain connected to the data, computations, and intermediate evidence that support them. We introduce \textbf{Bioinfoysis}, a multi-agent harness that represents each request as a persistent, artifact-grounded analysis run. Bioinfoysis combines global planning with step-wise, evidence-driven replanning: the planner maintains an executable checklist and revises pending steps using structured handoffs returned after each worker execution. These handoffs bind intermediate results to their responsible agent, checklist step, and plan generation, preventing stale evidence from being silently reused after replanning. A controlled runtime validates generated scripts, tables, and figures before they are used in downstream analysis or reporting, while role-specific context, persistent memory, and governed bioinformatics skills support reliable execution over long analysis trajectories. We evaluate Bioinfoysis on BixBench and two question-answering tracks of LAB-Bench 2. On BixBench, Bioinfoysis achieves state-of-the-art accuracy of 82.4\%. Across four underlying language models, Bioinfoysis increases average accuracy from 27.81\% to 64.13\% on SeqQA2 and from 3.13\% to 31.25\% on DbQA2. These results demonstrate that reliable bioinformatics automation depends not only on model capability, but also on the harness that governs planning, execution, memory, and evidence flow. We hope that the emergence of Bioinfoysis will play a driving and leading role in the development of the bioinformatics community. Our demo website can be seen in https://report.bioinfoysis.com/.

</details>


### [156] [STAIR (STructure Aware Information Retriever): A novel dataset and LLM based retriever for document structure augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03874)
*Vineet Kumar,Meghanadh Pulivarthi,vishwajeet kumar,Jaydeep Sen,Riyaz Ahmad Bhat,Sachindra Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STAIR is a novel retrieval system that leverages Table of Contents (ToC) structure to enable LLMs to exploit global semantic structure in corpora, addressing the "lost in the middle" problem in RAG systems. It uses a finetuned Differentiable Search Index (DSI) and achieves 82.6% Recall@1 on the released SearchTome benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current retrievers chunk long context into length-based chunks, losing rich semantic global structure. LLMs also suffer from the "lost in the middle" problem even with improved long context handling. This research aims to enable LLMs to exploit global document structure (like ToC) for more accurate and hallucination-free retrieval.

Method: The researchers developed STAIR, a retrieval system that uses Table of Contents structure to help a finetuned Differentiable Search Index (DSI) store and retrieve information from model parameters. They created SearchTome, a diverse benchmark from 18 books across 6 domains for evaluation. They conducted thorough ablation studies to evaluate the approach.

Result: STAIR achieves Recall@1 of 82.6%, significantly outperforming DSI (76.9%), BM25 (59.5%), DPR (68.7%), and Mistral (13.8%). The system demonstrates very low hallucination rates (< 0.05%) and can generalize well with very few training samples. The improvement over DSI is statistically significant.

Conclusion: Leveraging global document structure like Table of Contents significantly improves retrieval accuracy and reduces hallucinations in LLM-based IR systems. The released SearchTome benchmark will enable further research in this novel direction of ToC-based retrieval.

Abstract: Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) is a key component for generating accurate and hallucination free answers using Large Language Models (LLMs). LLMs are improving at handling long context, but still suffer from "lost in the middle" problem. Thus, precise and accurate retrieval is important. Current retrievers chunk long context into length-based manageable chunks - in the process throwing away rich and informative semantic global structure in the corpus. We introduce a novel retrieval system STAIR that empowers an LLM to exploit global structure in a corpus such as a Table of Contents (ToC) to efficiently store and retrieve information from its model parameters. Our thorough and careful ablation studies with a finetuned Differentiable Search Index (DSI) system show that ToC helps build a low hallucination (less than 0.05%) generative Information Retrieval (IR) system and can generalize to examples where very few training samples are available. To further research in this novel direction of ToC based retrieval we release SearchTome - a diverse benchmark created from 18 books across 6 diverse domains to further research in this novel direction. STAIR achieves a high Recall@1 score of 82.6% on SearchTome as compared to DSI (76.9%), where the difference is found to be statistically significant. STAIR easily beats other strong baselines such as BM25 (59.5%), DPR (68.7%) and out-of-the-box Mistral (13.8%).

</details>


### [157] [Xiaomi-TabLDM: A Tabular Foundation Model Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03880)
*Xiaomi-TabLDM Team,:,Penghui Wang,Wei Liu,Hong Wang,Chengyue Huang,Yuxi Sun,Zirui Wang,Hongming Huang,Quan Wang,Chunxiao Liu,Erli Meng,Bin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Xiaomi-TabLDM is a tabular large data foundation model for classification and regression that uses in-context learning, pretrained on synthetic data generated from structural causal models (SCMs). It achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks while maintaining favorable efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for a general-purpose tabular data model that can achieve strong prediction accuracy without task-specific fine-tuning. Existing tabular models often require fine-tuning for each new task, which is computationally expensive. The key insight is that pretraining on synthetic data from SCMs can enable flexible context utilization and efficient capacity scaling.

Method: The model uses three key technical innovations: (1) Three-stage training strategy with dual-stream feature grouping, (2) lightweight Attention Residual mechanism, and (3) sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. The model is pretrained exclusively on synthetic data generated from structural causal models (SCMs) to expand coverage and diversity of tabular data.

Result: Xiaomi-TabLDM achieves: (1) Rank 1st on OpenML-CTR23, (2) Rank 2nd on regression across TALENT, TabArena, and BCCO benchmarks, (3) On TabArena regression: second-highest Elo with 82% less training time and 68% less prediction time than top-ranked TabFM. The model also supports test-time compute scaling for additional performance gains.

Conclusion: Xiaomi-TabLDM demonstrates that pretraining on synthetic SCM data can produce a competitive foundation model for tabular data. It establishes a new performance standard while offering favorable efficiency trade-offs. The test-time scaling capability further extends its utility for resource-constrained scenarios.

Abstract: We introduce Xiaomi-TabLDM, a tabular large data foundation model for classification and regression via in-context learning, which delivers superior prediction accuracy without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. Pretrained exclusively on synthetic data generated from structural causal models (SCMs), our model enables more flexible context utilization and more efficient capacity scaling.
  i) A new performance standard. Strong regression performance across benchmarks: Xiaomi-TabLDM ranks 1st on OpenML-CTR23 and 2nd on regression across TALENT, TabArena, and BCCO, demonstrating consistently strong regression performance across four complementary benchmark suites. Favorable performance--efficiency trade-off: Xiaomi-TabLDM combines strong predictive performance with substantially lower computational cost. For example, on TabArena regression, it achieves the second-highest Elo while using 82% less training time and 68% less prediction time than the top-ranked TabFM.
  ii) Large-scale synthetic pretraining. Xiaomi-TabLDM expands the coverage and diversity of synthetic tabular data used for pretraining. We also adopt a three-stage training strategy together with dual-stream feature grouping, lightweight Attention Residual, and sparse Mixture-of-Experts, enabling Xiaomi-TabLDM to learn richer feature interactions and expert specialization across diverse tabular tasks.
  iii) Test-time scaling. Xiaomi-TabLDM further extends tabular prediction through test-time compute scaling, where allocating additional computation at inference time consistently improves predictive performance over the base model.

</details>


### [158] [Inferring Affective Consciousness in an Artificial Agent: A Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03883)
*Mark Solms,St John Grimbly,Bruce Bassett,Evert Boonstra,Rowan Hodson,Nicolas Kuske,Kival Mahadew,Benjamin Rosman,Charel van Hoof,Jonathan Shock*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Creatures that display 'hedonic place preference behaviour' are thought by many scientists to experience feelings, on the assumption that their attraction to pleasure-producing substances which lack nutritional value (e.g. cocaine, morphine) cannot easily be attributed to unconscious instinctual behaviour. In this paper, we discuss how a simple artificial agent that instantiates attributes of an affective system engaging in felt uncertainty about its intrinsic needs in relation to environmental resources can similarly display hedonic place preference behaviour -- through an apparently subjective form of information processing -- while simultaneously being entirely deter-ministic. We outline some implications of this artificially engineered behaviour for our understanding of the physical basis of consciousness and the experience of free will.

</details>


### [159] [Lose the Order, Keep the Hierarchy: Deordering HTN Plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03912)
*Takudzwa Togarepi,Gaspard Quenard,Damien Pellier,Humbert Fiorino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper adapts two classical planning plan deordering techniques to the Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) planning domain and evaluates them on IPC 2023 benchmarks, showing substantial reduction in ordering constraints compared to Optiplan.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While HTN planning has been extensively studied for plan generation, post-plan optimization, particularly plan deordering, remains under-researched. Plan deordering removes unnecessary ordering constraints while maintaining plan validity, which is well-studied in classical planning but not in HTN settings.

Method: The authors adapt two established plan deordering techniques from classical planning by extending them to account for hierarchical decomposition constraints. They evaluate their approaches on the IPC 2023 Partial-Order HTN benchmarks and compare them against Optiplan, an HTN planner that generates partially ordered plans directly.

Result: Both implementations showed a substantial reduction in the number of ordering constraints. While critical path length also decreased, the improvements were less pronounced.

Conclusion: The adapted deordering techniques successfully reduce ordering constraints in HTN plans, though the impact on critical path length is modest. This demonstrates the feasibility of extending classical planning optimization methods to hierarchical planning domains.

Abstract: Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) planning is a powerful planning formalism based on task decomposition. Although most of the literature studied plan generation, comparatively less attention has been paid to post-plan optimization. In particular, plan deordering has been extensively studied in classical planning but remains under-researched in the HTN setting. Plan deordering removes unnecessary ordering constraints between actions in a plan whilst keeping the plan valid. In this paper, we adapt two established plan deordering techniques from classical planning by extending the techniques to account for hierarchical decomposition constraints. We evaluate our proposed approaches on the IPC 2023 Partial-Order HTN benchmarks and we compare them against Optiplan, an HTN planner that generates partially ordered plans directly. Our results show a substantial reduction in number of ordering constraints in both our implementations. Although we also observe a reduction in critical path length, the improvements are less pronounced.

</details>


### [160] [Value-Preserving Architectures for Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03920)
*Alessandro Pesare,Tommaso Dolci,Katja Hose,Emanuel Sallinger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how architectural design choices in LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) can promote human-centered values (privacy, fairness, safety). It proposes three value-preserving architectural patterns: (1) privacy-aware federated topology, (2) distributed architecture for pluralism/diversity, and (3) guard-agent architecture for unfairness detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of agentic AI and LLM-based MAS creates opportunities for automating complex tasks but raises critical concerns about preserving human-centered values. Traditional software engineering focuses on functional correctness, but integrating LLMs into socio-technical systems requires responsible engineering and value alignment. Architectural design decisions (coordination mechanisms, communication protocols, topologies) shape system behavior and outcomes, yet their role in promoting values remains underexplored.

Method: The paper argues that architectural choices influence not only functionality and performance but also value-oriented system behavior. It investigates how different architectural designs support different human-centered values and proposes three value-preserving architectural patterns with representative real-world use cases.

Result: Three value-preserving architectural patterns are introduced: (1) privacy-aware architecture with federated topology, (2) distributed architecture to promote pluralism and diversity, and (3) guard-agent architecture to detect and mitigate unfairness.

Conclusion: By linking architectural design with human-centered values, this work lays the foundation for a unified set of architectural patterns and guidelines towards the design of trustworthy MAS.

Abstract: The emergence of agentic AI and LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) presents unprecedented opportunities for automating complex tasks, while simultaneously raising critical concerns about the preservation of fundamental human-centered values, such as privacy, fairness, and safety. Although software engineering has traditionally focused on functional correctness, the adoption of LLMs and AI agents into complex socio-technical systems has intensified the need for responsible software engineering and robust value alignment. In MAS, architectural design decisions, such as coordination mechanisms, communication protocols, and system topologies, play a central role in shaping system behavior and the outcomes they produce. This paper argues that architectural choices influence not only the functionality and performance of MAS but can also promote value-oriented system behavior. Therefore, we investigate how different architectural designs support different human-centered values, discussing the following value-preserving architectural patterns: (i) a privacy-aware architecture with a federated topology, (ii) a distributed architecture to promote pluralism and diversity, and (iii) a guard-agent architecture to detect and mitigate unfairness. Finally, we introduce representative use cases to illustrate the proposed architectures in real-world scenarios. By linking architectural design with human-centered values, this work lays the foundation for a unified set of architectural patterns and guidelines towards the design of trustworthy MAS.

</details>


### [161] [Speak for Me: Giving LLMs the Situational Awareness to Participate in a Meeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03923)
*Muneeb Khan,Frederic Kirstein,Terry Ruas,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAPA (Collaborative Agent Predictive Architecture) addresses the problem of LLM agents failing to recognize when to speak in online meeting delegation scenarios. The architecture uses a Perceiver-Controller-Predictor-Generator pipeline with a Recalibrator to update meeting state, achieving a dramatic reduction in silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5% while maintaining low hallucination (0.6%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that LLM agents in online meeting delegation lack the ability to recognize when they should contribute - they fail to track stances, coverage, and floor, missing opportunities to speak on behalf of absent participants. Prompt-only delegates stay silent on 51.4% of the absent participant's talking opportunities, indicating a significant recognition gap in current delegation systems.

Method: CAPA uses a modular architecture with five components: (1) Perceiver updates meeting state from each observed turn; (2) Predictor forecasts conversation continuation; (3) Controller decides whether to speak and which proposition to surface; (4) Generator phrases contributions in the participant's style; (5) Recalibrator updates meeting state from judge verdicts. Two judges score forecasts and actions against next observed turns. Evaluation uses an episode-level protocol measuring whether, when, and what delegates contribute around the participant's actual idea units, with schema-constrained LLM judges achieving Cohen's kappa = 0.71 alignment with human annotations.

Result: On 137 AMI meetings, CAPA reduces silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5%, doubles credited recovery (26.1% → 52.2%), and maintains hallucination at 0.6%. The failure mode shifts from omission to selection. Mechanism ablations identify the meeting state as the key factor closing the recognition gap, noting that raw-context scaling alone does not achieve this.

Conclusion: CAPA successfully addresses the online meeting delegation problem through its modular architecture. The meeting state tracking mechanism is identified as the critical component that enables recognition of speaking opportunities, beyond what raw-context scaling can achieve. The architecture shifts failure modes from silence (omission) to selection errors, with each residual near-miss attributable to specific modules for targeted improvement.

Abstract: In online meeting delegation, LLM agents fail to recognize when to speak. With no structured way to track stances, coverage, and floor, they miss the moments where they should contribute. Prompt-only delegates stay silent on 51.4% of the absent participant's talking opportunities on the AMI corpus. We present CAPA (Collaborative Agent Predictive Architecture), an architecture for online meeting delegation. A Perceiver updates the meeting state from each observed turn. A Predictor forecasts how the conversation will continue. A Controller decides whether to speak and which proposition to surface. A Generator phrases the chosen contribution in the participant's style. Two judges score the forecast and the action against the next observed turn. A Recalibrator updates the meeting state from those verdicts for future decisions. To evaluate online delegation, we introduce an episode-level protocol that scores whether, when, and what a delegate contributes around the participant's actual idea units. The protocol's schema-constrained LLM judges align with human annotations at Cohen's kappa = 0.71. On 137 AMI meetings, CAPA reduces the silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5%, doubles credited recovery (26.1 --> 52.2), and keeps hallucination at 0.6%. The failure mode shifts from omission to selection, with each residual near-miss attributable to a specific module of the architecture. Mechanism ablations identify the meeting state as the lever that closes the recognition gap, where raw-context scaling alone does not.

</details>


### [162] [Towards Numerical TOHTN Planning with SMT-based HTN-SAT Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03938)
*Gaspard Quenard,Takudzwa Togarepi,Damien Pellier,Humbert Fiorino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper extends SAT-based Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) planning to handle numerical fluents using SMT solvers, and introduces a benchmark suite for numerical Totally-Ordered HTN (TOHTN) planning evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HTN planning has received significant attention in recent years, but support for numerical reasoning remains very limited. This gap restricts the applicability of HTN planning to real-world problems involving quantities, measurements, and numeric constraints.

Method: The authors extend standard SAT-based encodings with SMT (Satisfiability Modulo Theories) to handle numeric fluents in numerical TOHTN planning. They also introduce a benchmark suite for numerical TOHTN planning.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that this simple encoding serves as a competitive baseline for numerical HTN planning.

Conclusion: This work opens the way to more expressive approaches to HTN planning by providing a foundation for numerical reasoning in the TOHTN planning domain.

Abstract: While HTN planning has received significant attention in recent years, support for numerical reasoning remains very limited. In this paper, we investigate numerical Totally-Ordered HTN (TOHTN) planning and show how standard SAT-based encodings can be naturally extended with SMT to handle numeric fluents. In addition, we introduce a benchmark suite for numerical TOHTN planning, providing a first common basis for evaluation in this setting. Experimental results show that this simple encoding already constitutes a competitive baseline. This work opens the way to more expressive approaches to HTN planning.

</details>


### [163] [More Criticism Does Not Make a Better Review: EquiReview-R](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03943)
*Zexing Zhang,Jichao Li,Tianyang Lei,Yude Fu,Yang Kewei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses a critical limitation in AI peer review systems: they generate many criticisms but fail to distinguish between missing important weaknesses (omission) and including unsupported allegations (overcritique). The authors propose EquiReview-R, which treats review as evidence-guided refinement of concerns, with separate handling of these two failure modes. The system resolves existing concerns against evidence, searches for missing issues, and returns stop/continue/defer decisions. Evaluation on unseen papers shows significant improvements: major overcritique reduced from 15.5% to 8.1%, with 9.9% omission upper bound while stopping on 52.4% of papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI reviewers can produce many specific criticisms, but more criticism isn't necessarily better. Reviews may miss consequential weaknesses (omission) or retain unsupported allegations (overcritique). These are opposite failures requiring different corrections, yet existing generation-oriented systems and aggregate measures obscure this distinction. The key insight is that revision must precede further search - nearly all concerns in high-recall reviews lack definitive evidential disposition, and earlier refinement mechanisms cannot revise them.

Method: The authors recast AI-assisted review as evidence-guided refinement of a structured concern set, treating omission and overcritique as separate risks. They introduce EquiReview-R which: (1) resolves existing concerns against localized evidence, (2) searches for missing issues from independent and review-conditioned perspectives, and (3) returns stop, continue, or defer decisions. They construct an evidence-linked trajectory corpus (ReviewTrace) to study review revision and expose failure modes.

Result: On a frozen cohort of previously unseen papers, EquiReview-R: satisfies the prespecified non-inferiority criterion for major omission; reduces major overcritique from 15.5% to 8.1%; attains a one-sided omission upper bound of 9.9%; stops on 52.4% of papers. Computation-matched controls, controlled pairs, and ablations demonstrate that the gain comes from revision rather than extra inference or shorter output.

Conclusion: The key contribution is distinguishing between omission and overcritique as separate risks in AI peer review. The evidence-guided refinement approach, with revision preceding search, significantly improves review quality. The release of ReviewTrace corpus enables further research on review revision, disagreement, and provenance.

Abstract: AI reviewers can now produce many specific criticisms, but more criticism is not necessarily a better review. A review may miss a consequential weakness or retain an allegation that available evidence does not support. These failures require opposite corrections, yet generation-oriented systems and aggregate measures obscure the distinction. We therefore recast AI-assisted review as evidence-guided refinement of a structured concern set, with omission and overcritique treated as separate risks. Building on this formulation, we introduce EquiReview-R, which resolves existing concerns against localized evidence, searches for missing issues from independent and review-conditioned perspectives, and returns stop, continue, or defer. To expose the failure mode that motivates this design, we construct an evidence-linked trajectory corpus. Its retrospective analysis shows why revision must precede further search: nearly all concerns in a high-recall review lack a definitive evidential disposition, while an earlier refinement mechanism cannot revise them. On a frozen cohort of previously unseen papers, EquiReview-R satisfies the prespecified non-inferiority criterion for major omission, reduces major overcritique from 15.5% to 8.1%, and attains a one-sided omission upper bound of 9.9% while stopping on 52.4% of papers. Computation-matched controls, controlled pairs, and ablations show that the gain comes from revision rather than extra inference or shorter output. We release the corpus as ReviewTrace, an evidence-linked resource for studying review revision, disagreement, and provenance.

</details>


### [164] [FiMI Banking: A Sovereign Model for Indian Retail Banking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03960)
*NPCI AI Research Team,Aman Kumar,Asit Desai,Chandra Bhushan,Harsh Sharma,Harshit Bhushan,Hrithik Kadam,Keyur Doshi,Kolisetty Sai Kapardheeswar,Krishanu Adhikary,Nadeem Shaik,Navya Prakash,Nitin Kukreja,Prashant Devadiga,Shamanth MH,Shantanu Pandey,Suvradip Paul,Yatharth Dedhia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FiMI Banking is a controlled Indian retail-banking setting designed to evaluate conversational AI systems for banking applications. The study compares two post-training approaches—preference optimization and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards—demonstrating that both methods address complementary requirements for reliable banking agents, with preference optimization improving safety (refusal rates from 52% to 80%) and RL enhancing edge-case and order-sensitive task performance while reducing token usage by 29%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose language models fail to meet banking requirements for grounded information, correct tool use, and cautious handling of sensitive situations. Banks need conversational systems that can answer product questions, assist with account requests, and operate safely within strict operational and regulatory constraints.

Method: Two post-training approaches were evaluated: (1) preference optimization for response-level behavior, and (2) reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for multi-turn tool-use tasks. The evaluation was conducted on FiMI Banking, a controlled Indian retail-banking setting built from vetted banking documents, structured ground truth, synthetic customer backgrounds, and banking tools.

Result: Preference optimization substantially improved safe behavior, with out-of-scope refusal rising from 52% to 80%. Reinforcement learning improved edge-case performance from 0.509 to 0.718 and order-sensitive task performance from 0.590 to 0.679, while using 29% fewer generated tokens.

Conclusion: Preference optimization and verifiable-reward reinforcement learning address complementary requirements for reliable banking agents, offering a promising direction for building safe and effective banking conversational systems.

Abstract: Banks need conversational systems that can answer product questions, assist customers with account-related requests, and operate safely within strict operational and regulatory constraints. General-purpose language models do not reliably meet these requirements. They fall short when a task requires grounded information, correct tool use, or cautious handling of bank-specific sensitive situations. We introduce FiMI Banking, a controlled Indian retail-banking setting. We build it from vetted banking documents, structured ground truth, synthetic customer backgrounds, and banking tools. We evaluate two post-training approaches: preference optimization for response-level behavior, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for multi-turn tool-use tasks. Preference optimization improves safe behavior substantially: out-of-scope refusal rises from 52% to 80%. Reinforcement learning improves edge-case performance from 0.509 to 0.718 and order-sensitive task performance from 0.590 to 0.679, while using 29% fewer generated tokens. These results show that preference optimization and verifiable-reward reinforcement learning address complementary requirements for reliable banking agents.

</details>


### [165] [Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03966)
*Wenbo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reveals a critical flaw in AI agent evaluation: tool-call rates are not intrinsic model properties but depend on the model-interface stack. The same model can score 0.00 or 0.96 depending on the serving adapter, due to silent filtering of well-formed calls by the interface before downstream processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observed that agent evaluations report tool-call rates read from the serving stack, which can be zero even when models emit well-formed calls—because the interface censors trajectories before downstream components see them. This raises questions about what tool-call rates actually measure.
