Abstract: Professional color editing requires precise control over both color (hue and saturation) and lightness, ideally through separate, independent controls. We present a real-time interactive color editing framework for 3D Gaussian Splatting that supports palette-based recoloring, per-palette tone curves for color-aware luminance adjustment, and pixel-level color constraints. Rather than training a new representation from scratch, we reparameterize the spherical harmonics of a pretrained vanilla 3DGS to encode view-dependent palette weights. We simultaneously solve for weights and palette colors via a loss based on image-space sparsity. Luminance editing is realized as a per-pixel weight shift along the achromatic axis, which we show is equivalent to a per-pixel palette-aware luminance edit. This view-space formulation addresses a core limitation of prior primitive-space methods, where alpha-blending breaks per-Gaussian sparsity and causes edits to bleed into unintended regions. Our edits run in tens of milliseconds via an iteratively reweighted least squares and damped block-coordinate descent that couples tone curves and palette shifts under view-space sparsity. Our representation can be efficiently baked back into a vanilla 3DGS, preserving compatibility with standard viewers. We demonstrate sparser, more localized edits than prior palette-based 3DGS methods, while enabling independent luminance control per palette color and view-consistent pixel-level constraints, capabilities previously unavailable for 3DGS.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [188] [The Geometry of Ignorance: LLMs Know When to Temper Bayesian Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02959)
*Toni J. B. Liu,Jiajun Bao,Yizhou Liu,Gurbir Arora,Nicolas Boullé,Raphaël Sarfati,Christopher J. Earls*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals that language models have a geometric structure in their unembedding matrix encoding a "direction of ignorance" - a single direction that represents the unigram prior of the training corpus. This prior loading factor λ can be measured via projection and decreases as context becomes more informative, with larger models showing lower prior reliance. The direction is also causally manipulable to steer predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what language models predict when they have insufficient context clues, and to uncover the geometric structure underlying the model's fallback behavior (Bayesian prior).

Method: Examined unembedding geometry across four model families (Llama, Qwen, Gemma, Pythia) spanning 0.4B to 405B parameters. Used projection of final prediction state onto the "direction of ignorance" to compute prior loading factor λ. Formal analysis showed this projection decomposes prediction into two orthogonal vectors corresponding to tempered Bayesian update factors.

Result: The direction of ignorance exists in all examined model families. λ declines steadily as context becomes more informative. The geometric-probabilistic interpretation makes λ comparable across models, with larger models generally showing lower prior reliance in high-context limits. The direction is causally active - manipulating λ steers predictions toward or away from the unigram prior in KL divergence.

Conclusion: Language models encode their unigram prior geometrically in the unembedding matrix. This "direction of ignorance" provides a principled way to measure and manipulate the model's prior reliance, offering new insights into model uncertainty and behavior across scales.

Abstract: What does a language model predict when it has few clues? The answer lurks in its unembedding geometry: a single direction of the unembedding matrix encodes the unigram distribution of the training corpus, which serves as the Bayesian prior the model falls back on when uncertain. This structure --- which we term the \emph{direction of ignorance} --- appears in all four model families examined (\texttt{Llama}, \texttt{Qwen}, \texttt{Gemma}, and \texttt{Pythia}), ranging from 0.4B to 405B parameters. Projecting the final prediction state onto this direction yields a per-token \emph{prior loading factor} $λ$, which, empirically, declines steadily as the context becomes more informative. Formally, the same projection decomposes the prediction state into two orthogonal vectors that correspond exactly to the two factors of a tempered Bayesian update: a unigram prior raised to the exponent $λ$ and a context-driven likelihood. This geometric-probabilistic interpretation calibrates $λ$, making it meaningfully comparable across model sizes and families, with larger models generally exhibiting lower prior reliance in the high-context limit. Finally, we show that the direction of ignorance is causally active: raising or lowering $λ$ at the final prediction state steers the prediction toward or away from the unigram prior in KL divergence.

</details>


### [189] [Equation Recast for Canonical Operator Learning Across Parametric PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02982)
*Qiyun Cheng,Valentin Duruisseaux,Cesar F. Clauser,Md Hossain Sahadath,Huihua Yang,Shaowu Pan,Nathaniel Ferraro,Anima Anandkumar,Wei Ji,Cristina Rea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Equation recast reformulates parametric operator learning as learning a single canonical operator by analytically deriving parameter-induced operator variations from the governing equation and absorbing them into effective sources, enabling zero-shot prediction across new parameter regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current parametric operator learning methods require substantial coverage of input functions and physical parameters, and models may fail silently outside the training distribution.

Method: Reformulate parametric operator learning as learning a canonical operator. Parameter-induced operator variations are analytically derived from governing equations and absorbed into effective sources, enabling canonical-domain mapping.

Result: Enables extrapolation, integrates sparse heterogeneous datasets in shared canonical representation, uses loss of convergence as warning signal for failure. Demonstrated on tokamak simulations unifying electron-temperature data across four device geometries.

Conclusion: Equation recast provides reusable neural PDE solvers combining equation-guided transfer, data efficiency, and monitorable inference.

Abstract: Learning solution operators across broad parameter ranges can require substantial coverage of both input functions and physical parameters, particularly for purely data-driven parametric models. In addition, the resulting models may fail silently outside the training distribution. We introduce equation recast, which reformulates parametric operator learning as the learning of a single canonical operator. Parameter-induced operator variations are derived analytically from the governing equation and absorbed into effective sources, enabling zero-shot prediction across new parameter regimes. Across multi-parameter, nonlinear, and singular PDE settings, equation recast supports extrapolation, integrates sparse heterogeneous datasets in a shared canonical representation, and uses loss of convergence as an internal warning signal for failure of the recast iteration. In high-fidelity tokamak simulations for nuclear fusion, the framework unifies electron-temperature data across four device geometries through canonical-domain mapping within one jointly trained operator. Equation recast provides a route toward reusable neural PDE solvers combining equation-guided transfer, data efficiency, and monitorable inference.

</details>


### [190] [From Euclidean to Graph-Structured Data: A Survey of Collaborative Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02984)
*Rémi Bourgerie,Šarūnas Girdzijauskas,Viktoria Fodor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey paper provides a comprehensive review of collaborative learning, extending from traditional Euclidean data (images, text) to graph-structured data. It addresses the fundamental limitations of centralized machine learning (scalability and privacy) and examines how collaborative approaches like federated and decentralized learning can be applied to graph data, which is prevalent in many real-world applications but underexplored in collaborative settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional machine learning approaches face scalability and privacy limitations when data collection, training, and inference occur in a single location. While collaborative learning approaches (federated/decentralized learning) have emerged to address these challenges, most research focuses on Euclidean data with regular structures. However, many real-world applications involve graph-structured data with relational patterns that are not well-captured by existing approaches. The opportunities and challenges of learning on graph data in collaborative settings remain largely underexplored.

Method: The survey methodology involves: 1) Reviewing foundational principles of collaborative learning for Euclidean data, organized along three dimensions: learning effectiveness, efficiency, and privacy preservation; 2) Extending the discussion to graph-structured data by introducing a taxonomy of graph distribution scenarios; 3) Characterizing associated statistical heterogeneities; 4) Developing standardized problem formulations and algorithmic frameworks; 5) Systematically identifying open challenges and promising research directions.

Result: The paper consolidates an emerging field by providing a comprehensive investigation of collaborative learning from Euclidean to graph-structured data. It establishes foundational principles for Euclidean collaborative learning, creates a taxonomy for graph distribution scenarios, characterizes statistical heterogeneities in graph learning, and develops standardized problem formulations and algorithmic frameworks for graph-based collaborative learning.

Conclusion: This survey establishes a foundation for the emerging field of collaborative learning on graph-structured data. It identifies this as a significantly underexplored area with substantial research potential. The paper concludes by systematically outlining open challenges and promising research directions, serving as a valuable reference for researchers seeking to advance this field.

Abstract: The conventional approach to machine learning, that is, collecting data, training models, and performing inference in a single location, faces fundamental limitations, including scalability and privacy, that restrict its applicability. To address these challenges, recent research has explored collaborative learning approaches, including federated learning and decentralized learning, where individual agents perform training and inference locally, with limited collaboration. Most collaborative learning research focuses on Euclidean data with regular, grid-like structure (e.g., images, text). However, these approaches fail to capture the relational patterns in many real-world applications, best represented by graphs. Learning on graphs relies on message-passing mechanisms to propagate information between connected nodes, making it conceptually well-suited for collaborative environments where agents must exchange information. Yet, the opportunities and challenges of learning on graph-structured data in collaborative settings remain largely underexplored. This survey provides a comprehensive investigation of collaborative learning from Euclidean to graph-structured data, aiming to consolidate this emerging field. We begin by reviewing its foundational principles for Euclidean data, organizing them along three core dimensions: learning effectiveness, efficiency, and privacy preservation. We then extend the discussion to graph-structured data, introducing a taxonomy of graph distribution scenarios, characterizing associated statistical heterogeneities, and developing standardized problem formulations and algorithmic frameworks. Finally, we systematically identify open challenges and promising research directions.

</details>


### [191] [Modern Transformers Are Implicit Hybrids: From Functional Differentiation to Principled Hybrid Architecture Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02986)
*Runlin Shi,Bojian Yin,Guoqi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes evidence-based methods for designing hybrid Transformer architectures that combine Full Attention (FA) and Linear Attention (LA). By introducing two metrics (RFIS and RPD), the authors discover a taxonomy of retrieval and positional heads separated by a "Global Positional Band" (GPBand), leading to the Head-wise Hybrid Architecture (HwH) that achieves improved retrieval and zero-shot length extrapolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The allocation of Full Attention and Linear Attention in hybrid architectures remains heuristic. The authors seek an evidence-grounded basis for head-level functional organization in RoPE-based Transformers, as behavioral probes do not yield a complete taxonomy.

Method: The authors propose two intervention metrics: (1) RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS) - measuring how each frequency affects a head's attention distribution, and (2) RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD) - isolating dependence on rotary positional modulation. They validate these on Qwen3-series models and Llama3.1, and use Controlled Transformers to show the GPBand follows training-length positional scale.

Result: The analysis reveals a complete taxonomy of retrieval and positional heads separated by a salient mid-low-frequency band (GPBand). HwH with FA-to-LA ratio below 1:3 retains strong language modeling and commonsense reasoning while improving retrieval and substantially strengthening zero-shot long-context extrapolation over Transformer, LA, and layer-wise hybrid baselines.

Conclusion: Two principles emerge: (1) positional modeling should operate only locally with global access through position-independent retrieval, and (2) both functions should be assigned at head granularity with layer-specific allocation. Principled hybrid architecture design is a promising route for future foundation models.

Abstract: Hybrid architectures combining Full Attention (FA) and Linear Attention (LA) are increasingly prominent, yet their allocation remains heuristic. We seek an evidence-grounded basis in head-level functional organization learned by RoPE-based Transformers. Behavioral probes do not yield a complete taxonomy, so we propose two intervention metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS), measuring how each frequency affects a head's attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD), isolating dependence on rotary positional modulation. On Qwen3-series models and Llama3.1, RFIS suggests and RPD verifies a complete taxonomy of retrieval and positional heads separated by a salient mid-low-frequency band. Controlled Transformers show that this boundary follows the training-length positional scale; we term it the Global Positional Band (GPBand). The analysis suggests a potential cause of zero-shot length-extrapolation failure and yields two principles: positional modeling should operate only locally, with global access through position-independent retrieval; and both functions should be assigned at head granularity with layer-specific allocation. We instantiate them in Head-wise Hybrid Architecture (HwH), using NoPE FA for global retrieval and LA for local positional modeling. With an FA-to-LA ratio below 1:3, HwH retains strong language modeling and commonsense reasoning while improving retrieval and substantially strengthening zero-shot long-context extrapolation over Transformer, LA, and a layer-wise hybrid baseline. Ablations validate both principles and component roles, highlighting principled hybrid architecture design as a promising route toward future foundation models.

</details>


### [192] [Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02987)
*Shrinivas Ramasubramanian,Daman Arora,Fahim Tajwar,Guanning Zeng,Qingyang Wu,Zhongzhu Zhou,Chenfeng Xu,Haiwen Feng,Yuda Song,Aarti Singh,Ruslan Salakhutdinov,J. Andrew Bagnell,Jeff Schneider,Andrea Zanette*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses a limitation in standard reinforcement learning: optimizing average reward can hide the difference between policies with identical means but different reward distributions. The authors propose TailRL, which optimizes the upper tails of the reward distribution instead of just the mean, turning continuous rewards into binary success events and giving more weight to rare, high-reward outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL optimizes average reward, which can mask important differences between policies. Two policies can achieve the same mean reward while having very different probabilities of producing rare but high-reward outcomes. This matters because the benefit of sampling (both during training and inference) depends on retaining probability mass on high-reward outcomes. The authors seek to optimize this "coverage" directly.

Method: The paper proposes Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning (TailRL), which maximizes the log-probability of exceeding a randomly chosen reward threshold. This approach converts the continuous reward problem into a family of binary success events (for each reward threshold, how likely is the policy to exceed it?). The gradient gives more weight to rare, high-reward rollouts and can be interpreted as a mixture of Best-of-(k) gradients. The method requires only a simple modification to the advantage function, making it compatible with existing RL pipelines.

Result: Across four domains—object localization, maze navigation, GUI grounding, and code optimization—TailRL demonstrates that it leverages rare high-reward training samples to avoid suboptimal solutions. The resulting models benefit more from additional samples at inference time compared to standard RL approaches.

Conclusion: TailRL provides a principled approach to optimizing for high-reward tail outcomes in reinforcement learning. By focusing on the distribution of rewards rather than just the mean, it achieves better sample efficiency and produces models that scale better with increased sampling at inference time. The method is simple to implement and compatible with existing RL algorithms.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning typically optimizes average reward. For generative policies, the average can hide an important distinction: two policies can achieve the same mean reward while having very different chances of producing a rare but high-reward rollout. This matters as sampling increases during training and inference, since its benefit depends on retaining probability mass on high-reward outcomes. We propose to optimize this coverage directly. Rather than considering only expected reward, we consider all of its upper tails: for each reward threshold, how likely is the policy to exceed it? This turns a continuous reward into a family of binary success events. We introduce Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning (TailRL), which maximizes the log-probability of exceeding a randomly chosen reward threshold. Its gradient gives more weight to rare, high-reward rollouts and can be interpreted as a mixture of Best-of-(k) gradients. TailRL requires only a simple modification to the advantage function, making it compatible with existing reinforcement learning pipelines. Across object localization, maze navigation, GUI grounding, and code optimization, TailRL leverages rare high-reward training samples to avoid suboptimal solutions and yields models that benefit more from additional samples at inference time.

</details>


### [193] [Mesh-Native Physics-Informed Graph Surrogates for TCAD-in-the-Loop Design Space Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02988)
*Leonid Popryho,Ayoub Sadeghi,Inna Partin-Vaisband*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a physics-informed graph attention network (GAT) surrogate model for high-fidelity TCAD drift-diffusion simulation of FinFET devices. The method operates on tetrahedral meshes, predicts fundamental unknowns (electrostatic potential, quasi-Fermi levels) at each node, and incorporates carrier-transport physics through finite-volume current-continuity residuals. It achieves size generalization and enables active learning via uncertainty quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity TCAD drift-diffusion simulation is computationally expensive for 3D FinFET structures, severely limiting multi-objective design space exploration. Existing ML surrogates lack physics-awareness and transferability across device geometries.

Method: Physics-informed GAT operating on tetrahedral TCAD mesh as a graph. Predicts per-node electrostatic potential and electron/hole quasi-Fermi levels. Training combines data loss with finite-volume current-continuity residuals. Uses deep ensemble for per-node uncertainty quantification to drive active learning.

Result: Sub-volt RMSE per field, orders of magnitude higher throughput than full simulator. Inference under 1 second per device for large multi-fin arrays. Enables Pareto-front exploration across device scales infeasible with direct TCAD.

Conclusion: The physics-informed GAT surrogate achieves accurate, fast prediction of drift-diffusion fields with size generalization. It enables efficient design space exploration for multi-fin FinFETs by combining speed with physics-embedded training and uncertainty-driven active learning.

Abstract: High-fidelity TCAD simulation of drift-diffusion transport remains the workhorse of emerging FinFET device design, but it is computationally expensive, especially for 3D structures where runtime escalates steeply with mesh complexity. This sharply limits multi-objective design space exploration. Existing machine-learning surrogates map a fixed set of design parameters to a few scalar device metrics, discarding the underlying physics and losing transferability across device geometries and families. A physics-informed graph attention network (GAT) surrogate is proposed. It operates directly on the tetrahedral TCAD mesh and predicts, at every mesh node, the electrostatic potential together with the electron and hole quasi-Fermi levels, the fundamental unknowns of the drift-diffusion system. Training combines a data loss with finite-volume current-continuity residuals, embedding carrier-transport physics into the objective. Operating on the mesh as a graph, the surrogate inherits size generalization: a model trained on few-fin meshes applies unchanged to substantially larger arrays, bounded at inference only by GPU memory. Per-node uncertainty from a deep ensemble drives an active-learning loop that screens large candidate pools in seconds and forwards only the most informative designs for full simulation. Benchmarked against Sentaurus Device on multi-fin tri-gate FinFETs, the surrogate reproduces the three drift-diffusion fields with sub-volt per-field RMSE and reaches a per-design throughput orders of magnitude higher than the full simulator. The advantage grows with device size: on large multi-fin arrays that are prohibitively slow to simulate directly, inference still completes in under a second per device, enabling Pareto-front exploration across device scales infeasible for direct TCAD sweeps.

</details>


### [194] [TRACE: Spatiotemporal Contact Memory Graph Network Simulator for Granular Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02991)
*Changjian Zhou,Negin Yousefpour,Jie Qi,Junfeng Fang,Guillermo A. Narsilio,Hans Petter Jostad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE is a graph-network simulator for granular dynamics that maintains persistent memory on contact edges to preserve inter-granular contact history, outperforming existing methods in accuracy and computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Granular dynamics depend strongly on contact history, which is difficult to preserve when particle contacts form, break, and rearrange. Existing simulators store temporal information in node features or node-level memory, but this approach loses important interaction history.

Method: TRACE uses edge-based memory with attention-based message passing and gated recurrent units (GRU) to store interaction history directly on contact edges. An edge-identity dictionary preserves memory as the contact graph changes. A physics-structured decoder predicts normal and tangential contact forces, enforces Coulomb friction limit, and applies equal-and-opposite internal forces. Training involves single-step pretraining followed by autoregressive rollout fine-tuning.

Result: TRACE reduces long-rollout position error by 31-62% and final-deposit error by 58-89% compared to GNS and NMGNS, while using fewer parameters and maintaining near-zero particle interpenetration. Achieves 12.2× and 8.9× speedups over MPM reference solver in 2D and 3D respectively.

Conclusion: Storing interaction history directly on contact edges enables more accurate and stable long-horizon granular dynamics simulations. TRACE successfully reproduces final deposit geometry and kinetic energy release during collapse, demonstrating the effectiveness of edge-centric memory architecture for physical simulation.

Abstract: Learned graph simulators provide an efficient alternative to high-fidelity solvers for granular dynamics. However, granular motion depends strongly on inter-granular contact history, which is difficult to preserve when particle contacts form, break, and rearrange. Existing simulators mainly store temporal information in node features or node-level memory. Here we introduce TRACE, a graph-network simulator that stores interaction history directly on contact edges. Each edge maintains a persistent memory updated by attention-based message passing and a gated recurrent unit, while an edge-identity dictionary preserves this memory as the contact graph changes. A physics-structured decoder predicts inter-granular normal and tangential contact forces, enforces the Coulomb friction limit, and applies equal-and-opposite internal forces. The model is trained with single-step pretraining followed by autoregressive rollout fine-tuning. We evaluate TRACE on 2D and 3D granular column-collapse benchmarks. In both cases, TRACE produces stable, physically consistent long-horizon rollouts, closely reproducing the final deposit geometry and the kinetic energy released during collapse. Compared with graph network simulator (GNS) and node-memory graph neural simulator (NMGNS), TRACE reduces long-rollout position error by 31-62% and final-deposit error by 58-89% across the two benchmarks, while using fewer parameters and maintaining near-zero particle interpenetration. TRACE also achieves 12.2$\times$ and 8.9$\times$ speedups over the material point method (MPM) reference solver in 2D and 3D, respectively. Our code is available at https://github.com/Data-Driven-Computational-Geotechnics/TRACE.

</details>


### [195] [No-Regret Bayesian Optimization with Finite-Library Input-Warped Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02993)
*Edvin Ketabati Augustinsson,Robert A. Bridges*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FLIWBO extends GP-UCB to include finite-library input warping, enabling automatic adaptation of input geometry while retaining high-probability convergence guarantees. The method selects warps from a library by any history-dependent rule, with a explicit √(N_ε) library-size cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GP-UCB convergence guarantees require a fixed kernel, but when raw coordinates poorly match the underlying geometry (e.g., log-scaled hyperparameters or localized peaks), input warping can greatly improve sample efficiency. Existing proofs cannot handle this adaptation, creating a gap between practical benefits and theoretical guarantees.

Method: FLIWBO (Finite-Library Input-Warped Bayesian Optimization) selects warps from a finite library of smooth input maps by any history-dependent rule. It adapts input geometry to accelerate learning while retaining high-probability convergence guarantees under mild hypotheses, with an explicit √(N_ε) library-size cost. The method was tested with GP-UCB as the base optimizer.

Result: Controlled diagnostics show finite-library warping repairs planted geometry mismatches and identifies FLIWBO failure cases. Across four benchmarks (warped synthetic objectives, confidence-fence trap, Fashion-MNIST HPO, and 20D MAS design), FLIWBO-UCB: (1) beats raw-coordinate GP-UCB under misspecified geometry, (2) escapes traps that defeat even oracle-warp expected improvement, (3) recovers much of the gain from manual log scaling, and (4) demonstrates feasibility under costly noisy evaluations in high dimensions.

Conclusion: FLIWBO successfully bridges the gap between practical input warping benefits and theoretical convergence guarantees. It outperforms raw-coordinate GP-UCB under geometry misspecification and leads among tested methods that admit matching regret guarantees, making it a practical solution for black-box optimization with unknown input geometries.

Abstract: Gaussian-process Bayesian optimization (GP-BO) excels at black-box optimization of costly functions, e.g., hyperparameter optimization (HPO) and multi-agent system (MAS) design. Convergence-rate guarantees exist for select methods, notably GP upper confidence bound (GP-UCB), but require a fixed kernel. Critically, the kernel encodes how input proximity affects objective value similarity. When raw coordinates poorly match this geometry - as with log-scaled hyperparameters or localized peaks - input warping can greatly improve sample efficiency, yet known GP-UCB proofs require a fixed kernel. We propose Finite-Library Input-Warped Bayesian Optimization (FLIWBO), which selects warps from a finite library of smooth input maps by any history-dependent rule. It adapts the input geometry to accelerate learning while retaining high-probability convergence guarantees under mild hypotheses, with an explicit $\sqrt(N_\varepsilon)$ library-size cost. Controlled diagnostics show that finite-library warping repairs planted geometry mismatches and identify FLIWBO failure cases. Across four repeated benchmarks - warped synthetic objectives, a confidence-fence trap, and Fashion-MNIST HPO - FLIWBO-UCB beats raw-coordinate GP-UCB under misspecified geometry, escapes traps that defeat even oracle-warp expected improvement, and recovers much of the gain from manual log scaling, while leading the tested methods that admit a matching regret guarantee. A 20-dimensional MAS design study further shows feasibility under costly noisy evaluations. Code for experiments is available: https://github.com/edvin-ketabati/bogp-paper-experiments.

</details>


### [196] [Evaluating Graph Neural Networks for Change-Criticality Classification in Maritime Navigation Charts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02996)
*Abhishek Potnis,Jacob Arndt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to classify changes in Electronic Navigational Charts (ENCs) as critical or non-critical risks to navigational safety. The approach represents spatial objects as nodes and their relationships as edges, encoding old and new ENC datasets as graph pairs for classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maintaining ENC datasets is challenging, and classifying changes based on their significance to navigational safety is important. The motivation is to develop a scalable approach for automating or improving ENC maintenance workflows by accurately identifying critical changes.

Method: The method involves representing ENC vector navigation datasets as graph structures where spatial objects serve as nodes, and spatial/semantic relationships form edges. Both old and new ENC datasets are encoded into a pair of graphs, framing the task as a graph-pair classification problem. Several GNN architectures and model configurations are trained and evaluated on ENC changes reviewed by maritime experts.

Result: Graph-based representations improve the classification of ENC updates, demonstrating the potential for scalable automation of ENC maintenance workflows.

Conclusion: GNNs are effective for classifying ENC changes as critical or non-critical risks to navigational safety. This approach provides a scalable solution for automating or improving ENC maintenance workflows.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are a class of neural networks suitable for learning on graph-structured data. Their application to spatial data is a natural extension, however its relatively unclear which message-passing operations, architectural configurations, and graph representation is best suited for classifying changes to objects in electronic navigational charts (ENCs)--geospatial vector datasets used for marine navigation. Maintaining these datasets is a challenge, and categorizing changes to objects in the ENC based on their significance to navigational safety is of particular importance. Here, we propose to represent these vector navigation datasets as a graph structure where the spatial objects serve as nodes and their spatial and semantic relationships form edges. We encode both the old ENC dataset and new ENC dataset into a pair of graphs and frame the task as a graph-pair classification problem. Building on this representation, we investigate the use of GNN architectures to classify whether the encoded graphs constitutes a critical or non-critical risk to navigational safety. We train and evaluate several GNN architectures and model configurations on ENC changes reviewed by maritime experts. Our results demonstrate that graph-based representations improve the classification of ENC updates, providing a scalable approach for automating or improving ENC maintenance workflows.

</details>


### [197] [Verify Before You Distill: Prompt-Level Teacher Gating for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02998)
*Zhiwei Zhang,Zechen Sun,Fei Zhao,Kang Peng,Bin Liang,Huayu Deng,Yao Hu,Kam-Fai Wong,Mu Chuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TGOPD introduces a teacher reliability verification mechanism for on-policy distillation, routing prompts to either dense OPD or verifier-grounded GRPO based on estimated teacher reliability from verifier-scored probes, achieving superior performance across multiple domains and scales while improving teacher GPU utilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla on-policy distillation (OPD) applies uniform token-level supervision without verifying teacher reliability per prompt. Since reverse KL is mode-seeking, a confidently wrong teacher can cause strong but misleading updates. Existing distributional proxies (entropy, likelihood agreement) measure uncertainty but don't verify outcome correctness.

Method: TGOPD estimates teacher reliability from a small set of verifier-scored teacher probes. Each prompt is routed: (1) exclusively to dense OPD when reliability check passes, or (2) to verifier-grounded GRPO otherwise. Also improves asynchronous OPD teacher utilization by using otherwise-idle capacity for reliability estimation.

Result: Across 4B and 35B student models in mathematics, code, and instruction following: TGOPD outperforms Vanilla OPD in all six single-domain settings and achieves higher seven-benchmark averages at both scales under multi-domain training. Teacher-node GPU utilization increased from 9.8% to 78.9% in the measured 4B single-domain run.

Conclusion: Prompt-level teacher reliability verification before dense supervision significantly improves distillation quality and computational efficiency across multiple domains and model scales.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) accelerates post-training by providing dense token-level supervision from a frozen teacher on the student's own rollouts. Vanilla OPD applies this supervision uniformly across prompts, without checking whether the teacher is reliable for each prompt. Because reverse KL is mode-seeking, a confidently wrong teacher can induce a strong yet misleading update. Distributional proxies, such as entropy or teacher-student likelihood agreement, measure uncertainty or agreement but do not directly verify outcome correctness. We introduce Teacher-Gated On-Policy Distillation (TGOPD), built on the principle that teacher reliability should be verified at the prompt level before dense supervision is admitted. TGOPD estimates reliability from a small set of verifier-scored teacher probes and routes each prompt exclusively to dense OPD when the reliability check passes or to verifier-grounded GRPO otherwise. Across 4B and 35B students in mathematics, code, and instruction following, TGOPD outperforms Vanilla OPD in all six single-domain settings and achieves higher seven-benchmark averages at both scales under multi-domain training. By using otherwise-idle teacher capacity for reliability estimation, TGOPD also reduces teacher-side compute waste in asynchronous OPD, increasing teacher-node GPU utilization from 9.8% to 78.9% in the measured 4B single-domain run.

</details>


### [198] [Causal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03003)
*Christopher Stith,Hossein Rahmani,Jesse C. Cresswell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Causal Foundation Models (CFMs), a novel paradigm that applies the foundation model approach from machine learning to causal inference. CFMs are pretrained neural networks capable of estimating causal quantities like average treatment effects on new datasets through in-context learning without requiring model updates or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional causal inference requires building a custom pipeline for each new problem—proposing a causal mechanism, selecting a compatible estimator, and training it. This is inefficient and lacks the scalability of modern machine learning approaches. Meanwhile, the foundation model paradigm has revolutionized ML across domains by pretraining once and applying to new tasks without fine-tuning. There was a need to bring this paradigm to causal inference.

Method: The paper provides a practical introduction to CFMs, covering necessary background in both causal inference and machine learning before discussing CFMs in detail. It includes example code and Jupyter notebooks for hands-on learning.

Result: An educational resource introducing the emerging field of CFMs, with practical examples and code implementations.

Conclusion: This work establishes a foundational understanding of CFMs, enabling researchers and practitioners to apply the foundation model paradigm to causal inference problems, potentially transforming how causal effects are estimated across diverse settings and modalities.

Abstract: Causal inference is the practice of estimating the effect of a treatment or intervention from data. It traditionally requires a bespoke pipeline for every new problem: first proposing a causal mechanism, selecting a compatible estimator, and finally training it. Meanwhile, across diverse settings and modalities, much of machine learning has shifted to the paradigm of foundation models: networks pretrained once at scale and applied to new tasks without fine-tuning. Causal foundation models (CFMs) bring this paradigm to causal inference. CFMs are pretrained neural networks that estimate causal quantities, such as the average treatment effect, on entirely new datasets using in-context learning without requiring model updates. This work provides a practical introduction to this emerging area. We summarize the necessary background in causal inference and machine learning before discussing CFMs. Throughout, we include example code and Jupyter notebooks.

</details>


### [199] [ObserverBench: Testing Mechanistic Estimates for Intervention and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03026)
*Vijay Erramilli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ObserverBench is a benchmark framework for evaluating whether internal estimators (observers) in mechanistic interpretability are adequate for their intended intervention, control, or safety tasks. It separates estimation accuracy from action loss to address the problem that accurate average estimates can still lead to poor actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that an internal estimate that is accurate on average can still choose a poor action when used for interventions such as activation steering, circuit removal, or safety monitoring. This motivates the need to evaluate observers not just by estimation accuracy but by the actual outcomes of the actions they guide.

Method: ObserverBench provides fixed task contracts that specify: the model, information boundary, allowed actions, decision rule, held-out cases, and loss. The benchmark reports estimation accuracy separately from the loss caused by the chosen action. It includes runnable baselines and table-based submissions for evaluating interpretability methods.

Result: In closed-loop control, observer errors matter at the starting point and along directions the allowed intervention can reach. On circuit-intervention tasks in GPT-2-small and Qwen2.5-7B, pairwise observers predict unseen effects more accurately without always choosing better actions; observers trained on action loss choose lower-loss actions. In safety triage, a score that perfectly separates violations can allocate a fixed intervention budget poorly when violations have different costs. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B-it, and prospectively frozen Qwen3.5-9B APPS tasks, AUROC can rank monitors differently from deployment loss, and the best information source changes across models. Sparse SAE readouts also trail their layer-matched dense controls on the reported Qwen panels.

Conclusion: Both estimation accuracy and action loss are needed to properly evaluate observers. The benchmark reveals that: (1) estimation accuracy doesn't guarantee good actions, (2) training observers on action loss improves outcomes, (3) standard metrics like AUROC can rank monitors differently from deployment loss, and (4) the best information source varies across models. ObserverBench provides fixed task contracts and runnable baselines for evaluating interpretability methods through the actions they enable.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability is increasingly used to guide interventions such as activation steering, circuit removal, and safety monitoring. Yet an internal estimate that is accurate on average can still choose a poor action.
  We present ObserverBench, a benchmark framework for testing whether an internal estimator---an observer---is adequate for the intervention, control, or safety task it directs. Each task fixes the model, information boundary, allowed actions, decision rule, held-out cases, and loss. The benchmark reports estimation accuracy separately from the loss caused by the chosen action.
  Theory and experiments show why both are needed. In closed-loop control, observer errors matter at the starting point and along directions the allowed intervention can reach. On circuit-intervention tasks in GPT-2-small and Qwen2.5-7B, pairwise observers predict unseen effects more accurately without always choosing better actions; observers trained on action loss choose lower-loss actions. In safety triage, a score that perfectly separates violations can allocate a fixed intervention budget poorly when violations have different costs. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B-it, and prospectively frozen Qwen3.5-9B APPS tasks, AUROC can rank monitors differently from deployment loss, and the best information source changes across models. Sparse SAE readouts also trail their layer-matched dense controls on the reported Qwen panels, under disclosed activation-density or checkpoint mismatches.
  ObserverBench provides fixed task contracts, runnable baselines, and table-based submissions for evaluating interpretability methods through the actions they enable.

</details>


### [200] [Learnable composition for neural operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03069)
*Zituo Chen,Baiming Zhang,Sili Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentDDM addresses the challenge of deploying neural operators across different domain geometries, sizes, or operating conditions than they were trained on. It proposes a two-stage approach: (1) pretrain a neural operator on small subdomains, then (2) freeze the operator and train only a lightweight composition module for the new target setting. This reduces the number of costly high-fidelity simulations needed for adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operators are efficient surrogates for physical simulation but suffer from accuracy degradation when deployed on settings (domain geometry, size, operating conditions) that differ from training. Existing supervised adaptation methods require costly high-fidelity simulations even for small target datasets, making deployment expensive. The goal is to reduce this deployment cost by designing pretraining and transfer jointly.

Method: LatentDDM uses a two-stage approach: (1) Local pretraining - train a neural operator to predict fields on small subdomains; (2) Transfer with frozen operator - for new settings, freeze the pretrained operator and train only a lightweight module that composes local predictions to handle the target domain. This is evaluated on two problems: steady Darcy flow (varying domain size) and unsteady flow around pitching airfoil (varying pitching frequency).

Result: For steady Darcy flow on larger domains, LatentDDM achieves 36-56% lower error compared to capacity-matched full-domain models after adaptation with only 16 target simulations. For unsteady flow around a pitching airfoil, it improves 20-step field rollouts both zero-shot and after few-shot calibration, particularly for fast-pitching scenarios beyond training range.

Conclusion: The co-designed approach of local pretraining combined with composition-level transfer offers a promising design principle for physical foundation models. It significantly reduces the computational cost of adapting neural operators to new deployment settings while maintaining accuracy across varying domain sizes and operating conditions.

Abstract: Neural operators are fast, differentiable surrogates for physical simulation, but their accuracy often degrades when domain geometry, size, or operating conditions differ from training. Supervised adaptation can recover accuracy, but even a small target set requires costly high-fidelity simulations. We therefore ask how pretraining and transfer can be designed together to reduce this deployment cost. LatentDDM first pretrains a neural operator to predict fields on small subdomains. For a new setting, it freezes this operator and trains only a lightweight module that composes the local predictions. We evaluate our method on two complementary problems: steady Darcy flow, where long-range pressure coupling must extend across increasingly large porous domains, and unsteady incompressible flow around a pitching airfoil, where rollout errors compound as target pitching frequencies exceed the training range. Compared with the capacity-matched models that process the full domain at once, LatentDDM's error is 36-56% lower on larger Darcy domains after adaptation with 16 target simulations. It also improves 20-step field rollouts in fast-pitching airfoil flow, both zero-shot and after few-shot calibration. These results identify the co-designed local pretraining and composition-level transfer as a promising design principle for physical foundation models.

</details>


### [201] [LeanStream: A Speculate-and-Refine Streaming Framework for Efficient on-Device LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03079)
*Renyuan Liu,Yuyang Leng,Kaiyan Liu,Yuzhou Zhong,Shaohan Hu,Chun-Fu,Chen,Peijun Zhao,Heechul Yun,Shuochao Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeanStream is a streaming speculate-and-refine framework for efficient on-device LLM inference that progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results, enabling fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference is attractive for privacy and responsiveness but challenging on mobile/embedded devices due to model weights exceeding available DRAM. Prior systems face a trade-off between accurate sparse execution decisions (requiring latest context) and efficient computation-I/O overlap (requiring early prediction), leading to serialization or redundant weight fetches.

Method: LeanStream uses a streaming speculate-and-refine framework that progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results, enabling fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O. Implemented on both mobile and embedded platforms.

Result: LeanStream reduces memory usage by 4.8× to 7.5× at the best throughput achieved by prior work, while further improving token generation throughput by 1.6× to 2.1×.

Conclusion: LeanStream significantly advances on-device LLM inference by achieving substantial memory reduction and throughput improvements, making it more practical for privacy-sensitive and latency-critical applications on mobile and embedded devices.

Abstract: On-device LLM inference is attractive for privacy and responsiveness, but remains challenging on mobile and embedded devices because model weights far exceed available DRAM. Prior systems exploit activation sparsity and offload weights to SSD or flash storage, but face a fundamental systems trade-off: accurate sparse execution decisions require the latest context, whereas efficient computation-I/O overlap requires early prediction. As a result, existing designs either serialize execution or incur redundant weight fetches, extra computation, and large cache overheads. We present LeanStream, a streaming speculate-and-refine framework for efficient on-device LLM inference. LeanStream progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results, enabling fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O. We implement LeanStream on both mobile and embedded platforms. Compared with prior on-device LLM inference systems, LeanStream reduces memory usage by 4.8$\times$ to 7.5$\times$ at the best throughput achieved by prior work, while further improving token generation throughput by 1.6$\times$ to 2.1$\times$.

</details>


### [202] [The Gradient Does Not See Rank: Rank-Indifference in Matrix-CODI on ProsQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03090)
*Samuel Larson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Matrix-valued latent tokens in chain-of-thought models do not use rank for reasoning - ablating rank to low dimensions does not affect accuracy, suggesting the model doesn't use superposition of parallel reasoning paths despite having a d x d matrix bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether matrix latents carry parallel reasoning paths via superposition, and whether rank serves as an observable tracking these paths. If superposition is used, truncating Z to low rank should hurt accuracy on tasks requiring multiple reasoning components.

Method: Trained matrix-CODI model across four regimes (three on ProsQA, one on GSM8K-Aug). Tested rank-k projection ablation across varying ranks. Tried four different readout architectures: bilinear reparametrization, bilinear+GELU, SVD-augmented (singular values through MLP), and quadratic (Z Z^T). Also used linear probe analysis and vanilla GPT-2 SFT as control.

Result: Rank-k projection curves remain flat within 0.6 percentage points across all conditions. Effective rank spans {4, 12, 13} with accuracy 81.0 +/- 2.0%. All four readout variants show flat curves (Spearman p-values: 0.63, 0.14, 0.82, 0.46). Linear probe on Z underperforms raw pretrained hidden states (AUC 0.673 vs 0.846). Vanilla GPT-2 control also shows flat rank-k curve.

Conclusion: Matrix latents are rank-blind - the model does not exploit superposition for parallel reasoning. The rank-k ablation confounds rank-blindness with position-irrelevance, and the matrix bottleneck provides no observable benefit for reasoning tasks tested.

Abstract: Continuous chain-of-thought models compress reasoning into latent tokens. Matrix-valued variants, which route each latent token through a d x d matrix bottleneck, introduce rank as a single-sample structural observable on the latent matrix Z. If matrix latents carry parallel reasoning paths via superposition, rank should track them, and truncating Z to low rank should hurt accuracy on tasks whose solutions plausibly require multiple components. Across four training regimes of a matrix-CODI model (three on ProsQA, one on GSM8K-Aug below the learning threshold), the rank-k projection ablation curve is flat to within 0.6 percentage points. A three-seed replication yields 81.0 +/- 2.0 percentage points accuracy while the final effective rank of Z spans {4, 12, 13}; the loss does not reward any particular rank. To test whether rank-blindness arises from the flatten-then-project readout alone, we trained four readouts: a bilinear reparametrization, a bilinear-plus-GELU readout nonlinear in Z, an SVD-augmented readout feeding singular values through an MLP, and a quadratic readout in Z Z^T. All four rank-k curves remain flat (Spearman p-values 0.63, 0.14, 0.82, 0.46). The flat curves persist for readouts nonlinear in Z. A linear probe on Z underperforms a raw pretrained hidden state at target prediction (AUC 0.673 vs. 0.846). A negative control on vanilla GPT-2 SFT (no matrix bottleneck, no Z, three seeds, n=500) reproduces a flat rank-k curve under the same intervention paradigm with pooled-mean range 0.20pp, and a random-h sensitivity floor lands at the same accuracy: the rank-k ablation alone conflates rank-blindness with position-irrelevance.

</details>


### [203] [Distilling deep optical flow stereo methods to retrieve dense three-dimensional wind fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03100)
*Thomas J. Vandal,Dong L. Wu,James L. Carr,Derek J. Posselt,Elise Penn,Tristan Ballard,August Posch,Kate Duffy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces deep optical flow to replace traditional window-based cross-correlation for stereo wind retrieval from geostationary satellites. A knowledge distillation approach transfers stereo wind knowledge to a single-satellite student model, eliminating multi-satellite overlap requirements while emulating height uncertainties for quality assurance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional AMVs suffer from circular dependency (NWP background states), inaccurate heights, high computational cost, and sparse retrievals. Stereo winds improve accuracy but remain computationally heavy with limited coverage. This work aims to improve efficiency while maintaining or improving accuracy.

Method: 1) Replace window-based tracking with deep optical flow for stereo matching; 2) Fine-tune using self-supervised geometric residual loss combined with supervised radiosonde reconstruction; 3) Distill stereo teacher model into single-satellite student model; 4) Student emulates chi-square and height uncertainties from teacher for quality assurance; 5) Validation via triple collocation against radiosondes, operational AMVs, ERA5, and EarthCARE cloud profiles.

Result: Stereo winds improve performance beyond operational AMVs for water vapor bands (6.2, 6.9, and 7.3 μm), with degradation in the long-wave infrared (11.2 μm) band. The student model generates winds across full-disk GEO imagery globally with improved computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Deep optical flow combined with knowledge distillation enables efficient single-satellite wind retrieval with quality assurance comparable to stereo winds. This approach eliminates the need for multi-satellite overlap while improving accuracy for water vapor bands, though long-wave infrared performance requires further investigation.

Abstract: Geostationary atmospheric motion vectors (AMVs) provide the dense horizontal wind vectors (u,v) and heights ingested into data assimilation systems. Traditional AMVs track features using window-based cross-correlation and estimate heights via infrared brightness temperatures paired with numerical weather prediction (NWP) background states, creating a circular dependency that yields inaccurate heights, high computational cost, and sparse retrievals. Stereo winds from GEO-GEO and GEO-LEO geometrically resolve heights from parallax shifts across different poses, eliminating NWP dependence and improving accuracy, but they remain computationally heavy with limited coverage. In this work, we replace window-based tracking in stereo matching with deep optical flow for efficient, improved retrieval. Fine-tuning balances a self-supervised geometric residual loss with supervised radiosonde reconstruction. To eliminate multi-satellite overlap requirements, we distill the stereo teacher into a single-satellite student model. Chi-square and height uncertainties from the teacher are emulated by the student for quality assurance. The student generates winds across full-disk GEO imagery globally. Validation compares stereo and student models against radiosondes, operational AMVs, ERA5 reanalysis, and EarthCARE cloud profiles. Results through triple collocation show that stereo winds improve performance beyond operational AMVs for water vapor bands (6.2, 6.9, and 7.3 μm), wit degradation in the long-wave infrared (11.2 μm) band.

</details>


### [204] [Scaling Laws, Tabular Data and Actuarial Ratemaking Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03106)
*Ronald Richman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether scaling laws observed in deep learning apply to actuarial ratemaking with tabular insurance data. Using a motor insurance portfolio, the study compares model families (TabM, Transformers, MLPs) across increasing data fractions, finding that TabM exhibits markedly stronger data scaling than Transformers and MLPs, while Transformer variants show weak parameter scaling unless augmented with inductive biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether analogous scaling regularities arise in actuarial ratemaking, where data are tabular, heterogeneous, and noisy, and where classical models such as GLMs remain strong baselines.

Method: Train models from different families across increasing fractions of the training data and multiple random seeds, evaluating out-of-sample Poisson deviance (a likelihood-based loss for Poisson count predictions).

Result: All model families improve with additional data, but scaling exponents differ substantially: TabM exhibits markedly stronger data scaling than purely supervised tabular Transformers and standard MLP baselines. Transformer variants show weak parameter scaling unless augmented with additional inductive biases (TabM-style adaptation or self-supervision).

Conclusion: Effective scaling on actuarial tabular tasks depends on architecture and loss function objective design, with simple increases in Transformer size providing limited gains. Results provide quantitative guidance on model selection by data regime.

Abstract: Scaling laws in modern deep learning describe how held-out loss improves as model capacity, training data, and compute increase, often following power-law trends. We investigate whether analogous scaling regularities arise in actuarial ratemaking, where data are tabular, heterogeneous, and noisy, and where classical models such as GLMs remain strong baselines. Using a real-world motor insurance portfolio, we train models from different families across increasing fractions of the training data and multiple random seeds, evaluating out-of-sample Poisson deviance, a likelihood-based loss for Poisson count predictions in which lower values indicate better held-out fit. We find that all model families improve with additional data, but scaling exponents differ substantially: TabM exhibits markedly stronger data scaling than purely supervised tabular Transformers and standard MLP baselines. Transformer variants show weak parameter scaling unless augmented with additional inductive biases (TabM-style adaptation or self-supervision). These results provide quantitative guidance on model selection by data regime and suggest that effective scaling on actuarial tabular tasks depends on architecture and loss function objective design, with simple increases in Transformer size providing limited gains.

</details>


### [205] [Kernel Reboot: Breaking the Boundaries of Neural Tangent Kernels for Neural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03117)
*Amir Mallak,Alaa Maalouf,Lior Wolf,Daniela Rus,Dan Rosenbaum*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses fast high-quality neural field reconstruction from sparse observations by developing three algorithms: NTK-KIP (non-linear support set distillation), MetaQuill (meta-learned initialization for INRs), and MetaQuill-KIP (fusion of both approaches). The key innovation is combining the expressiveness of kernel methods with meta-learning to achieve reusable task priors while maintaining lightweight per-instance adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural fields (NFs) map continuous coordinates to signals (color, density), but fast high-quality reconstruction from sparse observations remains difficult. Classical Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regression provides closed-form fits but is fundamentally linear and cannot accumulate reusable task priors, limiting its practical applicability.

Method: Three algorithms are developed: (1) NTK-KIP learns a distilled support set of coordinates (and optional labels) enabling a finite NTK to inpaint large missing regions from little observed data, yielding compact non-linear representations; (2) MetaQuill meta-learns a shared initialization for an INR so new scenes can be adapted by updating only a small task-specific weight offset, providing true feature learning and reusable priors; (3) MetaQuill-KIP fuses both approaches by seeding tasks with KIP-style non-linear warm starts, then refining only small offsets around meta-learned initializations.

Result: MetaQuill-KIP achieves high-PSNR reconstructions and semantically plausible inpainting under very sparse observations, while requiring only lightweight per-instance adaptation. This approach avoids the need for large pretrained generative priors and costly per-image tuning required by diffusion-style baselines.

Conclusion: NTK-driven neural fields can be made both non-linear and meta-learnable, successfully narrowing the gap between analytic kernels and practical few-shot reconstruction. The fusion of kernel-inspired processing with meta-learning enables reusable priors without sacrificing adaptation efficiency.

Abstract: Neural fields (NFs) map continuous coordinates to signals such as color or density, but fast high-quality reconstruction from sparse observations remains difficult. Classical Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regression gives closed-form fits, yet it is fundamentally linear and cannot accumulate reusable task priors. We develop three algorithms that address these gaps. NTK-KIP learns a distilled support set of coordinates (and optional labels) so that a finite NTK can inpaint large missing regions from little observed data, yielding a compact non-linear representation instead of a raw kernel solve. MetaQuill meta-learns a shared initialization for an INR so that new scenes can be adapted by updating only a small task-specific weight offset, which provides true feature learning and a reusable prior. Finally, MetaQuill-KIP fuses both ideas: it seeds the task with a KIP-style non-linear warm start, then refines only that small offset around the meta-learned initialization. MetaQuill-KIP achieves high-PSNR reconstructions and semantically plausible inpainting under very sparse observations, while requiring only lightweight per-instance adaptation, whereas diffusion-style baselines typically depend on large pretrained generative priors and costly per-image tuning. This shows that NTK-driven neural fields can be made both non-linear and meta-learnable, narrowing the gap between analytic kernels and practical few-shot reconstruction.

</details>


### [206] [Routing Is Not Enough: Diagnosing Intra-Adapter Subspace Contention in MoE+LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03150)
*Mehreen Hossain Chowdhury,Nowshin Mahjabin,Ahmed Shafin Ruhan,Md Azam Hossain,Abu Raihan Mostofa Kamal,Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common assumption that MoE routing alone can separate domain-specific updates in multi-domain fine-tuning. The authors discover that despite near-disjoint expert routing, negative transfer still occurs due to gradient interference within shared low-rank adapter subspaces. They propose SpawnLoRA, which dynamically adds gated sub-adapters inside MoE experts to mitigate this interference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to test the fundamental assumption in MoE+LoRA multi-domain fine-tuning: that token-level routing naturally separates domain-specific updates. The authors observed that despite this assumption, negative transfer still occurs, and they wanted to understand why and how to fix it.

Method: 1. Test the routing separation assumption using Python code + biomedical text + mathematical reasoning
2. Introduce two diagnostic metrics: Jaccard routing overlap (expert sharing) and adapter-gradient cosine similarity (update compatibility)
3. Propose SpawnLoRA: dynamically adds gated sub-adapters inside MoE experts when adapter-level contention is detected, while keeping the router fixed
4. Evaluate on Phi-tiny-MoE-instruct and OLMoE-1B-7B across multiple mixture settings

Result: Despite near-disjoint expert routing across domains, adding biomedical data substantially increases code perplexity, indicating routing separation alone cannot prevent negative transfer. The interference arises from nearly orthogonal domain gradients competing within the same low-rank adapter subspace. SpawnLoRA effectively reduces negative transfer compared with standard and rank-adaptive LoRA.

Conclusion: Structural separation inside experts provides benefits beyond routing or rank expansion alone. The key insight is that gradient interference occurs at the adapter level, not just the routing level, requiring structural solutions within the expert modules themselves.

Abstract: Multi-domain fine-tuning often combines MoE routing with LoRA, assuming that token-level routing separates domain-specific updates. We test this assumption in MoE+LoRA using Python code paired with biomedical text and mathematical reasoning. Although these domains show near-disjoint expert routing, adding biomedical data substantially increases code perplexity, indicating that routing separation alone may not prevent negative transfer. To localize the failure, we introduce Jaccard routing overlap and adapter-gradient cosine similarity, which measure expert sharing and update compatibility, respectively. These diagnostics indicate that interference arises mostly from nearly orthogonal domain gradients competing within the same low-rank adapter subspace. We address this issue with SpawnLoRA, which dynamically adds gated sub-adapters inside MoE experts when adapter-level contention is detected, while keeping the router fixed. We evaluate SpawnLoRA on Phi-tiny-MoE-instruct and OLMoE-1B-7B across multiple mixture settings and find that it effectively reduces negative transfer compared with standard and rank-adaptive LoRA. These results demonstrate that structural separation inside experts provides benefits beyond routing or rank expansion alone.

</details>


### [207] [Frontier LLMs are effective batch optimizers: Assessing reasoning models in continuous and discrete settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03177)
*Frank Hu,Shriram Chennakesavalu,David Graff*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether modern frontier large language models (LLMs) can serve as effective batch optimizers in both continuous and discrete optimization settings. The study finds that while LLMs perform competitively as zero-shot optimizers for numerical test functions, they underperform classical optimization methods. However, LLMs excel in semantically rich discrete spaces that align with their pretraining data structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs have become attractive priors for optimization due to their large-scale pretraining, but their effectiveness in batch optimization settings remains underexplored, particularly for the current generation of reasoning LLMs.

Method: The researchers evaluated the performance of current frontier LLMs as batch optimizers in both continuous and discrete settings, comparing them against classical non-LLM optimization approaches.

Result: LLMs are competitive zero-shot batch optimizers for numerical test functions, but their performance is brittle compared to classical methods. However, they perform significantly better in semantically rich settings, particularly when navigating discrete spaces similar to their pretraining data.

Conclusion: LLM batch optimization behavior is most effective when reasoning over discrete spaces that structurally resemble their pretraining data, suggesting that their strengths lie in semantically-rich discrete optimization rather than numerical optimization.

Abstract: Frontier large language models (LLMs) have become attractive priors for optimization due to their large-scale pretraining that enables them to navigate a variety of optimization settings. However, the effectiveness of modern reasoning LLMs in batch optimization settings remains underexplored. Here we investigate the performance of the current generation of frontier LLMs as batch optimizers in both continuous and discrete settings. We find that while LLMs are competitive zero-shot batch optimizers for numerical test functions, their performance is brittle compared to classical non-LLM optimization approaches. However, LLM priors are significantly better in semantically rich settings, indicating that their batch optimization behavior is highly effective when navigating and reasoning over the discrete spaces most similar in structure to their pretraining data.

</details>


### [208] [Portable Causal Fairness Across Synthetic Data Generator Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03180)
*Steven Golob,Sikha Pentyala,Martine De Cock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper tests whether DECAF fairness mechanisms (previously tested only on one non-private GAN) transfer across nine different synthetic data generators from three families (marginals-based, GAN, diffusion), each with differentially private variants, across three privacy guarantee levels. The mechanism works universally, with causal diffusion achieving the best fairness-fidelity tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work (DECAF) showed that fairness can be enforced via causal graph edge cuts on a single non-private GAN. However, it was unknown whether this mechanism is specific to DECAF or generalizable to other generator families. Additionally, the effect of differential privacy on fairness was unexplored.

Method: The study ported three fairness definitions to nine generators across three families (marginals-based, GAN, diffusion) with differentially private variants. Experiments involved 2,520 matched-pair runs on Adult and COMPAS datasets, testing fairness constraints via causal graph edge cuts.

Result: The fairness mechanism transfers universally across all generator families. The new causal diffusion backbone produces the fairest release among all tested families while maintaining fidelity close to the marginals tier. Adding fairness cuts minimally impacts fidelity (downstream classifier loses only 0.07-0.15 AUC on average). Notably, adding privacy guarantees does not make the data less fair.

Conclusion: DECAF's fairness mechanism is generalizable beyond its original implementation. Causal diffusion generators offer the best fairness-fidelity tradeoff. Privacy guarantees and fairness can be achieved simultaneously without compromising each other.

Abstract: When a statistical agency or regulator releases synthetic data in place of sensitive records, it chooses the generator that produces the table, and can shape that generator so unfair pathways are absent. DECAF made this concrete on one non-private GAN: three fairness definitions become three sets of edge cuts on the generator's causal graph. Whether the mechanism belongs to DECAF, or to causal factorisation itself, was untested. We port all three definitions to nine generators from three unrelated families (marginals-based, GAN, and diffusion, each with differentially private variants), across three levels of formal privacy guarantee, over 2,520 matched-pair runs on Adult and COMPAS datasets. The mechanism transfers everywhere, and our new causal diffusion backbone yields the fairest release of any family we tested, at fidelity close to the marginals tier. Applying the cut barely moves fidelity, only costs a downstream classifier about $0.07$ to $0.15$ AUC on average, and adding privacy guarantees don't make the data less fair.

</details>


### [209] [Language-encoded network topology enables large language models to reason about complex networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03229)
*Ucchwas Talukder Utsha,Sakib Mostafa,James Zou,Md Tauhidul Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioGlyph is a system that translates network topology into an interpretable language of structural roles (hubs, community cores, connectors) to help LLMs answer structural reasoning questions about networks, outperforming existing representations by up to 26 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with structural reasoning about networks when given as edge lists, sentences, or tables because the structural meaning must be inferred rather than explicitly stated.

Method: BioGlyph combines graph partitioning and structural measurements to identify roles (hubs, community cores, cross-community connectors), then uses fixed rules to translate them into a universal vocabulary describing each element through its structural role, supporting evidence, and semantic consequences.

Result: Outperforms edge-based, numerical and learned representations by up to 26 percentage points in system accuracy across 20 networks spanning five domains. The gain is more prominent in dense, community-structured networks and diminishes in sparse networks. In yeast protein-interaction networks, cross-community connectors are enriched for essential genes while peripheral proteins are depleted.

Conclusion: BioGlyph provides an interpretable representation for both language models and scientists to reason about network structure, enabling LLMs to effectively answer structural reasoning questions and revealing biological organization in protein-interaction networks.

Abstract: Networks describe systems in biology and beyond, from protein interactions and social relationships to power grids and citation records. Reasoning about such systems requires understanding their structure: which elements are central, which connections bridge separate communities, and how it changes when elements are removed. Although large language models (LLMs) excel at natural language, they struggle with such questions when networks are given as edge lists, sentences or measurement tables, because their structural meaning must be inferred. Here we introduce BioGlyph, which compiles network topology into an interpretable and transferable language of structural roles. BioGlyph combines graph partitioning and structural measurements to identify roles such as hubs, community cores and cross-community connectors, and fixed rules to translate them into a universal vocabulary. The representation describes each element through its structural role, supporting evidence and semantic consequences, leaving both the network and the LLM unchanged. Across twenty networks spanning five domains, BioGlyph substantially improves open LLMs' ability to answer structural reasoning questions, outperforming edge-based, numerical and learned representations by up to 26 percentage points in system accuracy. Ablations show that the gain comes from explicitly encoding structural roles in semantically interpretable terms. The gain is more prominent in dense, community-structured networks and diminishes in sparse networks whose topology is more readily inferred from text. In a budding-yeast protein-interaction network, BioGlyph exposes biological organization: cross-community connectors are enriched for essential genes, whereas peripheral proteins are depleted. BioGlyph thus provides an interpretable representation for both language models and scientists to reason about network structure.

</details>


### [210] [The 2026 PNPL Competition: Word Classification and Efficient Cross-Subject Generalisation in LibriBrain100](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03231)
*Francesco Mantegna,Gereon Elvers,Dulhan Jayalath,Gilad Landau,Tasha Kim,Miran Özdogan,Luisa Kurth,Teyun Kwon,SungJun Cho,Benjamin Ballyk,Alex Fung,Anna Greer,Pratik Somaiya,Christian Herff,Yorguin Mantilla Ramos,Hamza Abdelhedi,Karim Jerbi,Greg Farquhar,Brendan Shillingford,Mark Woolrich,Oiwi Parker Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The 2026 PNPL competition introduces LibriBrain100 to advance non-invasive speech decoding toward practical brain-computer interfaces. It features two tracks: Deep (within-subject word classification at scale) and Broad (cross-subject generalization with limited fine-tuning data from 40 to 10 minutes).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the 2025 competition achieved high performance on speech detection (95.6% F1) and phoneme classification (73.6% F1) within subjects, a practical BCI requires generalization to new users from minutes of data, not hours. This addresses the critical gap between laboratory performance and clinical feasibility.

Method: The competition expanded the LibriBrain dataset to LibriBrain100 with 32 additional subjects (~40 minutes each) and increased within-subject data (~80 hours). Two complementary tracks were established: Deep track aims for maximum within-subject word classification performance; Broad track tests cross-subject generalization with progressively reducing fine-tuning data (40→20→10 minutes).

Result: The 2025 competition achieved significant advances with F1-macro scores of 95.6% (speech detection) and 73.6% (phoneme classification). The 2026 competition sets new challenges in word classification, with the Broad track specifically targeting the 10-minute fine-tuning threshold that represents clinically feasible BCI deployment.

Conclusion: This competition curriculum progresses toward a practical non-invasive BCI capable of restoring communication to people with profound paralysis by addressing the cross-subject generalization challenge with limited data—a critical step toward clinically viable speech decoding systems.

Abstract: The ambition of the 2025 PNPL competition (Landau et al., 2025) was to launch a multi-year curriculum for non-invasive speech decoding.
