Designed to progress from foundational tasks toward the linguistic complexity required for a practical brain-computer interface (BCI), it set the stage with speech detection and phoneme classification tasks. Winning submissions reached F1-macro scores of 95.6% and 73.6% on the respective tasks (Elvers et al., 2026), highly significant advances. This success was built on the LibriBrain dataset (Özdogan et al., 2025), the largest within-subject MEG dataset recorded at the time with ${\sim}50$ hours of data for one subject. However, while within-subject scale drives strong decoding performance, a practical BCI must generalise to new users from minutes of data, not hours.
  The 2026 PNPL competition responds to this challenge with LibriBrain100 (Mantegna et al., 2026), an extended LibriBrain dataset with 32 additional subjects (${\sim}40$ minutes each) plus even more within-subject data (${\sim}80$ hours). Advancing the curriculum of tasks to focus on word classification, two complementary tracks are presented in this competition: the Deep track targets within-subject word classification at scale, aiming at the best possible performance; the Broad track targets cross-subject generalisation, progressively reducing the amount of subject-specific fine-tuning data from ${\sim}40$ to ${\sim}20$ to ${\sim}10$ minutes, a duration that falls within a clinically feasible range and brings us a step closer to a non-invasive BCI capable of restoring communication to people living with profound paralysis.

</details>


### [211] [B2B Customer Conversion Prediction: A Document Representation, Graph Theory, and CatBoost Driven Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03239)
*Tianqi Wang,Sheikh Shams Azam,Wan Eih Huang,Anton Wiranata,Christopher G. Brinton,Jan P. Allebach*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses B2B customer targeting and conversion prediction in one-time selling contexts with long buying cycles. The authors propose a framework that aggregates individual contacts to customer level using multiple keys, clusters non-standardized company names using a novel architecture, generates features, and applies CatBoost model achieving 91% prediction accuracy. Personalized campaign recommendations are then derived from prediction results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In B2B contexts with one-time selling and long buying cycles (months to years), effectively targeting high-potential customers and recommending personalized campaigns is critical for marketing efficiency. The lack of systematic approaches for customer data aggregation, feature generation, and conversion prediction motivated this research.

Method: The paper proposes: (1) An algorithm to aggregate individual contacts to B2B customer level using multiple keys; (2) A novel architecture to cluster non-standardized keys (company names) handling irregularities like spelling mistakes and variants; (3) Feature definition and generation; (4) CatBoost model for customer conversion prediction; (5) Personalized campaign recommendations based on prediction results and model analysis.

Result: The framework achieves 91% prediction accuracy for customer conversion prediction. Based on this, personalized campaign recommendations are provided to foster conversion.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully addresses B2B customer data aggregation, feature generation, and conversion prediction. The 91% accuracy demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach, and the derived personalized campaign recommendations can help foster customer conversion in one-time selling B2B contexts.

Abstract: In the one-time selling B2B context, the buying cycle may last months or even years. During the long process, targeting customers that have a high potential to make purchases and recommending personalized campaigns accordingly are important for effective marketing. For this goal, we study the following problems, B2B customer data aggregation, customer feature generation, and prediction of whether a B2B customer would show interest in making a purchase (i.e., prediction of conversion into sales funnel). We propose an algorithm to aggregate individual contacts to the B2B customer level based on multiple keys. For non-standardized keys such as company names, we propose a novel architecture to cluster them in a domain encompassing irregularities such as spelling mistakes and spelling variants. We then define and generate a set of features and apply the CatBoost model for customer conversion prediction. Our framework achieves 91\% prediction accuracy. Based on the prediction results and analysis of the model, we then discuss personalized campaign recommendations to foster conversion.

</details>


### [212] [FlowBalance: Verifier-Grounded Self-Improvement from On-Policy Reasoning Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03241)
*Zixun Huang,Kishan Panaganti,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowBalance is a verifier-grounded self-improvement method for reasoning models that addresses the fragility of learning from on-policy experience. It uses a frozen training-time policy to generate token-level log-probability gains, calibrates these with verifier-derived group advantage through energy-based reweighting, and employs trajectory balance for normalization. The method achieves better performance than FlowRL on mathematical reasoning tasks (Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B), with improved training speed, stability, and strategy diversity while avoiding response-length collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The inner loop of self-improvement in reasoning models is fragile because terminal verifiers provide reliable but sparse supervision, while dense same-model guidance can reinforce false confidence or overconcentrate learning on narrow solution modes. Current methods like OPSD suffer from response-length collapse and lack correct-strategy diversity.

Method: FlowBalance introduces a verifier-grounded self-improvement approach that: (1) Uses a frozen training-time view of the same policy to produce token-level log-probability gains with privileged context; (2) Aggregates these into trajectory-level self-guidance scores; (3) Calibrates scores with verifier-derived group advantage—retaining guidance on positive-advantage trajectories, reversing on negative-advantage, and disabling when no outcome preference exists; (4) Applies energy-based exponential reweighting to a reference policy; (5) Uses profiled trajectory balance with one log-partition estimate per rollout group to fit the normalized target.

Result: FlowBalance improves average performance over FlowRL on both Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B models on mathematical reasoning tasks. It also improves training speed and stability, avoids direct OPSD's response-length collapse, and exhibits higher correct-strategy diversity in AIME24 diagnostic evaluations.

Conclusion: FlowBalance achieves outcome-calibrated self-guidance via trajectory balance without requiring a separate token-level imitation loss. The method provides theoretical guarantees including within-group contrast preservation, minimum-change reverse-KL characterization, monotonic verifier control of target reward, and exact correction against false-positive self-guidance on rejected responses.

Abstract: A reasoning model can improve from its own on-policy experience, but this inner loop is fragile: terminal verifiers provide reliable yet sparse supervision, while dense same-model guidance can reinforce false confidence or overconcentrate learning on a narrow solution mode. We introduce FlowBalance, a verifier-grounded self-improvement method that learns a normalized distribution over complete responses. For each on-policy trajectory, a frozen training-time view of the same policy uses privileged context to produce token-level log-probability gains, which are aggregated into a trajectory-level self-guidance score. FlowBalance calibrates this score with the verifier-derived group advantage: guidance is retained on positive-advantage trajectories, reversed on negative-advantage trajectories, and disabled when the rollout group provides no outcome preference. The resulting energy exponentially reweights a reference policy, and profiled trajectory balance fits the normalized target with one log-partition estimate per rollout group. This realizes outcome-calibrated self-guidance via trajectory balance, without a separate token-level imitation loss. Our analysis establishes within-group contrast preservation, a minimum-change reverse-KL characterization, monotonic verifier control of target reward, and an exact correction against false-positive self-guidance on rejected responses. On mathematical reasoning, FlowBalance improves average performance over FlowRL on both Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, while also improving training speed and stability, avoiding direct OPSD's response-length collapse, and exhibiting higher correct-strategy diversity in a controlled AIME24 diagnostic.

</details>


### [213] [Selective Hypergraph Refinement for Frozen Graph Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03265)
*Zimo Si*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Selective Hypergraph Refinement (SHR), a post-processing method that improves clustering results of frozen graph models by exploiting attribute hypergraphs to supplement higher-order relations, without modifying model parameters, node representations, or the original graph structure. Evaluation shows modest but positive gains (0.066-0.137 pp macro gain) with minimal changes to hard assignments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-clustering methods improve performance by optimizing model parameters and node representations, but there are limited means to further refine already trained and frozen models. The authors aim to address this gap by developing a post-processing technique that can improve clustering results without any model updates or additional training.

Method: The proposed SHR method uses an attribute hypergraph to supplement higher-order relations that ordinary graphs cannot express. It generates candidate residual directions from the hypergraph and evaluates their reliability using graph structure, node attributes, and matched-null evidence. Nodes are updated only when there is sufficient support; otherwise, their original assignments are retained. The method selectively updates nodes based on assignment gap and directional strength of refinement.

Result: In controlled common-suite evaluation: 13 of 15 backbone-dataset cells showed positive mean macro gain, one produced exact no-action, and one was negative. Cell-equal macro gain was 0.066 pp (95% bootstrap CI: [0.030, 0.107] pp), with only 0.209% of hard assignments changed on average. In broader 15-combination native-interface evaluation, macro gain was 0.137 pp at a mean change ratio of 0.375%.

Conclusion: Frozen clustering outputs retain a limited but measurable refinement space after training. The effect is heterogeneous across backbone-dataset pairs, and broader coverage increases exposure to negative transfer. The method demonstrates that post-processing can yield modest improvements without modifying the underlying model, though the gains are small and context-dependent.

Abstract: Existing graph-clustering methods typically improve clustering performance by optimizing model parameters and node representations. Effective means of further improving the clustering results of an already trained and frozen model, however, remain limited. We study post-processing for frozen graph clustering. After checkpoint fixation, the procedure uses no labels and updates neither model parameters, node representations, nor the original graph structure. Instead, it exploits an attribute hypergraph to supplement higher-order relations that ordinary graphs cannot readily express, thereby refining existing cluster assignments. Because global hypergraph refinement can yield both performance gains and erroneous updates, we propose Selective Hypergraph Refinement (SHR). The method generates candidate residual directions from the hypergraph and evaluates their reliability using graph structure, node attributes, and matched-null evidence. It updates only nodes with sufficient support and otherwise retains their original assignments. Further analysis shows that whether a node changes cluster is jointly governed by its native assignment gap and the directional strength of the refinement. In a controlled common-suite evaluation, 13 of 15 backbone-dataset cells had a positive mean macro gain, one produced exact no-action, and one was negative. The cell-equal macro gain was 0.066 pp (95% bootstrap CI, [0.030, 0.107] pp), while only 0.209% of hard assignments changed on average. A broader 15-combination native-interface evaluation yielded a macro gain of 0.137 pp at a mean change ratio of 0.375%. These results indicate that frozen clustering outputs retain a limited but measurable refinement space after training. The effect is heterogeneous across backbone-dataset pairs, and broader coverage also increases exposure to negative transfer.

</details>


### [214] [Latent Energy Action Planning with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03294)
*Phu Pham,Aniket Bera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEAP (Latent Energy Action Planning) optimizes complete action horizons through a frozen latent world model by coupling terminal latent goal matching with terminal-window state energy, achieving 94.8% mean success rate vs 77.5% for prior LeWM+CEM approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent world models enable efficient model predictive control from high-dimensional observations, but optimizing a single learned latent objective can produce action sequences whose decoder-predicted terminal descriptor fails to match the goal descriptor.

Method: LEAP treats the complete action horizon as a differentiable variable and optimizes it through a frozen LeWorldModel (LeWM). It couples terminal latent goal matching with a terminal-window state energy (requiring predicted terminal latent and decoder-predicted terminal descriptor to agree with goal), uses a frozen goal-conditioned proposal for initialization, a quasi-Newton solver for refinement through autoregressive rollout, and post-optimization projection to enforce admissible action range.

Result: Across four control domains using officially released LeWM checkpoints, LEAP raised mean success from 77.5% (LeWM+CEM) to 94.8%, a 17.3-percentage-point improvement, while retaining the frozen LeWM representation and predictor.

Conclusion: LEAP demonstrates that coupling terminal latent goal matching with terminal-window state energy in a differentiable planning framework significantly improves goal achievement while maintaining the frozen world model representation.

Abstract: Latent world models support efficient model predictive control from high-dimensional observations, yet optimizing a single learned latent objective can favor action sequences whose decoder-predicted terminal descriptor does not match the goal descriptor. We introduce Latent Energy Action Planning (LEAP), which treats the complete action horizon as a differentiable variable and optimizes it through a frozen LeWorldModel (LeWM). LEAP couples terminal latent goal matching with a terminal-window state energy. Low energy requires the predicted terminal latent to agree with the goal latent and the decoder-predicted terminal descriptor to agree with the goal descriptor. A frozen goal-conditioned proposal initializes the search, a quasi-Newton solver refines actions through the autoregressive rollout, and post-optimization projection enforces the admissible action range. Across four control domains using the officially released LeWM checkpoints, the complete LEAP planning system raises mean success from 77.5% for LeWM planned with the cross-entropy method (LeWM+CEM) to 94.8% under a matched protocol, a 17.3-percentage-point improvement, while retaining the frozen LeWM representation and predictor.

</details>


### [215] [Geometry-Aware Graph Construction via Adaptive Spectral Bandwidth Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03306)
*Ecem Bozkurt,Antonio Ortega*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the critical challenge of selecting the Gaussian bandwidth parameter (sigma) in kernelized graph methods. The authors propose an adaptive per-node bandwidth criterion that matches the kernel's effective rank to the local intrinsic dimension estimated via minimum spanning tree, ensuring spectral complexity aligns with the underlying manifold's geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Gaussian bandwidth parameter sigma in kernelized graph methods (spectral clustering, diffusion maps, sparse kernel regression) critically affects performance. When sigma is too small, local complexity is overestimated; when too large, geometric discrimination is lost. Current methods lack a principled way to choose sigma that adapts to local manifold structure.

Method: The authors propose a per-node adaptive bandwidth criterion that jointly matches the kernel's effective rank to the local intrinsic dimension estimated via minimum spanning tree. The approach anchors the search in the manifold-consistent log-log scaling regime.

Result: Evaluated on CIFAR-100 using six different encoders, the adaptive bandwidth method consistently improves leave-one-out (LOO) classification and label propagation (LP) accuracy compared to fixed-bandwidth methods and competing adaptive approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed adaptive bandwidth selection provides a principled and effective approach for kernelized graph methods, achieving consistent improvements in semi-supervised learning tasks by aligning spectral complexity with manifold intrinsic complexity.

Abstract: Kernelized graph methods - spectral clustering, diffusion maps, and sparse kernel -regression graphs - that use Gaussian kernels depend on the choice of Gaussian bandwidth sigma, which governs the spectral character of the local kernel operator. When sigma is too small, the kernel overestimates local complexity and treats each sample as an independent direction; when sigma is too large, the kernel collapses multiple directions together, the condition number diverges, and all geometric discrimination is lost. We propose a choice of scale to make the spectral complexity of the kernel consistent with the intrinsic complexity of the underlying manifold. We propose a per-node bandwidth criterion that operationalizes this principle by jointly matching the kernel's effective rank to the local intrinsic dimension estimated via minimum spanning tree, anchoring the search in the manifold-consistent log-log scaling regime. We evaluate SSL embeddings from six encoders on CIFAR-100, showing that adaptive bandwidth consistently improves leave-one-out (LOO) classification and label propagation (LP) accuracy over fixed-bandwidth methods and competing adaptive methods.

</details>


### [216] [Risk and Anomaly Identification for Distribution Network Optimal Operation Based on Reinforcement Learning and Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03308)
*Ziqi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the joint problem of risk identification (for in-distribution stochastic conditions) and anomaly detection (for out-of-distribution behaviors) in distribution networks. The authors propose a deep reinforcement learning (DRL) framework that integrates distributional and Bayesian DRL to quantify uncertainty by decomposing it into aleatoric (inherent risk) and epistemic (anomaly detection) components. Epistemic uncertainty drives exploration and OOD detection, while aleatoric uncertainty characterizes operational risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern distribution networks face pervasive uncertainty, requiring operators to identify both inherent risks in stochastic yet normal conditions and anomalies from out-of-distribution behaviors (e.g., unusual loads, extreme weather, cyber-physical attacks). Existing methods fail to jointly address these two distinct types of uncertainty in a unified framework.

Method: The paper proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework that combines distributional DRL and Bayesian DRL to achieve second-order uncertainty quantification. This decomposition separates total uncertainty into: (1) Aleatoric uncertainty - models inherent stochasticity in normal operations; (2) Epistemic uncertainty - captures model uncertainty due to limited knowledge, used for out-of-distribution detection and fallback control. The epistemic component also guides exploration during training.

Result: Simulation results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed DRL agent and the uncertainty quantification scheme in both characterizing operational risks and detecting anomalous out-of-distribution conditions.

Conclusion: The proposed uncertainty-aware DRL framework successfully integrates risk identification and anomaly detection for optimal distribution network operation. The decomposition of aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty provides a principled way to distinguish between inherent operational risks and out-of-distribution anomalies, enabling better decision-making for network operators.

Abstract: Reliable operation of modern distribution networks requires timely identification of operational risks and anomalous events under pervasive uncertainty. In practice, operators must identify risks that are inherent in stochastic yet in-distribution conditions, and anomalies that correspond to out-of-distribution behaviors such as unusual load patterns, extreme weather or cyber-physical attacks. This paper addresses this joint risk and anomaly identification problem for optimal distribution network operation and proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework that is explicitly uncertainty aware. We integrate distributional and Bayesian deep reinforcement learning to realize a second- order uncertainty quantification scheme that decomposes total uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components, which are respectively used to characterize inherent risk and out-of- distribution anomalies. The resulting epistemic estimates drive both exploration during training and out-of-distribution detec- tion with fallback control during deployment, whereas aleatoric estimates are used to characterize intrinsic operational risk. Simulation results demonstrate the performance of our DRL agent and the effectiveness of the uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [217] [DE-Venus: A Data-Efficient RLVR Framework for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03324)
*Shenzhi Yang,Guangcheng Zhu,Kai Tang,Zhengqing Zang,Xing Zheng,Haobo Wang,Yingfan Ma,Bowen Song,Bo Han,Bo An,Lei Feng,Weiqiang Wang,Junbo Zhao,Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DE-Venus is a unified framework for data-efficient reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) that treats supervision as evolving state across three modules: Active Data Selection, Weak Supervision Construction, and Training-Time Supervision Refinement. It supports seven representative methods and reduces annotation/training costs to 10% of labels or 13% of relevant data while achieving 63%--75% faster convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR improves LLM reasoning but is constrained by expensive on-policy rollouts and costs of obtaining reliable targets at scale. Existing methods address sample selection, incomplete supervision, or noisy labels separately, often entangling supervision logic with distributed training and hindering controlled comparison and reuse.

Method: DE-Venus organizes the supervision lifecycle into three modules: (1) Active Data Selection allocates training and annotation budgets; (2) Weak Supervision Construction derives learning signals from unlabeled examples; (3) Training-Time Supervision Refinement filters or corrects unreliable supervision. It expresses method-specific decisions as offline dataset transitions or online transformations of targets, rewards, batches, and advantages while preserving verl's distributed execution contracts.

Result: Across public benchmarks and three business scenarios, separate configurations preserve or improve model quality with only 10% of labels or as little as 13% of relevant data; selected business configurations also reduce observed convergence steps by 63%--75%.

Conclusion: DE-Venus reduces annotation and training costs without sacrificing scalable RL execution, providing a unified framework that enables controlled comparison and reuse of different RLVR methods.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves large language model reasoning, but its practical scaling is constrained by expensive on-policy rollouts and the cost of obtaining reliable targets at scale. Existing methods address sample selection, incomplete supervision, or noisy labels separately, often entangling supervision logic with distributed training and hindering controlled comparison and reuse. We present DE-Venus, a unified framework for data-efficient RLVR that treats supervision as evolving state across data preparation and policy optimization. It organizes this lifecycle into three modules: Active Data Selection allocates training and annotation budgets; Weak Supervision Construction derives learning signals from unlabeled examples; and Training-Time Supervision Refinement filters or corrects unreliable supervision. DE-Venus supports seven representative methods and a data-selection pipeline by expressing method-specific decisions as offline dataset transitions or online transformations of targets, rewards, batches, and advantages while preserving verl's distributed execution contracts. Across public benchmarks and three business scenarios, separate configurations preserve or improve model quality with only 10% of labels or as little as 13% of relevant data; selected business configurations also reduce observed convergence steps by 63%--75%. DE-Venus thus reduces annotation and training costs without sacrificing scalable RL execution.

</details>


### [218] [A Large Open Multi-Energy Corpus of Soil Compaction Tests, with Machine-Learning Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03337)
*Sompote Youwai,Chana Phutthananon,Warat Kongkitkul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper releases a large corpus of 2,854 compaction tests from six public sources (162 provenance groups, 4 Proctor energy levels, fines 1.5-100%). The optimum degree of saturation averages 0.815 (CV=11%). A tabular foundation model achieves R²=0.824 for density and R²=0.784 for water content. Compactive energy is marginally negligible but conditionally decisive. Predictions respect zero-air-voids physical limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous correlations between compaction parameters and soil classification are limited by small sample sizes (1-400 specimens), single-laboratory data, single compactive energy, and are seldom released publicly. This study aims to create a comprehensive, open-access dataset and baseline model for engineering fill specification.

Method: Compiled 2,854 laboratory compaction tests from six public sources. Audited each record to its named Proctor method with no inferred energy. Screened data using zero-air-voids condition (removed 11.8% of harmonised records, 5.7% of those with measured specific gravity). Built tabular foundation models using classification suite and compaction standard as predictors. Applied symbolic regression to yield closed-form equations coupled through a phase relation.

Result: Optimum degree of saturation = 0.815 at CV=11%. Foundation model R²: 0.824 (density), 0.784 (water content) under random folds; 0.727/0.696 with provenance-based folds; 0.520/0.614 with whole source held out. Compactive energy negligible marginally but decisive conditionally. Modified-Proctor density: R²=0.740 with energy, -0.651 without. Symbolic regression yields closed forms respecting zero-air-voids limit.

Conclusion: A material share of published compaction data is physically impossible. The optimum degree of saturation is a baseline (mean=0.815, CV=11%), not a constant. Compactive energy is conditionally decisive—essential for modified Proctor but negligible for standard Proctor. The predictive model is suitable for screening, not acceptance, of engineered fill specifications.

Abstract: Every engineered fill is specified by a maximum dry density and an optimum moisture content. Each determination needs a full Proctor test. Published correlations rest on one to four hundred specimens, usually from one laboratory at one compactive energy, and are seldom released. This paper releases a corpus without those limits. It holds 2,854 laboratory compaction tests from six public sources, across 162 provenance groups and four Proctor energy levels, with fines from 1.5 to 100%. Every record is audited to the Proctor method its source names, and no energy is inferred. Screening on the zero-air-voids condition removed 11.8% of harmonised records, and 5.7% of those with a measured specific gravity. A material share of published compaction data is physically impossible. The optimum degree of saturation over the corpus is 0.815 at a coefficient of variation of 11%. That is a baseline, not a constant. Both parameters are then estimated from one classification suite and the compaction standard. A tabular foundation model reaches R2 0.824 for density and 0.784 for water content under random folds. It reaches 0.727 and 0.696 with folds drawn around provenance, and 0.520 and 0.614 with a whole source held out. Compactive energy is negligible marginally yet decisive conditionally. Density on the 66 modified-Proctor records is predicted at R2 0.740 with it and -0.651 without. Symbolic regression yields closed forms coupled through a phase relation. No predicted pair can then exceed the zero-air-voids line. The predictions are for screening, not acceptance.

</details>


### [219] [Gradients Know What Outcomes Don't: Unlocking Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning with Gradient-Aligned Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03342)
*Leqi Zheng,Jinbo Su,Fang Niu,Chaokun Wang,Weiping Wang,Jiajun Zhang,Shannan Yan,Jie Wu,Zhaolu Kang,Rong Fu,Hang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAR proposes a novel dense reward mechanism for RLVR that uses cosine similarity between policy gradients and expert-anchor gradients, achieving <9% overhead while improving chain-of-thought reasoning in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Binary outcome rewards in RLVR cannot distinguish among correct trajectories, and existing dense reward alternatives either ignore expert solutions in training corpora or require expensive offline annotation.

Method: GAR uses truncated backpropagation through the output projection layer to extract compact gradient vectors for each rollout, then computes cosine similarity with an expert-anchor gradient to produce a dense, reasoning-aware reward signal.

Result: GAR consistently improves over GRPO and baselines on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B models across competition-level math benchmarks, and transfers to GPQA Diamond and MMLU-Pro without domain-specific data.

Conclusion: The proposed cosine similarity admits multiplicative decomposition into prediction-error and activation-pattern factors, providing theoretical characterization of the alignment signal. GAR achieves superior performance with minimal computational overhead while generalizing across domains.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) drives chain-of-thought reasoning in large language models, yet its binary outcome reward cannot distinguish among correct trajectories. Existing dense reward alternatives, from surface heuristics to process reward models, either ignore the expert solutions already present in training corpora or require expensive offline annotation. We propose Gradient-Aligned Reward (GAR), which operates in the policy's own gradient space: truncated backpropagation through the output projection layer extracts a compact gradient vector for each rollout, and cosine similarity with an expert-anchor gradient yields a dense, reasoning-aware reward with less than 9% wall-clock overhead. We prove that this cosine admits a multiplicative decomposition into prediction-error and activation-pattern factors, providing a concrete characterization of what the alignment signal measures. On Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, GAR consistently improves over GRPO and other baselines on competition-level math benchmarks and transfers to GPQA Diamond and MMLU-Pro without domain-specific data. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LQgdwind/GAR.

</details>


### [220] [From Zero to Hero: An Open LLM Ecosystem for Armenian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03350)
*Erik Arakelyan,Khatun Avetisyan,Meri Davtyan,Heghine Grigoryan,Nane Khachatryan,Hayk Shahsuvaryan,Henrik Sergoyan,Vahan Martirosyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the lack of open Armenian LLMs by creating two datasets (ArmWeb with 4.37M news documents and ArmSTEM with 373K translated STEM problems), and training arm-gemma-e4b which outperforms all existing open Armenian models. The study reveals that news-only pretraining improves fluency but erodes knowledge, which can be reversed by adding verified STEM data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the gap that Armenian, a morphologically rich and low-resource language, lacks open LLMs with reproducible data and training recipes. Existing public Armenian corpora have issues including overlap with evaluation panels.

Method: The authors curated two datasets: ArmWeb (4.37M Armenian news documents) and ArmSTEM (373K parallel English-Armenian math/science problems with step-by-step solutions translated and verified via LLM judgment and human evaluation). They performed continued pretraining on Gemma-4-E4B using these datasets.

Result: arm-gemma-e4b outperforms all existing open Armenian models and its unadapted base model. News-only continued pretraining improves fluency while eroding knowledge, and a small share of verified translated STEM data reverses this knowledge loss. The study also found significant overlap between public Armenian corpora and web-derived evaluation panels.

Conclusion: The paper presents the first open Armenian LLM with complete training data and recipe. The key findings are that news data alone improves fluency at the cost of knowledge, and STEM data can restore knowledge without sacrificing fluency. All data, models, and code are openly released.

Abstract: Pretraining data for Armenian, a morphologically rich and low-resource language, is scarce, and no open Armenian LLM has been released with the data and recipe needed to reproduce it. To address this gap, we curate and release two datasets. ArmWeb is an extensively validated corpus of 4.37M Armenian news documents. ArmSTEM is a parallel English-Armenian collection of 373K math and science problems with step-by-step solutions, translated into Armenian and verified through both answer-preserving LLM judgment and human evaluation. Continued pretraining of Gemma-4-E4B on these datasets yields arm-gemma-e4b, which outperforms every existing open Armenian model as well as its unadapted base, and is the first open Armenian LLM with complete training data and recipe. Our ablations show that news-only continued pretraining improves fluency while eroding knowledge, a pattern we also observe in existing Armenian models, and that a small share of verified translated STEM data reverses the loss. We further find that the largest public Armenian corpora overlap web-derived evaluation panels heavily, including a train/test self-overlap inside FineWeb-2. We openly release all data, models, and code.

</details>


### [221] [Time Without Timesteps: Simulating Coupled Dynamical Systems via Self-Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03358)
*Liyu Zerihun,Mark Shinyoung Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a neural surrogate approach for simulating coupled dynamical systems, where instead of traditional time-stepping, learned operators map full driving trajectories to full output trajectories. The method uses waveform relaxation to cast simulation as a fixed-point problem over complete trajectories, with convergence predicted by the spectral radius of the operator's Jacobian.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional numerical simulation uses causal time-marching where each state is computed from the previous one. This approach becomes inefficient for coupled systems and poses challenges for parallel computation and memory-efficient backpropagation. The authors seek an alternative formulation that treats entire trajectories as the computational unit rather than individual time steps.

Method: The method trains neural surrogates for each subsystem type that map full driving trajectories and initial conditions directly to full output trajectories. Following classical waveform relaxation, coupled systems are assembled by enforcing self-consistency among these trajectories, converting simulation into a fixed-point problem over complete trajectories rather than stepwise rollout. The gradient loses its time recursion and becomes a linear system solved by GMRES, independent of solver depth in memory.

Result: On coupled van der Pol oscillators and Hodgkin-Huxley neuron networks, the method achieves convergence in 4-10 Newton iterations compared to 1500 steps in reference integrators. The spectral radius of the learned operator's Jacobian accurately predicts convergence behavior. Where convergence fails, unrolled backpropagation diverges and Neumann adjoint fails, while the implicit gradient remains accurate to 0.04%. Surrogate error degrades performance in some cases.

Conclusion: The approach succeeds when the learned operator is accurate and the spectral radius is below the convergence threshold. It enables parallel-in-time computation and memory-efficient differentiation. However, surrogate error limits applicability, and the method's success depends on predicting convergence via spectral radius analysis. The implicit gradient provides a reliable alternative when forward convergence fails.

Abstract: Numerical simulation of dynamical systems is usually organized as a causal march through time: each state is computed from the previous one. We explore a different formulation for coupled systems. For each subsystem type we train a neural surrogate mapping a full driving trajectory and initial condition directly to a full output trajectory; following classical waveform relaxation, coupled systems are assembled by enforcing self-consistency among these trajectories: simulation becomes a fixed-point problem over complete trajectories rather than a stepwise rollout. On coupled van der Pol oscillators and Hodgkin-Huxley neuron networks, sequential depth becomes the number of solver iterations: 4-10 Newton iterations where the reference integrator takes 1500 steps. The gradient likewise loses its time recursion: it becomes a linear system solved by GMRES at memory independent of solver depth. A single scalar measured from the learned operator, the spectral radius of its Jacobian, predicts in advance where the coupled solve will converge; past that boundary, unrolled backpropagation diverges and a Neumann adjoint fails, while the implicit gradient remains correct to 0.04%. We report where the approach succeeds and where surrogate error degrades it.

</details>


### [222] [SimpleDesign: A Joint Model for Protein Sequence and Structure Codesign](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03377)
*Jiarui Lu,Yuyang Wang,Yizhe Zhang,Jiatao Gu,Navdeep Jaitly,Joshua M. Susskind,Miguel Ángel Bautista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SimpleDesign is a novel multi-modal protein design model that uses a single-stage end-to-end training approach (instead of the traditional multi-stage autoencoder + latent space generation) to co-design protein sequences and structures. It employs a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture to handle both modalities while maintaining global attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing protein design models typically require multi-stage training: first training autoencoders to tokenize data into latent representations, then training generative models in that latent space. This approach is complex and potentially inefficient. The authors hypothesize that single-stage training directly in data space can achieve comparable or better performance for co-design tasks.

Method: SimpleDesign uses a single-stage end-to-end objective combining: (1) discrete cross-entropy for sequence generation and (2) regression objective for structure prediction. The model employs a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture that enables modality-specific processing while maintaining global self-attention over both sequence and structure modalities. Training was performed on over 2 million sequence-structure pairs.

Result: The model achieves strong performance across co-design (joint sequence-structure generation) and unconditional sequence/structure generation benchmarks, demonstrating that single-stage training in data space is viable for multi-modal protein design.

Conclusion: Single-stage end-to-end training is sufficient to obtain performant co-design models, eliminating the need for multi-stage training pipelines. The Mixture-of-Transformer architecture effectively handles the different modalities of protein sequences and structures while maintaining global context.

Abstract: Proteins are fundamental to biological processes, with their function determined by the complex interplay between the amino acid sequence and the three-dimensional structure. Developing generative models capable of understanding this intrinsically multi-modal relationship is crucial for fields like drug discovery and protein engineering. Existing models often rely on a multi-stage training process where autoencoders that tokenize data into latent representations are trained in a first stage. Secondly, a generative model is trained on the latent representation of the autoencoder(s), i.e., generative modeling in a latent space. We hypothesize that this multi-stage training is not necessary to obtain performant co-design models and thus present SimpleDesign, an effective multi-modal protein design model trained directly in the data space. SimpleDesign leverages a single-stage end-to-end objective that combines discrete cross-entropy for sequences and a regression objective for structures. In order to effectively model the difference in sequence and structure modalities, we develop a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture that allows modality-specific processing while keeping global self-attention over both modalities. We train SimpleDesign on over 2M sequence-structure pairs achieving strong performance across co-design and unconditional sequence/structure generation benchmarks.

</details>


### [223] [RecurTrace: Adaptive Latent Reasoning with Loop-Time Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03379)
*Yuxiang Wang,Kunyu Feng,Yingda Shen,Haoning Xu,Junyu Wang,Zhizheng Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RecurTrace introduces loop memory attention and oracle-supervised halting to improve latent recurrence in language models, achieving 56.9% accuracy on MathQA with 2.0 loops, outperforming fixed-loop and adaptive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior latent recurrence methods have two key limitations: each iteration only sees the previous output and cannot access earlier computations, and fixed loop counts waste depth on easy inputs while providing insufficient computation for hard ones.

Method: RecurTrace uses two components: (1) Loop Memory Attention, allowing each looped layer to attend to its own states from previous iterations along the loop-time axis, enabling access to earlier computations; (2) A halting head that reads loop state and predicts whether to continue, trained with oracle supervision that identifies when additional depth still reduces loss.

Result: On MathQA, RecurTrace achieves 56.9% accuracy with 2.0 average loops, outperforming the best fixed loop depth by 2.2 points at matched compute. ACT and PonderNet collapse to one loop; CALM reaches 54.1% with 5.6 loops; LoopUS-Conf and TaH-Mismatch achieve 55.3% and 55.7% respectively. RecurTrace also improves generation accuracy over same-budget fine-tuned baselines across 0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, and 8B models, with gains growing from 0.6 to 3.4 points with model size.

Conclusion: RecurTrace effectively addresses prior limitations in latent recurrence by enabling direct access to earlier computations and adaptively determining loop count, with gains that scale with model size.

Abstract: Repeating a small block of middle layers increases a language model's effective inference depth without adding parameters or generating extra tokens, and recent work shows that this latent recurrence improves reasoning. However, two design choices limit these gains. Each iteration sees only the previous output and cannot directly access earlier computations. Moreover, a fixed loop count wastes depth on easy inputs while leaving hard ones with too little computation. We introduce RecurTrace, which addresses both limitations using the loop's own trajectory. Specifically, Loop Memory Attention lets each looped layer attend to its own states from previous iterations along the loop-time axis, so the model can revisit earlier computations instead of relying on the latest state alone. A halting head then reads the loop state and predicts whether to continue, with supervision from an oracle that identifies when additional depth still reduces loss. In a controlled MathQA comparison on the same looped backbone, RecurTrace achieves 56.9% accuracy with an average of 2.0 loops, exceeding the best fixed loop depth by 2.2 points at matched compute. By comparison, ACT and PonderNet collapse to one loop, and CALM reaches only 54.1% with 5.6 loops, while the stronger LoopUS-Conf and TaH-Mismatch baselines reach 55.3% at 3.2 loops and 55.7% at 2.1 loops. Finally, RecurTrace improves generation accuracy over same-budget fine-tuned baselines at 0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, and 8B, with the gain growing with model size from 0.6 to 3.4 points.

</details>


### [224] [TIGPO: Temporal Instance-Graph Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03383)
*Jinwei Gan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TIGPO addresses credit assignment in long-horizon LLM agents by maintaining persistent transition graphs across policy updates, enabling historical transitions to contribute to advantage estimation. It allocates rollout budgets between Exploration and Revisit slots to reconnect current exploration with historical experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based policy optimization methods construct graphs independently within each policy update, discarding transitions discovered by earlier policies. This limits advantage estimation to small, batch-local rollout groups, reducing credit assignment quality for long-horizon tasks.

Method: TIGPO maintains a persistent transition graph for each task, allowing transitions from different policy versions to jointly determine credit. It allocates fixed rollout budgets between Exploration slots (ordinary task sampling) and Revisit slots (delayed reattempts of previously explored tasks). For each revisit, the current rollout group is paired with its earlier Exploration group to construct a cross-temporal reference that stabilizes advantage estimation.

Result: TIGPO consistently outperforms prior group-based and graph-based policy optimization methods on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks.

Conclusion: TIGPO successfully extends graph-based credit assignment across policy updates by using persistent transition graphs and cross-temporal references, enabling more stable and accurate advantage estimation while leveraging historical experience without replaying it in the policy loss.

Abstract: Graph-based policy optimization improves credit assignment for long-horizon LLM agents by organizing rollout trajectories into state-transition graphs. However, existing methods construct graphs independently within each policy update, discarding transitions discovered by earlier policies and limiting advantage estimation to small, batch-local rollout groups. We propose \emph{Temporal Instance-Graph Policy Optimization} (TIGPO), which extends graph-based credit assignment across policy updates. TIGPO maintains a persistent transition graph for each task, allowing valid transitions discovered by different policy versions to jointly determine credit for current rollouts. To actively reconnect current exploration with historical experience, TIGPO allocates a fixed rollout budget between Exploration slots for ordinary task sampling and Revisit slots for delayed reattempts of previously explored tasks. For each revisit, TIGPO pairs the current rollout group with its corresponding earlier Exploration group to construct a cross-temporal reference. The enlarged reference is designed to stabilize relative advantage estimation under small rollout groups, while comparison on the same task directly captures policy improvement across training stages. Historical transitions and scores serve only as structural and detached statistical references and are never replayed in the policy loss. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop demonstrate that TIGPO consistently outperforms prior group-based and graph-based policy optimization methods.

</details>


### [225] [Inferred Generative-Process Diversity Predicts Correlated Failure Across Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03422)
*Ross Tieman,Evan Markou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces "generative-process diversity" as a fundamental measure of diversity between language models, distinct from semantic similarity. Using Normalised Compression Distance between raw model outputs, the authors demonstrate that this measure captures population structure missed by semantic similarity and predicts cross-task variation in correlated failure among 38 language models across 10 benchmark families. The negative partial rank association (-0.216) indicates that higher generative-process diversity correlates with reduced correlated failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify a gap in how diversity is measured in multi-model systems. Current approaches using semantic similarity only capture differences in output meaning, missing fundamental differences in how models generate those outputs. This matters for system resilience, as independent components may fail correlatedly if their underlying processes are similar.

Method: The authors propose using Normalised Compression Distance (NCD) between raw model outputs, residualised against a permutation control, as a measure of "generative-process diversity." This approach draws from Algorithmic Information Theory. They evaluated 38 language models across 10 disjoint benchmark families, comparing this measure against semantic similarity and model-pair capability in predicting correlated failure.

Result: The measure identifies population structure missed by semantic similarity. It predicts cross-task variation in chance-corrected correlated failure among model pairs across ten benchmarks beyond semantic similarity and model-pair capability. The cross-benchmark partial rank association is -0.216 (95% CI: [-0.309, -0.122]), and the estimate is negative on all ten benchmarks.

Conclusion: Generative-process diversity (measured via NCD) offers a novel and practical approach for investigating diversity of multi-model systems in safety-relevant contexts. Higher generative-process diversity is associated with reduced correlated failure, which has implications for building more resilient systems composed of multiple language models.

Abstract: Diversity is a widely observed factor in the resilient function of collective systems, yet the type of diversity that matters depends on the properties and failure modes of the system. This distinction is important for systems composed of multiple language models. Different models may be treated as independent components even when their behaviour and failures remain strongly correlated. Assessments of language-model populations using semantic similarity demonstrate limited semantic diversity, but this captures only differences in the meaning of observed outputs. We argue that a more fundamental notion of model diversity is generative-process diversity, the differences between processes capable of generating the observed outputs. Drawing from Algorithmic Information Theory, we use Normalised Compression Distance between raw model outputs, residualised against a permutation control, as a measure of inferred generative-process diversity. Across 38 language models, this measure identifies population structure missed by semantic similarity and predicts cross-task variation in chance-corrected correlated failure among model pairs across ten disjoint benchmark families, beyond semantic similarity and model-pair capability. The cross-benchmark partial rank association is $-0.216$ with a 95% interval of $[-0.309,-0.122]$, and the estimate is negative on all ten benchmarks. These results indicate that increased generative-process diversity is associated with reduced correlated failure in model pairs that is not attributable to semantic similarity or capability. Inferred generative-process diversity offers a novel and practical approach for investigating diversity of multi-model systems in safety-relevant contexts.

</details>


### [226] [TraveL: Transformer-based Multi-view Path Distributional Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03427)
*Fang He,Tao-yang Fu,Wang-chien Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes TraveL, a Transformer-based Multi-view Distributional Representation Learning framework for road networks that captures varied traveler behaviors and regional correlations to learn distributional path representations. The model encodes paths with travel starting times to decode possible traveler behaviors, using regional attention for road segment relationships and K-S test for training supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing path representation learning methods only exploit co-occurrence relationships among road segments and paths, but fail to capture: (1) varied traveler behaviors that differ based on starting times and conditions, and (2) regional correlations showing relationships between road segments in different areas. This limits the quality of representations for path-related applications.

Method: The proposed TraveL framework uses: (1) A Transformer-based encoder to map paths and travel starting times into distributional representations that can decode traveler behavior samples; (2) A regional attention mechanism to encode regional correlations among road segments; (3) Kolmogorov-Smirnov (K-S) test to compare sampled traveler behavior against ground truth to facilitate training.

Result: TraveL outperforms state-of-the-art methods on synthetic and real-world datasets with: 14.7% improvement in Mean K-S distance for travel time distribution estimation, 16.7% improvement in MAE for path similarity prediction, and 3.97% improvement in MAE for destination prediction.

Conclusion: The multi-view distributional representation learning approach successfully captures both traveler behavior variation and regional correlations, providing more informative representations that benefit various path-related applications including travel time distribution estimation, path similarity prediction, and destination prediction.

Abstract: Path representation learning (PRL) for road networks has received increasing research attention, due to various path-related applications. Existing works on PRL typically exploit the co-occurrence relationship among road segments and paths to learn a vector as the path representation, without exploring the varied traveler behaviors and the regional correlation on the path. In this work, we propose to learn distributional representations, which provide valuable information for use in path-related applications, by capturing the varied traveler behaviors as well as the various dependencies within regions of road segments. We propose a novel Transformer-based Multi-view Distributional Representation Learning (TraveL) framework to encode a path along with a travel starting time to a distributional representation, which can be used to decode possible samples of on-path traveler behavior. Moreover, by analyzing the regional correlation which reveals various road segment relationships, we propose a regional attention to encode these correlations in a path. Also, we explore the idea of Kolmogorov-Smirnov (K-S) test to compare the sampled traveler behavior against the collected ground truth to facilitate training. Experimental results show that the proposed TraveL model outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on both synthetic and real-world datasets, by 14.7% in Mean K-S distance for travel time distribution estimation, 16.7% in Mean Absolute Error (MAE) for path similarity prediction, and 3.97% in MAE for destination prediction.

</details>


### [227] [It's the Problem, Not the Path: Budget and Difficulty Confounds in LLM Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03436)
*Yigit Utku Bulut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges two major claims about LLM reasoning traces: (1) that they contain "breakthrough" moments where solutions suddenly become possible, and (2) that early signals can predict outcomes. The authors supply counterfactual controls for both claims using restart experiments and difficulty-controlled tests. Results show that only 1/178 problem-model cells survives as prefix-limited, continuing the model's own prefix beats restarting in all 9 tested cases (primarily compute compression rather than expanded reachability), and early-window internal signals contain no detectable outcome information beyond problem-difficulty baseline. High pooled probe AUROCs are misleading without within-problem evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current measurements of LLM reasoning traces are missing counterfactual controls at the level of the claim - specifically whether "breakthrough" moments are truly transformative or merely compute compression, and whether early-window signals contain genuine outcome information or just reflect problem difficulty.

Method: Two experiments: (1) A restart-controlled truncation probe comparing per-anchor continuation solve rates against from-scratch restart curves at matched total token budget across 178 problem-model cells (89 MATH problems × 2 small open models); (2) A pre-registered, difficulty-controlled test of early-window internal signals, plus two generation-free analyses of public corpora including a trace-blind difficulty proxy and reconstruction of published early-window positive results.

Result: Only 1/178 cells survives as prefix-limited; restart dose-response separates compute-starved from capability-limited models; continuing own prefix beats restarting in 9/9 cases (primarily compute compression). Early-window signals show no detectable outcome information beyond difficulty baseline - trace-blind proxy reaches AUROC 0.873 on 192K DeepSeek-R1 generations, but within-problem analysis shows AUROC 0.496 at t=4 (chance level).

Conclusion: High pooled probe AUROCs cannot establish within-attempt information; proper evaluation requires question-only baselines or within-problem evaluation. The "breakthrough" narrative and early-legible fate claims are largely unsupported when proper counterfactual controls are applied.

Abstract: Reasoning traces of large language models are widely read as containing "breakthrough" moments and early-legible fates. Both readings rest on measurements missing a counterfactual control at the level of the claim; we supply both controls. First, a restart-controlled truncation probe separates when a solution fits the continuation budget from when a prefix carries value that fresh computation cannot buy, comparing per-anchor continuation solve rates against from-scratch restart curves at matched total generated-token budget. Applied to 178 problem-model cells (89 MATH problems x two small open models, an outcome-blind but difficulty-targeted cohort), exactly 1 of 178 cells survives as prefix-limited; restart dose-response separates a compute-starved model from a capability-limited one; and wherever the matched budget lies inside the restart grid, continuing the model's own prefix beats restarting (9 of 9) -- predominantly compute compression rather than expanded reachability. Second, a pre-registered, difficulty-controlled test finds no detectable outcome information in early-window internal signals beyond a problem-difficulty baseline, and two generation-free analyses of public corpora show why this control is needed: a trace-blind difficulty proxy reaches AUROC 0.873 on 192K DeepSeek-R1 generations -- inside the published probe range -- and a closely matched reconstruction of the closest published early-window positive recovers a comparable pooled result (0.849) while within problem it is statistically indistinguishable from chance at all ten anchors (0.496 at t=4); a post-hoc within-targeted probe finds only a small average residual, concentrated in three low-failure problems. High pooled probe AUROCs cannot by themselves establish within-attempt information; a question-only baseline or within-problem evaluation is required.

</details>


### [228] [Guide, Not Bind: Why Defeasible Priors Fail in Augmented Lagrangian Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03442)
*Sairam Sundararaman,Sara Girdhar,Manit Narasimha Murthy,Samrudh N,Bhaskarjyoti Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies two critical failures in differentiable causal discovery methods that encode expert priors as forbidden-edge constraints using Augmented Lagrangian (ALM) penalty. First, sequential penalty-ramping ALM suppresses true edges before detection. Second, correlation-matching objective ties true and reverse edges to identical cost. The authors provide necessary conditions for any adaptive relaxation mechanism and prove that covariance matching outperforms correlation matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The "guide, not bind" design in differentiable causal discovery assumes that adaptive relaxation will override wrong expert priors when data contradicts them. However, the authors discover this assumption fails in practice, leading to false negatives where true edges are suppressed.

Method: The authors provide theoretical analysis with propositions and lemmas, and conduct empirical validation across 3,072 training runs with graphs ranging from 4 to 32 nodes. They analyze the mechanism of failure and propose fixes.

Result: Two independent failures are identified: (1) Sequential penalty-ramping ALM suppresses true edges before counterfactual detection can occur; DADU violates all three necessary conditions and suppresses true edges in 87-97% of trials. (2) Correlation-matching ties true and reverse edges to identical cost (2r²) because normalization discards variance information; covariance matching separates them by a margin of at least w₀⁴.

Conclusion: The "guide, not bind" design fails fundamentally. Even repairing both issues only partially restores functionality. The paper provides three necessary conditions for adaptive relaxation, proves why correlation-matching fails, and demonstrates covariance matching as a superior alternative.

Abstract: Differentiable causal discovery methods increasingly encode expert priors as forbidden-edge constraints enforced by an Augmented Lagrangian (ALM) penalty, on the assumption that a data-adaptive relaxation mechanism will discount and eventually override a rule the data consistently contradicts. We show this design, which we call \emph{guide, not bind}, fails for two independent, precisely characterized reasons, and that directly repairing both restores it only partially. First, sequential penalty-ramping ALM suppresses a wrongly-forbidden true edge before any counterfactual check can detect it: we give three necessary conditions any adaptive relaxation must satisfy to avoid this (Proposition~\ref{prop:conditions}), prove that DADU---the natural relaxation rule this paper introduces as the object of study---violates all three (Corollary~\ref{cor:dadu_failure}), and confirm the failure across 3{,}072 training runs spanning graphs from 4 to 32 nodes, where a single wrong prior suppresses a true edge in 87--97\% of trials under DADU. Second, and independent of any fix to the mechanism, we prove in closed form that the standard correlation-matching objective ties a true edge and its reverse to an identical cost of exactly $2r^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:tie}), not because the underlying equal-variance model is unidentifiable, but because normalizing to correlation discards exactly the variance information that would make it identifiable; covariance matching instead separates the two directions by a provable margin of at least $w_0^4$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:separation}).

</details>


### [229] [Beyond Straightness: Non-Crossing Flow Matching via Quantile AlignTree Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03443)
*Junyi Lin,Mengyu Li,Jingxuan Hu,Kejun He,Cheng Meng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QAT-FM is a novel flow matching method that uses a quantile-aligned tree structure to efficiently couple a Gaussian prior with target data distributions. It achieves O(Nd log N) construction time and O(d) sampling complexity, making it suitable for large-scale high-dimensional generative tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow Matching performance relies on the quality of coupling between source and target distributions. Existing methods have significant limitations: independent coupling leads to path crossings and local velocity ambiguity, while optimal transport (OT)-based couplings are computationally expensive to construct.

Method: QAT-FM constructs a hierarchical coupling between a Gaussian prior and target data via a quantile-aligned tree structure. This approach enables efficient coupling construction in O(Nd log N) time and supports per-pair source sampling with O(d) complexity. The method also extends naturally to conditional generation while preserving global Gaussian alignment.

Result: Theoretically, QAT-FM satisfies marginal consistency, induces non-crossing linear interpolation paths, and improves path separation at intermediate times compared to independent coupling, thereby alleviating local velocity ambiguity. Empirically, experiments on diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate competitive generative performance while substantially reducing coupling construction costs.

Conclusion: QAT-FM provides an efficient and theoretically grounded solution for flow matching that balances computational tractability with improved path quality. It offers a scalable approach for large-scale high-dimensional generative tasks with provable guarantees on non-crossing paths and reduced velocity ambiguity.

Abstract: The performance of Flow Matching largely depends on the quality of the coupling between the source and target distributions. However, independent coupling often leads to path crossings and local velocity ambiguity, while OT-based couplings typically incur high construction costs. To address this challenge, we propose Quantile AlignTree Flow Matching (QAT-FM), an efficient structured coupling strategy that constructs a hierarchical coupling between a Gaussian prior and the target data distribution via a quantile-aligned tree structure. QAT-FM constructs the coupling in $\mathcal{O}(Nd\log N)$ time and supports per-pair source sampling with $\mathcal{O}(d)$ complexity, enabling scalable training for large-scale high-dimensional generative tasks. Theoretically, we prove that the QAT coupling satisfies marginal consistency, induces non-crossing linear interpolation paths, and consistently improves path separation at intermediate times compared with independent coupling, thereby alleviating local velocity ambiguity. QAT-FM further extends naturally to conditional generation, enabling structured conditional coupling while preserving global Gaussian alignment. Experiments across diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate that QAT-FM achieves competitive generative performance while substantially reducing coupling construction cost.

</details>


### [230] [A Two-Stage Forecasting System for CPU Workload Prediction in Private Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03457)
*Ashir Javeed,Anton Borg,Håkan Grahn,Lars Lundberg,Dhyey Patel,Sogand Shirinbab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage integrated forecasting model for cloud resource prediction that first forecasts customer service requests (TPS) and then estimates CPU workload from TPS forecasts, using XGBoost with adaptive online retraining to handle concept drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cloud resource forecasting approaches directly estimate CPU workload from historical traces, ignoring the relationship between customer service demand and resource consumption. This leads to suboptimal forecasting accuracy and robustness for proactive resource provisioning in dynamic cloud environments.

Method: A two-stage cascaded learning architecture using XGBoost: (1) First stage forecasts Transactions Per Second (TPS) from historical data; (2) Second stage predicts CPU workload from the TPS forecast. The model uses adaptive online retraining with an expanding-window strategy to address concept drift in evolving cloud workloads.

Result: Evaluated on real-world traces from 10 applications in a private cloud. Most applications achieved SMAPE below 7%. Best application: MAE=0.7372, RMSE=1.1866, SMAPE=3.57%, R2=0.9185. The model showed stable recursive forecasting across a 60-step prediction horizon with controlled error accumulation.

Conclusion: The two-stage integrated model outperforms conventional direct CPU forecasting in terms of robustness, computational efficiency, and interpretability. It is well-suited for proactive resource management and intelligent auto-scaling in cloud computing environments.

Abstract: Accurate cloud resource forecasting is essential for proactive resource provisioning, maintaining Quality of Service (QoS), and reducing operational costs in dynamic cloud environments. The existing forecasting approaches predominantly estimate future CPU workload directly from historical resource traces, which often overlook the relationship between customer service demand and subsequent resource consumption. This study proposes a two-stage integrated forecasting model that explicitly models this dependency by first forecasting customer service requests, expressed as Transactions Per Second (TPS), and subsequently estimating future CPU workload from the TPS forecast. Both the forecasting component and resource prediction component employed the XGBoost model within a cascaded learning architecture, complemented by adaptive online retraining using an expanding-window strategy to address concept drift in continuously evolving cloud workloads. The proposed work was evaluated using real-world traces collected from a private cloud environment comprising ten applications. Experimental results demonstrate robust forecasting performance by achieving Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (SMAPE) below $7\%$ for most applications, with the best-performing application achieving an MAE of $0.7372$, RMSE of $1.1866$, SMAPE of $3.57\%$, and an R2 of $0.9185$. Horizon-wise drift analysis confirmed stable recursive forecasting behavior with controlled error accumulation across a 60-step prediction horizon. Compared with the conventional direct CPU forecasting method, the proposed two-stage integrated model gives improved forecasting robustness, computational efficiency, and interpretability, making it well-suited for proactive resource management and intelligent auto-scaling in cloud computing environments.

</details>


### [231] [Mind the Gap: Robustness Risks in PII Detection Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03464)
*Adeel Zafar,Slawomir Nowaczyk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reveals that PII detection systems achieving strong benchmark scores actually fail significantly under real-world distribution shifts (noisy, unstructured, informal inputs). Testing three architectures (encoder-based NER, rule-based, and LLM-based) reveals distinct complementary failure modes, suggesting no single architecture is reliable across all PII categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current PII detection evaluations use standard benchmarks that mask deployment-critical weaknesses. Missed PII entities constitute direct privacy and security risks, so understanding failure modes under realistic conditions is crucial for data protection infrastructure.

Method: Constructed a stress test benchmark spanning seven categories of natural distribution shift. Evaluated three representative systems from different architectural families: encoder-based NER (SpaCy), rule-based hybrid detection (Presidio), and generative LLM extraction (Qwen2.5-3B).

Result: All three architectures exhibit significant degradation on out-of-distribution inputs, but with distinct failure modes: encoder models fail on unseen surface forms and boundary detection; rule-based systems fail on non-standard formats; LLMs exhibit entity-type confusion and generation instability.

Conclusion: Aggregate benchmark scores obscure deployment-critical weaknesses. No single architecture is uniformly reliable across PII categories. The authors propose a hybrid detection pipeline with QA-driven feedback loop for iterative risk mitigation, and release their benchmark to support OOD-aware evaluation.

Abstract: Personally Identifiable Information (PII) detection is a foundational component of data protection infrastructure where missed entities constitute direct privacy and security risks. Although modern PII systems report strong performance on standard benchmarks, we show that these evaluations mask substantial robustness failures under realistic distribution shifts encountered in deployment. Rather than comparing state-of-the-art accuracy, we study how different PII detection paradigms fail under noisy, unstructured, and informal inputs. We construct a stress test benchmark spanning seven categories of natural distribution shift and evaluate representative systems from three widely deployed architectural families: encoder-based NER (SpaCy), rule-based hybrid detection (Presidio), and generative LLM extraction (Qwen2.5-3B).
  All three exhibit significant degradation on out-of-distribution inputs, but with distinct and complementary failure modes. Encoder models primarily fail on unseen surface forms and boundary detection, rule-based systems fail on non-standard formats, and LLMs exhibit entity-type confusion and generation instability. These results show that aggregate benchmark scores obscure deployment-critical weaknesses and that no single architecture is uniformly reliable across PII categories. Motivated by these findings, we propose a hybrid detection pipeline with a QA-driven feedback loop for iterative risk mitigation, and release our benchmark to support OOD-aware evaluation of PII systems.

</details>


### [232] [Spectral characteristics of autoencoder parameters as a vector representation of data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03495)
*Maria Nikitina,Anton Bishuk,Oleg Bakhteev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether autoencoder model parameters can serve as dense vector representations of the training data samples. The authors propose using spectral characteristics (singular values) of autoencoder parameter matrices to form vector representations, and theoretically link these to the eigenvalues of the data covariance matrix. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST demonstrate that models trained on different data subsets can be distinguished with high accuracy using this parameter-based representation approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to explore whether the parameters of trained autoencoders can inherently capture information about the statistical properties of the training data, effectively serving as sample representations without requiring complex feature extraction or using original data samples.

Method: The authors form vector representations based on the spectral characteristics (singular values) of autoencoder parameter matrices. They conduct theoretical analysis linking singular values of model parameter matrices to eigenvalues of the covariance matrix of training data, establishing a mathematical connection between parameter space and data space.

Result: Experimental results on CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST datasets confirm that the resulting vector representations achieve high accuracy in distinguishing between models trained on different data subsets, without using complex vector generation algorithms or original samples.

Conclusion: The parameters of trained autoencoders can be viewed as sample representations, effectively capturing statistical properties of the training data in their spectral characteristics.

Abstract: This paper examines the relationship between the parameters of autoencoder models and the statistical properties of the data on which they are trained. Autoencoders are defined as models with an encoder-decoder architecture, trained to reconstruct input data through a compressed latent representation. It is proposed that the model parameters can be viewed as a dense vector representation of the corresponding sample. To test this hypothesis, a theoretical and experimental study is conducted in which a vector representation is formed based on the spectral characteristics of the autoencoder parameter matrices. Theoretical analysis shows that the singular values of the model parameter matrices are related to the eigenvalues of the covariance matrix of the training data, ensuring the transfer of information between the data space and the parameter space. Experimental results on the CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST datasets confirm that the resulting vector representations allow for a high degree of accuracy in distinguishing between models trained on different data subsets, without resorting to complex vector generation algorithms or using the original samples. These results suggest that the parameters of trained autoencoders can be viewed as sample representations.

</details>


### [233] [Restricted Eigenvalues Beyond Gaussian Width: Threshold Occupancy under Heavy Tails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03504)
*Shi Fu,Huibo Xu,Qixin Zhang,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper resolves a COLT 2015 open problem by showing that restricted eigenvalue (RE) bounds for heavy-tailed designs do NOT follow the same sample complexity laws as sub-Gaussian designs, even under uniform small-ball conditions. The obstruction is "simultaneous threshold occupancy" - constant-width polyhedral cones can have zero empirical RE on every sample path up to half the ambient dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
