Motivation: The COLT 2015 open problem asked whether the same sample size law (1 + w(A)^2, where w(A) is Gaussian width) that governs stable recovery for isotropic sub-Gaussian measurements would also hold for heavy-tailed designs using only a uniform small-ball condition. This was a fundamental question in high-dimensional statistics.

Method: The authors construct explicit counterexamples using constant-width polyhedral descent cones with fixed small-ball constants. They demonstrate "exact threshold encoding" in narrow spherical caps and lifts to full polyhedral descent-cone sections. They analyze worst-case sample complexity as β→0 for fixed threshold VC dimension.

Result: The proposed law fails in its dimension-free, arbitrary-set form. Sharp worst-case sample complexity is Θ(β^{-1}[d log(1/β) + log(1/δ)]) for threshold VC dimension d. Under isotropy: Gaussian measurements succeed with O(1 + log(1/δ)) samples, while heavy-tailed isotropic designs fail pathwise for n ≲ √(p/log p). Gaussian smoothing yields everywhere-positive C^∞ density but retains arbitrarily poor RE.

Conclusion: Simultaneous threshold occupancy is the missing obstruction that prevents the sub-Gaussian sample complexity law from extending to heavy-tailed designs. Under exact isotropy and all finite moments, a distribution-free fallback governed by affine dimension times squared enclosing radius is sharp on this family. The result has significant implications for the theory of norm-regularized estimators.

Abstract: Restricted eigenvalue (RE) bounds govern stable recovery by norm-regularized estimators. For isotropic sub-Gaussian measurements, the benchmark sample size is $1+w(A)^2$, where $w(A)$ is the Gaussian width of the normalized descent cone. The COLT 2015 open-problem note (Banerjee et al., 2015) asked whether the same law follows for heavy-tailed designs from a uniform small-ball condition alone. We give an explicit and systematic negative answer to the general question as formulated there: the proposed law fails in its full dimension-free, arbitrary-set form, and the missing obstruction is simultaneous threshold occupancy. A constant-width polyhedral descent cone with fixed small-ball constants has zero empirical RE on every sample path up to half the ambient dimension. More generally, every finite range space admits exact threshold encoding in an arbitrarily narrow spherical cap and a lift to a full polyhedral descent-cone section. For every fixed threshold VC dimension $d$, as $β\downarrow0$, the sharp worst-case sample complexity is $Θ(β^{-1}[d\log(1/β)+\log(1/δ)])$. The separation persists under exact isotropy and all finite moments: on the same constant-width cone, Gaussian measurements succeed with $O(1+\log(1/δ))$ samples, whereas an isotropic heavy-tailed design fails pathwise for $n\lesssim\sqrt{p/\log p}$. Gaussian smoothing yields an everywhere-positive $C^\infty$ density while retaining arbitrarily poor RE. Under isotropy, a distribution-free fallback governed by affine dimension times squared enclosing radius is sharp on this family.

</details>


### [234] [An Adversarial Zero-Shot Learning Approach for Anomaly Detection in Multivariate IoT Traffic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03505)
*Mahshid Rezakhani,Tolunay Seyfi,Fatemeh Afghah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel framework for zero-shot multivariate time-series anomaly detection in IoT networks using a sequence-based VAE with adversarial learning and contrastive loss, featuring encoder/decoder adaptor layers for domain adaptation and a destination-based segmentation strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anomaly detection in IoT networks faces challenges due to device diversity, lack of labeled data, and domain variability across different environments.

Method: The proposed framework combines adversarial learning and contrastive loss within a sequence-based VAE architecture. It jointly optimizes domain-invariant latent representations and semantically structured embedding spaces. Encoder and decoder adaptor layers align feature distributions across domains while preserving contextual semantics. A destination-based segmentation strategy models real-world IoT communication structures.

Result: Evaluated on six datasets across industrial, enterprise, general-purpose, smart home, and military automation domains (44 transfer scenarios), demonstrating strong zero-shot generalization in cross-domain settings and competitive performance against a contrastive domain-adaptation baseline.

Conclusion: The framework enables effective zero-shot domain adaptation for IoT anomaly detection without requiring labeled data or raw feature transfer, showing strong generalization across heterogeneous and privacy-constrained IoT environments.

Abstract: Anomaly detection in Internet of Things (IoT) networks presents unique challenges due to the diversity of devices, lack of labeled data, and domain variability across environments. In this paper, we propose a novel framework for multivariate time-series anomaly detection that leverages adversarial learning and contrastive loss within a sequence-based Variational Autoencoder (VAE) architecture. Our method enables zero-shot domain adaptation by jointly optimizing domain-invariant latent representations and semantically structured embedding spaces, without requiring labeled data or raw feature transfer. To address the heterogeneity of IoT deployments, we introduce encoder and decoder adaptor layers that align feature distributions across domains while preserving contextual semantics. Additionally, we propose a destination-based segmentation strategy to better model real-world communication structures in IoT traffic. Our framework is comprehensively evaluated on six distinct datasets spanning industrial, enterprise, general-purpose, smart home, and military automation domains across 44 transfer scenarios. Experimental results demonstrate strong zero-shot generalization in several cross-domain settings and competitive performance against a contrastive domain-adaptation baseline under realistic, heterogeneous, and privacy-constrained IoT conditions.

</details>


### [235] [LongCounsel-8: A Benchmark Suite for Longitudinal Depression Tracking from Multi-Session Counseling Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03507)
*Jiayi Li,Zhaomin Wu,Bingsheng He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LongCounsel-8 is a benchmark suite for tracking depression severity across multiple counseling sessions, containing 7,749 five-session counseling trajectories across three datasets. It addresses the scarcity of longitudinal counseling data with standardized depression labels by using profile-grounded simulation, empirically informed state construction, and indirect behavioral realization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing resources for depression tracking either provide multi-session conversations without depression labels or labeled interviews in a single session. There is a lack of longitudinal counseling data with standardized session-level depression labels, which constrains progress on tracking both current symptom severity and how it changes across sessions.

Method: The benchmark combines three approaches: (1) profile-grounded simulation using real-world client profiles, (2) empirically informed state construction based on depression trajectories and symptom compositions, and (3) indirect behavioral realization to express controlled depression states naturally without exposing target labels.

Result: Simulated self-reports closely recover the controlled states across the benchmark, supporting label fidelity. Experiments reveal three key findings: (1) lower single-session score error does not guarantee accurate trend identification; (2) existing methods are consistently less reliable on worsening trajectories; and (3) additional session history may reduce accuracy of trend prediction.

Conclusion: LongCounsel-8 establishes a foundation for advancing depression assessment from static, single-session prediction toward reliable longitudinal tracking of mental-health change. The findings highlight the need for methods that can accurately identify trends (improvement or worsening) rather than just minimizing single-session score errors.

Abstract: Tracking depression from multi-session counseling dialogues requires estimating both current symptom severity and how it changes across sessions. Yet progress on this task is constrained by the scarcity of longitudinal counseling data with standardized session-level depression labels. Existing resources typically provide either multi-session conversations without depression labels or labeled interviews in a single session. Building such a benchmark poses three challenges: maintaining longitudinal consistency and diversity, grounding symptom progression in empirical patterns, and expressing controlled depression states naturally without exposing target labels. To address these challenges, we introduce LongCounsel-8, a benchmark suite of three independently generated datasets totaling 7,749 five-session counseling trajectories, grounded in real-world client profiles, depression trajectories, symptom compositions, and counseling patterns. We combine profile-grounded simulation, empirically informed state construction, and indirect behavioral realization to address these challenges. Across the benchmark, simulated self-reports closely recover the controlled states, supporting label fidelity. Experiments on existing depression tracking methods reveal three key findings: (1) lower single-session score error does not guarantee accurate identification of trend, i.e., improvement or worsening; (2) existing methods are consistently less reliable on worsening trajectories; and (3) additional session history may reduce the accuracy of trend prediction. Together, these findings establish LongCounsel-8 as a foundation for advancing depression assessment from static, single-session prediction toward reliable longitudinal tracking of mental-health change.

</details>


### [236] [LeanGRPO: Eliminating Redundant Recomputation in Diffusion RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03528)
*Sijie Wang,Zhiqiang Tan,Xinrui Yang,Shaohuai Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeanGRPO is a method that eliminates redundant recomputation in diffusion reinforcement learning by enabling gradient tracking during rollout and reusing computation graphs for updates, achieving up to 1.83x speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion RL methods (DanceGRPO, FlowGRPO) recompute selected timesteps with gradient tracking after rollout, which is mathematically redundant when using the same backend for rollout and update.

Method: LeanGRPO restructures the data-parallel layout and introduces two recompute-free training schedules: (1) LeanGRPO-Retain enables gradient tracking during rollout and reuses computation graphs for backward; (2) LeanGRPO-Reweight enables gradients during rollout, backpropagates each selected step with provisional advantage, then corrects with true advantage after trajectory completion.

Result: LeanGRPO achieves up to 1.83x end-to-end speedup while preserving the original optimization objective, demonstrated across FlowGRPO/DanceGRPO with FLUX.1-dev and Wan models.

Conclusion: LeanGRPO successfully eliminates redundant computation in diffusion RL by enabling gradient reuse between rollout and update phases, with two scheduling options targeting different model scales and input sizes.

Abstract: Diffusion reinforcement learning (RL) has recently achieved significant success in post-training image and video generative models. However, most diffusion RL methods, including DanceGRPO and FlowGRPO, recompute selected timesteps with gradient tracking after rollout. Under on-policy training with the same backend for rollout and update, this recomputation is mathematically redundant. Intuitively, the rollout and policy update steps can reuse the same feed-forward backbone to avoid redundant computation, but doing so can incur a large memory overhead during rollout. To address the issue, we present LeanGRPO by restructuring the data-parallel layout and introducing two recompute-free training schedules for trajectory-logprob diffusion RL: (1) LeanGRPO-Retain enables gradient tracking during rollout and directly reuses the resulting computation graphs and saved activations for backward during update, requiring no recomputation; and (2) LeanGRPO-Reweight also enables gradients during rollout, but immediately backpropagates each selected step using a provisional advantage and delays gradient synchronization, then corrects the provisional gradients with the true advantage after the trajectory is completed. These schedules target different model scales and input sizes. Across FlowGRPO/DanceGRPO with FLUX.1-dev and Wan, LeanGRPO achieves up to 1.83x end-to-end speedup while preserving the original optimization objective.

</details>


### [237] [Coupled Scaling: A Representational Accessibility Framework for Neural Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03533)
*Jie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Coupled Scaling," a framework that explains neural network scaling behavior based on the relationship between task structure and the geometry accessible to an architecture-optimization system. Unlike existing theories that derive scaling from data geometry or data-model spectrum, this approach accounts for how different architectures and optimization methods can reach different representations when trained on the same data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural scaling theories fail to account for the fact that systems trained on identical data can exhibit different scaling behaviors when architecture or optimization changes the representations they can efficiently reach. The authors aim to develop a task-conditioned framework that captures how finite-budget scaling depends on the interplay between task structure and architectural support.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical mode-truncation model where loss separates into: (1) target energy outside architectural support, and (2) an unresolved supported tail. They analyze the residual for arbitrary priority orders, showing it lies between the best-N supported tail and the tail beyond the largest completed high-value prefix. Using cumulative-tail log-rates (γ_{A,T}) and coverage log-rates (ρ_{A,O,T}), they derive the residual exponent bounds. Under bounded off-prefix gain conditions, the completed prefix becomes rate-determining, yielding α_{A,O,T} = ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}. For power-law decaying support (a_{A,T,j} ≍ j^{-b_{A,T}}), this gives α_{A,O,T} = ρ_{A,O,T}(b_{A,T} - 1). A fixed-kernel specialization derives the training-time exponent from the near-zero tail of a task-weighted spectral measure.

Result: The residual exponent is bounded in the interval [ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}, γ_{A,T}], where the lower bound applies under bounded off-prefix gain. For power-law decaying support, the exponent scales as ρ_{A,O,T}(b_{A,T} - 1). The framework also motivates two experimental tests: (1) static task-relevant geometry should track loss at common budget, and (2) multiscale geometry should track coupling-specific exponent ordering, including potential reversal across contrasting tasks.

Conclusion: The Coupled Scaling framework successfully separates architectural support from finite-budget acquisition, providing a more nuanced understanding of neural scaling. It introduces testable predictions about geometry-scaling relationships and motivates the need for factorial experiments that measure geometry separately from scaling fit. The authors audit existing emergence trajectories to identify necessary controls for direct testing of their framework.

Abstract: Existing theories derive neural scaling from data geometry or a specified data-model spectrum, but systems trained on the same data can scale differently when architecture or optimization changes the representations they can efficiently reach. We introduce Coupled Scaling, a task-conditioned framework in which finite-budget scaling depends on the relation between task structure and the geometry accessible to an architecture-optimization system. In a solvable mode-truncation model, loss separates into target energy outside architectural support and an unresolved supported tail. For an arbitrary priority order, the residual lies between the best-N supported tail and the tail beyond the largest completed high-value prefix. If the cumulative-tail and coverage log-rates are $γ_{A,T}$ and $ρ_{A,O,T}$, the residual exponent lies in $[ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T},γ_{A,T}]$. Under bounded off-prefix gain, the completed prefix is rate-determining and $α_{A,O,T}=ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}$; for $a_{A,T,j}\asymp j^{-b_{A,T}}$, this gives $α_{A,O,T}=ρ_{A,O,T}(b_{A,T}-1)$. A fixed-kernel specialization derives the training-time exponent from the near-zero tail of a task-weighted spectral measure defined independently of the loss fit. The framework separates architectural support from finite-budget acquisition and motivates two tests: static task-relevant geometry should track loss at a common budget, while multiscale geometry should track coupling-specific exponent ordering, including reversal across contrasting tasks. An audit of released emergence trajectories identifies the controls needed for a direct factorial test that measures geometry separately from the scaling fit.

</details>


### [238] [WeatherNext 3: Increasing resolution and performance of global weather models with raw observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03582)
*Stephan Rasp,Boris Babenko,Dominic Masters,Andrew El-Kadi,Samier Merchant,Guy Shalev,Ilan Price,Fred Zyda,Remi Lam,Sasha Shysheya,Matthew Willson,Stratis Markou,Shreya Agrawal,Suhani Vora,Mohammed Alewi Hassen,Sunny Mak,Tom R. Andersson,Megan Bela,Akib Uddin,Nofar Peled Levi,Ben Gaiarin,Ferran Alet,Aaron Bell,Peter Battaglia,Alvaro Sanchez-Gonzalez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WeatherNext 3 is a new AI-based weather forecasting model that addresses key limitations of existing AI weather models - their lower resolution compared to physics-based models and their reliance on analysis data rather than direct observations. It achieves hourly forecasts at 0.1 degree resolution by ingesting geostationary satellite data, and can predict temperature and dewpoint at any location using sparse station data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art AI weather models have two key shortcomings: (1) lower spatial and temporal resolution than physics-based models, and (2) they are initialized with and trained on analysis data rather than direct observations, inheriting any biases in the analysis.

Method: WeatherNext 3 uses three key approaches: (1) hourly forecast generation by ingesting low-latency geostationary satellite data; (2) high resolution (0.1 degree, hourly time steps) for single-level variables including solar radiation and cloud cover; (3) learning to predict satellite-derived precipitation estimates, tropical cyclone positions, and station observations to model sparse station data for 2m temperature and dewpoint at any location conditioned on local geographical features.

Result: WeatherNext 3 establishes new state-of-the-art for probabilistic medium-range forecasting skill. It achieves substantially lower error than competing global models for temperature and dewpoint predictions, even when evaluated against unseen stations.

Conclusion: WeatherNext 3 moves operational AI-based weather forecasting beyond emulating the traditionally distinct stages of data assimilation, forecasting and post-processing, pushing the frontier of performance and granularity for global weather prediction.

Abstract: State-of-the-art AI weather models have shown impressive medium-range forecast skill and computational efficiency, but suffer two key shortcomings: their forecasts have lower spatial and temporal resolution than the best physics-based models and they are exclusively initialized with and trained on analysis data. As a result, they cannot directly make use of observations, and any biases in the analysis are inherited by the forecast. WeatherNext 3 addresses these shortcomings and establishes a new state-of-the-art for probabilistic medium-range forecasting skill. First, WeatherNext 3 generates new forecasts every hour (rather than every 6 hours like traditional global models) by ingesting low-latency geostationary satellite data. Second, WeatherNext 3's temporal and spatial resolution are on par with physics-based global models, with hourly time steps and 0.1 degree resolution for single-level variables, including solar radiation and cloud cover. Third, WeatherNext 3 moves beyond traditional analysis variables by learning to predict satellite-derived precipitation estimates, as well as tropical cyclone and station observations. Modelling sparse station data allows WeatherNext 3 to make 2m temperature and dewpoint predictions at any location and time, conditioned on local geographical features, with substantially lower error than competing global models, even when evaluated against unseen stations. Together, WeatherNext 3's capabilities move operational AI-based weather forecasting beyond emulating the traditionally distinct stages of data assimilation, forecasting and post-processing, which helps to further push the frontier of performance and granularity for global weather prediction.

</details>


### [239] [Neural-Network Maxent: a general extension with learned nonlinearity, applied to time-series for Desert Locust distribution modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03603)
*Alessandro Grassi,Edoardo Kimani Bellotto,Wassim El Azami,Sabrina Outmani,Maximilien Houel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RNN Maxent is a novel extension of the Maxent species distribution modeling framework that uses Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) neural networks to capture temporal relationships in environmental time-series data for predicting Desert Locust habitat suitability, outperforming standard Maxent methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Maxent cannot capture nonlinear, temporal relationships in ecological data; it flattens time-series covariates (precipitation, soil moisture, vegetation indices) into independent features, discarding sequential structure that carries critical signal for understanding species habitat suitability.

Method: RNN Maxent replaces Maxent's fixed feature dictionary with a GRU neural network trained end-to-end via backpropagation, while preserving Maxent's presence-only statistical foundations, background normalization, and probability calibration. Applied to Desert Locust using 50-day environmental time series from ERA5 Land, MODIS, and Sentinel-3, with a 7-day gap between covariates and presence records for forecasting.

Result: RNN Maxent improves performance across metrics compared to standard Maxent: ROC AUC 0.862 (std 0.036) vs 0.792; F1 0.671 (std 0.056) vs 0.590.

Conclusion: The neural network approach successfully captures temporal relationships in environmental data, significantly outperforming standard Maxent for Desert Locust habitat modeling and providing better forecasting behavior through the temporal gap approach.

Abstract: Species Distribution Modelling (SDM) is essential for understanding how environmental conditions shape biodiversity, particularly for destructive pests such as the Desert Locust (Schistocerca gregaria), whose breeding dynamics are tightly coupled to rapidly evolving environmental conditions. Maxent has become the dominant method for presence-only data, but its reliance on a linear combination of hand chosen feature transforms limits its ability to capture the nonlinear, temporal relationships common in ecological monitoring, where covariates such as precipitation, soil moisture, and vegetation indices evolve meaningfully over time. Standard implementations flatten time-series covariates into independent features, discarding sequential structure that carries critical signal. We introduce RNN Maxent, an extension of the Maxent framework that replaces the fixed feature dictionary with a neural network, specifically a Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), trained end to end via backpropagation. The approach preserves Maxent's presence only statistical foundations, background normalization, and probability calibration, differing only in that the nonlinearity is learned from data rather than fixed in advance. We apply RNN Maxent to map suitable habitat for the Desert Locust using 50 day environmental time series derived from ERA5 Land, MODIS, and Sentinel 3, maintaining a 7 day gap between covariates and presence records to yield forecasting behavior. Compared against standard Maxent, RNN Maxent improves performance across metrics (ROC AUC 0.862 std 0.036 vs. 0.792; F1 0.671 std 0.056 vs. 0.590).

</details>


### [240] [On the Interaction Between Model Compression and Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03604)
*Francesco Corti,Dong Wang,Young D. Kwon,Cecilia Mascolo,Olga Saukh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the interaction between model compression and test-time adaptation (TTA), revealing that while compressed models maintain supervised accuracy, their TTA performance degrades significantly with increasing compression due to reduced representational diversity and structural constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks need both efficiency (via compression) and adaptability (via TTA) in real-world deployment. However, how these two techniques interact remains poorly understood, creating a critical gap in our knowledge of deploying models in the wild.

Method: Systematic analysis using ResNet-18 and ViT-Base on CIFAR-10-C and ImageNet-C benchmarks. Multiple compression methods combined with standard TTA techniques were evaluated using a diagnostic framework examining representational expressivity and adaptation subspace compatibility.

Result: A consistent gap was found: compressed models retain high accuracy under supervised adaptation but show significantly degraded TTA performance with increasing compression. This degradation stems from reduced representational diversity and structural constraints limiting recoverability. The effects strongly depend on the compression method used.

Conclusion: Compression strategies need to be designed specifically to preserve adaptability, not just accuracy. The choice of compression method significantly impacts TTA performance, highlighting the need for joint optimization of compression and adaptation mechanisms.

Abstract: Deep neural networks deployed in the wild must be both efficient and adaptable, requiring model compression and test-time adaptation (TTA). While both are well studied in isolation, their interaction remains poorly understood. We systematically analyze how structured compression affects a model's ability to adapt under distribution shift. Using ResNet-18 and ViT-Base on CIFAR-10-C and ImageNet-C, we evaluate multiple compression methods combined with standard TTA techniques. We introduce a diagnostic framework that examines representational expressivity and adaptation subspace compatibility. Our results reveal a consistent gap: although compressed models retain high accuracy under supervised adaptation, their TTA performance degrades significantly with increasing compression. We show that this stems from reduced representational diversity and structural constraints that limit recoverability. These effects strongly depend on the compression method, highlighting the need to design compression strategies that preserve adaptability.

</details>


### [241] [Local Updates, Global Learning (LUGL): Playing Games with non-incremental Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03660)
*David Milec,Spyridon Samothrakis,Michael Fairbank,Dennis J. N. J. Soemers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LUGL enables gradient-boosted trees (LightGBM) to work in RL game-playing by decoupling data collection from model fitting, achieving competitive or superior performance to neural network methods (DQN, DeepCFR) across 9 benchmark games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks dominate RL for games, but gradient-boosted trees (LightGBM) outperform NNs on tabular data in supervised learning. Game states are inherently tabular, making tree-based methods potentially suitable. However, GBTs fail in RL settings due to distributional shift from non-stationary self-play training.

Method: LUGL framework alternates between: (1) Local updates phase - agent plays self-play games and accumulates tabular updates (Q-values, V-values, policies, or regret values) in a finite table; (2) Global learning phase - table trains a function approximator that generalizes to unseen states before table reset.

Result: Tested on 4 perfect-information games (Tic-tac-toe, Connect-4, Othello, Hex) and 5 imperfect-information games (Kuhn's poker, Leduc Hold'em, Liar's Dice, Goofspiel, Flop5 Hold'em). LightGBM-based agents achieved competitive or superior performance compared to DQN and DeepCFR across all benchmarks.

Conclusion: The strong community bias towards NNs in game-playing may be unwarranted, as tree-based methods (LightGBM) achieve competitive or superior performance across all tested benchmarks, suggesting GBTs are viable alternatives to NNs for game-playing RL.

Abstract: The dominance of Neural Networks (NNs) in RL is partially due to their incremental learning capability, which naturally suits the online, non-stationary nature of self-play training. However, gradient-boosted trees like LightGBM are widely recognised as the state of the art for tabular data in supervised learning, often outperforming NNs in accuracy and efficiency. Game states are inherently tabular---discrete actions, categorical card identities, structured board positions---which makes them an ideal candidate for tree-based methods. We introduce LUGL (Local Updates, Global Learning), a framework that decouples data collection from model fitting, enabling non-incremental learners such as GBTs to operate in RL settings where they would otherwise fail due to distributional shift. LUGL alternates between a local updates phase, where the agent plays self-play games and accumulates tabular updates (Q-values, V-values, policies, or regret values) in a finite table, and a global learning phase, where the table is used to train a function approximator that generalises to unseen states before the table is reset. We test our approach in four standard perfect-information games (Tic-tac-toe, Connect-4, Othello, and Hex) and five imperfect-information games (Kuhn's poker, Leduc Hold'em, Liar's Dice, Goofspiel, and Flop5 Hold'em), and show that our results are competitive with or superior to DQN and DeepCFR. Our experiments demonstrate that the community's strong bias towards NNs in game-playing may be unwarranted, since LightGBM-based agents achieve competitive or superior performance across all tested benchmarks.

</details>


### [242] [Extracting Forgotten Prompts from Targeted Unlearned Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03662)
*Au Ashley Hoi-Ting,Meghdad Kurmanji,William F. Shen,Nicholas D. Lane,Ligang He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Targeted Active Search (TAS), a novel attack that can extract forgotten prompts from unlearned LLMs by using retained data and black-box access, rather than just recovering the answers to known forgotten prompts as previous attacks did.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing machine unlearning methods (NPO, DPO, LUNAR) use refusal alignment to suppress forgotten data, but prior attacks assume the forgotten prompts are already known. The authors identify a new vulnerability: the forgotten prompts themselves can be extracted using only retained data and black-box model access.

Method: TAS operates in two phases: (1) Entity identification - constructs canonical templates and entity pools, then selectively queries the model using the most informative template-entity pair under a limited query budget to identify forgotten entities; (2) Prompt reconstruction - instantiates prompt templates with identified entities to probe the unlearned model and reconstruct forgotten prompts.

Result: Experiments across three unlearning methods, three datasets, and three LLMs show that TAS achieves 100% accuracy in recovering forgotten entities and reconstructs up to 95% of forgotten prompts, while using up to 99.7% fewer queries than naive probing approaches.

Conclusion: The study reveals a significant security vulnerability in current machine unlearning approaches, demonstrating that even when prompts are supposedly "forgotten," they can be reconstructed through strategic querying. This highlights the need for more robust unlearning methods that protect against prompt extraction attacks.

Abstract: Recent unlearning methods (e.g. NPO, DPO, LUNAR) make use of refusal alignment to suppress forgotten data. However, it has been shown that refusal responses might leave traces of unlearning, and recent attacks have been able to successfully recover some of the unlearned knowledge. In this paper, we uncover a new vulnerability. Existing attacks typically assume that the forgotten prompts are already known to the adversary and focus on recovering their answers. However, we show that the forgotten prompts themselves can be extracted by using the retained data and black-box access to the model. Our attack, Targeted Active Search (TAS), first identifies the forgotten entities by constructing canonical templates and entity pool, and selectively querying the model using the most informative template-entity pair under a limited query budget. Once the entities are identified, TAS instantiates prompt templates with those entities to probe the unlearned model and reconstruct the forgotten prompts. Experiments across three unlearning methods with three datasets and three LLMs shows that TAS recovers the forgotten entity with $100\%$ accuracy and reconstructs up to $95\%$ of forgotten prompts, all while using up to $99.7\%$ fewer queries than naive probing.

</details>


### [243] [Out-of-Distribution Generalisation with Sequence Models in Offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03667)
*Oussama Hidaoui,Omer Ebead,Ulrich Armel Mbou Sob,Siddarth Singh,Juan Claude Formanek,Felix Chalumeau,Omayma Mahjoub,Sasha Abramowitz,Ruan John de Kock,Wiem Khlifi,Louay Ben Nessir,Simon Verster Du Toit,Daniel Rajaonarivonivelomanantsoa,Asim Awad Osman,Arnol Manuel Fokam,Refiloe Shabe,Arnu Pretorius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses zero-shot task generalization in offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). The authors propose a multi-task approach using extended offline sequence modelling architectures that handle variable agent counts across tasks. Through large-scale experiments across four environments, they demonstrate that task diversity (not dataset size) is the dominant factor for achieving robust zero-shot transfer, with 3.2x mean improvement over single-task models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalising to unseen tasks remains a fundamental challenge in offline multi-agent reinforcement learning. The authors aim to understand what factors drive robust zero-shot transfer - is it dataset size or task diversity?

Method: Extended offline sequence modelling architectures to handle multi-task observation and action spaces alongside variable agent counts across tasks. Conducted large-scale experiments across four environments (Connector, RWARE, SMAX, and LBF).

Result: Scaling task diversity—rather than sheer dataset size—is the dominant factor in achieving robust zero-shot transfer. The multi-task approach achieves a mean improvement of 3.2x on held-out test tasks compared to single-task models and consistently outperforms strong behaviour cloning baselines.

Conclusion: Development of generalisable MARL agents should prioritise the diversity of the training distribution with varying numbers of agents. This provides a roadmap for scaling offline MARL effectively.

Abstract: Generalising to unseen tasks remains a fundamental challenge in offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). In this work, we present a principled analysis of zero-shot task generalisation in the offline setting and conduct an extensive empirical investigation into the scaling behaviour governing task diversity, dataset size, and network capacity. To facilitate this study, we extend offline sequence modelling architectures to handle multi-task observation and action spaces alongside variable agent counts across tasks. Our primary finding is that scaling task diversity---rather than sheer dataset size is the dominant factor in achieving robust zero-shot transfer. Through large-scale experiments across four challenging environments (Connector, RWARE, SMAX, and LBF), we demonstrate that our multi-task approach achieves a mean improvement of 3.2x on held-out test tasks compared to single-task models and consistently outperforms strong behaviour cloning baselines. These results suggest that the development of generalisable MARL agents should prioritise the diversity of the training distribution with varying numbers of agents, providing a roadmap for scaling offline MARL effectively.

</details>


### [244] [Resolution-Aware Experimental Design under Partial Identifiability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03686)
*Sofianos Panagiotis Fotias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Resolution-Aware Experimental Design (RAED), a novel framework for selecting experiments under partial identifiability where nuisance uncertainty creates ambiguity in structural interpretations. RAED selects experiments based on minimizing expected structural candidate set size while controlling false exclusions, addressing a fundamental limitation of information-based approaches that can fail when structural resolution varies across experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard approach to experimental design chooses experiments expected to provide the most information. However, under partial identifiability, persistent nuisance uncertainty causes the same observation to carry different structural meanings. Existing information-gain criteria can paradoxically prefer experiments with poorer structural resolution, creating a fundamental tension between information quantity and structural validity.

Method: The authors develop RAED, which selects experiments by the smallest expected nonempty structural candidate set achievable subject to false-exclusion control. They prove an exact cross-nuisance aliasing separation showing experiments can be preferred by structural and full-latent information gain while having arbitrarily poorer valid structural resolution. They develop a learned score-based implementation with finite-sample nuisance-average and positive-tail calibration, and characterize a rare-tail sample-complexity obstruction.

Result: Under constrained sensing, two subsurface-flow benchmarks show genuine RAED-expected-information-gain (EIG) experiment-selection disagreements, with the clearest and largest held-out resolution differences in WCA. In a fluvial benchmark, tail protection changes the selected physical experiment and replaces hard-region false exclusions primarily with explicit ambiguity. In a mechanistic methane-oxidation benchmark, a prospectively specified 5% false-exclusion tolerance yields a nontrivial finite-sample population guarantee for tail-sensitive nuisance risk, with 95% joint confidence across all three structural families.

Conclusion: RAED preserves expected ordering under genuine composite Blackwell comparison while addressing the fundamental limitation that information-based criteria can prefer experiments with poorer structural resolution. The method provides practical value through finite-sample guarantees and demonstrates meaningful differences from information-based approaches in real-world applications including subsurface flow, fluvial systems, and methane oxidation.

Abstract: Experimental design is commonly framed as choosing the experiment expected to provide the most information. Under partial identifiability however, persistent nuisance uncertainty can make the same observation carry different structural meanings. We introduce Resolution-Aware Experimental Design (RAED), which selects an experiment by the smallest expected nonempty structural candidate set achievable subject to false-exclusion control. We prove an exact cross-nuisance aliasing separation: an experiment can be preferred by structural and full-latent information gain, average classification, and nuisance-marginalized informativeness while having arbitrarily poorer valid structural resolution. RAED nevertheless preserves the expected ordering under a genuine composite Blackwell comparison. To make this criterion operational, we develop a learned score-based implementation with finite-sample nuisance-average and positive-tail calibration, and characterize a rare-tail sample-complexity obstruction. Under constrained sensing, two subsurface-flow benchmarks exhibit genuine RAED--expected-information-gain (EIG) experiment-selection disagreements, with the clearest and largest held-out resolution differences in WCA. In a fluvial benchmark, tail protection changes the selected physical experiment and replaces hard-region false exclusions primarily with explicit ambiguity. In a mechanistic methane-oxidation benchmark, a prospectively specified 5\% false-exclusion tolerance also yields a nontrivial finite-sample population guarantee for tail-sensitive nuisance risk, with 95\% joint confidence across all three structural families.

</details>


### [245] [Federated Causal Discovery via Regression-Directed Cumulants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03705)
*Pablo Torrijos,Fabio Stella,José A. Gámez,José M. Puerta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces FedRCD, a family of federated causal discovery algorithms based on higher-order cumulant tensors to overcome limitations of DirectLiNGAM and FedISHC in federated settings. However, the authors discover that cumulant-based methods rank variables by a "variance ladder" rather than population asymmetry, and marginal standardization collapses these methods to near-random ordering, while scale-invariant DirectLiNGAM remains unaffected but cannot be federated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the challenge of performing causal discovery in federated environments where data cannot be centralized due to privacy regulations like GDPR. Standard LiNGAM methods (like DirectLiNGAM) cannot be straightforwardly federated, and the existing federated method (FedISHC) fails under near-symmetric noise conditions.

Method: The authors use higher-order cumulant tensors that depend only on joint distributions and add across independent sample groups, enabling single communication rounds for horizontal, vertical, and hybrid data partitions. They propose three FedRCD variants trading off communication rounds against algebraic noise, including exact federated counterparts of HC and HC-LiNGAM algorithms, with single-round variants supporting exact unlearning.

Result: At sample sizes typical of real deployments, cumulant-based federated methods do not rank variables by population asymmetry. Instead, they rank by a variance ladder induced by the DAG along directed paths (cumulant counterpart of varsortability). Marginal standardization collapses every cumulant method to near-random ordering, while scale-invariant DirectLiNGAM (not federable) remains unaffected.

Conclusion: The FedRCD family provides federated causal discovery but with significant limitations: cumulant-based methods suffer from varsortability-like behavior, and standardization destroys their performance. The tradeoff between communication efficiency and accuracy remains challenging, with scale-invariant DirectLiNGAM being superior but not federable under the proposed protocol.

Abstract: In this paper we study linear non-Gaussian acyclic models (LiNGAM) when used in federated environments. These causal models allow one to go beyond Markov equivalence. However, in many domains data are scarce, and increasing the sample size by centralising data from different clients is not advisable due to regulations such as the GDPR. The federated environment offers an attractive option to balance privacy and causal discovery accuracy. Unfortunately, the standard centralised estimator in the LiNGAM setting, i.e., DirectLiNGAM, cannot be straightforwardly federated. Higher-order cumulant tensors offer a way around this obstacle: they depend only on the joint distribution of the variables involved and add exactly across independent sample groups, so a single communication round suffices in horizontal, vertical, and hybrid partitions.
  However, FedISHC, i.e., the current federated method along these lines, breaks down under near-symmetric noise. To overcome the above limitation, we introduce the FedRCD family of causal discovery algorithms, and investigate three variants that trade off communication rounds against algebraic noise; two of them are exact federated counterparts of the centralised high-order cumulant (HC) and HC-LiNGAM algorithms, and the single-round variants further effectively support exact unlearning at any granularity, from a single observation to a whole client. Numerical experiments show that at sample sizes typical of real deployments, the entire cumulant-based federated family does not actually rank variables by the population asymmetry that the scores encode at zero. It ranks them by a variance ladder induced by the DAG along its directed paths, the cumulant counterpart of varsortability. Marginal standardisation collapses every cumulant method to near-random ordering, while scale-invariant DirectLiNGAM, not federable under this protocol, is unaffected.

</details>


### [246] [Projected Riemannian Gradient Descent for the Bures-Wasserstein Barycenter: Dimension-Independent Linear Convergence at Unit Step Size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03762)
*A. Afham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper resolves a dichotomy in Bures-Wasserstein barycenter computation by proposing a Projected RGD algorithm that achieves dimension-independent linear convergence at unit step size, using a novel Projection Lemma for eigenvalue clipping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RGD analyses for BW barycenter computation present a dichotomy: unit-step guarantees have worst-case exponential dimension dependence, while dimension-independent guarantees require small step sizes that sacrifice empirical speed. This limits practical applicability in machine learning, optimal transport, and quantum information.

Method: The paper introduces a Projected RGD algorithm with unit step size, based on a novel Projection Lemma showing that eigenvalue clipping to [α, β] is the closed-form, non-expansive BW-metric projection. The projection reuses eigendecomposition computations, maintaining the same cost as unprojected iterations.

Result: The algorithm achieves dimension-independent linear convergence with rate (1 - κ^{-3/2}), where κ is the condition number. This polynomially improves on the best small-step guarantee (κ^{3/2} vs κ^{5/2} iteration complexity). The analysis also extends to the invariant matrix projection problem, identifying it as unit-step RGD on a totally geodesic submanifold.

Conclusion: The proposed Projected RGD algorithm resolves the dichotomy between unit-step and dimension-independent guarantees, providing both practical efficiency and theoretical guarantees. The Projection Lemma is a key technical contribution that enables this analysis and extends to related problems.

Abstract: The computation of the Bures-Wasserstein (BW) barycenter of an ensemble of positive definite matrices arises throughout machine learning, optimal transport, and quantum information. Riemannian gradient descent (RGD) at unit step size -- the fixed-point iteration used in practice -- converges rapidly, yet existing analyses present a dichotomy: unit-step guarantees carry worst-case exponential dependence on the dimension, while dimension-independent guarantees require small step sizes that forfeit the empirical speed. We resolve this dichotomy, not by improving the guarantees for unit-step RGD, but by proposing a Projected RGD algorithm that achieves dimension-independent linear convergence at unit step size. The achieved rate, $(1 - κ^{-3/2})$, where $κ$ is the condition number of the ensemble, also polynomially improves on the best small-step guarantee ($κ^{3/2}$ versus $κ^{5/2}$ iteration complexity). The crux is a novel Projection Lemma: clipping the eigenvalues of a positive matrix to an interval $[α, β]$ is the closed-form, non-expansive (1-Lipschitz) BW-metric projection onto the set $\{S : αI \leq S \leq βI\}$ -- a statement which, unlike its known one-sided counterpart, does not follow from convexity. The projection is moreover free: it reuses an eigendecomposition the next iteration must perform in any case, so the projected and unprojected iterations cost the same per step. The same analysis covers the invariant matrix projection problem of Brahmachari et al. (2025), whose fixed-point algorithm we identify as unit-step RGD on a totally geodesic submanifold, thereby extending the dimension-independent guarantee to that setting verbatim.

</details>


### [247] [From Nowcasting to Forecasting: Adapting a Reanalysis-Trained](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03763)
*Mikko Partio,Leila Hieta,Ossi Laine*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning model (CloudCast v2) using conditional flow matching improves 12-hour cloud-cover forecasts by 10% MAE compared to its predecessor, extending effective forecasting beyond the typical nowcasting range while maintaining spatial detail from satellite observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate cloud-cover forecasts are important for temperature prediction, radiation forecasting, and solar-power operations. Short-range forecasting preserves observed cloud placement but skill decreases when clouds evolve through formation, dissipation and deformation. Longer lead times require accounting for atmospheric evolution, but operational NWP forecasts may not accurately represent the satellite-observed cloud state at initialization.

Method: Developed CloudCast v2, a machine-learning model for 12-hour cloud-cover forecasting. First trained on Copernicus European Regional Reanalysis (Ridal2024) to learn cloud-evolution dynamics, then adapted to satellite-derived cloud fields using conditional flow matching (Lipman2023), a generative method that transforms noise into cloud-cover forecasts conditioned on observed initial cloud fields and NWP inputs.

Result: CloudCast v2 reduces mean absolute error by 10% relative to CloudCast v1 over the 1-12 h range. It also overtakes CloudCast v1 in fractions skill score after approximately 3-6 h, depending on the cloudiness category.

Conclusion: Observation-initialized machine-learning forecasts can extend beyond the usual 1-3-hour nowcasting range while retaining spatial detail from satellite cloud fields.

Abstract: Accurate cloud-cover forecasts are important for temperature prediction, radiation forecasting, and solar-power operations. Short-range forecasting methods can preserve observed cloud placement during the first forecast hours, but their skill decreases when cloud fields evolve through formation, dissipation and deformation. Longer lead times require accounting for atmospheric evolution, but operational numerical weather prediction (NWP) forecasts may not accurately represent the satellite-observed cloud state at initialization. We develop CloudCast v2, a machine-learning model for 12-hour cloud-cover forecasting from observation-based initial conditions. The model is first trained on the Copernicus European Regional Reanalysis (Ridal2024) to learn cloud-evolution dynamics, and is then adapted to satellite-derived cloud fields using conditional flow matching (Lipman2023), a generative method that transforms noise into cloud-cover forecasts conditioned on the observed initial cloud fields and NWP inputs. CloudCast v2 reduces mean absolute error by 10% relative to its predecessor, CloudCast v1 (Partio2025), over the 1-12 h range. It also overtakes CloudCast v1 in fractions skill score, a neighborhood-based measure of spatial agreement, after approximately 3-6 h, depending on the cloudiness category. These results show that observation-initialized machine-learning forecasts can extend beyond the usual 1-3-hour nowcasting range while retaining spatial detail from satellite cloud fields.

</details>


### [248] [OBER+: Continuity-Aware Reporting and Traceable Continuous Improvement in Outcome-Based Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03770)
*Elakkiya Rajasekar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OBER+ is an institutional attainment platform extension that computes the step from measured shortfalls to evaluated corrective actions in outcome-based education. It uses five connected stages to track attainment, signal shortfalls, grade outcomes, record decisions, and quantify changes. A special rule handles outcome statement changes. Testing on two real courses revealed that naive reading would show a 25-point collapse due to outcome redefinition, and six of ten figures differed from original calculations, identifying a previously undetected defect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Institutions practicing outcome-based education routinely compute learning outcome attainment, but there is a lack of evidence on how this computation informs decisions. Current curriculum analytics do not adequately address the gap between measuring shortfalls and implementing corrective actions. Additionally, no mechanism exists to handle outcome statement changes when computing attainment across deliveries.

Method: The researchers applied OBER+ rules to live institutional records of two real courses across multiple deliveries. They tracked five stages: accumulation of attainment data, shortfall signaling, grading against regulatory cutoffs, decision recording against a catalog of practices, and quantifying subsequent movement. A special rule was developed to compare successive outcome statements, preventing naive reading of attainment across outcome changes. They analyzed 15 statement pairs from three transitions using similarity ordering without thresholds or labeling.

Result: Three key findings emerged: (1) Every outcome of a core course was substantively redefined between consecutive deliveries, with subject matter moving between outcome numbers—a naive reading would have reported a 25-point collapse between quantities that do not refer to the same learning; (2) Recomputing the platform's figures using documented rules showed six of ten differed by more than rounding, identifying a defect since reported to the institution; (3) Across 15 statement pairs, five were identical, and among the ten that were not, the outcome carrying a given number was nearest to a differently numbered earlier outcome in six cases, using an ordering of similarities requiring no threshold or labeling.

Conclusion: The paper presents OBER+, a computational system that addresses critical gaps in outcome-based education analytics. It provides a concrete implementation with five connected stages for tracking learning outcome attainment across course deliveries, including mechanisms to handle outcome statement changes. The system was validated against live institutional data, revealing significant issues: a 25-point collapse was actually due to outcome redefinition, and six of ten figures differed from the platform's original calculations. The contribution is a reproducible rule-based framework that any attainment platform can implement, with empirical evidence demonstrating its value in revealing hidden patterns in curriculum data.

Abstract: Institutions practising outcome-based education compute learning outcome attainment routinely, while reviews of curriculum analytics report an absence of evidence on how that computation informs decisions. This paper presents OBER+, an extension of a deployed institutional attainment platform that computes the step from a measured shortfall to an evaluated corrective action. Five connected stages accumulate attainment across deliveries of a course, signal a shortfall and a persistent shortfall, grade it on cutoffs the regulator already uses, record the decision against a catalogue of practices annotated with their evidence, log the change, and quantify the subsequent movement in the shortfall. A further rule compares successive statements of an outcome, so attainment is never read as a series across a point at which the outcome changed. Applying the rules to the live record of two real courses produced three results. Every outcome of a core course was substantively redefined between consecutive deliveries, with subject matter moving between outcome numbers, so a naive reading would have reported a twenty-five point collapse between quantities that do not refer to the same learning. Recomputing the platform's figures from its documented rule showed six of ten differing by more than rounding explains, in a pattern that identified a defect since reported to the institution. Across fifteen statement pairs from three transitions, five were identical character for character, and among the ten that were not, the outcome carrying a given number was nearest to a differently numbered earlier outcome in six, a result resting on an ordering of similarities and requiring no threshold and no labelling. The contribution is a computational design for outcome-based reporting, stated as rules any attainment platform can implement, with evidence of what they make visible in a live institutional record.

</details>


### [249] [Landmark-Based Discrimination of Injury-Associated Athlete-Sessions from Minute-Resolution Multimodal Football Monitoring Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03790)
*Evangelos Chatzidimitriou,Konstantinos Tserpes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a mismatch in sports injury prediction: minute-by-minute monitoring data vs. session-level injury labels. The authors propose a fixed-landmark approach that creates one representation per athlete-session at specific time points (10, 20, 30 min), avoiding the need to assign minute-level injury labels. Using elite women's football data (3,743 sessions, 22 injury-associated), they evaluated multiple representations and models, achieving ROC-AUC 0.367-0.607 with significant uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that athlete monitoring data (GPS, heart rate, etc.) is recorded minute-by-minute throughout training or matches, but injury information is only known at the session level (whether the entire session was injury-associated or not). This creates a modeling challenge: assigning the same session-level label to every minute assumes injury status is known at each exact time point, which is not accurate since within-session injury onset is unknown. The authors aimed to address this temporal misalignment between fine-grained monitoring data and coarse-grained injury labels.

Method: The authors propose a fixed-landmark, one-representation-per-athlete-session formulation. Instead of labeling every minute, they construct one representation per athlete-session at each landmark (fixed time points like 10, 20, or 30 minutes into a session) using only information observed up to that point. This keeps the target at the session level while examining how discrimination changes as more within-session information becomes available. They evaluated: (1) Pre-session (PRE) representations using only pre-session features, (2) Cumulative (CUM) representations accumulating information within the session, (3) Dynamic (DYN) representations using the most recent observations, and (4) Combined representations. Validation included athlete-disjoint validation, athlete-cluster bootstrap uncertainty, sensitivity analyses, and benchmarks using Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and XGBoost.

Result: Using 2020 SoccerMon data with 3,743 athlete-sessions from 48 elite women's football athletes (22 injury-associated sessions from 5 athletes), the primary CUM+DYN Logistic Regression yielded ROC-AUC ranging from 0.367 to 0.607 and PR-AUC from 0.0080 to 0.0150 across different landmarks. Results showed wide uncertainty intervals. PRE-containing representations showed higher point estimates at several landmarks but remained statistically uncertain.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a novel approach to handling the temporal granularity mismatch between minute-by-minute monitoring data and session-level injury labels in sports analytics. While the fixed-landmark framework provides a principled way to incorporate increasingly available within-session data, the prediction performance remains modest (ROC-AUC below 0.61) with substantial uncertainty, likely due to the limited number of injury cases (22 out of 3,743 sessions). The wide uncertainty intervals and the challenge of predicting rare injury events from monitoring data highlight the difficulty of this prediction task. Future work could explore additional features, larger datasets, or alternative modeling approaches to improve discrimination.

Abstract: Athlete monitoring data may be recorded minute by minute throughout a match or training session, while injury information may only indicate whether the entire session was injury-associated.
  This creates a modelling problem: assigning the same session-level label to every minute would imply that injury status is known at each exact time, even though within-session injury onset is unknown.
  Our novelty is a fixed-landmark, one-representation-per-athlete-session formulation that directly addresses this mismatch. Instead of labelling every minute, we construct one representation per athlete-session at each landmark using information observed up to that point. This keeps the target at the session level and avoids unsupported minute-level injury supervision.
  A landmark is a fixed time point within the same session, such as 10, 20, or 30 minutes. At each landmark, we assess whether the whole session is injury-associated or non-injury-associated and examine how discrimination changes as more within-session information becomes available.
  Using 2020 SoccerMon data, we analyse 3,743 athlete-sessions from 48 elite women's football athletes, including 22 injury-associated sessions from five athletes. We evaluate pre-session, cumulative, dynamic, and combined representations with athlete-disjoint validation, athlete-cluster bootstrap uncertainty, common-cohort sensitivity analysis, alternative negative-athlete fold allocations, equal-athlete weighting, and Logistic Regression, Random Forest, and XGBoost benchmarks.
  Primary CUM+DYN Logistic Regression yields ROC-AUC 0.367-0.607 and PR-AUC 0.0080-0.0150 across landmarks, with wide uncertainty. PRE-containing representations show higher point estimates at several landmarks but remain uncertain.

</details>


### [250] [From Ordered Bernoulli Levels to Critical-Line Geometry: Integer Quantization, Bernoulli Residual Phase, and Prime-Power Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03801)
*Y. Kenan Yılmaz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores the ordered Bernoulli-word kernel f(p,n,k)=p^k(1-p)^(n-k) and its geometric properties under complex continuation, connecting it to the Riemann zeta function's critical line zeros through a novel quantization framework involving integer decomposition and periodic residuals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation appears to be investigating the deep connections between the Bernoulli kernel (related to binomial distributions), complex analysis, and the distribution of zeta function zeros, potentially offering new insights into the structure of prime numbers through Dirichlet series and Euler products.

Method: The method involves: (1) studying inverse-integer level sets of the Bernoulli kernel, (2) using complement-preserving complex continuation to create conjugation-symmetric geometry, (3) applying quadratic coordinate analysis with exact integer quantization, (4) decomposing critical-line zero ordinates into nearest integers plus periodic residuals, (5) circularizing to isolate phase variables, and (6) using unique factorization into prime-generator coordinates.

Result: The key results include: identifying p=1/2 as the unique real split-independent anchor, establishing the conjugation-symmetric vertical geometry via z=1/2±iu, finding the sharp minimum at the central point in Q(z)=1/4+u^2, and developing a framework linking Dirichlet series and Euler product assemblies through a complex exponent s.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a geometric framework connecting Bernoulli kernels to zeta function zeros, with exact identities and classical zeta connections, while explicitly acknowledging that no proof of the Riemann Hypothesis is claimed. The work maintains separation between proven identities, classical connections, numerical controls, and open conditional Weyl tests.

Abstract: We study the ordered Bernoulli-word kernel f(p,n,k)=p^k(1-p)^(n-k) and the geometry generated by its inverse-integer level sets. The binary level 2^(-n) selects p=1/2 as the unique real split-independent anchor. Under complement-preserving complex continuation, the pair becomes z=1/2+iu and 1-z=1/2-iu, producing a conjugation-symmetric vertical geometry before any zeta-function input is introduced. The quadratic coordinate Q(z)=z(1-z)=1/4+u^2 has a sharp minimum at the central point and admits an exact integer quantization. For critical-line zero ordinates gamma_k, the induced levels L_k=1/4+gamma_k^2 are decomposed exactly as L_k=N_k+delta_k, where N_k is the nearest integer and delta_k is a periodic first-Bernoulli residual. Circularization gives Z_k=exp(2 pi i delta_k), isolating gamma_k^2 mod 1 as the residual phase variable. Unique factorization resolves the integer shells into prime-generator coordinates, while a distinct complex exponent s lifts the same construction to the Dirichlet atoms m^(-s), linking the Dirichlet-series and Euler-product assemblies. Exact identities, classical zeta connections, numerical controls, and open conditional Weyl tests are kept explicitly separate. No proof of the Riemann Hypothesis is claimed.

</details>


### [251] [Free Pause Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03807)
*John Langford,Nathan Godey,Giovanni Monea,Yoav Artzi,Harry Dong,Ying Fan,Gustavo de Rosa,Zheng Zhan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "free pause token" mechanism that provides extra compute for next-token prediction by using a parallel prediction stream over a weight-shared backbone, rather than adding an extra token to the sequence. This approach achieves 2-3 centinats improvement on a 1B model with minimal inference cost (1.14x training compute overhead).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard pause tokens (thinking tokens) improve predictions but add sequence length, KV cache, and latency at inference. The authors seek a method that provides similar benefits without these inference costs.

Method: The "free pause token" uses a parallel prediction stream over a weight-shared backbone instead of adding a physical token to the sequence. This allows extra compute during training while keeping inference efficient.

Result: 2-3 centinats improvement in next-token prediction on a 1B parameter model. At inference: no context length increase, no KV cache growth, minimal latency. Training compute overhead reduced to as low as 1.14x compared to optimized pretraining.

Conclusion: The approach achieves isoflop, isoparameter, and isotoken improvements over standard next-token trained transformers, making it a cost-effective method for enhancing LLM performance.

Abstract: A free pause token gives a language model extra compute to form each next-token prediction (as a pause, or thinking, token does) but carries that compute in a parallel prediction stream over a weight-shared backbone rather than as an extra token in the sequence. It improves next-token prediction by 2-3 centinats in practice on a 1B parameter model. Because the pause rides an existing position instead of adding one, it is free to use: at inference it adds no context length, no KV cache, and essentially no latency with the growth in inference flops typically irrelevant as it is not the active bottleneck on throughput. The only primary cost is in training, where additional training compute versus an optimized pretraining pipeline is reduced to as low as x1.14 while preserving most of the benefits. The result is an isoflop, isoparameter, and isotoken improvement over standard next token trained transformers.

</details>


### [252] [A Peer-Relative Representation Learning Framework for Energy Inefficiency Identification in Mobile Network Sites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03809)
*Eliud Nyakweba Koto,Jaco du Toit,Adham Stoltz,Johan du Preez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study addresses undetected energy inefficiencies in mobile network operator sites by proposing an unsupervised peer-relative approach using a novel energy-aware Minimum Distortion Embedding (MDE) formulation. The method leverages the premise that structurally and operationally similar sites should have comparable energy consumption, using an energy-based repulsion mechanism to identify anomalously high-energy sites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile network operators face significant operational costs from energy consumption, but site-level inefficiencies (faulty cooling controllers, idle radio equipment, parasitic auxiliary loads) remain undetected due to lack of ground-truth inefficiency labels and the possibility that historical measurements already embed inefficiencies.

Method: The proposed energy-aware MDE extends standard MDE with an energy-based repulsion mechanism. This encourages sites with anomalously high energy consumption relative to comparable peers to be displaced from their local neighborhoods in the embedding space. The resulting low-dimensional representation preserves structural similarity while encoding energy-related deviations.

Result: The approach enables identification of potentially inefficient sites through peer-relative comparison. Derived anomaly scores provide a practical mechanism for prioritizing field investigations, allowing operators to focus engineering resources on sites most likely to yield energy savings. Experimental results demonstrate the method outperforms conventional anomaly detection baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides a robust foundation for large-scale energy-efficiency optimization in mobile networks, offering a practical solution for prioritizing sites requiring field investigation and yielding potential energy savings.

Abstract: Energy consumption is one of the largest operational expenditure items for mobile network operators, yet site-level energy inefficiencies such as faulty cooling controllers, idle radio equipment, and parasitic auxiliary loads often remain undetected because no ground-truth inefficiency labels exist and historical measurements may already contain embedded inefficiencies. This study proposes an unsupervised peer-relative approach based on the premise that sites with similar structural and operational characteristics should exhibit comparable energy consumption. To capture these relationships, a novel energy-aware Minimum Distortion Embedding (MDE) formulation is introduced that extends the standard MDE objective with an energy-based repulsion mechanism. This encourages sites with anomalously high energy consumption relative to comparable peers to become displaced from their local neighbourhoods in the embedding space. The resulting low-dimensional representation simultaneously preserves structural similarity and encodes energy-related deviations, enabling the identification of potentially inefficient sites through peer-relative comparison. The derived anomaly scores provide a practical mechanism for prioritising field investigations, allowing mobile network operators to focus engineering resources on sites most likely to yield energy savings. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms conventional anomaly detection baselines and provides a robust foundation for large-scale energy-efficiency optimisation in mobile networks.

</details>


### [253] [Witnesses Explain Anomalies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03826)
*Lamine Diop*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WAND is an unsupervised tabular anomaly detector that is explainable by design. It scores points by how far their projection escapes a sub-Gaussian extreme-value baseline, with the "witness directions" (vectors in feature space) serving as natural explanations at no extra computational cost. It achieves competitive detection performance on 47 ADBench datasets while providing more accurate and faithful explanations than post-hoc methods like SHAP/LIME.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current unsupervised anomaly detectors give scores without explaining which features drive the anomaly. Explanations are typically added post-hoc using SHAP or LIME, which require thousands of re-queries per point and only provide approximations. There is a need for an anomaly detector that is explainable by design.

Method: WAND organizes computation around directions on the unit sphere, scoring each point by how far its projection escapes a sub-Gaussian extreme-value baseline. The "witness directions" that flag a point serve as its natural explanation - a per-feature attribution obtained at no cost over scoring. The score is differentiable, allowing explanations to be recovered by gradients. The method includes a probe-efficiency bound guaranteeing every anomaly has a witness.

Result: Across 47 ADBench datasets, WAND attains the best mean Friedman rank at ROC-AUC parity with 16 unsupervised baselines. Its native explanations are more accurate and faithful than post-hoc SHAP/LIME and ECOD at a fraction of the query cost. Scoring is linear in sample size.

Conclusion: WAND provides a practical, interpretable solution for explainable anomaly detection, offering interpretability at no accuracy cost by integrating explanation capability directly into the detection mechanism rather than bolting it on post-hoc.

Abstract: Unsupervised anomaly detection scores each point of an unlabelled, contaminated sample in a single pass, and increasingly must also explain why a point is flagged. Yet the dominant detectors give a score with no account of which features drive it, and explanations are bolted on post-hoc with SHAP or LIME, which re-query the detector thousands of times per point and only approximate it. We introduce WAND, an unsupervised tabular anomaly detector that is explainable by design. WAND organises its computation around directions on the unit sphere, scoring each point by how far its projection escapes a sub-Gaussian extreme-value baseline. The originality of our approach is that the witness directions that flag a point, being vectors in feature space, are its explanation, a per-feature attribution obtained at no cost over scoring and, since the score is differentiable, recoverable by gradients. Scoring is linear in the sample size, and a probe-efficiency bound guarantees every anomaly a witness, hence an explanation. Across 47 ADBench datasets WAND attains the best mean Friedman rank at ROC-AUC parity with 16 unsupervised baselines, so the gain is interpretability at no accuracy cost; its native explanations are more accurate and faithful than post-hoc SHAP/LIME and ECOD at a fraction of the query cost. WAND is thus a practical, interpretable solution for explainable anomaly detection.

</details>


### [254] [Multi-step Proximal Policy Improvement in Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03842)
*Soohyun Choi,Seonvin Cho,Songnam Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a geometric framework for offline reinforcement learning by modeling policies as probability manifolds. It introduces Multi-step Proximal Policy Improvement (MPI), which composes sequential re-centered proximal steps to enable controlled policy improvement beyond dataset support while maintaining proximity to dataset actions. Experiments on D4RL benchmarks show MPI improves performance of strong baselines like TD3+BC, ReBRAC, and IQL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline RL faces a fundamental tension: policy updates must stay near dataset-supported actions for reliable value estimates, yet meaningful improvement often requires moving beyond the behavior distribution. Existing methods struggle to balance these competing requirements in a principled way.

Method: The paper develops a geometric view of offline actor updates by modeling policies as probability manifolds with a chosen metric geometry. Under this lens, a broad class of offline actor objectives are interpreted as Single Proximal Policy Improvement (SPI) - an implicit discretization of a manifold gradient flow induced by a critic-defined energy. Building on this insight, MPI is proposed as a plug-in refinement mechanism that composes sequential re-centered proximal steps, enabling controlled policy improvement beyond dataset support while retaining proximal control at each refinement. The framework accommodates multiple policy geometries and admits practical instantiations for deterministic and diagonal-Gaussian policies.

Result: Experiments on D4RL benchmarks show that small numbers of MPI refinements improve strong offline baselines (TD3+BC, ReBRAC, and IQL) on many tasks. Focused diagnostics distinguish re-centered refinement from fixed-objective update scheduling and characterize limitations under critic error.

Conclusion: The geometric framework provides a unified view of offline actor updates, and MPI offers a principled way to balance staying near dataset support while enabling policy improvement beyond it. The method is effective as a plug-in refinement that improves multiple strong baseline algorithms across various tasks.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning (RL) must reconcile two competing requirements: policy updates should stay near dataset-supported actions to keep value estimates reliable, yet meaningful gains often require moving beyond the behavior distribution. We develop a geometric view of offline actor updates by modeling policies as a probability manifold endowed with a chosen metric geometry. Under this lens, a broad class of offline actor objectives can be interpreted as a single proximal policy improvement step (SPI), i.e., an implicit discretization of a manifold gradient flow induced by a critic-defined energy. Building on this insight, we propose multi-step proximal policy improvement (MPI), a plug-in refinement mechanism that composes sequential re-centered proximal steps. MPI enables controlled policy improvement beyond dataset support while retaining proximal control at each refinement.
