The framework accommodates multiple policy geometries and admits practical instantiations for deterministic and diagonal-Gaussian policies. Experiments on D4RL benchmarks show that small numbers of MPI refinements improve strong offline baselines, including TD3+BC, ReBRAC, and IQL, on many tasks. Focused diagnostics further distinguish re-centered refinement from fixed-objective update scheduling and characterize limitations under critic error.

</details>


### [255] [Pushing the (Decision) Boundaries: Dynamically Calibrating Differentially Private Noise to Explainability in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03851)
*Michael Khavkin,Kichang Lee,Jaeho Jin,JeongGil Ko,Eran Toch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XCal-FL is a novel explainability-driven local training algorithm for differentially private federated learning that dynamically calibrates DP noise using three complementary signals (prediction logit variations, counterfactual margins, and saliency concentration) to improve both predictive performance and explanation fidelity in medical imaging classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the limitation that DP noise in federated learning distorts learned representations and degrades explanation fidelity, which is particularly problematic in trust-critical applications like clinical diagnosis where both accurate predictions and trustworthy explanations are needed. Prior work used static feature-importance signals for noise adaptation, limiting explainability to post-hoc analysis.

Method: XCal-FL proposes a closed-loop, explainability-driven local training algorithm that dynamically calibrates DP noise from three complementary signals: (1) prediction logit variations measuring causal influence on model confidence, (2) counterfactual margins capturing decision-boundary sensitivity, and (3) saliency concentration quantifying spatial coherence of model attention. The method enforces formal DP guarantees via adaptive privacy accounting.

Result: Experiments on three medical imaging datasets show that XCal-FL improves predictive performance by over 10% and explanation fidelity by up to 5× over static-noise FL, while outperforming state-of-the-art adaptive DP methods in fidelity. It also achieves higher privacy-budget efficiency, converting each unit of cumulative privacy loss into larger gains in both accuracy and explanation fidelity.

Conclusion: The key finding is that explanation fidelity exhibits non-linear dynamics with privacy loss, unlike predictive performance which scales roughly linearly. This suggests explainability is a distinct dimension of the privacy trade-off that cannot be inferred from utility alone, with important implications for training and privacy-budget allocation in decision-critical applications.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) with Differential Privacy (DP) is increasingly adopted to preserve data confidentiality in distributed machine learning. However, DP noise distorts learned representations and degrades explanation fidelity, limiting differentially private FL where trustworthy explanations are required, such as assistive clinical diagnosis. Prior work adapted DP noise with static feature-importance signals, restricting explainability to post hoc analysis and precluding noise calibration to explanation quality during training. We propose XCal-FL, a closed-loop, explainability-driven local training algorithm for image classification in cross-silo FL that dynamically calibrates DP noise from three complementary signals: (1) prediction logit variations, measuring causal influence on model confidence, (2) counterfactual margins, capturing decision-boundary sensitivity, and (3) saliency concentration, quantifying spatial coherence of model attention, while enforcing formal DP guarantees via adaptive privacy accounting. Experiments on three medical imaging datasets across varying FL configurations show that XCal-FL yields more accurate and interpretable global models, improving predictive performance by over 10\% and explanation fidelity by up to 5$\times$ over static-noise FL, and outperforming state-of-the-art adaptive DP methods in fidelity. XCal-FL also achieves higher privacy-budget efficiency, turning each unit of cumulative privacy loss into larger gains in both accuracy and explanation fidelity. Our analysis further reveals that, unlike predictive performance, which scales roughly linearly with privacy loss, explanation fidelity exhibits non-linear dynamics. These findings suggest explainability is a distinct dimension of the privacy trade-off that cannot be inferred from utility alone, with implications for training and privacy-budget allocation in decision-critical applications.

</details>


### [256] [High-Dimensional Learning Dynamics of Attention-Indexed Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03858)
*Yizhou Xu,Margarita Sagitova,Lenka Zdeborová,Florent Krzakala*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies the training dynamics of attention mechanisms in foundation models, focusing on attention-indexed models that can represent multi-layer and multi-head architectures. The key finding is that attention parameterization itself acts as an architectural implicit bias, with tied attention (S=WW^T) enabling automatic symmetry-breaking and weak recovery in Θ(d²log d) samples, while untied attention (S=UV^T) exhibits a fast-slow dynamics mechanism where the pre-activation mean evolves on a faster timescale than overlaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding why attention mechanisms work well in practice despite limited theoretical understanding of their training dynamics, particularly when attention matrices have extensive rank. The goal is to uncover the implicit biases introduced by different attention parameterizations.

Method: 1) High-dimensional limit analysis of population-loss landscape characterized by finite set of trace order parameters; 2) Analysis of online stochastic gradient descent (SGD) governed by infinite hierarchy of matrix moments, showing exponential approximation by finite truncated system; 3) Comparative analysis of tied attention (S=WW^T) versus untied attention (S=UV^T) parameterizations.

Result: Population-loss landscape has finite trace order parameters in high-dimensional limit; SGD moments can be exponentially well-approximated by finite truncated system; Tied attention induces automatic symmetry-breaking yielding weak recovery in Θ(d²log d) samples; Untied attention exhibits fast-slow mechanism where pre-activation mean evolves on fast timescale while overlaps evolve on slower timescale; Weak recovery occurs when fast dynamics break initial symmetry.

Conclusion: Attention parameterization itself serves as an architectural implicit bias. Direct optimization of attention matrix S can get trapped in uninformative states, but tied attention (S=WW^T) provides automatic symmetry-breaking mechanism, while untied attention (S=UV^T) relies on fast-slow dynamics to escape symmetry. Both achieve weak recovery on Θ(d²log d) sample complexity scale.

Abstract: Attention mechanisms are central to modern foundation models, yet their training dynamics remain poorly understood, especially when the attention matrices have extensive rank. In this work, we study attention-indexed models, a broad framework that can represent multi-layer and multi-head attention architectures. First, we show that, in a suitable high-dimensional limit, the population-loss landscape is characterized by a finite set of trace order parameters. In contrast, online stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is governed by an infinite hierarchy of matrix moments, which we show can be exponentially well-approximated by a finite truncated system. Second, this framework reveals that attention parameterization itself can act as an architectural implicit bias. Direct optimization of an attention matrix $S\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ can remain trapped in an uninformative state. Tied attention ($S=WW^\top$) induces an automatic symmetry-breaking mechanism and yields weak recovery in $Θ(d^2\log d)$ samples. For untied attention, $S=UV^\top$, we uncover a fast-slow mechanism: the pre-activation mean first evolves on a fast timescale, while the overlaps evolve on a slower one. Weak recovery on the $Θ(d^2\log d)$ scale occurs when the state selected by the fast dynamics breaks the initial symmetry.

</details>


### [257] [Differentiable Interval Bottlenecks for Interpretable Anomaly Detection in Numerical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03878)
*Lamine Diop,Marc Plantevit*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DIFFINT is an interpretable autoencoder-based anomaly detector where the latent bottleneck is structured as soft, axis-aligned interval memberships. Each latent unit corresponds to a human-readable hyper-rectangle in feature space, enabling practitioners to understand which feature ranges contributed to an anomaly score without any discretization or binarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstruction-based anomaly detectors (like standard autoencoders) are accurate but opaque - they flag samples as anomalous without explaining which features caused the detection. This lack of interpretability limits their practical utility in real-world applications where understanding the cause of anomalies is crucial.

Method: DIFFINT uses a deep autoencoder with a structured latent bottleneck consisting of soft, axis-aligned interval memberships learned end-to-end from raw numerical data. Each latent unit corresponds to a hyper-rectangle in feature space. An instance is encoded by how strongly it falls inside each interval, and reconstruction error serves as the anomaly score. The method includes: (1) a certified reconstruction-error lower bound for points outside the learned support, (2) a graded suppression mechanism for abnormal features, and (3) a closed-form, label-free importance score that ranks (unit, feature) pairs without requiring anomaly labels.

Result: On 48 ADBench benchmarks against 22 baselines under a common [-1, 1]-normalized protocol, DIFFINT achieves the best mean rank overall on both metrics: 4.10 on ROC-AUC and 4.16 on AUPR. It leads among inlier-only detectors and is competitive with the strongest contaminated-data detectors. It is the only interpretable detector in the statistically-tied leading cluster of seven methods.

Conclusion: DIFFINT successfully bridges the gap between high accuracy and interpretability in anomaly detection. It provides human-readable interval-based explanations while achieving state-of-the-art performance comparable to opaque methods. The theoretical guarantees (certified lower bounds) and label-free importance scores make it particularly valuable for practical deployment where auditability and transparency are required.

Abstract: Reconstruction-based anomaly detectors are accurate but opaque: a deep autoencoder flags a sample without telling a practitioner which feature ranges made it anomalous. We propose DIFFINT, an autoencoder whose latent bottleneck is structured as a set of soft, axis-aligned interval memberships learned end-to-end directly from raw numerical data, without any discretization or binarization. Each latent unit corresponds to a human-readable hyper-rectangle in feature space; an instance is encoded by how strongly it falls inside each interval relative to the other units, and its reconstruction error is the anomaly score. This keeps the power of differentiable representation learning while exposing an inspectable internal structure. We make the inductive bias precise: a certified reconstruction-error lower bound for points that fall outside every active coordinate of the learned support (with a Lipschitz-enforced decoder), and a graded, empirically verified suppression mechanism for the usual case in which only a few features are abnormal; and we provide a closed-form, label-free importance that ranks each (unit, feature) pair from quantities the model already maintains, turning trained intervals into auditable candidate constraints without ever seeing an anomaly label. On 48 ADBench benchmarks against 22 baselines under a common [-1, 1]-normalized protocol, DIFFINT attains the best mean rank overall on both metrics (4.10 on ROC-AUC, 4.16 on AUPR); among inlier-only detectors it leads its regime clearly, and it is competitive with the strongest contaminated-data detectors (see the stratified and complete-case analyses). It is the only interpretable detector in the statistically-tied leading cluster of seven methods.

</details>


### [258] [Beyond Endpoint Scores: Time- and Capacity-Conditioned Evaluation of Continual Knowledge Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03900)
*Heejin Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the common practice of evaluating continual knowledge-updating methods using only a single checkpoint and adapter rank. Through experiments on a 24-month Wikidata stream, the authors demonstrate that method rankings are highly sensitive to evaluation timing and replay-side adaptation capacity. They propose reporting performance trajectories and capacity sweeps rather than single-point comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that prior work often declares superiority based on one final checkpoint and one conventional adapter rank. They hypothesize this is insufficient to identify the true better operating point, as the apparent winner may change across different evaluation conditions.

Method: The authors fixed a periodic hierarchy and compared it against cumulative replay over a 24-month Wikidata knowledge stream. They systematically varied three factors: (1) evaluation month, (2) replay LoRA rank, and (3) query formulation. Experiments were conducted on Qwen2.5-1.5B, Llama-3.2-1B, and held-out paraphrases.

Result: The method ranking changed dramatically across the evaluation region. On Qwen2.5-1.5B: the hierarchy showed a 5.0-point advantage over rank-8 replay but an 11.6-point deficit against rank-72 replay. At high ranks, a consolidation-aligned endpoint suggested a tie while time-averaged replay led by 9-13 points. The same rank-conditioned reversal appeared on Llama-3.2-1B and held-out paraphrases.

Conclusion: Method ranking in continual updating depends jointly on when performance is measured and how much replay-side adaptation capacity the baseline receives. The authors propose a new reporting protocol: (1) report trajectories and capacity sweeps, (2) declare a robust winner only when ordering is stable across evaluation regions, (3) otherwise report winner regions and retention-stability-cost frontiers. Under this protocol, the periodic hierarchy is a lower-update-cost operating point, not a quality winner.

Abstract: Continual knowledge-updating methods are often declared superior from one final checkpoint and one conventional adapter rank. We show that this can be insufficient to identify the better operating point. Holding a periodic hierarchy fixed, we compare it with cumulative replay over a 24-month Wikidata stream while varying evaluation month, replay LoRA rank, and query formulation. The apparent winner changes across this region: on Qwen2.5-1.5B, the hierarchy's 5.0-point advantage over rank-8 replay becomes an 11.6-point deficit against rank-72 replay, and at high ranks a consolidation-aligned endpoint can suggest a tie while time-averaged replay leads by 9-13 points. The same rank-conditioned reversal appears on Llama-3.2-1B and held-out paraphrases.
  These results show that method ranking in continual updating can depend jointly on when performance is measured and how much replay-side adaptation capacity the baseline receives. We therefore propose reporting trajectories and capacity sweeps, and declaring a robust winner only when the ordering is stable across the evaluation region; otherwise, comparisons should report winner regions and retention-stability-cost frontiers. Under this protocol, the periodic hierarchy is a lower-update-cost operating point, not a quality winner.

</details>


### [259] [RATL: Learning from Retrieved Residuals for Robust Multivariate Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03937)
*Yuchen He,Yueyang Cang,Zhiyuan Ning,Ningyu Wang,Li Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RATL proposes a retrieval-augmented approach for multivariate time-series forecasting that retrieves historical forecast residuals (instead of target values) from similar contexts to correct a frozen base forecaster. It uses a set-aware router to select and combine residual trajectories, improving performance across various settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RAG methods work well for discrete outputs but struggle with continuous regression due to differences in output level, numerical scale, and local dynamics. Additionally, conventional forecasting pipelines use residuals for optimization but don't retain them as accessible memory for inference. This paper addresses these limitations by shifting the retrieved object from historical target values to model-specific historical forecast errors.

Method: RATL is a plug-in residual-retrieval and feedback-correction method that: 1) freezes a base forecaster to construct retrieval keys, 2) uses the base model's historical forecast residuals as train-only memory, 3) at inference time, retrieves residual trajectories from similar historical contexts subject to causal availability constraints, 4) uses a set-aware router operating over forecast blocks and variables to select and combine retrieved trajectories.

Result: Experiments show that: 1) historical residuals matched to current context contain reusable forecasting information, 2) RATL improves frozen base forecasters in most experimental settings, 3) learned routing strengthens raw residual feedback, 4) validation-based correction-strength selection limits residual over-injection. Using iTransformer as the primary base forecaster, RATL outperforms multiple strong forecasting baselines and shows transferability across backbones.

Conclusion: RATL provides a plug-in, residual-memory-based paradigm for learned feedback correction in continuous-output forecasting by shifting the retrieved object from historical target values to base-model-specific historical forecast errors. This approach successfully improves forecasting performance while maintaining compatibility with various backbone models.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) complements parametric models with retrieved external evidence. The same idea is attractive for continuous-output regression, but directly reusing retrieved target values is often not robust when samples differ in output level, numerical scale, or local dynamics. Moreover, conventional forecasting pipelines generally use residuals for model optimization and error diagnosis, but do not retain individual historical residual examples as memory that can be accessed at inference time.For multivariate time-series forecasting, we propose RATL, a plug-in residual-retrieval and feedback-correction method. RATL freezes a base forecaster to construct retrieval keys and turns its historical forecast residuals into a train-only memory specific to that base model. At inference time, RATL retrieves residual trajectories from similar historical contexts subject to causal availability constraints, then uses a set-aware router operating over forecast blocks and variables to select and combine these trajectories. Experiments show that historical residuals matched to the current context contain reusable forecasting information and that RATL improves frozen base forecasters in most experimental settings. Ablations further show that learned routing strengthens raw residual feedback, while validation-based correction-strength selection limits residual over-injection.On real-world benchmarks, we use iTransformer as the primary frozen base forecaster, compare against multiple strong forecasting baselines, and test transferability across backbones. The results show that RATL can further improve base-forecaster performance in most settings.Overall, RATL shifts the retrieved object from historical target values to base-model-specific historical forecast errors, providing a plug-in, residual-memory-based paradigm for learned feedback correction in continuous-output forecasting.

</details>


### [260] [Headroom-Drift Replay: A Primitive for Principled Replay Control in GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03941)
*Hyun Bin Park,Du-Seong Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Headroom-Drift Replay, a principled replay selection method for GRPO-based RL training of reasoning models. It isolates replay's contribution by using two complementary mechanisms: Headroom (ranking stored groups by remaining learning value) and Drift (filtering by policy compatibility). The method requires no auxiliary generation or training machinery while improving over naive replay and matching broader replay methods across mathematical reasoning, multimodal reasoning, and Agentic Search benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-based post-training for reasoning models is bottlenecked by repeated fresh rollout generation, especially in agentic settings where environment interaction dominates cost. While replay can reduce this burden, existing methods embed it within larger training pipelines (exploration, experience restructuring, mixed-policy optimization), making replay's independent contribution difficult to isolate. The paper asks: how far can principled replay selection alone go?

Method: Headroom-Drift Replay is a group-level replay control primitive for GRPO that separates replay reuse into two decisions: (1) Headroom ranks stored groups by remaining learning value, estimating how much further a group can be learned from; (2) Drift gates stored groups by compatibility with the current policy, filtering out groups that have drifted too far from the current policy distribution. The fresh on-policy stream remains unchanged, and the method adds no auxiliary generation or training machinery.

Result: Across mathematical reasoning, multimodal reasoning, and Agentic Search benchmarks, this single intervention outperforms naive replay and matches or exceeds broader replay methods on Avg Mean@32. In Agentic Search specifically, where environment interaction dominates cost, it delivers comparable quality at materially lower wall-clock time.

Conclusion: Principled replay selection alone can achieve significant improvements over naive replay and match or exceed more complex replay methods, demonstrating that replay's contribution can be isolated and optimized as a standalone intervention. The Headroom-Drift mechanism provides a simple yet effective way to balance reusing past trajectories with maintaining compatibility with the current policy, reducing computational cost while preserving or improving model quality.

Abstract: RL-based post-training for reasoning models is increasingly bottlenecked by repeated fresh rollout generation, particularly in agentic settings where environment interaction dominates wall-clock cost. Replay can reduce this burden by reusing past trajectories, but existing methods typically embed it within larger training pipelines involving exploration, experience restructuring, or mixed-policy optimization. This makes replay's own contribution difficult to isolate. We ask a focused question: how far can principled replay selection alone go? We introduce Headroom-Drift Replay, a group-level replay control primitive for GRPO that separates reuse into two decisions. Headroom ranks stored groups by remaining learning value, while Drift gates them by compatibility with the current policy. The fresh on-policy stream remains unchanged, and the method adds no auxiliary generation or training machinery. Across mathematical reasoning, multimodal reasoning, and Agentic Search benchmarks, this single intervention outperforms naive replay and matches or exceeds broader replay methods on Avg Mean@32. In Agentic Search, where environment interaction dominates cost, it delivers comparable quality at materially lower wall-clock time.

</details>


### [261] [VestigeKV: The NoPE-MLA KV Cache Carries Its Own Eviction Signal in a Vestigial Branch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03949)
*WenJie Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VestigeKV is a KV cache compression method for NoPE MLA models that uses a 64-dimensional decoupled branch (vestige of RoPE) as a query-independent salience signal to partition cache into attended tier and GPU-resident archive. Achieves 1.00 retrieval at 8x and 0.92 at 32x compression from 8k to 65k context, with zero performance degradation vs full-row selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-lived KV cache must be compressed before queries exist, but existing methods (H2O, SnapKV) fail on NoPE MLA models (0.00-0.33 needle retrieval) because token importance hasn't been observed yet. Selection by observed attention cannot work when the queries that will read the cache don't exist at compression time.

Method: VestigeKV uses a query-independent signal already present in the cache: the 64-dimensional decoupled branch, which is a vestige of RoPE that NoPE training repurposes into a salience channel. Reading 11% of each row, it partitions the cache - top-m rows stay in the attended tier (GPU), every other row moves to a GPU-resident archive reachable per step by a certified trigger. No training, quantization, weight or kernel changes required.

Result: Retrieval holds at 1.00 under 8x and 0.92 under 32x compression from 8k to 65k context, with zero gap to full-row selection. Attended tier is 0.25 KB vs 8.1 KB per-token cache at 32x. The method only works on NoPE models - on RoPE MLA it collapses to 0.08 (vs 0.42 for plain eviction). Query-independent salience exists only without rotation.

Conclusion: VestigeKV provides a practical KV cache compression method for NoPE MLA models with minimal overhead and near-perfect retrieval. The method leverages the NoPE training's repurposing of RoPE's decoupled branch into a salience channel. It may extend to other NoPE models like Kimi K3's Gated-MLA variant, but only works on NoPE architectures, not RoPE-based models where query-universal exact merging is provably impossible.

Abstract: The problem. A long-lived KV cache must be compressed before the queries that will read it exist; selection by observed attention (H2O, SnapKV) collapses there (0.00-0.33 needle retrieval on a NoPE MLA model), because a token's importance has not yet been observed. The method. On Kimi Linear, VestigeKV evicts by a query-independent signal the cache already carries: the 64-dimensional decoupled branch, a vestige of RoPE that NoPE training repurposes into a salience channel. Reading 11% of each row, it partitions the cache: the top-m rows stay in the attended tier; every other row moves -- exactly, never deleted -- to a GPU-resident archive reachable per step by a certified trigger. No training, no quantization, no weight or kernel change. Cost. Nothing measurable: retrieval holds at 1.00 under 8x and 0.92 under 32x from 8k to 65k context, zero gap to full-row selection. The attended tier is 0.25 KB of Kimi Linear's 8.1 KB per-token cache at 32x; the archive stays bit-exact and GPU-resident, with host offload as the VRAM-reclaiming variant. The recall tier -- the standard configuration -- holds 128x at 1.00. Kimi K3 is reported to use a NoPE Gated-MLA variant; if its cache layout matches, the method plausibly extends there -- we make no claim beyond the measured model. NoPE exclusivity. The identical operator on a RoPE MLA collapses to 0.08 (plain eviction: 0.42); query-independent salience itself exists only without rotation (top-1 targets span 2.3-6.7% of tokens vs. 10.2-46.8%), and query-universal exact merging is provably impossible under RoPE. All thresholds were frozen before data; 20 archived verdicts and 8 closed routes accompany the paper.

</details>


### [262] [OSR: Output Space Redistribution for Adaptive Label Removal in Classification Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03972)
*Minyi Peng,Darian Gunamardi,Ivan Tjuawinata,Yongsen Zheng,Kwok-Yan Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of label removal in classification systems with evolving taxonomies. The authors propose a novel approach that uses statistical redistribution in the output space to approximate post-retraining confidence vectors, serving as a modular output filter that avoids data-dependent computations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classification systems frequently need to remove or update categories due to evolving taxonomies. Existing solutions (retraining-based and feature-space-adjustment-based) have significant limitations: they require access to original data, have high computational/storage costs, produce inconsistent results, scale poorly, and degrade model utility. There is a need for a more efficient and privacy-preserving approach.

Method: The proposed method leverages statistical redistribution in the output space to approximate the post-removal confidence vectors of a retrained model. It functions as a modular output filter that operates on existing label outputs and prior confidence scores, bypassing the need for feature-space adjustments or loss-function convergence. The approach is data-independent, requiring only existing labels and prior output confidences.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive performance against full retraining, with improvements in computational efficiency and privacy preservation across several classification tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed statistical redistribution approach in output space offers a practical solution for label removal in dynamic classification systems. It achieves performance comparable to full retraining while significantly reducing computational overhead and addressing privacy concerns by avoiding reliance on original training data.

Abstract: Label removal occurs frequently in classification systems with evolving taxonomies, where categories must be dynamically updated or eliminated. To accommodate such changes, classification models must adapt accordingly. Existing solutions, broadly categorized as retraining-based and feature-space-adjustment-based, share common limitations despite their variations, including reliance on access to original data, substantial computational and storage costs, inconsistent results, poor scalability, and degradation of model utility. To address this, we propose a novel approach that leverages statistical redistribution in the output space to approximate the post-removal confidence vectors of a retrained model. Applicable as a modular output filter, our method bypasses the burden of feature-space adjustments or loss-function convergence, alleviating scalability limitations. Furthermore, by requiring only existing labels and prior output confidences, the method potentially mitigates privacy concerns inherent to data-dependent solutions. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive performance against full retraining, with improvements in computational efficiency and privacy preservation across several classification tasks.

</details>


### [263] [RobustSeiz: An Open-Source Framework for Benchmarking the Robustness of EEG Seizure Detection Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04007)
*Mohammad Mohammadi,Alireza Zarei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RobustSeiz is an open-source, model-agnostic framework for stress-testing seizure detectors under clinically realistic distribution shifts (environment, noise, adversarial) before deployment, using standardized EEG corpora and comprehensive evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite strong performance on held-out EEG data, seizure detectors may fail under real-world acquisition variability, artifacts, and adversarial inputs. Current evaluation lacks standardized stress-testing under distribution shifts, creating a gap between benchmark performance and real-world reliability.

Method: Standardize four public scalp-EEG corpora (CHB-MIT, TUSZ, Siena, SeizeIT1) into BIDS-EEG format; evaluate subject-independent detectors on held-out splits; sweep environment, noise, and adversarial transforms over predefined hyperparameter grids; report sample/event-level sensitivity, precision, F1, false positives per 24h, onset timing (Lead/Lag), and Monte Carlo dropout predictive agreement; implement Dockerized GPU pipeline with experiment registry and full-evaluation/research-subset modes.

Result: Demonstrated with a contemporary seizure detector on TUSZ across complete shift grid; AWGN analysis shows perturbation severity changes detection quality, onset timing, and predictive agreement.

Conclusion: RobustSeiz provides a shared benchmarking standard for evaluating seizure-detector robustness under realistic clinical stressors, extending pre-deployment assessment beyond clean-data accuracy.

Abstract: Despite strong performance on held-out electroencephalography (EEG) data, seizure detectors may fail under real-world acquisition variability, artifacts, and adversarial inputs. We introduce RobustSeiz, an open-source, model-agnostic framework that provides a standardized, reproducible protocol for stress-testing and comparing seizure detectors under controlled, clinically motivated distribution shifts before deployment. We standardize four public scalp-EEG corpora (CHB-MIT, TUSZ, Siena, and SeizeIT1) into BIDS-EEG trees and evaluate subject-independent detectors on held-out splits. Environment, noise, and adversarial transforms are swept over predefined hyperparameter grids. Each run reports sample- and event-level sensitivity, precision, F1, false positives per 24 h, Lead and Lag onset timing, and Monte Carlo dropout predictive agreement. RobustSeiz includes a Dockerized GPU pipeline, experiment registry, and full-evaluation and research-subset modes. We demonstrate the framework with a contemporary seizure detector on TUSZ across the complete implemented shift grid; an AWGN analysis illustrates how perturbation severity changes detection quality, onset timing, and predictive agreement. RobustSeiz provides a shared benchmarking standard for evaluating seizure-detector robustness under realistic clinical stressors, extending pre-deployment assessment beyond clean-data accuracy.

</details>


### [264] [Unlocking Lossless Speedups in LLMs via Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04010)
*Subham Sekhar Sahoo,Lingjie Chen,Khiem Pham,Jonathan Geuter,Chaitanya Dwivedi,Varad Pimpalkhute,Yash Akhauri,Alexander Moreno,Mikhail Yurochkin,Zhenting Wang,Mostafa Elhoushi,Nolan Dey,Shane Bergsma,Joel Hestness,John Thickstun,Eric Xing,Zhengzhong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Uno introduces diffusion-augmented LLMs that combine autoregressive next-token prediction with parallel token generation via diffusion. It uses separate AR weights and lightweight diffusion weights, enabling up to 3× speedups over base AR models without sacrificing quality or requiring a separate draft model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLMs suffer from slow, sequential token generation due to their next-token prediction structure, creating a bottleneck in inference speed.

Method: Decouple model parameters into AR weights (trained with standard NTP objective) and lightweight diffusion weights (trained via Diffusion Distillation). Introduce Ψ-Spec samplers for lossless acceleration and inference-time scaling at fixed context length.

Result: Uno achieves higher throughput than speculative-decoding methods at every batch size, delivers up to 3× speedups over base AR models, and the 8B Uno outperforms the 26B DiffusionGemma and Mercury 2 across agentic tool use, coding, and long-context reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Diffusion-augmented LLMs (Uno) can accelerate generation without sacrificing AR model quality, outperforming both speculative decoding and diffusion LLMs while being trainable from scratch or as augmentations to existing AR LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) owe much of their success to next-token prediction (NTP), but their autoregressive (AR) structure requires slow, sequential token generation. To overcome this bottleneck, we introduce diffusion-augmented LLMs, a new class of models that defines an AR model distribution while using diffusion to draw multiple tokens in parallel from that distribution. We decouple the parameters of these models into two sets: AR weights, trained using the standard NTP objective, and lightweight diffusion weights, trained to generate multiple tokens simultaneously. The diffusion weights are learned through a simple Diffusion Distillation phase that adds negligible overhead to existing LLM training pipelines. We also introduce $Ψ$-Spec, a family of samplers that enables lossless acceleration and inference-time scaling at a fixed context length. Unlike speculative decoding, our method requires no separate draft model. Unlike diffusion LLMs (d-LLMs), it accelerates generation without sacrificing the quality of the underlying AR model. The resulting models, called Uno, can be trained from scratch or built by augmenting existing open-weight AR LLMs. Uno achieves higher throughput than leading speculative-decoding methods at every evaluated batch size and delivers up to $3\times$ speedups over the base AR model, including at the largest batch size supported by the device. Notably, our 8B Uno model outperforms the leading open d-LLM, the 26B DiffusionGemma, and the proprietary Mercury 2 across all evaluated benchmarks in agentic tool use, coding, and long-context reasoning. We release code and checkpoints at: https://s-sahoo.github.io/uno/

</details>


### [265] [A location-invariant estimator of extremal quantile treatment effects for heavy-tailed distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04018)
*Xin Yu,Shuwei Huang,Jicheng Liu,Jielin Tang,Bolin Wang,Yunxiao Zhang,Tian Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses a methodological issue in estimating quantile treatment effects (QTEs) at extreme quantile levels for heavy-tailed potential outcomes. The authors propose a location-invariant QTE estimator by adapting the Fraga estimator of extreme value index (EVI) to the causal setting using inverse propensity score weighting, and by using a difference-based extrapolation scheme that eliminates the location parameter. The paper establishes asymptotic properties and provides valid inference methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The existing extremal QTE estimators for heavy-tailed potential outcomes rely on extrapolation combined with causal extreme value index (EVI) estimators. However, these estimators lack location invariance—a critical property since the population QTE is invariant under location shifts of the potential outcome distributions. This inconsistency motivates the development of a location-invariant estimator.

Method: The proposed method has two main components: (1) Adapting the location-invariant Fraga estimator of the EVI to the causal setting using inverse propensity score weighting to account for treatment assignment; (2) Replacing the original extrapolation formula with a difference-based scheme where the location parameter cancels when quantile differences are taken. This yields a location-invariant QTE estimator.

Result: The authors establish the consistency and asymptotic normality of the proposed extremal QTE estimators. They also provide a consistent variance estimator, enabling asymptotically valid inference. Simulation studies confirm the location invariance, stability with respect to the threshold selection, and proper coverage of the confidence intervals.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully addresses the location invariance issue in extreme QTE estimation for heavy-tailed outcomes. The resulting estimator is both theoretically justified (consistent and asymptotically normal) and practically reliable (location-invariant with valid inference). This represents a significant contribution to causal inference with extreme outcomes.

Abstract: Quantile treatment effects (QTEs) measure the effect of a treatment on the distribution of an outcome, and their estimation at extreme quantile levels is of central interest in applications where the target quantiles lie far beyond the range of the data. For heavy-tailed potential outcomes, existing extremal QTE estimators rely on extrapolation combined with a causal extreme value index (EVI) estimator, but the resulting estimator is not invariant under a common location shift of the potential outcome distributions, even though the population QTE is. We address this issue in two steps. First, we adapt the location-invariant Fraga estimator of the EVI to the causal setting using inverse propensity score weighting. Second, we replace the original extrapolation formula with a difference-based scheme, under which the location parameter cancels when quantile differences are taken. The resulting QTE estimator is therefore location invariant. We establish the consistency and asymptotic normality of the proposed extremal QTE estimators, and provide a consistent variance estimator, leading to asymptotically valid inference. A simulation study confirms the location invariance, the stability with respect to the threshold, and the coverage of the proposed methods.

</details>


### [266] [Subspace Inference Enables Efficient Active Reward Learning from Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04066)
*Yutai Zhou,Erdem Bıyık*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PreferenceEKF is a sample-efficient method for active preference learning in RLHF that uses sequential Bayesian filtering via extended Kalman filter in a low-dimensional parameter subspace to track reward model uncertainty, enabling efficient acquisition function computation for active reward learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLHF is powerful but sample-inefficient for learning reward models from human preferences. Active learning could help but requires effective uncertainty quantification for large neural network reward models, which remains a key challenge due to computationally prohibitive posterior inference over full neural network parameters.

Method: The paper proposes PreferenceEKF, which performs sequential inference via an extended Kalman filter within a low-dimensional parameter subspace, continuously updating the reward model posterior as new preference queries arrive. This enables scalable sampling of neural network parameters to efficiently compute acquisition functions for active reward learning.

Result: Experiments on D4RL and V-D4RL benchmarks show that PreferenceEKF achieves better sample efficiency, runtime, scalability, and calibration compared to other Bayesian deep learning approaches. The learned reward models lead to competitive offline reinforcement learning policy performance.

Conclusion: Scalable Bayesian methods like PreferenceEKF show significant potential for preference-based reward modeling in RLHF, offering a practical solution for uncertainty quantification in large neural network reward models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) has emerged as a powerful yet sample-inefficient approach for learning reward models from human preferences, making active learning a critical component in synthesizing informative preference queries. However, effective uncertainty quantification required for active learning remains a key challenge for large neural network reward models. In this paper, we introduce PreferenceEKF, a sample-efficient approach that tracks reward model uncertainty by framing active preference learning as a sequential Bayesian filtering problem. Instead of relying on computationally prohibitive posterior inference over the full neural network parameter space, our method performs sequential inference via an extended Kalman filter within a low-dimensional parameter subspace, continuously updating the reward model posterior as new preference queries arrive. Our approach enables scalable sampling of neural network parameters to efficiently compute acquisition functions for active reward learning. Experiments on the D4RL and V-D4RL benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves better sample efficiency, runtime, scalability, and calibration compared to other Bayesian deep learning approaches, and the learned reward models lead to competitive offline reinforcement learning policy performance. This highlights the potential of scalable Bayesian methods for preference-based reward modeling in RLHF. Our code is available at https://github.com/yutaizhou/bnn_pref.

</details>


### [267] [Conditioning Degenerate Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04090)
*Uğur Aydın,Tamer Başar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses limitations of score-based guidance in conditioned generative models when diffusion coefficients are singular or conditional densities are non-existent/non-smooth. The authors propose using causal optimal transport to define approximate loss functions that identify minimum-entropy controls for guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current conditioned generative models heavily rely on score functions for guidance during training. However, these methods fail when the generative model has a singular diffusion coefficient or when the underlying conditional densities either do not exist or are not smooth. This limitation motivates the need for an alternative approach that works under minimal assumptions.

Method: The approach relies on causal optimal transport and its characterization through the predictable representation property of (conditioned) diffusion processes. The method uses the martingale problem approach (à la Üstünel) to establish well-posedness, enabling the definition of approximate loss functions for guidance.

Result: The method identifies a minimum-entropy control for guidance under minimal assumptions, providing an alternative framework that works when standard score-based methods fail.

Conclusion: The proposed causal optimal transport-based framework offers a theoretically grounded approach for guidance in conditioned generative models, applicable even in challenging cases with singular diffusion coefficients or non-smooth conditional densities.

Abstract: Current conditioned generative models heavily rely on score functions for guidance during training. When the generative model is a diffusion process with a singular diffusion coefficient and the underlying (conditional) densities either do not exist or are not smooth, we use causal optimal transport to define \emph{approximate} loss functions that identify a minimum-entropy control for guidance under minimal assumptions. Our approach relies on causal optimal transport and its characterization through the predictable representation property of (conditioned) diffusion processes whose associated martingale problem is well posed, à la Üstünel.

</details>


### [268] [Hardware-Aware FP4 FlashAttention-4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04105)
*Robert Hu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the inefficiency of FP4 tensor cores in attention mechanisms by proposing Direct-P for noncausal inference and a causal path for training, achieving significant speedups on NVIDIA GB200 hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FP4 tensor cores in Blackwell architectures do not automatically accelerate attention because softmax conversion and on-chip dependencies dominate when matrix products shrink. Additionally, MXFP4 probability/value training trajectories diverge, making training with low-precision probabilities problematic.

Method: The authors propose two solutions: (1) Direct-P maps scores directly to FP4 probabilities for noncausal inference, bypassing softmax conversion overhead; (2) A causal path that passes forward quantization directly into backward, reconstructing probabilities from saved quantized queries and keys and using FP8 gradient operands.

Result: Direct-P achieves up to 2.13× the bfloat16 (BF16) forward throughput on NVIDIA GB200. The causal path accelerates a complete single-GPU 8-billion-parameter update by up to 1.14×. Matched distributed training retains FP8 probabilities and values, while all tested MXFP4 probability/value training trajectories diverge.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that direct mapping to FP4 probabilities and careful management of quantization paths can unlock the performance potential of FP4 tensor cores for attention, though training with low-precision probabilities remains challenging as trajectories diverge.

Abstract: Blackwell's 4-bit floating-point (FP4) tensor cores do not automatically make attention faster because softmax conversion and on-chip dependencies dominate once its matrix products shrink. We address this with \emph{Direct-P} for noncausal inference and a causal path that passes the forward quantization directly into backward. Direct-P maps scores directly to FP4 probabilities and reaches up to 2.13$\times$ the bfloat16 (BF16) forward throughput on an NVIDIA GB200. The causal path reconstructs probabilities from saved quantized queries and keys and uses 8-bit floating-point (FP8) gradient operands, accelerating a complete single-GPU 8-billion-parameter update by up to 1.14$\times$. Matched distributed training retains FP8 probabilities and values; every tested MXFP4 probability/value training trajectory diverges.

</details>


### [269] [Constant regret in general games via higher-order optimism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04113)
*Omar Abbadi,Rida Laraki,Panayotis Mertikopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces HOOD (Higher-Order Optimism with Discounting), an uncoupled learning algorithm for N-player normal form games with up to K actions per player that guarantees O(N³log²K) individual regret. It combines discounted (N+1)-th order predictors with entropic regularization over a lifted strategy space to dampen oscillations in play.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Achieving constant regret in general N-player games has been challenging due to large oscillations in the induced sequence of play. Previous attempts to achieve this goal were hindered by this instability issue. The motivation is to develop an algorithm that can guarantee low individual regret uniformly over the horizon of play in arbitrary normal form games.

Method: The proposed method is a variant of Optimistic Follow-the-Regularized-Leader (OptFTRL) that combines two key ingredients: (1) a discounted (N+1)-th order predictor, and (2) entropic regularization over a suitable "lifting" of the game's strategy space. This combination is designed to dampen large oscillations of the induced sequence of play in a controlled manner.

Result: The algorithm achieves O(N³log²K) individual regret, uniformly over the horizon of play. This is a significant improvement over the related work of Liu, Farina, and Ozdaglar who achieved O(N²¹log⁴K) regret using higher-order optimism with an exponential moving average estimator.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that combining higher-order optimism with discounting and entropic regularization over a lifted strategy space successfully removes a key stumbling block in achieving constant regret in general games. The approach is similar to but independent from concurrent work, with a substantially better regret bound (O(N³log²K) vs O(N²¹log⁴K)).

Abstract: We introduce an uncoupled learning algorithm which, when employed by all players of an arbitrary $N$-player normal form game with up to $K$ actions per player, guarantees $O(N^3\log^2 K)$ individual regret, uniformly over the horizon of play. The proposed algorithm - which we call higher-order optimism with discounting (HOOD) is a variant of optimistic follow-the-regularized-leader (OptFTRL) that combines a discounted $(N+1)$-th order predictor with entropic regularization over a suitable "lifting" of the game's strategy space. This combination of ingredients is purposefully designed to dampen large oscillations of the induced sequence of play in a controlled manner, removing in this way a key stumbling block of previous attempts to achieve constant regret in general games. Our approach bears several striking similarities to the concurrent - and completely independent - work of Liu, Farina, and Ozdaglar (arXiv:2608.31166), who very recently derived an $O(N^{21}\log^{4} K)$ regret bound through the use of higher-order optimism and an exponential moving average estimator.

</details>


### [270] [Prospective Coding Improves Learning in Deep Continuous-Time Recurrent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04134)
*Shivang Rawat,Mirko Morello,Flaviano Morone,David J. Heeger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of signal delay and gradient attenuation in deep continuous-time recurrent networks by developing Recursive Quadrature Filters (RQFs)—biologically motivated complex-valued temporal filters that are a special case of diagonal state-space models—and introducing prospective-input coding to make each layer's bottom-up input temporally aligned.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temporal integration gives continuous-time recurrent networks memory, but in deep stacks it also delays bottom-up signals and attenuates top-down errors. This depth-dependent gradient attenuation is particularly problematic when using spatial-only backpropagation.

Method: 1) Developed RQFs as band-pass filters with learnable parameters controlling tuning frequency and bandwidth, derived from an energy model. 2) Made each layer's bottom-up input prospective using a parameter-free two-tap update that leaves recurrent transition and parallel scan unchanged. 3) Extended the prospective correction to general diagonal SSMs.

Result: Under full BPTT, prospective variants match or outperform non-prospective controls in every model and configuration. A width-32 six-layer RQF achieved 96.09% accuracy on raw-audio Speech Commands with only 31.9k parameters. A width-64 six-layer RQF achieved 83.56% on the challenging 16,384-step Path-X task.

Conclusion: RQFs serve as a parameter-efficient recurrent substrate, and prospective-input coding provides an effective input-side correction for mitigating depth-dependent gradient attenuation in deep continuous-time recurrent networks.

Abstract: Temporal integration gives continuous-time recurrent networks memory, but in deep stacks it also delays bottom-up signals and attenuates top-down errors. We develop Recursive Quadrature Filters (RQFs), biologically motivated complex-valued temporal filters that are a special case of diagonal state-space models (SSMs), and ask whether this failure mode can be addressed by making each layer's bottom-up input prospective. Starting from an energy model, we derive the RQF dynamics and show that each RQF is a band-pass filter whose learnable parameters control its tuning frequency and bandwidth. We then make each layer's bottom-up input prospective using a parameter-free two-tap update that leaves the recurrent transition and parallel scan unchanged. We extend this correction to general diagonal SSMs and show that it mitigates depth-dependent gradient attenuation when temporal gradients are truncated, i.e., spatial-only backpropagation. We evaluate the intervention in RQFs, S5, and ORGaNICs (a nonlinear gated RNN) trained using full backpropagation through time (BPTT) and spatial-only backpropagation. Under full BPTT, prospective variants match or outperform their non-prospective controls in every model and configuration. A non-residual width-32 six-layer RQF reaches 96.09% accuracy on raw-audio Speech Commands with 31.9k parameters; a width-64 six-layer RQF reaches 83.56% on the 16,384-step Path-X task. These results identify RQFs as a parameter-efficient recurrent substrate and prospective-input coding as an input-side correction for deep continuous-time recurrent networks.

</details>


### [271] [A Low-Cost, Open Platform for End-to-End Autonomous Driving on a Miniature Ackermann Vehicle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04147)
*Gustavo Claudio Karl Couto,Eric Aislan Antonelo,Gabriel George Zipperer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A low-cost, open experimental platform for end-to-end autonomous driving using miniature Ackermann vehicles, combining a physical vehicle, printed urban track, and Webots digital twin for sim-to-real research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable controlled experiments connecting simulation-based autonomous driving methods to real-world execution, providing a practical testbed for sim-to-real studies with reproducible research capabilities.

Method: Implemented command-conditioned behavior cloning where a neural policy receives on-board camera images and high-level navigation commands to output steering and speed. Used a digital twin to generate synthetic driving data and a learned sim-to-real image translator to reduce appearance gaps.

Result: Real closed-loop experiments achieved mean cross-track error of 6.1 cm (close to 4.7 cm human demonstrations). In simulation, widening FOV from 58° to 120° reduced error from 35.6 to 3.3 cm. Only the higher-capacity policy trained on synthetic data combined with real demonstrations completed all four track routes in closed loop.

Conclusion: The open platform serves as a practical testbed for sim-to-real studies. The command-conditioned imitation learning baseline demonstrates effective transfer from simulation to real-world execution. The platform is released to support reproducible research.

Abstract: This paper presents a low-cost, open experimental platform for research in end-to-end autonomous driving with miniature Ackermann vehicles. The platform combines a physical vehicle, a printed urban track, data collection tools, trajectory registration, and a Webots digital twin, enabling controlled experiments that connect simulation-based autonomous-driving methods to real-world execution. As a first baseline, we implement command-conditioned behavior cloning, in which a neural policy receives an on-board camera image and a high-level navigation command and outputs steering and speed. The system is evaluated both on the physical vehicle and in simulation. In real closed-loop experiments, the learned policy follows lanes and executes commanded turns, reaching a mean cross-track error of 6.1 cm with respect to the reference route, close to the 4.7 cm observed in human demonstrations. In the digital twin, camera field of view has a strong effect on performance, reducing the mean cross-track error from 35.6 to 3.3 cm when widened from 58 to 120 degrees. Using the digital twin to generate synthetic driving data and a learned sim-to-real image translator to reduce the appearance gap, we further show that a higher-capacity policy trained on this synthetic data combined with real demonstrations is the only configuration that completes all four track routes in closed loop, whereas the compact baseline and the same network trained on real data alone complete fewer. These results establish the open platform as a practical testbed for sim-to-real studies and provide an initial command-conditioned imitation-learning baseline; we release it to support reproducible research.

</details>


### [272] [Robust PAC Learning of Concurrent Stochastic Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04189)
*Angel Y. He,David Parker*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first PAC learning framework for general-sum concurrent stochastic games (CSGs) with transition uncertainty. The algorithm uses data-driven L¹ confidence sets over transition kernels and solves robust CSGs to find social-welfare optimal ε-Nash equilibria, with a mechanism to either return an ε-NE or certify that no exact NE exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address two key challenges in multi-agent reinforcement learning: (1) learning in stochastic games with transition uncertainty, and (2) principled reasoning about Nash equilibrium existence. This is the first PAC framework for general-sum CSGs with these properties.

Method: The method involves: (1) maintaining data-driven L¹ confidence sets over transition kernels, (2) solving a robust CSG to compute social-welfare optimal ε-NE, (3) using a robust MDP-based exploration mechanism for joint state-action coverage, and (4) introducing a Nash margin characterization for equilibrium existence reasoning.

Result: Under a minimum reachability condition p_reach > 0, the algorithm terminates after polynomial trajectory samples with sample complexity Ō(R_max² H⁴ |S|² |A| / (p_reach ε²)). It either returns an ε-approximate NE with optimal social-welfare value, or provides a sound certificate that no exact NE exists. Empirical results show near-optimal performance and correct equilibrium handling.

Conclusion: The framework provides theoretical guarantees for PAC learning of Nash equilibria in general-sum CSGs with transition uncertainty, with polynomial sample complexity and principled handling of equilibrium existence. This represents a significant advance in multi-agent reinforcement learning theory.

Abstract: We introduce the first Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learning framework for general-sum concurrent stochastic games (CSGs) with transition uncertainty, while addressing the challenge of Nash equilibrium (NE) existence. Our algorithm maintains data-driven $L^1$ confidence sets over transition kernels and solves a robust CSG to compute a social-welfare optimal $\varepsilon$-NE, using a robust MDP-based exploration mechanism to drive joint state-action coverage. Crucially, we introduce a Nash margin characterisation that enables principled reasoning about equilibrium existence: the framework either returns an $\varepsilon$-approximate NE whose social-welfare value is $\varepsilon$-close to optimal, or provides a sound certificate that no exact NE exists. Under a minimum reachability condition $p_{\mathrm{reach}}>0$ over relevant state-action pairs, the algorithm terminates after a polynomial number of trajectory samples, with sample complexity $\widetilde{O}\left( {R_{\max}^2 H^4 |S|^2 |A| / (p_{\mathrm{reach}} \varepsilon^2)} \right)$. Empirical results on benchmark CSGs demonstrate near-optimal performance, correct handling of equilibrium (non-)existence, and sample complexity consistent with theory.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [273] [Requirements After the First Edit: Mining Late Requirement Emergence and Rework in Real-World Coding-Agent Sessions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03028)
*Bowen Jiang,Haowei Cheng,Yuhong Fu,Anne Koziolek,Jialong Li,Weixing Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates the problem of late requirement arrivals in coding agent sessions, where users provide requirements after implementation has begun. Analyzing 3,553 SWE-chat sessions, the authors find that requirement arrivals cause roughly twice as much code invalidation (deletion/replacement of prior agent-authored lines) compared to non-requirement edits. This pattern persists throughout sessions and is not mitigated by advance warning in controlled experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents often implement changes before users fully articulate their requirements, mirroring a classic requirements engineering problem where stakeholders cannot express constraints until seeing part of the system. While traditional projects suffer schedule/budget overruns at release-cycle granularity, coding agents face this volatility continuously. Existing work has limitations: benchmarks fix requirements before implementation by design, and observational studies only report pushback frequency without quantifying the actual code invalidation caused by late requirements.

Method: The authors analyzed 3,553 eligible SWE-chat sessions, coding post-implementation requirement arrivals along three dimensions. Where repository state could be replayed, they linked each requirement arrival to a proxy metric: deletion or replacement of prior agent-authored lines. They conducted robustness checks including user-turn and net-deletion validation, and performed a controlled experiment testing delayed disclosure and advance warning effects.

Result: A requirement's arrival is followed by roughly twice as much code invalidation as matched non-requirement edits. This finding is robust to user-turn and net-deletion checks, though causality is not demonstrated. The burden shows no detectable decline over a session and no detected association with operation type once multiplicity is accounted for. The controlled experiment shows delayed disclosure relocates implementation post-reveal, while advance warning produces no detected effect on overwriting.

Conclusion: This work establishes late requirement emergence as a measurable source of code invalidation in coding agent workflows. The ~2x invalidation factor represents a quantifiable cost of volatile requirements in AI-assisted coding. The persistence throughout sessions and lack of mitigation from advance warning suggest inherent challenges in this human-AI collaborative paradigm. Future work could explore causal mechanisms and intervention strategies.

Abstract: Coding agents often implement changes before users have fully articulated their requirements, echoing a pattern from requirements engineering: stakeholders cannot express a constraint until part of the system exists to react to. This volatility is associated with schedule and budget overruns in traditional projects, but only at release-cycle granularity. Existing work on coding agents narrows this gap only partway: curated benchmarks fix requirements before implementation by design, and observational studies report pushback frequency without linking arrivals to the code invalidation they cause. We address this using 3,553 eligible SWE-chat sessions, coding post-implementation requirement arrivals along three dimensions and, where repository state can be replayed, linking each arrival to a proxy: deletion or replacement of prior agent-authored lines. A requirement's arrival is followed by roughly twice as much invalidation as matched non-requirement edits, robust to user-turn and net-deletion checks, though not demonstrated as causal. This burden shows no detectable decline over a session and no detected association with operation type once multiplicity is accounted for; several intervals remain wide. A controlled experiment shows delayed disclosure relocates implementation post-reveal, while advance warning produces no detected effect on overwriting. These results establish late requirement emergence as a measurable source of code invalidation.

</details>


### [274] [Large Language Models and Language Server Protocol: a match made in context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03086)
*Alessandro Schena,Ilgiz Mustafin,Julia Kotovich*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Eiffel-tools is a Language Server Protocol implementation for Eiffel that uses Large Language Models combined with a static verifier to aid in developing statically verified software. It generates programmatic prompts, verifies LLM output, and iteratively retries until verification passes. The tool achieves 76-95% bug-fixing success rates on public datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improve software development by combining LLMs with formal verification, enabling precise code generation and specification using language-specific knowledge while ensuring correctness through static verification.

Method: The method involves using a Language Server Protocol implementation with LLMs, providing interactive/non-interactive commands, crafting rich programmatic prompts, integrating a static verifier for output validation, and implementing retry mechanisms until verification passes.

Result: The tool can fix 76% to 95% of bugs depending on the model and prompts used, demonstrating a trade-off between the number of fixing attempts and success rate.

Conclusion: The combination of LLMs and formal verification is effective for bug fixing, with success rates varying based on model choice and prompt design, suggesting a promising approach for statically verified software development.

Abstract: This article introduces Eiffel-tools, a language server protocol (LSP) implementation for the Eiffel programming language that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to aid the development of statically verified software. The tool provides various interactive and non-interactive commands to produce code and specifications. It uses language and project specific knowledge to precisely direct the LLM and verifies the output using a static verifier. It crafts rich programmatic prompts for the input and corrects or rejects the output. Furthermore, it handles the retries until the program passes verification. The tool's bug fixing capability is evaluated on 2 public datasets using 3 models. The tool can fix 76% to 95% of bugs by combining LLMs and a formal verifier depending on the model and prompts used. The results show the trade-off between the number of fixing attempts and the success rate.

</details>


### [275] [Compound Prompt Constraints in LLM Code Generation: A Factorial Study of Format, Persona, and Urgency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03156)
*Shrenik Jadhav,Nickalsa LaPlaca,Caleb Stone,Ashok Raja,Omar Ochoa,Vidhyashree Nagaraju*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper reveals that compound constraints in LLM code generation prompts (formatting + persona + urgency) cause architecture-dependent super-additive degradation, with GPT-4o family showing 3-12 percentage point drops beyond additive predictions, while GPT-4.1 remains resistant and o3-mini shows improved performance with structured outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production software engineering pipelines combine multiple constraints in LLM prompts, but prior research only examined single factors in isolation. This study addresses the gap by investigating joint effects of output formatting, persona assignment, and urgency framing on code generation reliability.

Method: Full-factorial empirical study with 3×3×3 design (27 combinations). Evaluated on 164 HumanEval+ problems across five models (GPT-4o family, GPT-4.1 family, o3-mini) using greedy decoding, yielding 22,140 evaluations. Used format-aware extraction pipeline to separate formatting from reasoning failures. Assessed significance with McNemar's test, odds ratios, and 95% confidence intervals. Decomposed compound conditions into additive predictions and residual interaction terms.

Result: Compound constraints produce architecture-dependent degradation not predictable from single-factor experiments. GPT-4o family shows consistent super-additive effects with pass@1 reductions 3-12 percentage points beyond additive predictions; largest interaction is -12.2 pp on GPT-4o-mini for JSON + expert persona + moderate urgency. JSON combinations produce larger interactions than XML. GPT-4.1 family is largely resistant. o3-mini shows qualitatively different pattern where structured output constraints can improve performance.

Conclusion: Vulnerability to compound constraints is architecture-dependent rather than size-dependent. Individually neutral or beneficial constraints can combine to cause substantial degradation. Compound-prompt testing should be standard in reliability assessment for LLM-assisted engineering pipelines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in software engineering pipelines for code generation, where production prompts often combine multiple constraints. This paper presents a full-factorial empirical study of how output formatting, persona assignment, and urgency framing jointly affect LLM code-generation reliability. We evaluate all 27 combinations in a controlled 3x3x3 design and decompose each compound condition into an additive prediction and a residual interaction term that captures super-additive degradation. The study uses all 164 HumanEval+ problems across five OpenAI models from the GPT-4o family, GPT-4.1 family, and o3-mini, yielding 22,140 greedy-decoding evaluations. A format-aware extraction pipeline separates formatting failures from reasoning failures, and significance is assessed with McNemar's test, odds ratios, and 95% confidence intervals.
  Results show that compound constraints can produce architecture-dependent degradation not predictable from single-factor experiments. The GPT-4o family exhibits consistent super-additive effects, with pass@1 reductions 3-12 percentage points beyond additive predictions; the largest interaction is -12.2 pp on GPT-4o-mini for JSON + expert persona + moderate urgency. JSON combinations generally produce larger interactions than XML. In contrast, the GPT-4.1 family is largely resistant, while o3-mini shows a qualitatively different pattern in which structured output constraints can improve performance. These findings show that vulnerability is architecture-dependent rather than size-dependent, that individually neutral or beneficial constraints can combine to cause substantial degradation, and that compound-prompt testing should be standard in reliability assessment for LLM-assisted engineering pipelines.

</details>


### [276] [Two Truths and A Lie? Benchmarking Off-the-Shelf LLMs for Requirements Quality Assessment: Performance, False Alarms, and Misses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03230)
*Jannatul Shefa,Alejandro Salado,Paul Wach,Taylan G. Topcu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First benchmarking study evaluating off-the-shelf LLMs (10 models from OpenAI and Anthropic across 5 generations) for requirements quality assessment against expert-derived ground truth. Results show LLMs detect only 47% of issues while false-flagging 11%, with performance degrading when systems engineering judgment is required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Requirements engineering governs downstream quality in systems engineering; defective requirements cause rework and cost overruns. LLMs are being explored for automated quality assessment, but empirical evidence on their trustworthiness is scarce.

Method: Benchmarking analysis of 10 LLM models (OpenAI and Anthropic families, 5 generations each) across 100 independent runs, 2 requirement sets, and 5 sampling temperatures, evaluated against expert-derived ground truth based on INCOSE quality criteria.

Result: 1) Asymmetric error profile: best Anthropic model detects only 47% of expert-identified issues while false-flagging 11%; 2) Performance significantly degrades where SE judgment required (necessity/correctness issues almost always missed); 3) Non-monotonic generational progress (newer models not necessarily better); 4) Temperature effects are modest and non-monotonic, indicating characteristic deficiencies rather than stochasticity.

Conclusion: Off-the-shelf LLMs are not yet trustworthy autonomous evaluators for requirements quality. Caution warranted for Agentic AI developers - orchestrating these LLM modules may compound deficiencies. Near-term role should be human-in-the-loop decision support.

Abstract: Requirements engineering (RE) governs the quality of everything downstream in systems engineering (SE); defective requirements that survive review cycles propagate into design rework, schedule delays, and cost overruns. Because requirements are often written in natural language, recent advances in generative AI have raised expectations that large language models (LLMs) can absorb requirement quality assessment, a task otherwise slow and human expertise-intensive. Yet empirical evidence on whether LLMs can be trusted to do so remains scarce. This study presents the first benchmarking analysis of off-the-shelf LLM performance for requirement quality evaluation. Against an expert-derived ground truth built on INCOSE quality criteria, we evaluate ten models spanning two families (OpenAI and Anthropic) and five generations each, across one hundred independent runs, two requirement sets, and five sampling temperatures. Four contributions follow. First, we quantify a strongly asymmetric error profile: across all models and runs, the best-performing Anthropic model detects a median of only 47% of expert-identified issues while false-flagging 11%. Second, performance degrades significantly where SE judgment is required, as necessity and correctness issues are almost always missed. Third, generational progress is non-monotonic, so newer models cannot be assumed better. Fourth, this error behavior shifts only modestly and non-monotonically across sampling temperatures, indicating characteristic model deficiencies rather than inherent stochasticity. Off-the-shelf LLMs are therefore not yet trustworthy autonomous evaluators. Findings also warrant caution for Agentic AI developers: orchestrating these LLM modules in specialized architectures risks compounding these deficiencies rather than correcting them. Their defensible near-term role is human-in-the-loop decision support.

</details>


### [277] [Refusing the Impossible: A Taxonomy and Benchmark for Code Hallucination in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03267)
*Vishnu Asutosh Dasu,Ashish Kundu,Gang Tan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces "code hallucination" as a distinct problem where LLMs generate code that looks plausible but is ungrounded in reality (e.g., importing non-existent packages or implementing algorithmically impossible solutions). The authors propose a 3-dimensional taxonomy (groundedness, manifestation level, behavior) and test 12 models on 270 adversarial unsolvable prompts and 91 solvable controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often produce code that compiles and runs but is not grounded in reality - importing non-existent packages or implementing algorithms that violate proven theorems. This "code hallucination" is distinct from ordinary bugs in grounded programs, and requires systematic study.

Method: The authors propose a 3-dimensional taxonomy: (1) groundedness (absolute violations vs. relative fabrications), (2) manifestation level (syntactic, semantic, factual), and (3) behavior (confident fabrication to degenerate output). They build an adversarial suite of 270 unsatisfiable prompts across 6 languages and 24 subcategories, plus 91 matched solvable controls. Responses are judged by a two-tier protocol validated against human labels (82% agreement, κ=0.73).

Result: Across 12 open-weight code and reasoning models (4,332 judged responses), models produce ungrounded code on about 60% of unsatisfiable prompts and refuse only 27%, while wrongly refusing 0% of the solvable controls.

Conclusion: LLMs frequently generate ungrounded code when faced with unsolvable tasks, with only 27% refusal rate. The problem is significant as such code can appear syntactically correct and run without errors while being fundamentally incorrect. The proposed taxonomy provides a useful framework for understanding code hallucination.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often produce code that looks plausible but is not grounded in reality. The code may import packages that do not exist or claim to implement algorithms that violate proven theorems, while still compiling and running.
