We study \emph{code hallucination} as \emph{ungrounded generation} and separate it from ordinary \emph{code error} (bugs in otherwise grounded programs). We propose a taxonomy with three dimensions: \textbf{groundedness} (absolute violations of universal truths vs.\ relative fabrications of contingent or ecosystem-specific facts), \textbf{manifestation level} (syntactic, semantic, or factual), and \textbf{behavior} (from confident fabrication to degenerate output), organized into a severity ordering. We build an \textbf{adversarial} suite of deliberately unsatisfiable tasks where the correct response is to refuse and categorize the responses under our taxonomy. The suite contains \textbf{270 prompts} across six languages and 24 subcategories, paired with \textbf{91 matched solvable controls}, and responses are judged by a two-tier protocol validated against human labels (82\% agreement, $κ{=}0.73$). Across twelve open-weight code and reasoning models (4{,}332 judged responses), models produce ungrounded code on about 60\% of unsatisfiable prompts and refuse only 27\%, while wrongly refusing 0\% of the solvable controls.

</details>


### [278] [TIPCODER: Reinforcement Learning Boosted Test-time Instruction Proposer for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03309)
*Minyu Chen,Sihao Wu,Ling-I Wu,Song Qin,Jingyang Li,Lei Ning,Jianxin Xue,Guoqiang Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TipCoder is a test-time instruction proposer that generates problem-specific auxiliary tips to improve code generation by addressing missing constraints, edge cases, and misleading reasoning paths in original prompts. It uses RL with marginal-utility reward to optimize the proposer, and applies exploration-selection design with Reward Model for post-hoc selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many coding failures stem from missing constraints, overlooked edge cases, or misleading reasoning paths induced by the original prompt. Current test-time scaling for code generation explores solution space by sampling, but instance-level instruction-space exploration is underexplored.

Method: 1) Distill multi-turn debugging trajectories into proactive guidance; 2) Optimize Proposer with reinforcement learning using marginal-utility reward; 3) At inference: generate both base solution and tip-guided solution; 4) Apply Reward Model for post-hoc selection between candidates.

Result: TipCoder provides consistent instruction-level test-time scaling strategy across evaluated code-generation benchmarks and target Code LLMs, comparing favorably with stochastic sampling and generic prompt optimization baselines under shared reward-model-based selection protocol.

Conclusion: Instruction-space exploration (generating problem-specific tips) is a complementary direction to solution-space exploration for test-time scaling in code generation. The exploration-selection design allows tips to expose additional candidate potential while reducing regressions from unnecessary guidance.

Abstract: Test-time scaling for code generation typically explores the solution space by sampling multiple programs from a fixed instruction. We study a complementary direction: instance-level instruction-space exploration. Our observation is that many coding failures stem from missing constraints, overlooked edge cases, or misleading reasoning paths induced by the original prompt. To address this, we propose TipCoder, a test-time instruction proposer that generates problem-specific auxiliary tips before code synthesis. TipCoder distills multi-turn debugging trajectories into proactive guidance and further optimizes the Proposer with reinforcement learning using a marginal-utility reward. At inference time, it generates both a base solution and a tip-guided solution, and applies a Reward Model for post-hoc selection. This exploration-selection design allows tips to expose additional candidate potential while reducing regressions from unnecessary guidance. Across the evaluated code-generation benchmarks and target Code LLMs, TipCoder provides a consistent instruction-level test-time scaling strategy, comparing favorably with stochastic sampling and generic prompt optimization baselines under a shared reward-model-based selection protocol.

</details>


### [279] [The Psychological Costs of Artificial Intelligence Adoption in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03456)
*Adam Alami,Elda Paja,Abhishek Tiwari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is increasingly used to augment software engineering (SE) workflows. While code generation remains the main use case, organizations are actively seeking AI integration in other practices such as test cases generation and code reviews. Organizational AI adoption strategies seem to focus on tangible outcomes such as productivity. However, AI is a disruptive force, introduced into settings where role identity, team norms, and the sources of job satisfaction were well established before the recent advances in generative AI. Historically, technological disruptions have caused psychological and social strains in workplaces, ranging from anxiety and eroded meaning to deskilling and disrupted professional identities. The assumption that AI for SE is cost-free may not be accurate. Therefore, in this study we sought to understand the psychological costs software professionals experience during organizational AI adoption. We carried out a case study in a large software development services company, one year after the company launched its AI adoption. We collected qualitative data through meetings and semi-structured interviews (N = 21). We found that software professionals experience accountability anxiety, craft identity disruption, meaning and satisfaction erosion, cognitive and workload intensification, and uncertainty distress. Practitioners manage these costs through practices that restore control, mitigate them through protective and identity-preserving adaptations, or absorb them, carrying what neither can resolve. We contribute to AI-human collaboration in SE by repositioning AI adoption as a human transition, not only a technological and organizational one.

</details>


### [280] [Code Transformation Rule Synthesis using LLMs: Potential and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03592)
*Axel Allain,Aymeric Blot,Djamel Eddine Khelladi,Mathieu Acher*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates three LLMs (GPT-5.4, GPT-oss-120B, Llama3.1-8B) on transformation rule synthesis using three domain-specific languages (Comby, GritQL, Ast-Grep) across six datasets covering four software evolution tasks (API misuse correction, program repair, API migration, language version migration).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs have limited explainability, lack determinism, and can be costly for repetitive tasks on large codebases. The study aims to evaluate whether LLMs can effectively synthesize transformation rules for software evolution tasks.

Method: Empirical study comparing three LLMs on six datasets across four software evolution tasks. Evaluated rule applicability, correctness, generalizability (via meta-variables), and reuse potential. Compared LLM performance against anti-unification algorithm.

Result: GPT-5.4 achieves highest rule applicability and produces transformations closest to ground truth. Smaller models (GPT-oss-120B, Llama3.1-8B) work well for simple tasks but struggle with complex migrations. LLMs outperform anti-unification in correctness but underperform in rule applicability. High reuse scores observed in first quartile of datasets.

Conclusion: LLMs show great potential for generating sound, correct, generalizable, and reusable transformation rules for software evolution tasks, with strong performance from frontier models like GPT-5.4.

Abstract: Due to their black-box nature, LLMs suffer from limited explain- ability and a lack of determinism. Their usage cost can also rise, particularly with repetitive tasks on large codebases. To mitigate this, we conduct a novel empirical study targeting three domain- specific languages for transformation rules, namely Comby, GritQL, and Ast-Grep. We evaluate three LLMs (GPT-5.4, GPT-oss-120B, and Llama3.1-8B) on six diverse datasets covering four software- evolution tasks: API misuse correction, program repair, API migra- tion, and language version migration. Our results provide evidence that transformation rule synthesis moves beyond proof-of-concept with strong frontier models. GPT-5.4 achieves consistently high rule applicability rates and produces transformations closest to the ground truth across most benchmarks. Smaller and open-weight GPT-oss-120B and Llama3.1-8B models remain effective for simpler, localized changes but struggle with complex migration scenarios. We also observe non-negligible generalizability through the usage of meta-variables and through a high reuse score in the first quartile of many datasets. Finally, when compared to the anti-unification algorithm, LLMs outperform it in correctness, but underperform in rule applicability. Overall, our results show great potential for LLMs to generate sound, correct, generalizable, and reusable rules.

</details>


### [281] [Can LLMs Extract Architectural Design Decisions from Source Code Commits? - A Preliminary Exploratory Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03721)
*Amey Karan,Rudra Dhar,Mohamed Soliman,Karthik Vaidhyanathan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates four LLMs (Gemini 3 Pro, DeepSeek R1, Kimi K2, Qwen3) for extracting Architectural Design Decisions from source code commits, achieving BERT-F1 > 0.81 but with limitations including excessive length and missing rationale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Architectural Design Decisions (ADDs) are rarely explicitly documented and are often hidden in source code commits. Recovering them is important for Architectural Knowledge Management (AKM), but extracting ADDs from commits is challenging due to their implicit and unstructured nature. LLMs show promise for this task but their effectiveness remains underexplored.

Method: The study used four LLMs (Gemini 3 Pro, DeepSeek R1, Kimi K2, Qwen3) with zero-shot and few-shot prompting on 30 developer-written ADDs from open-source projects. Outputs were evaluated using ROUGE-L, BLEU, METEOR, and BERTScore metrics. One author manually reviewed the Gemini outputs.

Result: All models achieved BERT-F1 above 0.81. Few-shot prompting improved alignment (Gemini BERT-F1: 0.828 to 0.847). However, generated ADDs were often too long, implementation-focused, and missed the rationale behind the decision.

Conclusion: The study highlights opportunities for architecture-aware LLM systems and automated AKM. Key limitations include: generated ADDs are too verbose, focus on implementation details rather than architectural rationale, and miss the underlying reasoning behind decisions.

Abstract: Context: Architectural Design Decisions (ADDs) capture the rationale behind the structure and evolution of software systems but are rarely documented explicitly, and are often hidden inside source code commits. Recovering them is important for Architectural Knowledge Management (AKM). Problem: Extracting ADDs from commits is challenging due to their implicit and unstructured nature. Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in understanding code and text, yet their effectiveness for this task remains underexplored. Study: We present a preliminary study using four LLMs (Gemini 3 Pro, DeepSeek R1, Kimi K2, Qwen3) with zeroshot and fewshot prompting on 30 developer-written ADDs from open-source projects. We score outputs with ROUGE-L, BLEU, METEOR, and BERTScore, and one author manually reviews the Gemini outputs. Results: All models reach a BERT-F1 above 0.81, and fewshot prompting improves alignment (Gemini BERT-F1: 0.828 to 0.847). However, the generated ADDs are often too long, implementation-focused, and miss the rationale behind the decision. This highlights opportunities for architecture-aware LLM systems and automated AKM.

</details>


### [282] [No One Left Behind: Cross-Level Analysis for Sustainable Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03861)
*Masoum Salehi,Sandro Schulze,Jacob Krüger*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This vision paper introduces "sustainability antipatterns" as a conceptual framework to capture systemic conditions that produce unsustainable outcomes in software engineering. The authors argue that sustainability challenges are not isolated but emerge from interactions across organizational, process, and technical levels, and propose this framework to help identify cross-level misalignments that are difficult to recognize through traditional artifact-centric or dimension-specific analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current software engineering research addresses sustainability through fragmented approaches (energy efficiency, resource optimization, code maintainability) without explaining how sustainability problems emerge and reinforce across socio-technical levels. The authors argue that sustainability challenges are systemic rather than isolated, stemming from interactions among organizational priorities, development practices, and technical conditions. Existing research lacks a unified framework to expose these cross-level misalignments.

Method: This is a vision paper that proposes a conceptual framework. The authors introduce "sustainability antipatterns" as a way to capture conditions that systematically produce unsustainable outcomes. They demonstrate how this concept can expose cross-level misalignments between socio-technical levels and sustainability dimensions. The paper outlines future research directions for identifying, formalizing, detecting, and mitigating these antipatterns.

Result: The paper introduces the concept of sustainability antipatterns as a foundation for an integrated research agenda on sustainable software engineering. It provides a framework for understanding how sustainability problems emerge across organizational, process, and product levels, and offers concrete directions for future research in this area.

Conclusion: The authors envision sustainability antipatterns as a foundation for an integrated and actionable research agenda on sustainable software engineering. This framework aims to help researchers and practitioners recognize systemic sustainability challenges that span multiple socio-technical levels, moving beyond isolated dimension-specific analyses toward a more holistic understanding of software sustainability.

Abstract: Software engineers expect a software system to be efficient, maintainable, economically viable, and socially responsible throughout its lifecycle. Unfortunately, decisions made at the organizational, process, or product levels of software development - even when they improve one of these goals - often have unintended long-term consequences at other levels. In response, software-engineering research has explored different ways to achieve software sustainability, including energy efficiency, resource optimization, and code maintainability. However, existing research offers limited explanations for how sustainability problems emerge and reinforce one another across the socio-technical levels of software engineering. Moreover, we argue that sustainability challenges are not isolated but rather systemic: They stem from interactions among organizational priorities, development practices, and technical conditions. In this vision paper, we introduce the concept of sustainability antipatterns to capture conditions that systematically produce unsustainable outcomes. We demonstrate how this concept can help expose cross-level misalignments between socio-technical levels and sustainability dimensions that remain difficult to recognize through artifact-centric or dimension-specific analyses. To guide future research, we outline concrete directions for identifying, formalizing, detecting, and mitigating sustainability anti-patterns. We envision sustainability anti-patterns as a foundation for an integrated and actionable research agenda on sustainable software engineering.

</details>


### [283] [LabelMate: An LLM-Driven Framework for Refined Issue Report Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04055)
*Liam Johnston,Shayan Noei,Maram Assi,Ying Zou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LabelMate is an LLM-driven framework that automatically derives project-specific labels from historical issue reports and assigns labels to new issues without requiring pre-labeled training data, achieving 89.84% accuracy on 16,500 issues from 30 GitHub repositories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Issue reports in software repositories often remain unlabeled due to the substantial manual effort required to design label taxonomies and assign labels. Existing automated approaches have limitations including extensive manual intervention, generic labels, and dependence on pre-labeled datasets.

Method: LabelMate uses Large Language Models to (1) derive a comprehensive, project-specific label set from historical issue reports and (2) automatically assign relevant labels to new issue reports without requiring any pre-labeled training data. Evaluated on 16,500 issue reports from 30 popular GitHub repositories.

Result: The approach generates 275 coherent project-specific labels and achieves an average labeling accuracy of 89.84%, which is a statistically significant improvement over existing generic label assigning approaches.

Conclusion: LabelMate offers an efficient, domain-adaptive solution to streamline the issue labeling process, eliminating the need for pre-labeled training data while providing project-specific labels rather than generic ones.

Abstract: Software users often submit issue reports to a product's issue tracking system to report defects, suggest enhancements, or raise other product-related concerns. Labeling these issue reports supports effective planning and improves community engagement. However, many issue reports remain unlabeled due to the substantial manual effort required to design an appropriate label taxonomy, then assign suitable labels from this taxonomy to new issue reports. Existing automated labeling approaches attempt to mitigate these challenges. However, they suffer from key limitations, such as extensive manual intervention, the assignment of generic labels, and a dependence on existing labeled datasets. To address these limitations, we propose LabelMate, a novel Large Language Model (LLM)-driven framework that (1) derives a comprehensive, project-specific label set from historical issue reports and (2) automatically assigns relevant labels to new issue reports without requiring any pre-labeled training data. We evaluate LabelMate on 16,500 issue reports from 30 popular and diverse GitHub repositories. Based on this dataset, our approach generates a coherent list of 275 labels and achieves an average labeling accuracy of 89.84%, a statistically significant improvement over existing generic label assigning approaches. These results demonstrate that LabelMate offers an efficient, domain-adaptive solution to streamline the issue labeling process.

</details>


### [284] [When Models Edit Too Much: On the Fidelity of Minimal Code Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04061)
*Tongyao Zhu,Wei Hern Lim,Min-Yen Kan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies "over-editing" in LLMs—where models rewrite more code than necessary to fix bugs. Using 400 BigCodeBench problems with injected AST-level corruptions, the authors find that even frontier models like GPT-5.5 exhibit over-editing. A preservation instruction reduces excess edits by 33% and improves Pass@1 by 2.3 points. Reinforcement learning outperforms supervised fine-tuning for learning minimal editing in out-of-domain settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are increasingly used for code editing, correctness alone is insufficient—useful repairs should also be minimal, reviewable, and faithful to the original implementation. The authors identify "over-editing" as a critical but overlooked quality dimension in code repair.

Method: The authors construct an evaluation framework from 400 BigCodeBench problems by injecting controlled AST-level corruptions into reference solutions, creating tasks with known minimal patches. They measure over-editing using excess Levenshtein distance and added cognitive complexity. They test frontier LLMs, evaluate a preservation instruction, and compare supervised fine-tuning versus reinforcement learning for learning minimal editing.

Result: Over-editing is widespread across frontier LLMs including GPT-5.5. A preservation instruction reduces average excess Levenshtein distance from 0.195 to 0.131 (33% reduction), decreases added cognitive complexity by 26.6%, and increases Pass@1 by 2.3 points. Supervised fine-tuning overfits to seen corruption patterns, while reinforcement learning provides the best out-of-domain edit-fidelity and performance-retention trade-off.

Conclusion: Edit fidelity is a distinct and measurable axis of code-repair quality that can be learned. Reinforcement learning is superior to supervised fine-tuning for achieving minimal edits in unseen scenarios, positioning over-editing reduction as a new optimization target for code LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to edit existing code, but correctness alone is not enough: useful repairs should also be minimal, reviewable, and faithful to the original implementation. We study over-editing, the tendency of a model to rewrite code beyond what is required to fix a bug. We construct an evaluation framework from 400 BigCodeBench problems by injecting controlled AST-level corruptions into reference solutions, giving each repair task a known minimal patch. Across frontier LLMs, over-editing is widespread even among strong models like GPT-5.5: high Pass@1 can coexist with unnecessarily large edits and added cognitive complexity. A preservation instruction substantially reduces this behavior, lowering average excess Levenshtein distance from 0.195 to 0.131, reducing added cognitive complexity by 26.6%, and increasing Pass@1 by 2.3 points. However, these gains do not simply follow from a larger reasoning budget or larger models. We next ask whether minimal editing can be learned directly during post-training. We observe that supervised fine-tuning overfits to seen corruption patterns, whereas reinforcement learning gives the best out-of-domain edit-fidelity and performance-retention trade-off. These results position edit fidelity as a distinct axis of code-repair quality and show that it can be measured and learned.

</details>


### [285] [ATIBA: Grounded Integrity and Quality Checking for Research Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04123)
*Veli Karakaya,Semih Çağlar,Yusuf Yiğit Korkmaz,Eray Tüzün*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ATIBA is a tool that automates five manuscript quality checks: reference integrity verification, venue/track compliance validation against Call-for-Papers pages, empirical standards compliance against ACM SIGSOFT standards, multi-mode AI review using GPT-5.4, and citation suggestion. It was evaluated through a user study with 13 participants, showing 85% mean agreement on perceived usefulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual manuscript checks (reference integrity, venue compliance, reporting standards) are repetitive, venue-specific, and often skipped or done inconsistently in practice.

Method: ATIBA implements five grounded checks: (1) reference-integrity check verifying citations against bibliographic sources; (2) venue/track compliance check derived from CFP pages with verbatim quotes; (3) empirical-standards compliance check against ACM SIGSOFT Empirical Standards with hallucination defense; (4) multi-mode AI review (venue-specific, formal, page-anchored) via GPT-5.4/Azure OpenAI; (5) citation-suggestion with source verification. All checks follow the principle: LLM judges but never invents evidence. Evaluated via moderated user study with 13 non-author participants.

Result: Agreement across six survey items ranged 69-92%, with mean of 85%, providing initial evidence of positive perceived usefulness across evaluated workflows.

Conclusion: ATIBA shows promise for perceived usefulness; objective accuracy remains to be measured. The tool addresses a real need but requires further validation.

Abstract: Checking a manuscript's reference integrity, its compliance with a target venue's specific submission rules, and its adherence to community reporting standards is manual, repetitive, and different for every venue so in practice it is done inconsistently or skipped. We present ATIBA, a tool that runs five grounded integrity and quality checks on a manuscript: a reference-integrity check that verifies each citation against bibliographic sources and flags retracted or unfindable references; a venue/track compliance check that derives submission criteria directly from a venue's own call-for-papers page and evaluates the manuscript against them, each verdict anchored to a verbatim quote from that page; an empirical-standards compliance check against the ACM SIGSOFT Empirical Standards, with a hallucination defence that discards any evidence quote it cannot locate verbatim in the manuscript; a multi-mode AI review (venue-specific, formal, and page-anchored annotation) powered by GPT-5.4 through Azure OpenAI; and a citation-suggestion feature that proposes candidate references for a manuscript and verifies each against bibliographic sources before it is shown to the user. All five checks are designed around the same principle: an LLM is only trusted to judge, never to invent the evidence it judges against. We evaluated ATIBA through a moderated user study with 13 non-author participants. Agreement across the six survey items ranged from 69% to 92%, with a mean of 85%, providing initial evidence of positive perceived usefulness across the evaluated workflows. These findings establish perceived usefulness; objective accuracy remains to be measured.

</details>


### [286] [SWE-Gate: Passing Functional Tests Is Not Enough for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04167)
*Xin He,Yanlin Wang,Mingwei Liu,Jiachi Chen,Hongyu Zhang,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SWE-Gate is a repository-level benchmark for software engineering agents that evaluates both functional correctness and review constraint compliance. It uses 303 repair instances from 75 Python repositories, deriving review constraints from real pull request comments. The benchmark reveals a significant gap: 221 of 644 functionally successful repairs failed to satisfy review constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing repository-level software engineering benchmarks only measure functional correctness (whether patches pass tests) but ignore review-derived acceptance constraints that determine patch acceptability in real-world development. This functional-only evaluation overestimates agents' actual capabilities.

Method: SWE-Gate derives review constraints from real pull request review comments and synthesizes repository-level repair instances around these constraints. Each instance includes separate functional and constraint tests, plus non-compliant and gold patches. Experiments tested four LLM backends with different capability levels under a common coding-agent scaffold.

Result: Among 644 repairs that passed functional tests, 221 failed to satisfy the provided review constraints. This represents a substantial gap between functional success and success under the complete repair specification.

Conclusion: Functional-only evaluation overestimates agents' ability to satisfy the full requirements of repository-level repair tasks. The benchmark provides a more realistic evaluation by incorporating review constraints alongside functional correctness.

Abstract: Repository-level software engineering benchmarks have significantly advanced the evaluation of coding agents, but existing benchmarks primarily measure whether generated patches pass functional tests and overlook review-derived acceptance constraints (review constraints) that often influence whether a patch is acceptable in real-world software development. We introduce SWE-Gate, a repository-level benchmark for software engineering agents that explicitly evaluates review constraint compliance alongside functional correctness. SWE-Gate derives review constraints from real pull request review comments and synthesizes repository-level repair instances around these constraints. Each instance provides separate functional and constraint tests, together with non-compliant and gold patches, enabling explicit separation between issue resolution capability and review constraint compliance. We construct SWE-Gate with 303 repository-level repair instances spanning 75 open-source Python repositories across diverse software domains. Experiments with four LLM backends spanning different capability levels under a common coding-agent scaffold reveal a substantial gap between functional success and success under the complete repair specification: among 644 repairs that pass the functional tests, 221 fail to satisfy the provided review constraints. These findings show that functional-only evaluation overestimates agents' ability to satisfy the full requirements of repository-level repair tasks. The replication package including code, data, and experimental results is available at https://github.com/DeepSoftwareAnalytics/SWE-Gate.

</details>


### [287] [ExecRetrieval: Measuring the Functional-Correctness Gap in Code-Embedding Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01865)
*Aaryan Kapoor,Md Abdullah Al Hafiz Khan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ExecRetrieval is a new benchmark for evaluating code retrieval systems that contains execution-verified buggy variants (near-identical to canonical implementations) to test whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct from incorrect code. The benchmark includes 939 Python tasks with paired canonical/buggy implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code-retrieval benchmarks lack execution-verified buggy variants of canonical implementations, leaving unanswered whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct code from near-clone-but-incorrect code in retrieval settings.

Method: Created ExecRetrieval with 939 Python tasks, each paired with one execution-verified canonical implementation and up to four execution-verified buggy distractors generated by mechanical mutations (single targeted edits). Evaluated 23 dense embedding configurations plus BM25 using paired McNemar tests and query-level bootstrap intervals.

Result: Top hosted system achieves exec@10 = 1.00 but only exec@1 = 0.331. Rank-1 misses are paired with buggy variants 91.5-99.4% of the time across four leading systems. Canonical implementations score below at least one distractor in 67-78% of queries.

Conclusion: Even leading code retrieval systems struggle with functional discrimination—they can find relevant code in top-10 results but fail at rank-1 when distinguishing correct from near-clone incorrect code. The benchmark reveals a significant gap between syntactic retrieval and functional correctness.

Abstract: Embedding-based code retrieval is a core component of coding agents and retrieval-augmented code generation, where retrieving correct code matters more than retrieving lexically similar code. Existing code-retrieval benchmarks do not plant controlled, execution-verified single-edit variants of each query's canonical implementation in the search pool, leaving the question of whether embeddings can functionally discriminate correct from near-clone-but-incorrect code unanswered in a retrieval setting. Resolving this requires a benchmark whose search pool itself contains the relevant counterfactuals -- execution-verified buggy variants near-identical to each canonical -- so that a retriever's rank ordering can be directly tested for functional discrimination rather than topical or identity overlap. We introduce ExecRetrieval, 939 Python tasks each paired with one execution-verified canonical implementation and up to four execution-verified buggy distractors, each generated by a mechanical mutation making a single targeted edit, and evaluate 23 dense embedding configurations plus BM25 under provider-native invocation with paired McNemar tests and query-level bootstrap intervals. With near-clone counterfactuals in the pool, the top hosted system reaches exec@10 = 1.00 but only exec@1 = 0.331; rank-1 misses are paired buggy variants 91.5-99.4% of the time across the four leading systems, and the canonical scores below at least one of its four paired distractors in 67-78% of queries on the leading systems. The full dataset, execution oracle, embedding matrices, environment snapshot, and pairwise statistical tests are released at the URL in Appendix D.

</details>
